On the structure and representations of Quantum graph algebras at roots of unity
Stéphane Baseilhac 1 , Matthieu Faitg 2 , Philippe Roche 3

Abstract
. We study the specializations ℒ g , n ϵ \displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon} at roots of unity ϵ \displaystyle\epsilon of odd order of the graph algebras, associated to a simply-connected complex semi-simple algebraic group G \displaystyle G and a compact oriented surface Σ g , n ∘ \displaystyle\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ} with genus g \displaystyle g , n \displaystyle n punctures, and one boundary component. We prove that the central localizations of ℒ g , n ϵ \displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon} , and its subalgebra ℒ g , n u ϵ \displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}} of invariant elements under the coadjoint action of a small quantum group, are central simple algebras of PI degrees that we compute. Also, we describe their centers, and show they are integrally closed rings.



1,3
IMAG, Univ Montpellier, CNRS, Montpellier, France.

2
Univ Toulouse, CNRS, IMT, Toulouse, France.

1
stephane.baseilhac@umontpellier.fr,
2
matthieu.faitg@univ-tlse3.fr,
3
philippe.roche@umontpellier.fr

Keywords: Hopf algebras, quantum groups, invariant theory, representation theory, quantized character varieties

AMS subject classification 2020: 17B37, 20G42, 16T20

1.
Introduction

Let
\displaystyle G
be a simply-connected complex semi-simple algebraic group, and
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}
its Lie algebra. Let
\displaystyle\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ}
be a compact oriented surface of genus
\displaystyle g
, with
\displaystyle n
punctures and one boundary component.

The quantum graph algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
is a quantization of the algebra of functions on the variety of representations
\displaystyle{\rm Hom}_{\rm Grp}\bigl(\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ}),G\bigr)
endowed with the Fock-Rosly Poisson structure
[
40
]
. It was introduced in the mid ’
\displaystyle 90
s by Alekseev-Grosse-Schomerus
[
1
,
2
,
3
,
4
]
and Buffenoir-Roche
[
24
,
25
]
, and has been actively studied in recent years in connection with non-semisimple TQFTs (see, e.g., the survey
[
45
]
).
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
is a module-algebra over the Drinfeld-Jimbo quantum envelopping algebra
\displaystyle U_{q}
of
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}
, and can be naturally identified with
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}^{\otimes(2g+n)}
as a module with a coadjoint action of
\displaystyle U_{q}
, where
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}
is the quantum coordinate algebra of
\displaystyle G
. There is an explicit isomorphism (called “holonomy”) between
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
and the stated
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}
-skein algebra of
\displaystyle\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ,\bullet}
, the surface
\displaystyle\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ}
with one marked point on the boundary component
[
14
,
39
]
. The subalgebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle U_{q}$}}}
of
\displaystyle U_{q}
-invariant elements of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
quantizes the character variety
\displaystyle{\rm Hom}_{\rm Grp}\bigl(\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ}),G\bigr)/\!\!/G
, and the holonomy isomorphism identifies it with the
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}
-skein algebra of
\displaystyle\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ}
. Thus, we see that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
provides tools from quantum group theory to study the skein algebras and their representations.

Studying these representations when
\displaystyle q
is specialized to roots of unity is a challenging problem, both from the perspectives of quantum group theory and quantum topology, where these representations are expected to form state spaces of TQFTs defined for manifolds endowed with
\displaystyle G
-characters of the fundamental group.

In this paper we tackle this problem for the specializations
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
at roots of unity
\displaystyle\epsilon
of odd order (plus some mild assumptions), and its subalgebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}}
of invariant elements under the action of a small quantum group
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}
. The algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}}
should not be confused with the specialization of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle U_{q}$}}}
at
\displaystyle\epsilon
, which it contains strictly. That specialization was denoted by
\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{g,n}^{A,\epsilon}
in
[
12
]
. It can be shown that
\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{g,n}^{A,\epsilon}\cong(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}})^{U(\mathfrak{g})}
; details will appear in
[
13
]
.

Our main results are that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}}
are domains whose central localizations are central simple algebras, the computation of their PI-degrees, and a complete description of their centers, which are integrally closed rings. From classical tools of PI ring theory we can deduce the maximal dimension of the irreducible representations of
\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}=\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
or
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}}
, and identify the
Azumaya locus
of
\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}
(that is, the set of central characters of the irreducible representations of
\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}
whose dimension is maximal) with the complement of the discriminant subvariety of
\displaystyle{\rm MaxSpec}(\mathcal{Z}(\mathfrak{A}))
.

An intermediate result within our constructions is a surjective morphism from
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
to the quantum graph algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(u_{\epsilon})
associated to a small quantum group
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}
(see Th.
1.2
). It is known
[
38
,
28
]
that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(u_{\epsilon})
is the algebra of endomorphisms of the state space of Kerler–Lyubashenko’s TQFT for the modular category
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}\text{-}\mathrm{mod}
(
[
50
]
, also see
[
17
]
for a more recent approach to this TQFT), so one can expect this result will have interesting consequences in quantum topology.

For
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sl}_{m+1}
and
\displaystyle n=0
, the PI-degree of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,0}^{\epsilon}
could be derived from
[
49
, Th. 4]
, by using the holonomy isomorphism of
[
14
, Th. 2]
, mentioned above, between
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
and the stated
\displaystyle\mathfrak{sl}_{m+1}
-skein algebra of
\displaystyle\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ,\bullet}
. A combinatorial description of the center of that stated skein algebra is given in
[
49
, Th. 2]
.

As compared to skein theoretic tools like those used in
[
49
]
, an advantage of our treatment of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
is that we can deal with all complex simple Lie algebras
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}
, and we do it in an uniform way. No presentation of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
by generators and relations is required, and tools from invariant theory can be used to study subalgebras of invariant elements like
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}}
, for instance describe the structure of its center.

For
\displaystyle n=0
, the algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,0}^{\epsilon}
and the specialization of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,0}^{{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle U_{q}$}}}
at
\displaystyle\epsilon
have been studied in
[
43
]
using coends at roots of unity, factorization homology, and the theory of Poisson orders. In particular, the center
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,0}^{\epsilon})
was computed explicitly (that is, identified with
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,0}^{\epsilon})
in the notations below), and it was showed that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,0}^{\epsilon}
is a finitely generated
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,0}^{\epsilon})
-module, and that the Azumaya locus of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,0}^{\epsilon}
is the preimage of the big Bruhat cell
\displaystyle G^{0}\subset G
by the quantum moment map
\displaystyle\mu_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
(see
[
43
, Th. 1.1]
).

Besides the generalizations to
\displaystyle n>0
, a main difference between
[
43
]
and this paper is that we define and study the algebras
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}}
by means of twists, co-R-matrices, and specializations of integral forms of quantum groups (following our previous works
[
11
,
12
,
14
]
). Thus our approach lies entirely within Hopf algebra and quantum group theory. In our results a key rôle is played by (variants of) the Alekseev morphism, and its interaction with the quantum moment map
\displaystyle\mu_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
. This is crucial in our proof that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}}
are domains, whose central localizations are central simple algebras, and to eventually compute their PI-degrees. We have tried to let no point unclear.

In a subsequent version of this paper we will add a detailed appendix about specializations to more general roots of unity (see Remark
1.1
below). In other forthcoming works, we will study the specializations at roots of unity of the algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{U_{q}}
, maximal order issues, and provide concrete indecomposable representations of these algebras when
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
.

Statements and plan of the paper.
The proofs of our main results are contained in §
5
–
6
. Arguments that follow fairly directly from the existing literature or can be obtained after simple adaptations are detailed in the appendices
A
–
D
. In §
2
-
4
we introduce mainly the material on graph algebras and quantum groups, and prove also some important preliminary results about them.

Section
2
introduces notations, and basic results on specializations of
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}[q^{\pm 1}]
-modules and the central localization of domains, used throughout the paper.

Section
3
recalls basic properties of the graph algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
associated with a Hopf algebra
\displaystyle H
with invertible antipode. Guided by the case
\displaystyle H=U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}(\mathfrak{g})
and its specializations, we discuss with some extent the weakest structures on
\displaystyle H
required to define
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
, that we call a
\displaystyle(H,\Phi^{\pm})
datum. Such a datum implies the existence of a co-R-matrix which is used to define the product in
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
. Also, we prove in this setup a number of general facts on the quantum moment map (QMM)
\displaystyle\mu_{g,n}
(§
3.3
) and the Alekseev morphism
\displaystyle\Phi_{g,n}
(§
3.5
), in particular concerning sufficient conditions for their injectivity. These will play a key role in the sequel.

Section
4.1
-
4.7
contain background results on quantum groups. Let
\displaystyle G
be a complex semi-simple and simply-connected Lie group with Lie algebra
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}
, and
\displaystyle\Lambda
a lattice such that
\displaystyle Q\subset\Lambda\subset P
, where
\displaystyle Q
is the root lattice and
\displaystyle P
the weight lattice of
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}
. Let
\displaystyle A=\mathbb{C}[q,q^{-1}]
. We use the integral forms
\displaystyle U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}
(De Concini-Kac-Procesi
[
29
,
32
]
) and
\displaystyle\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}
(De Concini-Lyubashenko
[
31
]
) of the Drinfeld-Jimbo quantum group
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}:=U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}(\mathfrak{g})
associated to
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}
and
\displaystyle\Lambda
, and the integral form
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{A}
(Lusztig
[
54
]
) of the quantum coordinate algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}:=\mathcal{O}_{q}(G)
. We recall some facts on these algebras, their specializations
\displaystyle U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}
,
\displaystyle\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}
at an odd
\displaystyle l
-th root of unity
\displaystyle\epsilon
(satisfying some mild assumptions, see §
2.2
), the quantum Frobenius epimorphism
\displaystyle\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}\colon\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}\to U(\mathfrak{g})
and its dual monomorphism
\displaystyle\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}^{*}:\mathcal{O}(G)\to\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}(G)
, and the small quantum group
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}
(also called Frobenius-Lusztig kernel). Here are some important features which are recalled in detail. The inclusion map
\displaystyle U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}\to\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
has a remarkable degeneracy at the specialization to
\displaystyle q=\epsilon
, as it acquires a big kernel, the quotient of
\displaystyle U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
by this kernel being precisely
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
. The specialization
\displaystyle\langle\text{-},\text{-}\rangle_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}:{\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}\times\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
of the evaluation pairing is non-degenerate, a known fact for which we provide a proof in Lemma
4.8
using Lusztig’s
[
54
]
idempotent completion of
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
(recalled in Appendix
B
). Also, by using an embedding of
\displaystyle\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}
in the canonical basis completion of Lusztig’s idempotent completion (App.
B
), we provide an alternative proof (Prop.
4.14
) of the following important exact sequence of Hopf algebras (
[
20
, Th. III.7.10]
,
[
7
]
), where
\displaystyle\pi
is the restriction of the evaluation map to
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}\subset\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
:

(1)

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 5.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-5.5pt\raise This preliminary material allows us in §
4.8
to relate the co-quasitriangular structures of
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}
and
\displaystyle(u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}})^{*}
.

Section
5.1
gives the definition of the graph algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}:=\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}})
, its integral form
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}
, and specialization
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
; these definitions use the results of §
4
to specify the datum
\displaystyle(H,\Phi^{\pm})
from §
3
. As a
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}
-vector space we have
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}=\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}^{\otimes(2g+n)}
by definition.

It should be noted that the co-R-matrix on
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}
takes values in the field
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})
which is an extension of
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q)
by a formal variable
\displaystyle q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
satisfying
\displaystyle(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})^{D}=q
, where
\displaystyle D
is the smallest positive integer such that
\displaystyle DP\subset Q
(the values of
\displaystyle D
, depending on
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}
, are recalled in §
2.1
). So the theory needs to fix
\displaystyle q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
: the algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
is defined over
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})
, and
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}
over
\displaystyle A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}:=\mathbb{C}[q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}},q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}^{-1}]
. As for specializations, we consider only roots of unity
\displaystyle\epsilon
of odd order
\displaystyle l
such that
\displaystyle{\rm gcd}(l,D)=1
; hence there exists
\displaystyle\bar{D}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\displaystyle\bar{D}D\equiv 1\pmod{l}
and
\displaystyle\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}:=\epsilon^{\bar{D}}
is a
\displaystyle D
-th root of
\displaystyle\epsilon
such that
\displaystyle\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}^{l}=1
. We always specialize
\displaystyle q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
to this
\displaystyle l
-th root
\displaystyle\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
. Being determined by
\displaystyle\epsilon
, we denote this specialization
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
.

Remark 1.1
.

The specialization of
\displaystyle q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
to an arbitrary
\displaystyle Dl
-th root of unity instead of
\displaystyle\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
fixed above induces several technical complications. The case of arbitrary
\displaystyle D
-th roots of
\displaystyle\epsilon
(which are in general
\displaystyle Dl
-th roots of 1) will be discussed in an appendix added to a future version of the present paper, where we will prove adapted versions of Theorems
1.2
and
1.3
below.

In Section
5.4
we show that the image of the Frobenius embedding

(2)

(\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}^{*}_{\epsilon})^{\otimes(2g+n)}\colon\mathcal{O}(G)^{\otimes(2g+n)}\to\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}

is a central subalgebra, where
\displaystyle\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}^{*}_{\epsilon}:\mathcal{O}(G)\to\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}(G)
is the dual of the Frobenius morphism
\displaystyle\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}\colon\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}\to U(\mathfrak{g})
. We denote
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}):=\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}^{*}_{\epsilon}\bigl(\mathcal{O}(G)\bigr)^{\otimes(2g+n)}
. On the other hand we have the graph algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}})
which can be defined as in §
3.2
by means of the standard quasitriangular structure of the small quantum group
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
, coming from the Drinfeld double construction. Our next result shows that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}})
is the quotient of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
by the ideal generated by the augmentation ideal of
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\cong\mathcal{O}(G)^{\otimes(2g+n)}
. More precisely, let
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}^{+}(G)=\ker(\varepsilon)\cap\mathcal{O}(G)
be the augmentation ideal of
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(G)
, which consists of the functions vanishing at the unit element of
\displaystyle G
. Using the results of §
4
we deduce the following generalization of the exact sequence (
1
):

Theorem 1.2
.

(see Th.
5.20
)
The algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
is a central extension of the
\displaystyle l^{(2g+n)\dim(\mathfrak{g})}
-dimensional algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}})
by
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}^{+}(G)^{\otimes(2g+n)}
, where
\displaystyle l
is the order of the root of unity
\displaystyle\epsilon
.

For
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sl}_{m+1}
and
\displaystyle(g,n)=(0,1)
a similar quotient of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
has been obtained in
[
27
]
by means of a presentation by generators and relations.

One can show that the extension in Th.
1.2
is compatible with natural actions
[
4
,
38
]
of the mapping class group
\displaystyle{\rm MCG}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ})
on
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(G)^{\otimes(2g+n)}
,
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}})
. This will be developed in
[
13
]
, among other topologically-flavored results.

By factorisability of the Hopf algebra
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
(because the order
\displaystyle l
of
\displaystyle\epsilon
is odd,
[
56
]
) we can deduce from Th.
1.2
that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
has an irreducible representation of dimension
\displaystyle l^{g.\mathrm{dim}(\mathfrak{g})+nN}
(Prop.
5.22
). Also, the Alekseev morphism
\displaystyle\Phi_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
on
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
from §
3.5
, defined by the datum
\displaystyle\Phi^{\pm}
from §
5.1
, is injective (Prop.
5.9
; to be precise this statement proves injectivity of the
modified
Alekseev morphism on
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
discussed below, but the same arguments work for
\displaystyle\Phi_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
), and from this we show that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
is a domain (Prop.
5.21
). We can thus consider the central localization

Q(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}):=Q(\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}))\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})}\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}.

We prove:

Theorem 1.3
.

(see Prop.
5.17
, Th.
5.24
)
The algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
is a free
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})
-module of rank
\displaystyle l^{(2g+n).\mathrm{dim}(\mathfrak{g})}
, and
\displaystyle Q(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})
is a division algebra and a central simple algebra of PI-degree
\displaystyle l^{g.\mathrm{dim}(\mathfrak{g})+nN}
, where
\displaystyle N
is the number of positive roots of
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}
.

The (easier) case
\displaystyle g=0
was obtained for
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,n}^{\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}
,
\displaystyle\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
arbitrary, in
[
12
, Th. 4.9 and Th. 5.2]
.

Recall that
\displaystyle\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
is the specialization of the De Concini-Lyubashenko integral form
\displaystyle\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
. Fundamental in the theory is a coadjoint action of
\displaystyle\Gamma^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}_{\epsilon}
on
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
, which makes it a module algebra. The inclusion of the De Concini-Kac-Procesi integral form
\displaystyle U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}\subset\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
factorizes after specialization as
\displaystyle U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}\twoheadrightarrow u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}\subset\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
, and we consider the subalgebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}}
of invariant elements for the coadjoint action of the small quantum group
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}
. We stress that it strictly contains the subalgebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{{\scalebox{0.35}{Q}}}}
(see (
124
) for their precise relation), which will be considered in
[
13
]
.

The sections
5.2
-
5.3
are devoted to a modification

\widehat{\Phi}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}\colon\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}\to\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{\otimes g}_{\epsilon}\otimes(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{\otimes n}

of the
Alekseev morphism

\displaystyle\Phi_{g,n}^{\epsilon}\colon\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}\to\mathcal{H}\mathcal{H}^{\otimes g}_{\epsilon}\otimes(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{\otimes n}
(where
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{\epsilon}
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}\mathcal{H}_{\epsilon}
are the specializations to
\displaystyle\epsilon
of certain extensions of the Heisenberg double of
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}
), and to the
quantum moment map
(QMM)
\displaystyle\mu_{g,n}^{\epsilon}\colon\Phi_{0,1}^{\epsilon}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})\to\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
; both play a key role in the proof of Th.
1.4
below. Besides being injective (Prop.
5.9
) and equivariant (Prop.
5.5
) like
\displaystyle\Phi_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
, a key property of
\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
is a certain compatibility with the QMM
\displaystyle\mu_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
which is not satisfied by
\displaystyle\Phi_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
(Prop.
5.12
). It allows us to prove that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}}
is the centralizer of the image of
\displaystyle\mu_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
in
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
(Th.
5.13
). We show that
\displaystyle\mu_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
is injective by a “reduction to the classical QMM” (Prop.
5.27
); for completeness we show also that
\displaystyle\mu_{g,n}
, the QMM defined over
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q)
, is injective (Prop.
5.11
). From this, Theorem
1.3
, and the double centralizer theorem, we can prove :

Theorem 1.4
.

(see Th.
5.28
)
If
\displaystyle(g,n)\neq(0,1)
, the central localization
\displaystyle Q(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}})
is a division algebra, and a central simple algebra of PI-degree
\displaystyle l^{g.\mathrm{dim}(\mathfrak{g})+N(n-1)-m}
, where
\displaystyle l
is the order of
\displaystyle\epsilon
,
\displaystyle N
is the number of positive roots of
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}
and
\displaystyle m
is the rank of
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}
.

The case
\displaystyle g=0
was obtained in
[
12
, Theorem 5.4]
by a similar procedure. In this case the QMM is much simpler, being essentially given by the iterated coproduct
\displaystyle\Delta^{(n)}
on
\displaystyle U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
. Also, the modified morphism
\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
is unnecessary, because the compatibility of
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,n}^{\epsilon}
with
\displaystyle\mu_{0,n}^{\epsilon}=\Delta^{(n)}
is much easier, and injectivity of the latter is obvious.

The proof of Th.
1.4
relies heavily on the structure of the centers of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}}
, namely the fact that the multiplication map
\displaystyle j
in Th.
1.5
below is an isomorphism, and
\displaystyle j_{2}
is injective. To keep clear the line of reasoning of Th.
1.4
we have preferred to postpone the proof of these facts to §
6
. We introduce a central subalgebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})
of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
, which is essentially the specialization of the center of the subalgebra
\displaystyle 1^{\otimes 2g}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\subset\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
. By using the quantum Killing form (see App.
A
) we show in Lem.
6.2
that
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}^{\epsilon}
restricts to isomorphisms
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})\cong\mathcal{Z}_{1}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})\cap\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})\cong\mathcal{Z}_{1}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})\cap\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})
, the latter intersection having been described by De Concini-Kac-Procesi in their study of the center
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})
of
\displaystyle U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
in
[
30
,
32
]
. Let
\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}:=\mu_{g,n}^{\epsilon}\circ\Phi_{0,1}^{\epsilon}:\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\to\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
, and
\displaystyle(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1})(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}):=\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})
. Then, by following some of the arguments of De Concini-Kac-Procesi we can prove:

Theorem 1.5
.

(Th.
6.1
, Cor.
6.5
)
1. The rings
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}})
are integrally closed and Noetherian.

2. The multiplication maps

\displaystyle\displaystyle j\colon\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\otimes_{(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1})(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})}\mathcal{Z}_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})

\displaystyle\displaystyle\to\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})

\displaystyle\displaystyle j_{2}\colon\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\otimes_{\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}\left((\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1})(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})\right)}\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})\bigr)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\to\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}})

are isomorphisms.

3.
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}})
coincide with the trace rings of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}}
respectively. Moreover they are free
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})
-modules of respective ranks
\displaystyle l^{mn}
and
\displaystyle l^{m(n+1)}
.

We also provide a simple consequence of this result for the Frobenius embedding (
2
) of
\displaystyle\bigl(\mathcal{O}(G)^{\otimes(2g+n)}\bigr)^{G}
, which is the coordinate ring of the variety of
\displaystyle G
-characters of
\displaystyle\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ})
. Namely, setting
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{\rm inv}:=(\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}^{*})^{(2g+n)}\bigl[\bigl(\mathcal{O}(G)^{\otimes(2g+n)}\bigr)^{G}\bigr]
we prove in Cor.
6.6
that the following multiplication map is an isomorphisms of algebras

\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{\rm inv}\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{\rm inv}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})}\mathcal{Z}_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\to\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})^{\Gamma_{\epsilon}}.

This result will be developed in
[
13
]
.

Applications to representations.
As a consequence of Th.
1.3
and
1.4
(see Th.
2.5
), the simple modules of
\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}:=\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
or
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}}
have dimension equal or less than the PI-degree
\displaystyle d_{\mathfrak{A}}
of
\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}
described in these statements, each
\displaystyle\chi\in\mathrm{MaxSpec}(\mathfrak{A})
is the central character of some simple
\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}
-module, and there is a “Unicity theorem for representations”, that is, there is an open and dense Zariski subset
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}
of
\displaystyle\mathrm{MaxSpec}(\mathcal{Z})
such that every
\displaystyle\chi\in\mathcal{S}
is the central character of a unique simple
\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}
-module (up to isomorphism) which has dimension
\displaystyle d_{\mathfrak{A}}
.

Th.
1.5
provides an algebraic description of
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}
by means of the following general facts from the theory of PI rings (see, e.g.,
[
61
, Chap. 13]
,
[
68
, Chap. 6]
,
[
8
]
). Let
\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}
be a division PI algebra, with center
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}
a Noetherian and integrally closed ring. By Th.
1.3
,
1.4
and
1.5
we can take

\mathfrak{A}=\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}\ {\rm or}\ \mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}}

with PI-degree
\displaystyle d_{\mathfrak{A}}
as described in these statements. The
discriminant ideal

\displaystyle{\rm Disc}(\mathfrak{A})
is the ideal of
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}
generated by the elements
\displaystyle{\rm det}\bigl[\bigl(t_{\mathrm{red}}(x_{i}x_{j})\bigr)_{1\leq i,j\leq d_{\mathfrak{A}}}\bigr]
, where
\displaystyle x_{1},\ldots x_{d_{\mathfrak{A}}}\in\mathfrak{A}
and
\displaystyle t_{\mathrm{red}}:\mathfrak{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}
is the reduced trace map of
\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}
(see
[
65
, §10]
). The set
\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{A})\subset{\rm MaxSpec}(\mathcal{Z})
of zeros of
\displaystyle{\rm Disc}(\mathfrak{A})
is a closed (proper) subvariety of
\displaystyle{\rm MaxSpec}(\mathcal{Z})
, called the discriminant variety. Now, given a central character
\displaystyle\chi\in{\rm MaxSpec}(\mathcal{Z})
, put
\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}^{\chi}:=\mathfrak{A}/{\rm Ker}(\chi)\mathfrak{A}
. Since
\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}
is a domain, Nakayama’s lemma (in the form of
[
10
, Cor. 2.5]
) implies that
\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}^{\chi}\neq 0
. Then we have (see e.g.
[
29
, Lem. 3.7]
, or
[
20
, Th. I.13.5]
for a more general statement):

Corollary 1.6
.

\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}^{\chi}
is isomorphic to
\displaystyle M_{d_{\mathfrak{A}}}(\mathbb{C})
if and only if
\displaystyle\chi\notin\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{A})
, and if
\displaystyle\chi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{A})
then every irreducible representation of
\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}^{\chi}
has dimension less than
\displaystyle{d_{\mathfrak{A}}}
.

The set
\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{A}}:={\rm MaxSpec}(\mathcal{Z})\setminus\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{A})
is the
Azumaya locus
of
\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}

[
20
, III.1.6-III.1.7]
. Loci of irreducible representations of dimension
\displaystyle<d
can be reached by using lower discriminant ideals (see the Main Theorem of
[
22
]
).

It is in general a difficult problem to describe Azumaya loci. However
\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}=\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}}
have a natural structure of
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})
-Poisson order described in
[
21
, §2.1-2.2]
, which gives
\displaystyle{\rm MaxSpec}\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\bigr)
a structure of Poisson variety and may be used to study
\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{A}}
geometrically. Indeed, the Poisson structure is given by the Fock-Rosly Poisson bracket (
[
42
]
; see also
[
43
, Prop. 4.3]
, and
[
11
]
for the detailed case of
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
) and by
[
21
, Th. 4.2]
the set
\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{A}}
is a union of symplectic leaves of the pull-back Poisson bracket by the restriction map
\displaystyle{\rm MaxSpec}(\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}))\to{\rm MaxSpec}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}))
. This viewpoint has been used in
[
43
]
(case
\displaystyle n=0
) and
[
48
]
(case of
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
).

We are grateful to Joao-Pedro Dos Santos for valuable discussions on topics in algebraic geometry. Part of this work was done during the postdoc contract of M.F. at IMT Toulouse, funded by the CIMI Labex ANR 11-LABX-0040.

2.
General preliminaries

In all this paper
\displaystyle\Bbbk
denotes a field and except otherwise stated “algebra” means “associative
\displaystyle\Bbbk
-algebra”. The center of an algebra
\displaystyle A
is denoted
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(A)
.

2.1.
Simple Lie algebras

We fix a complex finite dimensional simple Lie algebra
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}
and denote by
\displaystyle U(\mathfrak{g})
its universal enveloping algebra.

Denote by
\displaystyle m
the rank of
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}
and let
\displaystyle C:=(a_{ij})\in\mathrm{Mat}_{m}(\mathbb{Z})
be the Cartan matrix of
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}
. Let
\displaystyle d_{i}\in\{1,2,3\}
the smallest positive integers such that
\displaystyle(d_{i}a_{ij})
is a symmetric matrix. We fix a Cartan subalgebra
\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}\subset\mathfrak{g}
, and a basis of simple roots
\displaystyle\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{m}\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}
. We denote by
\displaystyle(\cdot,\cdot)
the scalar product on
\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}^{*}
defined by the Killing form of
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}
, normalized so that
\displaystyle(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j}):=d_{i}a_{ij}
. Then
\displaystyle(\alpha,\alpha)=2
for the short roots
\displaystyle\alpha
. The set of positive roots is denoted
\displaystyle\phi^{+}=\{\beta_{1},\cdots,\beta_{N}\}
, so
\displaystyle{\rm dim}({\mathfrak{g}})=2N+m
, and we have the Cartan decomposition
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{n}^{-}\oplus\mathfrak{h}\oplus\mathfrak{n}^{+}
. We put
\displaystyle\rho:=\textstyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\beta_{i}
, and the coroot of a simple root
\displaystyle\alpha_{i}
is
\displaystyle\check{\alpha}_{i}:=2\alpha_{i}/(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i})
.

Let
\displaystyle P=\textstyle\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{Z}.\varpi_{i}\subset{\mathfrak{h}}^{*}
be the lattice of integral weights, with the fundamental weights
\displaystyle\varpi_{i}
satisfying
\displaystyle(\varpi_{i},\check{\alpha}_{j})=\delta_{i,j}
,
\displaystyle P_{+}=\textstyle\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{N}.\varpi_{i}
the set of integral dominant weights, and
\displaystyle Q=\textstyle\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{Z}.\alpha_{i}
,
\displaystyle\check{Q}=\textstyle\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{Z}.\check{\alpha}_{i}
are the the root and coroot lattices. We have
\displaystyle|P/Q|={\rm det}(a_{ij})
. We denote by
\displaystyle D
the smallest positive integer such that
\displaystyle DP\subset Q
; it is also the smallest positive integer such that
\displaystyle D(\lambda,\varpi_{i})\in d_{i}\mathbb{Z}
for all
\displaystyle\lambda\in P
,
\displaystyle i\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
. Note that
\displaystyle D
is the exponent of
\displaystyle P/Q
, and that
\displaystyle D=|P/Q|
except when
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}=D_{2n}
(see the table below), correcting an incorrect sentence in
[
12
, Page 6]
. The entries of
\displaystyle C^{-1}
belong to
\displaystyle D^{-1}\mathbb{N}
. The values of max
\displaystyle(d_{i})
,
\displaystyle D
and
\displaystyle P/Q
, the fundamental group of the adjoint group
\displaystyle G^{{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}}
(see below), are recalled in the following table (where
\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}_{n}:=\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}
):

type of
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}

\displaystyle A_{n\geq 1}

\displaystyle B_{n\geq 2}

\displaystyle C_{n\geq 3}

\displaystyle D_{n\geq 5}

\displaystyle n
odd

\displaystyle D_{n\geq 4}

\displaystyle n
even

\displaystyle E_{6}

\displaystyle E_{7}

\displaystyle E_{8}

\displaystyle F_{4}

\displaystyle G_{2}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{sl}_{n+1}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{so}_{2n+1}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{sp}_{2n}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{so}_{2n}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{so}_{2n}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{e}_{6}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{e}_{7}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{e}_{8}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{f}_{4}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}_{2}

\displaystyle\max(d_{i})

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle 2

\displaystyle 2

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle 2

\displaystyle 3

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle n+1

\displaystyle 2

\displaystyle 2

\displaystyle 4

\displaystyle 2

\displaystyle 3

\displaystyle 2

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle P/Q

\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}_{n+1}

\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}_{2}

\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}_{2}

\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}_{4}

\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}

\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}_{3}

\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}_{2}

\displaystyle\{0\}

\displaystyle\{0\}

\displaystyle\{0\}

Any intermediate lattice
\displaystyle Q\subset\Lambda\subset P
has rank
\displaystyle m=\dim(\mathfrak{h})
.

We denote by
\displaystyle W
the Weyl group of
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}
, by
\displaystyle w_{0}
the longest element of
\displaystyle W
, and by
\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{g})
the braid group of
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}
.

We denote by
\displaystyle G^{{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}}
the connected simple complex algebraic group with Lie algebra
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}
such that its maximal torus
\displaystyle T^{{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}}\subset G^{{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}}
associated with
\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}
has character group
\displaystyle{\rm Hom}_{\rm Ab}(T^{{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}},\mathbb{C}^{\times})\cong\Lambda
. In particular
\displaystyle G:=G^{{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}}
is the simply-connected group, and
\displaystyle G^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}={\rm Image}(Ad\colon G\to{\rm Aut}(\mathfrak{g}))
is the adjoint group. We denote by
\displaystyle B_{\pm}
the Borel subgroup of
\displaystyle G
with Lie algebra
\displaystyle\mathfrak{b}_{\pm}
, and by
\displaystyle T\subset G
the corresponding maximal torus.

2.2.
Parameters
\displaystyle q
and
\displaystyle q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}

We let
\displaystyle q
,
\displaystyle q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
be indeterminates such that
\displaystyle q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}^{D}=q
, and denote

A=\mathbb{C}[q,q^{-1}]\ ,A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}=\mathbb{C}[q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}},q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}^{-1}]\ ,\ q_{i}=q^{d_{i}}\ ,\ q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}i}=q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}^{d_{i}}.

By
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q)
we denote the field of rational fractions in the indeterminate
\displaystyle q
. We put
\displaystyle[0]_{q}!:=1
,
\displaystyle[x;0]_{q}:=1
,
\displaystyle(x;0)_{q}:=1
and for
\displaystyle k,t\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}
,

\begin{array}[]{ccc}[x;t]_{q}:=\prod_{s=1}^{t}\frac{xq^{-s+1}-x^{-1}q^{s-1}}{q^{s}-q^{-s}}&,&(x;t)_{q}:=\prod_{s=1}^{t}\frac{xq^{-s+1}-1}{q^{s}-1}\\
\left[k\right]_{q}:=\frac{q^{k}-q^{-k}}{q-q^{-1}}&,&[k]_{q}!:=[k]_{q}\cdots[1]_{q}.\end{array}

For
\displaystyle m,t\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\displaystyle m\geq t
we have
\displaystyle[q^{m};t]_{q}
,
\displaystyle(q^{m};t)_{q}\in A
, and they are quantum versions of the standard binomial coefficient
\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}m\\
t\end{pmatrix}
. Note that
\displaystyle[q^{m};t]_{q}=(q^{m};t)_{q}=0
if
\displaystyle m<t
, still with
\displaystyle m,t\in\mathbb{N}
.

We fix an integer
\displaystyle l\geq 3
and let
\displaystyle\epsilon
be a primitive
\displaystyle l
-th root of unity. For simplicity, in all the paper we require that:

•

\displaystyle l
is odd, and in case
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}
is of type
\displaystyle G_{2}
,
\displaystyle l
is coprime to
\displaystyle 3
. Thus
\displaystyle{\rm gcd}(l,d_{i})=1
, and
\displaystyle l
is the smallest positive integer such that
\displaystyle\epsilon_{i}^{l}=1
for all
\displaystyle i
.

•

\displaystyle{\rm gcd}(l,D)=1
; because
\displaystyle l
is odd, this is a constraint only in type
\displaystyle A_{n}
for
\displaystyle n
even and
\displaystyle E_{6}
.

In particular, there exists a unique integer
\displaystyle\overline{D}
modulo
\displaystyle l
such that
\displaystyle D\overline{D}\equiv 1\pmod{l}
. We set
\displaystyle\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}=\epsilon^{\overline{D}}
, so that
\displaystyle\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}^{D}=\epsilon
. Note that
\displaystyle\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
satisfies
\displaystyle\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}^{l}=1
. Also we put
\displaystyle\epsilon_{i}:=\epsilon^{d_{i}}
and
\displaystyle\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}i}:=\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}^{d_{i}}
.

2.3.
Specialization of
\displaystyle A
-modules

Let
\displaystyle z\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
and
\displaystyle A=\mathbb{C}[q^{\pm 1}]
. Then
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}
can be seen as an
\displaystyle A
-module
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}_{z}
by means of the evaluation morphism
\displaystyle q\mapsto z
. If
\displaystyle X
is an
\displaystyle A
-module, we denote by
\displaystyle X_{z}=X\otimes_{A}\mathbb{C}_{z}
the
specialization of
\displaystyle X
at
\displaystyle q=z
; it is a
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}
-vector space. For any
\displaystyle x\in X
, we let
\displaystyle x_{|z}=x\otimes_{A}1
. The map
\displaystyle X\to X_{z}
given by
\displaystyle x\mapsto x_{|z}
is surjective by definition and satisfies
\displaystyle(P\cdot x)_{|z}=P(z)x_{|z}
for all Laurent polynomial
\displaystyle P\in A
and
\displaystyle x\in X
.

Lemma 2.1
.

1. We have
\displaystyle X_{z}\cong X\big/(q-z)X
as
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}
-vector spaces. It follows that the kernel of the specialization map
\displaystyle x\mapsto x_{|z}
is
\displaystyle(q-z)X
.

2. If the elements
\displaystyle\{x_{i}\}
are an
\displaystyle A
-basis of
\displaystyle X
, then the elements
\displaystyle\{(x_{i})_{|z}\}
are a
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}
-basis of
\displaystyle X_{z}
.

Proof.

1. We check that
\displaystyle X\big/(q-z)X
satisfies the universal property of
\displaystyle X\otimes_{A}\mathbb{C}_{z}
. Let
\displaystyle u:X\times\mathbb{C}_{z}\to X\big/(q-z)X
,
\displaystyle(x,c)\mapsto cx+(q-z)X
which is obviously an
\displaystyle A
-balanced map, i.e.
\displaystyle u(P\cdot x,c)=u\bigl(x,P(z)c\bigr)
for all
\displaystyle P\in A
. Let
\displaystyle h:X\times\mathbb{C}_{z}\to G
be another morphism of abelian groups which is
\displaystyle A
-balanced. Define
\displaystyle\bar{h}:X\big/(q-z)X\to G
by
\displaystyle\bar{h}\bigl(x+(q-z)X\bigr)=h(x,1)
. It is easy to check that
\displaystyle\bar{h}
is well-defined and satisfies
\displaystyle\bar{h}\circ u=h
. By surjectivity of
\displaystyle u
this factorization of
\displaystyle h
is unique, which is the desired universal property. With this model of
\displaystyle X\otimes_{A}\mathbb{C}_{z}
we have
\displaystyle x\otimes_{A}1=u(x,1)=x+(q-z)X
, whence the second claim.

2. Assume we have a vanishing linear combination
\displaystyle\textstyle\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}(x_{i})_{|z}=0
with
\displaystyle\lambda_{i}\in\mathbb{C}
. Viewing the
\displaystyle\lambda_{i}
’s as constant polynomials in
\displaystyle A
, this reads
\displaystyle\textstyle\bigl(\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}\cdot x_{i}\bigr)_{|z}=0
. By item 1 it follows that
\displaystyle\textstyle\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}\cdot x_{i}=(q-z)x^{\prime}
for some
\displaystyle x^{\prime}\in X
. Write
\displaystyle x^{\prime}=\textstyle\sum_{i}P_{i}\cdot x_{i}
with
\displaystyle P_{i}\in A
. Identifying coefficients in the basis
\displaystyle\{x_{i}\}
gives
\displaystyle\lambda_{i}=(q-z)P_{i}
. Hence
\displaystyle\lambda_{i}=0
for all
\displaystyle i
, proving that the family
\displaystyle\{(x_{i})_{|z}\}
is linearly independent. It is a generating family because the specialization map is surjective.
∎

Let
\displaystyle Y
be another
\displaystyle A
-module and
\displaystyle f\in\mathrm{Hom}_{A}(X,Y)
. Then we have the
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}
-linear map
\displaystyle f_{z}=f\otimes_{A}\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{C}_{z}}:X_{z}\to Y_{z}
. It holds
\displaystyle f_{z}(x_{|z})=f(x)_{|z}
, which is another way of defining
\displaystyle f_{z}
. We record the basic properties:

(3)

f\text{ surjective}\>\implies\>f_{z}\text{ surjective},\qquad f\text{ isomorphism}\>\implies\>f_{z}\text{ isomorphism}.

The first assertion is clear (more generally any tensor product functor is right exact); for the second it suffices to note that
\displaystyle(f^{-1})_{z}=(f_{z})^{-1}
by functoriality. Injectivity is not preserved in general; so one must be careful with specializations of submodules, because the specialization of the inclusion map is in general no longer injective.

Note that, using associativity of
\displaystyle\otimes
and the fact that
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}_{z}
is an
\displaystyle(A,\mathbb{C})
-bimodule,

(X\otimes_{A}Y)_{z}=X\otimes_{A}Y\otimes_{A}\mathbb{C}_{z}=X\otimes_{A}Y_{z}=X\otimes_{A}\mathbb{C}_{z}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}Y_{z}=X_{z}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}Y_{z}.

Hence
\displaystyle(x\otimes_{A}y)_{|z}
is identified with
\displaystyle x_{|z}\otimes y_{|z}
, where we simply write
\displaystyle\otimes
instead of
\displaystyle\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}
.

We will use pairings over
\displaystyle A
, which are
\displaystyle A
-bilinear maps of the form
\displaystyle b:X\times Y\to A
. By the above remarks, the specialized version
\displaystyle b_{z}=b\otimes_{A}\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{C}_{z}}
is the
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}
-bilinear map
\displaystyle b_{z}:X_{|z}\times Y_{|z}\to\mathbb{C}
defined by
\displaystyle b_{z}(x_{|z},y_{|z})=b(x,y)_{|z}
.

2.4.
Central localization of a domain

A
domain
(a.k.a. ring without zero divisors) is a ring in which
\displaystyle xy=0
implies
\displaystyle x=0
or
\displaystyle y=0
. If
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}
is a commutative domain, we denote by
\displaystyle Q(\mathcal{Z})
its field of fractions.

Let
\displaystyle R
be a domain and denote by
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}:=\mathcal{Z}(R)
its center. The
central localization
of
\displaystyle R
is

Q(R):=Q(\mathcal{Z})\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}}R.

The functor
\displaystyle Q(\mathcal{Z})\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}}-:\mathcal{Z}\text{-}\mathrm{Mod}\to Q(\mathcal{Z})\text{-}\mathrm{Vect}
is isomorphic to the localization functor at
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}\setminus\!\{0\}
, see e.g.
[
68
, Prop. 1.10.18]
. Hence
\displaystyle Q(R)
can also be defined as the localization
\displaystyle(\mathcal{Z}\setminus\{0\})^{-1}R
, and their natural ring structures are isomorphic
[
68
, Cor. 1.10.18’]
. Explicitly,
\displaystyle\textstyle\frac{z}{z^{\prime}}\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}}r
is identified with the fraction
\displaystyle\textstyle\frac{zr}{z^{\prime}}
. Note that
\displaystyle Q(R)
has a natural structure of
\displaystyle Q(\mathcal{Z})
-algebra, and we denote by
\displaystyle\bigl[Q(R):Q(\mathcal{Z})\bigr]
its dimension over that field.

Lemma 2.2
.

1. The ring morphism
\displaystyle q_{R}:R\to Q(R)
,
\displaystyle r\mapsto 1\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}}r
is injective.

2. The center of
\displaystyle Q(R)
is
\displaystyle Q(\mathcal{Z})
, or more precisely
\displaystyle Q(\mathcal{Z})\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}}1
.

3. If
\displaystyle Z_{0}\subset\mathcal{Z}
is a subring such that
\displaystyle Q(\mathcal{Z})
is a finite extension of
\displaystyle Q(Z_{0})
, then
\displaystyle Q(R)\cong Q(Z_{0})\otimes_{Z_{0}}R
.

4. If
\displaystyle R
is finitely generated as a
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}
-module then
\displaystyle\bigl[Q(R):Q(\mathcal{Z})\bigr]<\infty
. If moreover
\displaystyle R
is free over
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}
then
\displaystyle\mathrm{rank}_{\mathcal{Z}}(F)=\bigl[Q(R):Q(\mathcal{Z})\bigr]
.

Proof.

1. Let
\displaystyle 0\neq r\in R
. The
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}
-linear map
\displaystyle m_{r}:\mathcal{Z}\to R
,
\displaystyle z\mapsto zr
is injective because
\displaystyle R
is a domain. The functor
\displaystyle Q(\mathcal{Z})\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}}-
being exact
[
68
, Th. 3.1.20]
, the resulting
\displaystyle Q(\mathcal{Z})
-linear map
\displaystyle\widetilde{m}_{r}:Q(\mathcal{Z})\to Q(R)
,
\displaystyle\textstyle\frac{z}{z^{\prime}}\mapsto\frac{z}{z^{\prime}}\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}}r
is injective. Hence
\displaystyle\widetilde{m}_{r}(1)=1\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}}r\neq 0
.

2. Note that a general element
\displaystyle\textstyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{a_{i}}{b_{i}}\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}}r_{i}
can be rewritten as
\displaystyle\textstyle\frac{1}{b_{1}\ldots b_{n}}\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}}\bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}r_{i}a_{i}\prod_{j\neq i}b_{j}\bigr)
and hence we are reduced to elements of the form
\displaystyle z^{-1}\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}}r
. Assume such an element is central. Then
\displaystyle 1\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}}r
is central as well, which by item 1 implies that
\displaystyle r
is central in
\displaystyle R
. But then
\displaystyle z^{-1}\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}}r=z^{-1}r\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}}1
and we are done.

3. The key is to prove that
\displaystyle Q(\mathcal{Z})\cong Q(Z_{0})\otimes_{Z_{0}}\mathcal{Z}
. For this let us first show that
\displaystyle Q(Z_{0})\otimes_{Z_{0}}\mathcal{Z}
is a field, which is easily seen to be equivalent to the claim that any non-zero element
\displaystyle 1\otimes_{Z_{0}}z
has an inverse. Let
\displaystyle P=\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{1}X+\ldots+\alpha_{d}X^{d}\in Q(Z_{0})[X]
be the minimal polynomial of
\displaystyle z\in Q(\mathcal{Z})
over
\displaystyle Q(Z_{0})
; note that
\displaystyle\alpha_{0}\neq 0
by minimality of
\displaystyle P
. In
\displaystyle Q(Z_{0})\otimes_{Z_{0}}\mathcal{Z}
we can rewrite the equality
\displaystyle P(z)=0
as
\displaystyle(1\otimes_{Z_{0}}z)\bigl[-(\alpha_{0}^{-1}\otimes_{Z_{0}}\bigl(\alpha_{d}z^{d-1}+\alpha_{d-1}z^{d-2}+\ldots+\alpha_{1}\bigr)\bigr]=1
, giving the inverse of
\displaystyle 1\otimes_{Z_{0}}z
. We deduce that the morphism
\displaystyle Q(Z_{0})\otimes_{Z_{0}}\mathcal{Z}\to Q(\mathcal{Z})
,
\displaystyle\frac{z_{0}}{z^{\prime}_{0}}\otimes_{Z_{0}}z\mapsto\frac{z_{0}z}{z^{\prime}_{0}}
is surjective; it is also injective because its source is a field. As a result

Q(\mathcal{Z})\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}}R\cong\bigl(Q(Z_{0})\otimes_{Z_{0}}\mathcal{Z}\bigr)\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}}R=Q(Z_{0})\otimes_{Z_{0}}\bigl(\mathcal{Z}\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}}R\bigr)\cong Q(Z_{0})\otimes_{Z_{0}}R.

4. If
\displaystyle\{r_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}\subset R
is a generating family over
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}
then any
\displaystyle z^{-1}\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}}r\in Q(R)
can be written as
\displaystyle z^{-1}\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}}\bigl(\textstyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}z_{i}r_{i}\bigr)=\textstyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}z^{-1}z_{i}\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}}r_{i}
, proving that
\displaystyle\{1\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}}r_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}\subset Q(R)
is a generating family over
\displaystyle Q(\mathcal{Z})
. If
\displaystyle\{r_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
is a basis then so is
\displaystyle\{1\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}}r_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
thanks to item 1.
∎

The central localization is especially interesting when
\displaystyle Q(R)
is finite over
\displaystyle Q(\mathcal{Z})
.

Lemma 2.3
.

If
\displaystyle\bigl[Q(R):Q(\mathcal{Z})\bigr]<\infty
then
\displaystyle Q(R)
is a division algebra. In particular, it is a central simple algebra over the field
\displaystyle Q(\mathcal{Z})
and
\displaystyle[Q(R):Q(\mathcal{Z})]=d^{2}
for some integer
\displaystyle d
called the PI-degree of
\displaystyle Q(R)
.

Proof.

The first claim uses the easy fact that a finite-dimensional algebra without zero divisors is a division algebra. In particular
\displaystyle Q(R)
is a simple
\displaystyle Q(\mathcal{Z})
-algebra, and its center is
\displaystyle Q(\mathcal{Z})
by Lemma
2.2
(2). The last claim is a general fact: if
\displaystyle S
is a finite-dimensional central simple algebra over a field
\displaystyle F
, then
\displaystyle[S:F]=d^{2}
. This follows from the existence of a splitting field, i.e. a field extension
\displaystyle\mathbb{F}
of
\displaystyle F
such that
\displaystyle\mathbb{F}\otimes_{F}S\cong M_{d}(\mathbb{F})
; see e.g.
[
68
, Cor. 2.3.25]
or
[
61
, §13.3]
.
∎

Although it looks a bit technical the next lemma will allow us to perform a “reduction to the classical case” in §
5.5
, when
\displaystyle R
is a specialization of a “quantum algebra”:

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
\displaystyle R,S
be domains,
\displaystyle Z_{0}\subset\mathcal{Z}(R)
be a central subring and
\displaystyle f:R\to S
be a ring morphism. Assume that
\displaystyle\bigl[Q(R):Q(Z_{0})\bigl]<\infty
and
\displaystyle f\bigl(Z_{0}\setminus\{0\}\bigr)\subset\mathcal{Z}(S)\setminus\{0\}
. Then
\displaystyle f
is injective.

Proof.

The dimension assumption implies
\displaystyle\bigl[Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(R)\bigr):Q(Z_{0})\bigl]<\infty
and thus
\displaystyle Q(R)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Q(Z_{0})

\displaystyle\otimes_{Z_{0}}R
thanks to Lemma
2.2
(3). Due to the assumption on
\displaystyle f
the following map is well-defined:

\widetilde{f}:Q(R)=Q(Z_{0})\otimes_{Z_{0}}R\to Q(S),\quad\frac{z_{0}}{z^{\prime}_{0}}\otimes_{Z_{0}}r\mapsto\frac{f(z_{0})}{f(z^{\prime}_{0})}\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}(S)}f(r).

The dimension assumption also gives
\displaystyle\bigl[Q(R):Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(R)\bigr)\bigr]<\infty
, so that
\displaystyle Q(R)
is a division algebra by Lemma
2.3
. Hence the ring morphism
\displaystyle\widetilde{f}
is necessarily injective. Recall the embeddings
\displaystyle q_{R}:R\hookrightarrow Q(R)
and
\displaystyle q_{S}:S\hookrightarrow Q(S)
from Lemma
2.2
(1). By very definition we have
\displaystyle q_{S}\circ f=\widetilde{f}\circ q_{R}
, which forces injectivity of
\displaystyle f
.
∎

Under mild assumptions on
\displaystyle R
the PI-degree of
\displaystyle Q(R)
defined in Lemma
2.3
is of extreme relevance for the representation theory of
\displaystyle R
, as we now recall. Assume that
\displaystyle R
is an associative algebra over an algebraically closed field
\displaystyle\Bbbk
, still without zero divisors. Let
\displaystyle V
be a simple
\displaystyle R
-module which is finite-dimensional over
\displaystyle\Bbbk
; then by Schur’s lemma if
\displaystyle z\in\mathcal{Z}=\mathcal{Z}(R)
there exists
\displaystyle\lambda_{z}\in\Bbbk
such that
\displaystyle z\cdot v=\lambda_{z}v
for all
\displaystyle v\in V
. The morphism

\chi_{V}:\mathcal{Z}\to\Bbbk,\quad z\mapsto\lambda_{z}.

is called the
central character
of
\displaystyle V
. More generally, we denote by
\displaystyle\mathrm{MaxSpec}(\mathcal{Z})
the set of characters of
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}
(i.e. algebra morphisms
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}\to\Bbbk
) and recall that it is endowed with the Zariski topology.

Theorem 2.5
.

Let
\displaystyle R
be an associative algebra over an algebraically closed field
\displaystyle\Bbbk
. Assume that
\displaystyle R
is a domain, finitely generated as an algebra, and finitely generated as a module over its center
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}
. Denote by
\displaystyle d
the PI-degree of
\displaystyle Q(R)
defined in Lemma
2.3
. Then:

1. Each
\displaystyle\chi\in\mathrm{MaxSpec}(\mathcal{Z})
is the central character of some simple
\displaystyle R
-module, which is finite-dimensional over
\displaystyle\Bbbk
.

2. Any finite-dimensional simple
\displaystyle R
-module has dimension
\displaystyle\leq d
.

3. (“Unicity theorem for representations”) There is an open and dense Zariski subset
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}
of
\displaystyle\mathrm{MaxSpec}(\mathcal{Z})
such that
\displaystyle R\big/R\ker(\chi)\cong M_{d}(\Bbbk)
for all
\displaystyle\chi\in\mathcal{S}
. It follows that every
\displaystyle\chi\in\mathcal{S}
is the central character of a unique simple
\displaystyle R
-module (up to isomorphism), which has dimension
\displaystyle d
.

Proof.

See for instance
[
20
, §III.1.6]
. A pedagogical detailed proof is also given in
[
41
, §2]
.
∎

Theorem
2.5
means that “generically” a character of
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}
determines a unique simple
\displaystyle R
-module.

3.
General facts on graph algebras

Here we explain how to define the so-called
graph algebra

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
when the Hopf algebra
\displaystyle H
is not necessarily quasitriangular in the strict sense (
i.e.
,
\displaystyle H
has an
\displaystyle R
-matrix in an appropriate completion of
\displaystyle H\otimes H
), as well as the associated notions of Alekseev morphism and quantum moment map. This discussion is of course guided by the case
\displaystyle H=U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
.

3.1.
A substitute for quasitriangularity

Let
\displaystyle\Bbbk
be a field and
\displaystyle H=(H,\cdot,1_{H},\Delta,\varepsilon,S)
be a Hopf
\displaystyle\Bbbk
-algebra with invertible antipode
\displaystyle S
. The
\displaystyle n
-th iterated coproduct of
\displaystyle H
is denoted
\displaystyle\Delta^{(n)}:=(\Delta\otimes\mathrm{id})\circ\Delta^{(n-1)}
,
\displaystyle n\geq 2
(and by convention
\displaystyle\Delta^{(1)}:={\rm Id}
,
\displaystyle\Delta^{(2)}:=\Delta
). We use Sweedler’s coproduct notation:
\displaystyle\Delta^{(n)}(x)=\textstyle\sum_{(x)}x_{(1)}\otimes\ldots\otimes x_{(n)}
, sometimes with implicit summation.

If
\displaystyle V
is a finite-dimensional
\displaystyle H
-module, then for any
\displaystyle v\in V
and
\displaystyle f\in V^{*}
the linear form
\displaystyle f(?\cdot v):H\to\Bbbk
given by
\displaystyle h\mapsto f(h\cdot v)
is called a
matrix coefficient
of
\displaystyle V
. Denote by
\displaystyle H^{\circ}
the
restricted dual
of
\displaystyle H
(a.k.a. finite dual), which is the subspace of
\displaystyle H^{*}
generated by matrix coefficients of finite-dimensional
\displaystyle H
-modules. Endow this space with the usual Hopf structure
\displaystyle(H^{\circ},\star,{\rm 1}_{H^{\circ}},\Delta_{H^{\circ}},\varepsilon_{H^{\circ}},S_{H^{\circ}})
, which is dual to that of
\displaystyle H
:

(4)

\begin{array}[]{c}(\varphi\star\psi)(x)=\sum_{(x)}\varphi(x_{(1)})\psi(x_{(2)}),\quad\varphi(xy)=\sum_{(\varphi)}\varphi_{(1)}(x)\varphi_{(2)}(y)=\bigl\langle\Delta_{H^{\circ}}(\varphi),x\otimes y\bigr\rangle\\[3.00003pt]
\varepsilon_{H^{\circ}}(\varphi)=\varphi(1_{H}),\quad 1_{H^{\circ}}=\varepsilon,\quad S_{H^{\circ}}(\varphi)=\varphi\circ S\end{array}

for all
\displaystyle\varphi,\psi\in H^{\circ}
and
\displaystyle x,y\in H
. The coregular actions
\displaystyle\rhd
,
\displaystyle\lhd
of
\displaystyle H
on
\displaystyle H^{\circ}
are defined by

(5)

\forall\,\varphi\in H^{\circ},\>\>\forall\,h,x\in H,\quad(h\rhd\varphi)(x)=\varphi(xh),\quad(\varphi\lhd h)(x)=\varphi(hx).

which can also be written as
\displaystyle h\rhd\varphi=\textstyle\sum_{(\varphi)}\varphi_{(1)}\,\varphi_{(2)}(h)
,
\displaystyle\varphi\lhd h=\textstyle\sum_{(\varphi)}\varphi_{(1)}(h)\varphi_{(2)}
.

Remark 3.1
.

More generally, if
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}
is a full subcategory of
\displaystyle H\text{-}\mathrm{mod}
(the finite-dimensional
\displaystyle H
-modules) which is stable by monoidal product, finite direct sums, and duals then there is a Hopf algebra
\displaystyle H^{\circ}_{\mathcal{C}}\subset H^{\circ}
spanned by the matrix coefficients of objects in
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}
; note that this space is stable by the coregular actions of
\displaystyle H
. One can replace
\displaystyle H^{\circ}
with such an
\displaystyle H^{\circ}_{\mathcal{C}}
everywhere in the discussion below.

We now make three assumptions on the Hopf algebra
\displaystyle H
.

Assumption 1:

\displaystyle H^{\circ}
separates the points of
\displaystyle H
,
i.e.
the duality pairing
\displaystyle\langle\>,\>\rangle:H^{\circ}\times H\to\Bbbk
is non-degenerate on the right (it is non-degenerate on the left by definition of
\displaystyle H^{\circ}
). We write indifferently
\displaystyle\varphi(h)
or
\displaystyle\langle\varphi,h\rangle
.

Assumption 2:
We are given a morphism of Hopf algebras
\displaystyle\Phi^{+}:H^{\circ}\to H^{\mathrm{cop}}
(where
\displaystyle H^{\mathrm{cop}}
is
\displaystyle H
with opposite coproduct) such that

(6)

\forall\,\varphi\in H^{\circ},\>\>\forall\,h\in H,\quad\sum_{(h)}h_{(1)}\Phi^{+}\bigl(\varphi\lhd h_{(2)}\bigr)=\sum_{(h)}\Phi^{+}\bigl(h_{(1)}\rhd\varphi\bigr)h_{(2)}.

Assumption 3:
There exists a
\displaystyle\Bbbk
-linear map
\displaystyle\Phi^{-}:H^{\circ}\to H
such that

(7)

\forall\,\varphi,\psi\in H^{\circ},\quad\varphi\bigl(\Phi^{-}(\psi)\bigr)=\psi\bigl(\Phi^{+}(S(\varphi))\bigr).

Several comments are in order. First, since
\displaystyle H^{\circ}
separates the points of
\displaystyle H
, it is easy to see that
\displaystyle\Phi^{-}
has exactly the same properties as
\displaystyle\Phi^{+}
: it is a morphism of Hopf algebras
\displaystyle H^{\circ}\to H^{\mathrm{cop}}
which satisfies (
6
).

It is a straightforward exercise to check from Assumption 2 that the bilinear form

(8)

\mathcal{R}:H^{\circ}\otimes H^{\circ}\to\Bbbk,\quad\mathcal{R}(\varphi\otimes\psi)=\psi\bigl(\Phi^{+}(\varphi)\bigr)

is a co-R-matrix in the sense of Majid
[
58
, Def. 2.2.1]
,
i.e.
we have

(9)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{(\varphi),(\psi)}\mathcal{R}(\varphi_{(1)}\otimes\psi_{(1)})\varphi_{(2)}\star\psi_{(2)}=\sum_{(\varphi),(\psi)}\mathcal{R}(\varphi_{(2)}\otimes\psi_{(2)})\psi_{(1)}\star\varphi_{(1)},

(10)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(\varphi\star\psi\otimes\eta)=\sum_{(\eta)}\mathcal{R}\bigl(\varphi\otimes\eta_{(1)}\bigr)\mathcal{R}\bigl(\psi\otimes\eta_{(2)}\bigr),

(11)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(\varphi\otimes\psi\star\eta)=\sum_{(\varphi)}\mathcal{R}\bigl(\varphi_{(1)}\otimes\eta\bigr)\mathcal{R}\bigl(\varphi_{(2)}\otimes\psi\bigr)

for all
\displaystyle\varphi,\psi,\eta\in H^{\circ}
. The equality (
9
) comes from (
6
) while (
10
)–(
11
) directly follow from the fact that
\displaystyle\Phi^{+}
is a morphism of Hopf algebra and the definition of
\displaystyle\star
. Note that these identities imply

(12)

\mathcal{R}\bigl(\varphi\otimes 1_{H^{\circ}}\bigr)=\mathcal{R}\bigl(1_{H^{\circ}}\otimes\varphi\bigr)=\varepsilon_{H^{\circ}}(\varphi).

The map
\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{-1}:H^{\circ}\otimes H^{\circ}\to\Bbbk
given by
\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{-1}(\varphi\otimes\psi)=\psi\bigl(\Phi^{+}(S(\varphi))\bigr)=\mathcal{R}\bigl(S(\varphi)\otimes\psi\bigr)
is the
convolution-inverse
of
\displaystyle\mathcal{R}
, meaning that

\sum_{(\varphi),(\psi)}\mathcal{R}(\varphi_{(1)}\otimes\psi_{(1)})\mathcal{R}^{-1}(\varphi_{(2)}\otimes\psi_{(2)})=\sum_{(\varphi),(\psi)}\mathcal{R}^{-1}(\varphi_{(1)}\otimes\psi_{(1)})\mathcal{R}(\varphi_{(2)}\otimes\psi_{(2)})=\varepsilon_{H^{\circ}}(\varphi)\varepsilon_{H^{\circ}}(\psi)

for all
\displaystyle\varphi,\psi\in H^{\circ}
. We note however that the existence of the map
\displaystyle\Phi^{+}
is stronger than the existence of a co-R-matrix; indeed,
\displaystyle\mathcal{R}
yields the map
\displaystyle H^{\circ}\to(H^{\circ})^{*}
given by
\displaystyle\varphi\mapsto\mathcal{R}(\varphi\otimes\text{-})
but it does not necessarily takes values in
\displaystyle H\subset(H^{\circ})^{*}
.

When
\displaystyle H
is quasitriangular with R-matrix
\displaystyle R\in H^{\otimes 2}
, we put
\displaystyle R^{+}:=R
,
\displaystyle R^{-}:=(R^{fl})^{-1}
, where
\displaystyle R^{fl}
is
\displaystyle R
with tensorands permuted. Note that
\displaystyle R^{-}
is also an
\displaystyle R
-matrix for
\displaystyle H
. Then there is the canonical choice

(13)

\Phi^{+}(\varphi)=(\varphi\otimes\mathrm{id})(R),\qquad\Phi^{-}(\varphi)=(\mathrm{id}\otimes\varphi)(R^{-1})=(\varphi\otimes\mathrm{id})(R^{-}).

When
\displaystyle H
is finite-dimensional, we have
\displaystyle H^{\circ}=H^{*}
and the existence of
\displaystyle\Phi^{+}
is equivalent to the existence of a co-R-matrix, which is itself equivalent to the existence of an R-matrix. Thus, in this case,
\displaystyle\Phi^{+}
is an alternative definition of quasitriangularity in terms of Hopf algebra morphisms
\displaystyle H^{*}\to H
satisfying (
6
); this was noted by Radford in
[
63
]
.

The quantum enveloping algebra
\displaystyle U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
and its dual quantum function algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}(G)
form an example of the above setup, as will be explained in §
4.2
.

Finally, we record the following commutation relations, for any signs
\displaystyle\sigma,\tau\in\{\pm\}

(14)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{(\varphi),(\psi)}\mathcal{R}^{(\sigma)}\bigl(\varphi_{(1)}\otimes\psi_{(1)}\bigr)\Phi^{\sigma}(\varphi_{(2)})\Phi^{\tau}(\psi_{(2)})&=\sum_{(\varphi),(\psi)}\mathcal{R}^{(\sigma)}\bigl(\varphi_{(2)}\otimes\psi_{(2)}\bigr)\Phi^{\tau}(\psi_{(1)})\Phi^{\sigma}(\varphi_{(1)})\\
\sum_{(\varphi),(\psi)}\mathcal{R}^{(\sigma)}\bigl(\varphi_{(1)}\otimes\psi_{(1)}\bigr)\Phi^{\tau}(\varphi_{(2)})\Phi^{\tau}(\psi_{(2)})&=\sum_{(\varphi),(\psi)}\mathcal{R}^{(\sigma)}\bigl(\varphi_{(2)}\otimes\psi_{(2)}\bigr)\Phi^{\tau}(\psi_{(1)})\Phi^{\tau}(\varphi_{(1)})\end{split}

where
\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{(+)}=\mathcal{R}
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{(-)}(\varphi\otimes\psi)=\mathcal{R}^{-1}(\psi\otimes\varphi)=\mathcal{R}\bigl(S(\psi)\otimes\varphi\bigr)
so that

(15)

\forall\,\varphi,\psi\in H^{\circ},\quad\mathcal{R}^{(\sigma)}(\varphi\otimes\psi)=\psi\bigl(\Phi^{\sigma}(\varphi)\bigr)

(note that
\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{(\sigma)}
is a co-R-matrix). The second equality in (
14
) is obvious as
\displaystyle\Phi^{\tau}
is an algebra morphism and
\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{\sigma}
satisfies (
9
); the first is independant of the second in case
\displaystyle\sigma
and
\displaystyle\tau
are distinct, and in this case it follows by taking
\displaystyle h=\Phi^{\sigma}(\varphi)
in the property (
6
) for
\displaystyle\Phi^{\tau}
(replacing
\displaystyle\varphi
with
\displaystyle\psi
).

3.2.
Definition of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)

Given a datum
\displaystyle(H,\Phi^{\pm})
as in the previous section, we define an algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
for any integers
\displaystyle g,n\geq 0
not both equal to
\displaystyle 0
. As a
\displaystyle\Bbbk
-vector space,
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)=(H^{\circ})^{\otimes(2g+n)}
. Let
\displaystyle b_{1},a_{1},\ldots,b_{g},a_{g},m_{g+1},\ldots,m_{g+n}
be formal symbols
1
1

1

These symbols have to be interpreted as the standard generators of the fundamental group of the oriented surface of genus
\displaystyle g
with
\displaystyle n
punctures and one boundary component.
and label each tensorand by one of these symbols as follows:

(16)

\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)=\underset{(b_{1})}{H^{\circ}}\otimes\underset{(a_{1})}{H^{\circ}}\otimes\ldots\otimes\underset{(b_{g})}{H^{\circ}}\otimes\underset{(a_{g})}{H^{\circ}}\otimes\underset{(m_{g+1})}{H^{\circ}}\otimes\ldots\otimes\underset{(m_{g+n})}{H^{\circ}}.

In this way we get
\displaystyle\Bbbk
-linear embeddings
\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{b_{i}},\mathfrak{i}_{a_{i}},\mathfrak{i}_{m_{g+j}}:H^{\circ}\to\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
defined by
\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{b_{1}}(\varphi)=\varphi\otimes 1_{H^{\circ}}\otimes\ldots\otimes 1_{H^{\circ}}

etc
. An associative product in
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
is defined by the following four rules, which use the co-R-matrix
\displaystyle\mathcal{R}:H^{\circ}\otimes H^{\circ}\to\Bbbk
from (
8
).

(i) Elements in
\displaystyle(H^{\circ})^{\otimes(2g+n)}
can be written as linear combinations of “ordered monomials”:

(17)

\varphi_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\varphi_{2g+n}=\mathfrak{i}_{b_{1}}(\varphi_{1})\mathfrak{i}_{a_{1}}(\varphi_{2})\ldots\mathfrak{i}_{b_{g}}(\varphi_{2g-1})\mathfrak{i}_{a_{g}}(\varphi_{2g})\mathfrak{i}_{m_{g+1}}(\varphi_{2g+1})\ldots\mathfrak{i}_{m_{g+n}}(\varphi_{2g+n}).

(ii) For all
\displaystyle s\in\{b_{1},a_{1},\ldots,b_{g},a_{g},m_{g+1},\ldots,m_{g+n}\}
, we have a “fusion relation”:

(18)

\mathfrak{i}_{s}(\varphi)\mathfrak{i}_{s}(\psi)=\sum_{(\varphi),(\psi)}\mathcal{R}\bigl(\psi_{(1)}\otimes S(\varphi_{(3)})\bigr)\mathcal{R}\bigl(\psi_{(3)}\otimes\varphi_{(2)}\bigr)\,\mathfrak{i}_{s}\bigl(\varphi_{(1)}\star\psi_{(2)}\bigr)

where we use the usual Hopf algebra structure on
\displaystyle H^{\circ}
recalled in (
4
).

(iii) For all
\displaystyle 1\leq i\leq g
, we have an exchange relation:

(19)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{a_{i}}(\varphi)\mathfrak{i}_{b_{i}}(\psi)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{(\varphi),(\psi)}\mathcal{R}\bigl(\psi_{(1)}\otimes S(\varphi_{(5)})\bigr)\mathcal{R}\bigl(\psi_{(2)}\otimes\varphi_{(1)}\bigr)\mathcal{R}\bigl(\psi_{(5)}\otimes\varphi_{(4)}\bigr)\mathcal{R}\bigl(\varphi_{(2)}\otimes\psi_{(4)}\bigr)\,\mathfrak{i}_{b_{i}}(\psi_{(3)})\mathfrak{i}_{a_{i}}(\varphi_{(3)}).

(iv) Put
\displaystyle\mathrm{ind}(b_{i})=\mathrm{ind}(a_{i})=i
,
\displaystyle\mathrm{ind}(m_{g+j})=g+j
. Then for all
\displaystyle s,t\in\{b_{1},a_{1},\ldots,b_{g},a_{g}
,
\displaystyle m_{g+1},\ldots,m_{g+n}\}
such that
\displaystyle\mathrm{ind}(t)>\mathrm{ind}(s)
, we have another exchange relation:

(20)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathfrak{i}_{t}(\varphi)\mathfrak{i}_{s}(\psi)=\\
&\sum_{(\varphi),(\psi)}\mathcal{R}\bigl(\psi_{(1)}\otimes S(\varphi_{(5)})\bigr)\mathcal{R}\bigl(\psi_{(2)}\otimes\varphi_{(1)}\bigr)\mathcal{R}\bigl(S(\psi_{(4)})\otimes\varphi_{(2)}\bigr)\mathcal{R}\bigl(\psi_{(5)}\otimes\varphi_{(4)}\bigr)\,\mathfrak{i}_{s}(\psi_{(3)})\mathfrak{i}_{t}(\varphi_{(3)}).\!\!\!\!\!\!\end{split}

The algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
is called the
graph algebra
of the surface
\displaystyle\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ}
(oriented, genus
\displaystyle g
,
\displaystyle n
punctures and one boundary circle). The rules (ii)–(iv) allow one to compute the product of any pair of elements of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
by (
17
).

Note that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)
is just the vector space
\displaystyle H^{\circ}
equipped with the product given by taking
\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{s}=\mathrm{id}_{H^{\circ}}
in the rule (ii); it is a version of Majid’s braided dual construction, also called transmutation
[
57
,
58
]
. The linear maps
\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{t}:\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)\to\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
are morphisms of algebras for any
\displaystyle t\in\bigl\{b_{1},a_{1},\ldots,b_{g},a_{g},m_{g+1},\ldots,m_{g+n}\bigr\}
.

Remark 3.2
.

1. When
\displaystyle H
is quasitriangular and the datum
\displaystyle\Phi^{\pm}
is chosen as in (
13
), the algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
defined above is exactly the same as in
[
14
]
.

The (right) coadjoint action
\displaystyle\mathrm{coad}^{r}
of any
\displaystyle h\in H
on any
\displaystyle\varphi\in H^{\circ}
is

(21)

\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h)(\varphi)=\sum_{(h)}S(h_{(2)})\rhd\varphi\lhd h_{(1)}=\sum_{(\varphi)}\bigl\langle\varphi_{(1)}\star S(\varphi_{(3)}),h\bigr\rangle\,\varphi_{(2)}

with the coregular actions (
5
). Thanks to the iterated coproduct
\displaystyle\Delta^{(2g+n)}:H\to H^{\otimes(2g+n)}
we get an action of
\displaystyle H
on
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)=(H^{\circ})^{\otimes(2g+n)}
, still denoted by
\displaystyle\mathrm{coad}^{r}
:

(22)

\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h)(\varphi_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\varphi_{2g+n})=\sum_{(h)}\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h_{(1)})(\varphi_{1})\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h_{(2g+n)})(\varphi_{2g+n}).

Equipped with this action,
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
becomes a right
\displaystyle H
-module-algebra
:

\forall\,h\in H,\>\>\forall\,a,b\in\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H),\quad\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h)(ab)=\sum_{(h)}\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h_{(1)})(a)\,\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h_{(2)})(b).

The subspace of invariant elements forms therefore a subalgebra called the
moduli algebra
and denoted by
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{H}_{g,n}(H)
(the notation
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\mathrm{inv}}(H)
is also used in some other papers on graph algebras):

(23)

\mathcal{L}^{H}_{g,n}(H)=\bigl\{x\in\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)\,\big|\,\forall\,h\in H,\>\>\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h)(x)=\varepsilon(h)x\bigr\}.

It is convenient to introduce the left coaction
\displaystyle\delta:\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)\to H^{\circ}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)
which is dual to the right action
\displaystyle\mathrm{coad}^{r}:\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)\otimes H\to\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)

(24)

\delta(\varphi)=\sum_{(\varphi)}\bigl(\varphi_{(1)}\star S(\varphi_{(3)})\bigr)\otimes\varphi_{(2)},\quad\text{written as }\delta(\varphi)=\sum_{[\varphi]}\varphi_{[1]}\otimes\varphi_{[2]}

with a Sweedler type notation for coactions. More generally we have a left coaction
\displaystyle\delta:\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)\to H^{\circ}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
dual to
\displaystyle\mathrm{coad}^{r}
, still denoted
\displaystyle\delta(x)=\textstyle\sum_{[x]}x_{[1]}\otimes x_{[2]}
; it yields a structure of left
\displaystyle H^{\circ}
-comodule-algebra. For instance in
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)
it is

\delta(\beta\otimes\alpha)=\sum_{[\beta],[\alpha]}(\alpha_{[1]}\star\beta_{[1]})\otimes\beta_{[2]}\otimes\alpha_{[2]}.

In particular
\displaystyle\varphi\in\mathcal{L}^{H}_{g,n}(H)
if, and only if,

(25)

\sum_{[\varphi]}\varphi_{[1]}\otimes\varphi_{[2]}=\varepsilon\otimes\varphi.

This coaction can be used to rewrite the exchange relation (
20
) as follows. As vector spaces we have the decomposition
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)=\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)^{\otimes g}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)^{\otimes n}
, which gives tensor-wise embeddings of algebras
\displaystyle\mathfrak{j}_{k}:\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)\to\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
for
\displaystyle k=1,\ldots,g
and
\displaystyle\mathfrak{j}_{g+l}=\mathfrak{i}_{m_{g+l}}:\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)\to\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
for
\displaystyle l=1,\ldots,n
; the relation with notations introduced after (
16
) is
\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{a_{k}}=\mathfrak{j}_{k}\circ\mathfrak{i}_{a}
and
\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{b_{k}}=\mathfrak{j}_{k}\circ\mathfrak{i}_{b}
, where
\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{b},\mathfrak{i}_{a}
are embeddings of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)=H^{\circ}
into
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)=H^{\circ}\otimes H^{\circ}
. Then by (
17
) we have

(26)

x_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes x_{g}\otimes\varphi_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\varphi_{n}=\mathfrak{j}_{1}(x_{1})\ldots\mathfrak{j}_{g}(x_{g})\mathfrak{j}_{g+1}(\varphi_{1})\ldots\mathfrak{j}_{g+n}(\varphi_{n})

and using basic properties of the co-R-matrix
\displaystyle\mathcal{R}
, (
20
) is easily seen to be equivalent to

(27)

\forall\,1\leq k<l\leq g+n,\quad\mathfrak{j}_{l}(y)\mathfrak{j}_{k}(x)=\sum_{[x],[y]}\mathcal{R}\bigl(x_{[1]}\otimes y_{[1]}\bigr)\,\mathfrak{j}_{k}(x_{[2]})\mathfrak{j}_{l}(y_{[2]}).

The properties (
26
)–(
27
) mean that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)=\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)^{\widetilde{\otimes}\,g}\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)^{\widetilde{\otimes}\,n}
, where
\displaystyle\widetilde{\otimes}
is the
braided tensor product
in the category of
\displaystyle H^{\circ}
-comodules endowed with the braiding defined by
\displaystyle\mathcal{R}
; see
[
14
, §4.1]
for details in the quasitriangular case.

Lemma 3.3
.

1. We have
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{H}(H)\subset\mathcal{Z}\bigl(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)\bigr)
.

2. The subspace
\displaystyle\bigl(1_{\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)}\bigr)^{\otimes g}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{H}(H)^{\otimes n}
is a central subalgebra of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
.

Proof.

1. Note that the product in
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)
can be rewritten as

(28)

\varphi\psi=\sum_{(\varphi),[\psi]}\mathcal{R}\bigl(\psi_{[1]}\otimes S(\varphi_{(2)})\bigr)\,\varphi_{(1)}\star\psi_{[2]}\quad\text{and}\quad\varphi\psi=\sum_{[\varphi],(\psi)}\mathcal{R}\bigl(\psi_{(1)}\otimes\varphi_{[1]}\bigr)\,\psi_{(2)}\star\varphi_{[2]}

thanks to the basic properties (
10
)–(
11
) of a co-R-matrix and also (
9
) for the second equality. By (
25
) and (
12
) the first equality gives
\displaystyle\psi\varphi=\psi\star\varphi
(we switch
\displaystyle\varphi
and
\displaystyle\psi
) while the second reduces to
\displaystyle\varphi\psi=\psi\star\varphi
, whence the claim.

2. Fix
\displaystyle 1\leq l\leq n
and
\displaystyle\varphi\in\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{H}(H)
. By (
26
) it suffices to show that
\displaystyle\mathfrak{j}_{g+l}(\varphi)
commutes with
\displaystyle\mathfrak{j}_{k}(x)
for all
\displaystyle 1\leq k\leq g+n
. For
\displaystyle k\neq g+l
the commutation is clear from (
27
) and (
12
). When
\displaystyle k=g+l
, by the first item
\displaystyle\mathfrak{j}_{g+l}(\varphi)
commutes with
\displaystyle\mathfrak{j}_{g+l}(\psi)
.
∎

The inclusion in Lemma
3.3
(1) is an equality for
\displaystyle H=U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})

[
11
, Prop. 6.19]
and also for
\displaystyle H
finite-dimensional and factorizable
[
37
, Th. 3.7]
. We remark that it is not true that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}^{H}(H)\subset\mathcal{Z}\bigl(\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)\bigr)
, which explains that Lemma
3.3
(2) only concerns the “
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,n}
part” of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
.

It should be noted that the definition of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
only uses the co-R-matrix
\displaystyle\mathcal{R}:H^{\circ}\otimes H^{\circ}\to\Bbbk
; the morphisms
\displaystyle\Phi^{\pm}
will enter in the game only in the next sections. Hence the
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
construction applies to any co-quasitriangular Hopf algebra
\displaystyle(O,\mathcal{R})
, not necessarily of the form
\displaystyle H^{\circ}
. We could denote the resulting algebra as
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}[O]
, which is
\displaystyle O^{\otimes(2g+n)}
as a vector space.
2
2

2

This notation is only used here and in the proof of Prop.
5.19
.
It generalizes the above definition because
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)=\mathcal{L}_{g,n}[H^{\circ}]
. This construction is “functorial”:

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
\displaystyle(O_{1},\mathcal{R}_{1})
and
\displaystyle(O_{2},\mathcal{R}_{2})
be co-quasitriangular Hopf algebras. Let
\displaystyle f:O_{1}\to O_{2}
be a morphism of Hopf algebras such that
\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{1}=\mathcal{R}_{2}\circ(f\otimes f)
. Then
\displaystyle f^{\otimes(2g+n)}:\mathcal{L}_{g,n}[O_{1}]\to\mathcal{L}_{g,n}[O_{2}]
is a morphism of algebras.

Proof.

For
\displaystyle r=1,2
, let
\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{b_{i}}^{r},\mathfrak{i}_{a_{i}}^{r},\mathfrak{i}_{m_{g+j}}^{r}:O_{r}\to\mathcal{L}_{g,n}[O_{r}]
be the embeddings from which the product of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}[O_{r}]
is defined. They satisfy
\displaystyle f^{\otimes(2g+n)}\circ\mathfrak{i}^{1}_{s}=\mathfrak{i}^{2}_{s}\circ f
. By (
17
), it suffices to check that
\displaystyle f^{\otimes(2g+n)}\bigl(\mathfrak{i}^{1}_{s}(\varphi)\mathfrak{i}^{1}_{t}(\psi)\bigr)=\mathfrak{i}^{2}_{s}\bigl(f(\varphi)\bigr)\mathfrak{i}^{2}_{t}\bigl(f(\psi)\bigr)
for all
\displaystyle\varphi,\psi\in F_{1}
and all
\displaystyle s,t\in\{b_{i},a_{i},m_{g+j}\}
. This is straightforward and left to the reader.
∎

3.3.
Quantum moment map

We continue with the datum
