\displaystyle(H,\Phi^{\pm})
as in §
3.1
. The following map is of extreme importance in the theory of graph algebras

(29)

\Phi_{0,1}:\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)\to H,\quad\varphi\mapsto\sum_{(\varphi)}\Phi^{+}(\varphi_{(1)})\Phi^{-}\bigl(S(\varphi_{(2)})\bigr)

where we use the usual coproduct (
4
) in
\displaystyle H^{\circ}
.
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}
is a morphism of
\displaystyle H
-module-algebras provided
\displaystyle H
is equipped with the right adjoint action

(30)

\forall\,h,x\in H,\quad\mathrm{ad}^{r}(h)(x)=\sum_{(h)}S(h_{(1)})xh_{(2)}.

This is shown thanks to (
14
) and (
6
). When
\displaystyle H
is quasitriangular with R-matrix
\displaystyle\textstyle R=\sum_{(R)}R_{(1)}\otimes R_{(2)}
and
\displaystyle\Phi^{\pm}
is chosen as in (
13
), this reads
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}(\varphi)=(\varphi\otimes\mathrm{id})(RR^{fl})
, where as usual
\displaystyle\textstyle R^{fl}=\sum_{(R)}R_{(2)}\otimes R_{(1)}
is the flip of
\displaystyle R
.

Now let us make the following assumptions on
\displaystyle(H,\Phi^{\pm})
:

•
\displaystyle H^{\circ}
is
coribbon
, meaning that there is a linear form
\displaystyle\mathsf{v}:H^{\circ}\to\Bbbk
satisfying
\displaystyle\mathsf{v}\circ S=\mathsf{v}
and
3
3

3

These axioms are dual to those of a ribbon element
[
26
, §4.2.C]
, in the sense that if
\displaystyle v\in H
is a ribbon element, then
\displaystyle\mathsf{v}=\langle-,v\rangle:H^{\circ}\to\Bbbk
is a coribbon element.

(31)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\textstyle\sum_{(\varphi)}\mathsf{v}(\varphi_{(1)})\varphi_{(2)}=\sum_{(\varphi)}\varphi_{(1)}\mathsf{v}(\varphi_{(2)})

(32)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\textstyle\mathsf{v}(\varphi\psi)=\sum_{(\varphi),(\psi)}\mathcal{R}^{-1}\bigl(\psi_{(3)}\otimes\varphi_{(3)}\bigr)\mathcal{R}^{-1}\bigl(\varphi_{(2)}\otimes\psi_{(2)}\bigr)\mathsf{v}(\varphi_{(1)})\,\mathsf{v}(\psi_{(1)})

(33)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathsf{v}^{2}(\varphi)=\textstyle\sum_{(\varphi)}\mathsf{u}(\varphi_{(1)})\mathsf{u}\bigl(S(\varphi_{(2)})\bigr)\quad\text{where }\mathsf{u}(\varphi)=\sum_{(\varphi)}\mathcal{R}\bigl(\varphi_{(2)}\otimes S(\varphi_{(1)})\bigr)

where
\displaystyle\mathsf{v}^{2}=(\mathsf{v}\otimes\mathsf{v})\circ\Delta
and
\displaystyle\mathsf{u}
is called the
co-Drinfeld element
. These axioms imply that
\displaystyle\mathsf{v}
is convolution-invertible because so is
\displaystyle\mathsf{u}

[
58
, Prop. 2.2.4]
and it holds
\displaystyle\mathsf{v}(1_{H^{\circ}})=1
.

• The morphism
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}
is injective.

We introduce auxiliary maps from which the so-called quantum moment map will be defined. First let

(34)

\mathfrak{d}_{1,0}:\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)\to\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H),\quad\varphi\mapsto\sum_{(\varphi)}\mathsf{v}^{2}(\varphi_{(1)})\,\mathfrak{i}_{b}(\varphi_{(2)})\mathfrak{i}_{a}\bigl(S_{\mathcal{L}}(\varphi_{(3)})\bigr)\mathfrak{i}_{b}\bigl(S_{\mathcal{L}}(\varphi_{(4)})\bigr)\mathfrak{i}_{a}(\varphi_{(5)})

where we use the usual coproduct (
4
) in
\displaystyle H^{\circ}
and
\displaystyle S_{\mathcal{L}}
is the antipode of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)
, uniquely defined by
\displaystyle\textstyle\sum_{(\varphi)}\varphi_{(1)}S_{\mathcal{L}}(\varphi_{(2)})=\sum_{(\varphi)}S_{\mathcal{L}}(\varphi_{(1)})\varphi_{(2)}=\varphi(1_{H})
. Explicitly:

(35)

\forall\,\varphi\in\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H),\quad S_{\mathcal{L}}(\varphi)=\textstyle\sum_{(\varphi)}\mathcal{R}^{-1}\bigl(\varphi_{(4)}\otimes\varphi_{(1)}\bigr)\mathsf{u}^{-1}(\varphi_{(2)})\,S_{H^{\circ}}(\varphi_{(3)})

with
\displaystyle\mathsf{u}
from (
33
) (see the discussion after
[
14
, eq. (3.24)]
). Now observe that as a vector space
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
is
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)^{\otimes g}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)^{\otimes n}
. Hence we can define

(36)

\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}:\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)\to\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H),\quad\varphi\mapsto\sum_{(\varphi)}\mathfrak{d}_{1,0}(\varphi_{(1)})\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathfrak{d}_{1,0}(\varphi_{(g)})\otimes\varphi_{(g+1)}\otimes\ldots\otimes\varphi_{(g+n)}.

The map
\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}
is easily seen to be a morphism of
\displaystyle H
-modules with respect to the right actions
\displaystyle\mathrm{coad}^{r}
.

Remark 3.5
.

Let
\displaystyle X
be a finite-dimensional
\displaystyle H
-module and
\displaystyle x^{i}(?\cdot x_{j})\in H^{\circ}
be the matrix coefficients of
\displaystyle X
in some basis
\displaystyle(x_{j})
. Then the matrix
\displaystyle\overset{X}{C}=\bigl[\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}\bigl(x^{i}(?\cdot x_{j})\bigr)\bigr]_{i,j}
with coefficients in
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
equals the matrix from
[
14
, Def. 7.11]
. The use of the antipode
\displaystyle S_{\mathcal{L}}
corresponds to matrix inverse in the notations of
[
14
]
.

Denote by
\displaystyle H^{\prime}
the image of the morphism
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}
from (
29
). The following map is called
quantum moment map
and will play an important role

(37)

\mu_{g,n}:H^{\prime}=\Phi_{0,1}\bigl(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)\bigr)\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt\Phi_{0,1}^{-1}\hskip 8.19447pt}\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}\hskip 8.19447pt}\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H).

From the definitions we have

(38)

\forall\,\varphi\in H^{\circ},\quad\Delta\bigl(\Phi_{0,1}(\varphi)\bigr)=\sum_{(\varphi)}\Phi_{0,1}(\varphi_{(2)})\otimes\Phi^{+}(\varphi_{(1)})\Phi^{-}\bigl(S(\varphi_{(3)})\bigr)

which implies that
\displaystyle\Delta(H^{\prime})\subset H^{\prime}\otimes H
(right coideal), and thus equality (
39
) below makes sense.

Theorem 3.6
.

[
14
, Th. 7.14]

\displaystyle\mu_{g,n}
is a morphism of algebras satisfying the quantum moment map property, i.e.

(39)

\forall h^{\prime}\in H^{\prime},\>\>\forall\,x\in\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H),\quad\mu_{g,n}(h^{\prime})\,x=\sum_{(h^{\prime})}\,\mathrm{coad}^{r}\bigl(S^{-1}(h^{\prime}_{(2)})\bigr)(x)\,\mu_{g,n}(h^{\prime}_{(1)}).

This can also be written as
\displaystyle\textstyle x\mu_{g,n}(h^{\prime})=\sum_{(h^{\prime})}\mu_{g,n}(h_{(1)}^{\prime})\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h_{(2)}^{\prime})(x)
.

Note in particular that
\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}:\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)\to\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
is a morphism of algebras.

The question of whether the quantum moment map is injective reduces to a ground case:

Lemma 3.7
.

1.
\displaystyle\mu_{g,n}
is always injective for all
\displaystyle g
and
\displaystyle n\neq 0
.

2. If
\displaystyle\mu_{1,0}
is injective then
\displaystyle\mu_{g,0}
is injective for all
\displaystyle g
.

Proof.

By very definition,
\displaystyle\mu_{g,n}
is injective if and only if
\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}
is injective. One can check that
\displaystyle(\varepsilon_{H^{\circ}})^{\otimes 2}\bigl(\mathfrak{d}_{1,0}(\varphi)\bigr)=\varepsilon_{H^{\circ}}(\varphi)
, where we use that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)=H^{\circ}\otimes H^{\circ}
as a vector space. Hence if
\displaystyle n>0
we have
\displaystyle\bigl[\bigl((\varepsilon_{H^{\circ}})^{\otimes 2}\bigr)^{\otimes g}\otimes(\varepsilon_{H^{\circ}})^{\otimes(n-1)}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{H^{\circ}}\bigr]\bigl(\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}(\varphi)\bigr)=\varphi
, thus proving claim 1. For claim 2, note similarly that
\displaystyle\bigl[\bigl((\varepsilon_{H^{\circ}})^{\otimes 2}\bigr)^{\otimes(g-1)}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{(H^{\circ})^{\otimes 2}}\bigr]\bigl(\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}(\varphi)\bigr)=\mathfrak{d}_{1,0}(\varphi)
.
∎

3.4.
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)
and the Heisenberg double

The
Heisenberg double

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})
is the vector space
\displaystyle H^{\circ}\otimes H
endowed with the algebra structure defined by the following rules:

(i)
\displaystyle H^{\circ}\otimes 1_{H}
and
\displaystyle 1_{H^{\circ}}\otimes H
are subalgebras of
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})
isomorphic to
\displaystyle H^{\circ}
and
\displaystyle H
. We write
\displaystyle\varphi
instead of
\displaystyle\varphi\otimes 1_{H}
, and
\displaystyle a
instead of
\displaystyle 1_{H^{\circ}}\otimes a
(where
\displaystyle 1_{H^{\circ}}=\varepsilon
).

(ii) With these identifications, we have the relations

(40)

\forall\,a\in H,\>\>\forall\,\varphi\in H^{\circ},\quad\varphi a=\varphi\otimes a,\quad h\varphi=\sum_{(a)}(a_{(1)}\rhd\varphi)\,a_{(2)}.

If we define a right action of
\displaystyle H
on
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})
by

(41)

(\varphi a)\cdot h=\sum_{(h)}\bigl(S(h_{(2)})\rhd\varphi\lhd h_{(3)}\bigr)S(h_{(1)})ah_{(4)}

then
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})
becomes a right
\displaystyle H
-module algebra
[
14
, §3.3]
. There is a representation

(42)

\rho:\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})\to\mathrm{End}_{\Bbbk}(H^{\circ}),\quad\rho(\varphi a)(\psi)=\varphi\star(a\rhd\psi)\qquad(\forall\,\psi\in H^{\circ}).

The morphism
\displaystyle\rho
is injective, without any assumption on
\displaystyle H

[
62
, Lem. 9.4.2]
.

Recall from (
17
) that any element in
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)
is a sum of products
\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{b}(\beta)\mathfrak{i}_{a}(\alpha)
for
\displaystyle\alpha,\beta\in\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)
. There is a morphism of right
\displaystyle H
-module-algebras
\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}:\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)\to\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})
uniquely characterized by

(43)

\forall\,\varphi\in H^{\circ},\quad\Phi_{1,0}\bigl(\mathfrak{i}_{a}(\varphi)\bigr)=\Phi_{0,1}(\varphi),\qquad\Phi_{1,0}\bigl(\mathfrak{i}_{b}(\varphi)\bigr)=\sum_{(\varphi)}\Phi^{+}(\varphi_{(1)})\varphi_{(2)}\Phi^{-}\bigl(S(\varphi_{(3)})\bigr)

where we use the morphism
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}:\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)\to H
from (
29
). In the case where
\displaystyle H
is quasitriangular with
\displaystyle\Phi^{\pm}
as in (
13
), the fact that
\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}
is a morphism of right
\displaystyle H
-module-algebras was proven in
[
1
]
(see also
[
14
, §3.3]
). In the present general setting one must show that the defining relations of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)
(fusion relation for
\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{b}
and
\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{a}
and exchange relation between
\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{b}
and
\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{a}
) are compatible with (
43
); the computations use (
14
) and the following exchange relation in
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})
:

(44)

\forall\,\sigma\in\{\pm\},\>\>\forall\,\varphi,\psi\in H^{\circ},\quad\Phi^{\sigma}(\varphi)\psi=\sum_{(\varphi),(\psi)}\mathcal{R}^{(\sigma)}\bigl(\varphi_{(2)}\otimes\psi_{(2)}\bigr)\psi_{(1)}\Phi^{\sigma}\bigl(\varphi_{(1)}\bigr)

which directly follows from (
40
) and (
15
). Details are left to the reader.

Let us now investigate how the QMM
\displaystyle\mu_{1,0}
interacts with the morphism
\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}
; thus we resume the assumptions on
\displaystyle H
from §
3.3
used to construct
\displaystyle\mu_{g,n}
(
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}
injective and existence of coribbon element
\displaystyle\mathsf{v}
). Recall that
\displaystyle H^{\prime}
denotes the image of the morphism
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}
from (
29
). For all
\displaystyle h^{\prime}=\Phi_{0,1}(\varphi)\in H^{\prime}
we define

(45)

\widehat{h^{\prime}}=\sum_{[\varphi]}\varphi_{[1]}\otimes S^{-1}\bigl(\Phi_{0,1}(\varphi_{[2]})\bigr)\in H^{\circ}\otimes H=\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})

using the coaction
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)\to H^{\circ}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)
from (
24
).

The definition of the elements
\displaystyle\widehat{h^{\prime}}
in the form of (
47
) below, and their remarkable properties given in Lemma
3.8
, are directly inspired from the computations in
[
38
, Lem. 4.1, also see §2.2]
which was for finite-dimensional
\displaystyle H
. Note that there the elements
\displaystyle\widehat{h}
were denoted
\displaystyle\widetilde{h}
, not to be confused with the elements
\displaystyle\widetilde{h}
in §
3.5
of the present paper. Also the equality
\displaystyle\mu_{1,0}\circ\Phi_{1,0}=\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{1,0}
from item 2 of Prop.
3.9
was proven in
[
36
, eq. (4.22)]
in the case where
\displaystyle H
is finite-dimensional.

Lemma 3.8
.

1. With the representation
\displaystyle\rho:\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})\to\mathrm{End}_{\Bbbk}(H^{\circ})
from (
42
) we have

(46)

\forall\,h^{\prime}\in H^{\prime},\>\>\forall\,\psi\in H^{\circ},\quad\rho\bigl(\widehat{h^{\prime}}\bigr)(\psi)=\psi\lhd S^{-1}(h^{\prime}).

2. In
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})
we have the relations

\textstyle\widehat{h^{\prime}h^{\prime\prime}}=\widehat{h^{\prime}}\,\widehat{h^{\prime\prime}},\qquad\widehat{h^{\prime}}\,\varphi a=\sum_{(h^{\prime})}\bigl(\varphi\lhd S^{-1}(h^{\prime}_{(2)})\bigr)a\,\widehat{\,h^{\prime}_{(1)}}

for all
\displaystyle h^{\prime},h^{\prime\prime}\in H^{\prime}
, and all
\displaystyle\varphi a\in\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})
. In particular
\displaystyle\widehat{h^{\prime}}\,a=a\,\widehat{h^{\prime}}
for all
\displaystyle a\in H\subset\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})
.

3. The right action (
41
) of
\displaystyle h\in H
on
\displaystyle\widehat{h^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})
is given by

\widehat{h^{\prime}}\cdot h=\textstyle\sum_{(h)}S(h_{(1)})h_{(4)}\,\widehat{S(h_{(2)})h^{\prime}h_{(3)}}.

4. The map
\displaystyle H^{\prime}\to\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})
,
\displaystyle h^{\prime}\mapsto\widehat{h^{\prime}}
is injective.

Proof.

1. Write
\displaystyle h^{\prime}=\Phi_{0,1}(\varphi)
. The proof is a direct computation (using Sweedler’s notation with implicit summation for coproducts and coactions):

\displaystyle\displaystyle\rho\bigl(\widehat{h^{\prime}}\bigr)(\psi)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\varphi_{[1]}\star\bigl[S^{-1}\bigl(\Phi_{0,1}(\varphi_{[2]})\bigr)\rhd\psi\bigr]=\bigl\langle\psi_{(2)},S^{-1}\bigl(\Phi_{0,1}(\varphi_{(2)})\bigr)\bigr\rangle\,\varphi_{(1)}\star S(\varphi_{(3)})\star\psi_{(1)}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\bigl\langle S^{-1}(\psi_{(2)}),\Phi^{-}\bigl(S(\varphi_{(3)})\bigr)\bigr\rangle\,\bigl\langle S^{-1}(\psi_{(3)}),\Phi^{+}(\varphi_{(2)})\bigr\rangle\,\varphi_{(1)}\star S(\varphi_{(4)})\star\psi_{(1)}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}\bigl(\psi_{(2)}\otimes S(\varphi_{(3)})\bigr)\,\mathcal{R}^{-1}\bigl(\varphi_{(2)}\otimes\psi_{(3)}\bigr)\,\varphi_{(1)}\star S(\varphi_{(4)})\star\psi_{(1)}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}\bigl(\psi_{(1)}\otimes S(\varphi_{(4)})\bigr)\,\mathcal{R}^{-1}\bigl(\varphi_{(2)}\otimes\psi_{(3)}\bigr)\,\varphi_{(1)}\star\psi_{(2)}\star S(\varphi_{(3)})

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}\bigl(\psi_{(1)}\otimes S(\varphi_{(4)})\bigr)\,\mathcal{R}^{-1}\bigl(\varphi_{(1)}\otimes\psi_{(2)}\bigr)\,\psi_{(3)}\star\varphi_{(2)}\star S(\varphi_{(3)})

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}\bigl(\psi_{(1)}\otimes S(\varphi_{(2)})\bigr)\,\mathcal{R}^{-1}\bigl(\varphi_{(1)}\otimes\psi_{(2)}\bigr)\,\psi_{(3)}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\bigl\langle S^{-1}(\psi_{(1)}),\Phi^{-}\bigl(S(\varphi_{(2)})\bigr)\bigr\rangle\,\bigl\langle S^{-1}(\psi_{(2)}),\Phi^{+}(\varphi_{(1)})\bigr\rangle\,\psi_{(3)}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\bigl\langle\psi_{(1)},S^{-1}\bigl[\Phi^{+}(\varphi_{(1)})\Phi^{-}\bigl(S(\varphi_{(2)})\bigr)\bigr]\bigr\rangle\,\psi_{(2)}=\psi\lhd S^{-1}\bigl(\Phi_{0,1}(\varphi)\bigr)=\psi\lhd S^{-1}(h^{\prime})

where the first equality is by definition of
\displaystyle\rho
, the second equality is by definition of the coaction (
24
) and of
\displaystyle\rhd
in (
5
), the third is by definition of
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}
in (
29
), the fourth is by (
15
), the fifth and sixth use (
9
), and the eighth is by (
15
) also using that
\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{-1}=\mathcal{R}\circ(\mathrm{id}\otimes S^{-1})
and the remaining equalities are easy.

2. These claims are easily deduced from item 1, thanks to injectivity of
\displaystyle\rho
. For instance:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\rho\bigl(\widehat{h^{\prime}h^{\prime\prime}}\bigr)(\psi)=\psi\lhd S^{-1}(h^{\prime}h^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\bigl(\psi\lhd S^{-1}(h^{\prime\prime})\bigr)\lhd S^{-1}(h^{\prime})

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\bigl[\rho\bigl(\widehat{h^{\prime}}\bigr)\circ\rho\bigl(\widehat{h^{\prime\prime}}\bigr)\bigr](\psi)=\rho\bigl(\widehat{h^{\prime}}\,\widehat{h^{\prime\prime}}\bigr)(\psi)

for all
\displaystyle\psi\in H^{\circ}
; hence
\displaystyle\rho\bigl(\widehat{h^{\prime}h^{\prime\prime}}\bigr)=\rho\bigl(\widehat{h^{\prime}}\,\widehat{h^{\prime\prime}}\bigr)
and
\displaystyle\widehat{h^{\prime}h^{\prime\prime}}=\widehat{h^{\prime}}\,\widehat{h^{\prime\prime}}
.

3. It suffices to check this equality through the injective morphism
\displaystyle\rho:\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})\to\mathrm{End}_{\Bbbk}(H^{\circ})
, which is a straightforward computation using (
46
).

4. Recall that
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})=H^{\circ}\otimes H
as a vector space. By definition of a coaction it holds
\displaystyle\varepsilon_{H^{\circ}}(\varphi_{[1]})\varphi_{[2]}=\varphi
. As a result writing
\displaystyle h^{\prime}=\Phi_{0,1}(\varphi)
we find
\displaystyle(\varepsilon_{H^{\circ}}\otimes S)\bigl(\widehat{h^{\prime}}\bigr)=h^{\prime}
.
∎

Thanks to item 2 in the previous lemma we have a morphism of algebras

\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{1,0}:H^{\prime}\to\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ}),\quad h^{\prime}\mapsto\textstyle\sum_{(h^{\prime})}\widehat{h^{\prime}_{(1)}}\,h^{\prime}_{(2)}

whose definition makes sense because
\displaystyle\Delta(H^{\prime})\subset H^{\prime}\otimes H
. The next result uses the right
\displaystyle H
-module-algebra structure (
41
) on
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})
.

Proposition 3.9
.

1.
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{1,0}
is a quantum moment map for
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})
, meaning that

\forall\,w\in\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ}),\>\>\forall\,h^{\prime}\in H^{\prime},\quad\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{1,0}(h^{\prime})\,w=\sum_{(h^{\prime})}\,\bigl(w\cdot S^{-1}(h^{\prime}_{(2)})\bigr)\,\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{1,0}(h^{\prime}_{(1)}).

2. It holds
\displaystyle\mu_{1,0}\circ\Phi_{1,0}=\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{1,0}
; said differently we have a commutative diagram

\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{d}_{1,0}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\Phi_{0,1}}
\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\Phi_{1,0}}
\displaystyle\textstyle{H^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{1,0}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\mu_{1,0}}
\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})}

Proof.

We use Sweedler’s notation with implicit summation.

1. Obtained by a straightforward computation using the relations established in Lemma
3.8
; this is left to the reader.

2. A preliminary step is required. For all
\displaystyle\varphi\in\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)
we claim that

(47)

\widehat{\Phi_{0,1}(\varphi)}=\mathsf{v}^{2}(\varphi_{(1)})\,\varphi_{(2)}\,\Phi^{+}\bigl(S(\varphi_{(3)})\bigr)\,\Phi^{-}(\varphi_{(4)})\,S(\varphi_{(5)})\in\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ}).

where
\displaystyle\mathsf{v}
is the coribbon element. In order to prove this, recall the co-Drinfeld element
\displaystyle\mathsf{u}:H^{\circ}\to\Bbbk
from (
33
). Its convolution inverse is

(48)

\mathsf{u}^{-1}(\alpha)=\mathcal{R}\bigl(S^{2}(\alpha_{(2)})\otimes\alpha_{(1)}\bigr),

and by
[
58
, Prop. 2.2.4]
it satisfies the key property

(49)

\forall\,\alpha\in H^{\circ},\quad S^{2}(\alpha)=\mathsf{u}(\alpha_{(1)})\alpha_{(2)}\mathsf{u}^{-1}(\alpha_{(3)}).

Using it and (
44
), straightforward computations give the first equality below, and then we obtain:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi_{0,1}(\varphi)}=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathsf{v}^{2}(\varphi_{(1)})\mathsf{u}^{-1}(\varphi_{(6)})\mathcal{R}\bigl(\varphi_{(3)}\otimes\varphi_{(7)}\bigr)\Bigl[\varphi_{(2)}\star S(\varphi_{(8)})\otimes\Phi^{+}\bigl(S(\varphi_{(4)})\bigr)\Phi^{-}(\varphi_{(5)})\Bigr]

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathsf{v}^{2}(\varphi_{(1)})\mathsf{u}^{-1}(\varphi_{(5)})\mathcal{R}\bigl(\varphi_{(3)}\otimes\varphi_{(7)}\bigr)\Bigl[\varphi_{(2)}\star S(\varphi_{(8)})\otimes S^{-1}\Bigl(\Phi^{-}\bigl(S(\varphi_{(6)})\bigr)\Phi^{+}(\varphi_{(4)})\Bigr)\Bigr]

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathsf{v}^{2}(\varphi_{(1)})\mathsf{u}^{-1}(\varphi_{(5)})\mathcal{R}\bigl(\varphi_{(4)}\otimes\varphi_{(6)}\bigr)\Bigl[\varphi_{(2)}\star S(\varphi_{(8)})\otimes S^{-1}\Bigl(\Phi^{+}(\varphi_{(3)})\Phi^{-}\bigl(S(\varphi_{(7)})\bigr)\Bigr)\Bigr]

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathsf{v}^{2}(\varphi_{(1)})\mathsf{u}^{-1}(\varphi_{(4)})\mathsf{u}^{-1}\bigl(S(\varphi_{(5)})\bigr)\Bigl[\varphi_{(2)}\star S(\varphi_{(7)})\otimes S^{-1}\Bigl(\Phi^{+}(\varphi_{(3)})\Phi^{-}\bigl(S(\varphi_{(6)})\bigr)\Bigr)\Bigr]

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\varphi_{(1)}\star S(\varphi_{(4)})\otimes S^{-1}\Bigl(\Phi^{+}(\varphi_{(2)})\Phi^{-}\bigl(S(\varphi_{(3)})\bigr)\Bigr)=\varphi_{[1]}\otimes S^{-1}\bigl(\Phi_{0,1}(\varphi_{[2]})\bigr)

where the second equality uses (
49
) together with
\displaystyle\Phi^{\pm}\circ S=S^{-1}\circ\Phi^{\pm}
, the third uses (
14
), the fourth uses the outcome of the following computation based on properties of
\displaystyle\mathsf{u}
recalled just above and the fact that
\displaystyle\mathcal{R}\circ(S\otimes\mathrm{id})=\mathcal{R}\circ(\mathrm{id}\otimes S^{-1})
:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathsf{u}^{-1}(\varphi_{(5)})\mathcal{R}\bigl(\varphi_{(4)}\otimes\varphi_{(6)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\mathsf{u}^{-1}(\varphi_{(4)})\mathcal{R}\bigl(S^{2}(\varphi_{(5)})\otimes\varphi_{(6)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\mathsf{u}^{-1}(\varphi_{(4)})\mathcal{R}\bigl[S\bigl(S(\varphi_{(5)})_{(2)}\bigr)\otimes S^{-1}\bigl(S(\varphi_{(5)})_{(1)}\bigr)\bigr]

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\mathsf{u}^{-1}(\varphi_{(4)})\mathcal{R}\bigl[S^{2}\bigl(S(\varphi_{(5)})_{(2)}\bigr)\otimes S(\varphi_{(5)})_{(1)}\bigr]=\mathsf{u}^{-1}(\varphi_{(4)})\mathsf{u}^{-1}\bigl(S(\varphi_{(5)})\bigr)

the fifth uses (
31
) and (
33
), and the last is by definitions of the coaction and of
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}
.

We now use (
47
) to prove the result in the Proposition. One can check that
4
4

4

These equalities are obvious in the matrix formalism explained in
[
14
, §3.2]
, which is why we don’t give further details.

\Phi_{1,0}\circ\mathfrak{i}_{a}\bigl(S_{\mathcal{L}}(\varphi)\bigr)=\Phi^{-}(\varphi_{(1)})\Phi^{+}\bigl(S(\varphi_{(2)})\bigr),\quad\Phi_{1,0}\circ\mathfrak{i}_{b}\bigl(S_{\mathcal{L}}(\varphi)\bigr)=\Phi^{-}(\varphi_{(1)})S(\varphi_{(2)})\Phi^{+}\bigl(S(\varphi_{(3)})\bigr).

From this and (
43
) and (
47
) we find

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}\circ\mathfrak{d}_{1,0}(\varphi)=\mathsf{v}^{2}(\varphi_{(1)})\Phi_{1,0}\bigl[\mathfrak{i}_{b}(\varphi_{(2)})\,\mathfrak{i}_{a}\bigl(S_{\mathcal{L}}(\varphi_{(3)})\bigr)\,\mathfrak{i}_{b}\bigl(S_{\mathcal{L}}(\varphi_{(4)})\bigr)\,\mathfrak{i}_{a}(\varphi_{(5)})\bigr]

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\mathsf{v}^{2}(\varphi_{(1)})\,\bigl[\Phi^{+}(\varphi_{(2)})\varphi_{(3)}\Phi^{-}\bigl(S(\varphi_{(4)})\bigr)\bigr]\,\,\bigl[\Phi^{-}(\varphi_{(5)})\Phi^{+}\bigl(S(\varphi_{(6)})\bigr)\bigr]

\displaystyle\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\quad\bigl[\Phi^{-}(\varphi_{(7)})S(\varphi_{(8)})\Phi^{+}\bigl(S(\varphi_{(9)})\bigr)\bigr]\bigl[\Phi^{+}(\varphi_{(10)})\Phi^{-}\bigl(S(\varphi_{(11)})\bigr)\bigr]

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\mathsf{v}^{2}(\varphi_{(1)})\,\Phi^{+}(\varphi_{(2)})\varphi_{(3)}\Phi^{+}\bigl(S(\varphi_{(4)})\bigr)\Phi^{-}(\varphi_{(5)})S(\varphi_{(6)})\Phi^{-}\bigl(S(\varphi_{(7)})\bigr)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\Phi^{+}(\varphi_{(1)})\widehat{\Phi_{0,1}(\varphi_{(2)})}\Phi^{-}\bigl(S(\varphi_{(3)})\bigr)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\widehat{\Phi_{0,1}(\varphi_{(2)})}\Phi^{+}(\varphi_{(1)})\Phi^{-}\bigl(S(\varphi_{(3)})\bigr)=\widehat{\Phi_{0,1}(\varphi)_{(1)}}\Phi_{0,1}(\varphi)_{(2)}=\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{1,0}\circ\Phi_{1,0}

where the first equality uses the definition of
\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{1,0}
in (
34
), the second uses the values of
\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}
, the third uses that
\displaystyle\Phi^{\pm}
is a morphism of algebras to apply the antipode axiom, the fourth uses (
31
) and (
47
), the fifth uses Lemma
3.8
(2) and the sixth is by (
38
).
∎

3.5.
Alekseev morphism and representations

The morphisms
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}:\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)\to H
and
\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}:\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)\to\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})
discussed previously can be combined into a morphism
\displaystyle\Phi_{g,n}
called
Alekseev morphism
. We recall here a general way of defining
\displaystyle\Phi_{g,n}
, following
[
14
, §5]
. This approach has the advantage to work for any datum
\displaystyle(H,\Phi^{\pm})
but does not interact well with the QMM
\displaystyle\mu_{g,n}
(see Rmk.
3.12
). This is an issue for later purposes and will force us to construct in §
5.2
another version of
\displaystyle\Phi_{g,n}
in the case
\displaystyle H=U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
.

Recall the Heisenberg double
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})
from §
3.4
. The
two-sided Heisenberg double

\displaystyle\mathcal{HH}(H^{\circ})
is the vector space
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})\otimes H=(H^{\circ}\otimes H)\otimes H
endowed with the product defined by the following rules introduced in
[
14
, §5.1]
:

(i)
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})\otimes 1_{H}
and
\displaystyle 1_{\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})}\otimes H
are subalgebras of
\displaystyle\mathcal{HH}(H^{\circ})
isomorphic to
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})
and
\displaystyle H
. We write
\displaystyle\varphi a
instead of
\displaystyle(\varphi a)\otimes 1_{H}
and
\displaystyle\widetilde{b}
instead of
\displaystyle 1_{\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})}\otimes b
.

(ii) With these identifications, we have the relations

(50)

\forall\,\varphi a\in\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ}),\>\>\forall\,b\in H,\quad\varphi a\,\widetilde{b}=\varphi a\otimes b,\quad\widetilde{b}\,\varphi a=\sum_{(b)}\bigl(\varphi\lhd S^{-1}(b_{(2)})\bigr)a\,\widetilde{b_{(1)}}.

Note in particular that
\displaystyle\widetilde{b}\,a=a\,\widetilde{b}
for all
\displaystyle a,b\in H
. The elements
\displaystyle\widetilde{b}
are a substitute for the elements
\displaystyle\widehat{h^{\prime}}
from Lemma
3.8
which are defined only for
\displaystyle h^{\prime}\in H^{\prime}
. The point is that we need elements satisfying the commutation relation (
50
) for all
\displaystyle h
in order to define the Alekseev morphism; since in general we do not know how
\displaystyle H^{\prime}
compares to
\displaystyle H
we add them formally instead of using the already present elements
\displaystyle\widehat{h^{\prime}}
. See Remark
3.12
for more comments.

There is a right
\displaystyle H
-module-algebra structure on
\displaystyle\mathcal{HH}(H^{\circ})=\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})\otimes H
given by

(51)

(X\otimes b)\cdot h=\sum_{(h)}(X\cdot h_{(1)})S(h_{(2)})h_{(5)}\otimes S(h_{(3)})bh_{(4)}

for all
\displaystyle X\otimes b\in\mathcal{HH}(H^{\circ})
and
\displaystyle h\in H
, where we use the already defined right
\displaystyle H
-module-algebra structure (
41
) on
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})
. The proof of this fact is left as an exercise for the reader; one must check that it is indeed a right action and it is compatible with the relations (
50
).

For all integers
\displaystyle g,n\geq 0
, let
\displaystyle\mathsf{D}_{g,n}:H\to\mathcal{HH}(H^{\circ})^{\otimes g}\otimes H^{\otimes n}
be the algebra morphism defined by

(52)

\mathsf{D}_{g,n}(h)=\sum_{(h)}\widetilde{h_{(1)}}\,h_{(2)}\otimes\ldots\otimes\widetilde{h_{(2g-1)}}\,h_{(2g)}\otimes h_{(2g+1)}\otimes\ldots\otimes h_{(2g+n)}

with the convention that
\displaystyle\mathsf{D}_{0,0}(h)=\varepsilon(h)
. As explained around (
26
) we have a decomposition
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)=\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)^{\widetilde{\otimes}\,g}\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)^{\widetilde{\otimes}\,n}
; this allows us to inductively define a map
\displaystyle\Phi_{g,n}:\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)\to\mathcal{HH}(H^{\circ})^{\otimes g}\otimes H^{\otimes n}
for all values of
\displaystyle g
and
\displaystyle n
. First since
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,n+1}(H)=\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,\mathcal{L}_{0,n}(H)
we let

(53)

\Phi_{0,n+1}(\varphi\otimes w)=\sum_{[\varphi]}\Phi_{0,1}(\varphi_{[2]})\otimes\mathsf{D}_{0,n}\bigl(\Phi^{+}(\varphi_{[1]})\bigr)\Phi_{0,n}(w)

for all
\displaystyle\varphi\in\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)
and
\displaystyle w\in\mathcal{L}_{0,n}(H)
, using the left
\displaystyle H^{\circ}
-coaction (
24
) which is dual to
\displaystyle\mathrm{coad}^{r}
. Second since
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g+1,n}(H)=\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
we let

(54)

\Phi_{g+1,n}(x\otimes w)=\sum_{[x]}\Phi_{1,0}(x_{[2]})\otimes\mathsf{D}_{g,n}\bigl(\Phi^{+}(x_{[1]})\bigr)\Phi_{g,n}(w).

for all
\displaystyle x\in\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)
and
\displaystyle w\in\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
. Note that actually
\displaystyle\Phi_{g,n}
takes values in the subalgebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})\otimes\mathcal{HH}(H^{\circ})^{\otimes(g-1)}\otimes H^{\otimes n}
. When
\displaystyle H
is quasitriangular and the datum
\displaystyle\Phi^{\pm}
is chosen as in (
13
), the definition of
\displaystyle\Phi_{g,n}
coincides with the one in
[
14
, §5.2]
. For general
\displaystyle\Phi^{\pm}
the arguments leading to the main of properties of
\displaystyle\Phi_{g,n}
must be rephrased without using the R-matrix; this is why we give a detailed proof of the following proposition.

Proposition 3.10
.

1. The following holds in the algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{HH}(H^{\circ})^{\otimes g}\otimes H^{\otimes n}
endowed with the usual tensor-wise product:

\forall\,h\in H,\>\>\forall\,x\in\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H),\quad\Phi_{g,n}\bigl(\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h)(x)\bigr)=\sum_{(h)}\mathsf{D}_{g,n}\bigl(S(h_{(1)})\bigr)\,\Phi_{g,n}(x)\,\mathsf{D}_{g,n}(h_{(2)}).

2. The map
\displaystyle\Phi_{g,n}
is a morphism of right
\displaystyle H
-module-algebras.
5
5

5

The target is equipped with the usual action of
\displaystyle H
on a tensor product, obtained by combining the action (
51
) on
\displaystyle\mathcal{HH}(H^{\circ})
and the right adjoint action (
30
) on
\displaystyle H

via
iterated coproduct
\displaystyle H\to H^{\otimes(g+n)}
.

Proof.

In this proof we use Sweedler’s notation with implict summation.

1. The proof is by induction. We start with the base cases
i.e.

\displaystyle(g,n)=(0,1)
and
\displaystyle(g,n)=(1,0)
. Since
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}:\bigl(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H),\mathrm{coad}^{r}\bigr)\to(H,\mathrm{ad}^{r})
is
\displaystyle H
-linear it satisfies the desired property. For
\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}:\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)\to\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})
, recall the
\displaystyle H
-module-algebra structure (
41
) on
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})
. It satisfies
\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}\bigl(\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h)(x)\bigr)=\Phi_{1,0}(x)\cdot h

[
14
, §3.3]
. The desired property is then established by the following computation for all
\displaystyle\varphi a\in\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})\subset\mathcal{HH}(H^{\circ})
and
\displaystyle h\in H

(55)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathsf{D}_{1,0}\bigl(S(h_{(1)})\bigr)\varphi a\mathsf{D}_{1,0}(h_{(2)})&=\widetilde{S(h_{(2)})}S(h_{(1)})\varphi a\,\widetilde{h_{(3)}}h_{(4)}\\
&=\bigl(S(h_{(2)})\rhd\varphi\lhd h_{(3)}\bigr)\widetilde{S(h_{(4)})}S(h_{(1)})\,a\,\widetilde{h_{(5)}}h_{(6)}\\
&=\bigl(S(h_{(2)})\rhd\varphi\lhd h_{(3)}\bigr)S(h_{(1)})ah_{(4)}=(\varphi a)\cdot h\end{split}

where the second and third equalities use (
50
).

Let us now prove the claim for
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,n}
by induction on
\displaystyle n
. As a preparation, note that the coaction
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)\to H^{\circ}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
is characterized by
\displaystyle\langle x_{[1]},h\rangle\,x_{[2]}=\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h)(x)
for all
\displaystyle h\in H
and
\displaystyle x\in\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
. One thus checks the two following facts:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\forall\,h\in H,\quad(h\rhd x_{[1]})\otimes x_{[2]}=x_{[1]}\otimes\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h)(x_{[2]}),

\displaystyle\displaystyle\forall\,h\in H,\quad\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h)(x)_{[1]}\otimes\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h)(x)_{[2]}=\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h_{(1)})(x_{[1]})\otimes\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h_{(2)})(x_{[2]}).

The first identity is easy, and true for any coaction
\displaystyle\textstyle x\mapsto\sum_{[x]}x_{[1]}\otimes x_{[2]}
. The second is obtained as follows, where
\displaystyle a\in H
:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\langle\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h_{(1)})(x_{[1]}),a\rangle\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h_{(2)})(x_{[2]})=\langle x_{[1]},h_{(1)}aS(h_{(2)})\rangle\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h_{(3)})(x_{[2]})

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>\,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h_{(3)})(\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h_{(1)}aS(h_{(2)}))(x))=\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h_{(1)}aS(h_{(2)})h_{(3)})(x)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>\,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathrm{coad}^{r}(ha)(x)=\mathrm{coad}^{r}(a)(\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h)(x))=\langle\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h)(x)_{[1]},a\rangle\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h)(x)_{[2]}.

Using these identities we have the following computation for all
\displaystyle h\in H
and
\displaystyle x\in\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
:

(56)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\begin{split}&\Phi^{+}\bigl(\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h)(x)_{[1]}\bigr)\otimes\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h)(x)_{[2]}=\Phi^{+}\bigl(\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h_{(1)})(x_{[1]})\bigr)\otimes\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h_{(2)})(x_{[2]})\\
=\>&\Phi^{+}\bigl(S(h_{(2)})\rhd x_{[1]}\lhd h_{(1)}\bigr)\otimes\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h_{(3)})(x_{[2]})\quad\text{\footnotesize by \eqref{defCoad}}\\
=\>&S(h_{(1)})\Phi^{+}\bigl(h_{(2)}S(h_{(4)})\rhd x_{[1]}\bigr)h_{(3)}\otimes\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h_{(5)})(x_{[2]})\quad\text{\footnotesize by \eqref{quasiCocommPhi}}\\
=\>&S(h_{(1)})\Phi^{+}\bigl(x_{[1]}\bigr)h_{(3)}\otimes\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h_{(5)})\bigl(\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h_{(2)}S(h_{(4)}))(x_{[2]})\bigr)\\
=\>&S(h_{(1)})\Phi^{+}\bigl(x_{[1]}\bigr)h_{(3)}\otimes\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h_{(2)})(x_{[2]})\end{split}

where the last equality uses that
\displaystyle\mathrm{coad}^{r}
is a right action. Hence for all
\displaystyle\varphi\otimes w\in\mathcal{L}_{0,n+1}(H)
we have

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Phi_{0,n+1}\bigl(\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h)(\varphi\otimes w)\bigr)=\Phi_{0,n+1}\bigl(\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h_{(1)})(\varphi)\otimes\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h_{(2)})(w)\bigr)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}\bigl(\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h_{(1)})(\varphi)_{[2]}\bigr)\otimes\mathsf{D}_{0,n}\bigl[\Phi^{+}\bigl(\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h_{(1)})(\varphi)_{[1]}\bigr)\bigr]\Phi_{0,n}\bigl(\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h_{(2)})(w)\bigr)\quad\text{\footnotesize by \eqref{AlekseevValue1}}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}\bigl(\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h_{(2)})(\varphi_{[2]})\bigr)\otimes\mathsf{D}_{0,n}\bigl[S(h_{(1)})\Phi^{+}(\varphi_{[1]})h_{(3)}\bigr]\Phi_{0,n}\bigl(\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h_{(4)})(w)\bigr)\quad\text{\footnotesize by \eqref{trucTechniquePreparation}}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle S(h_{(2)})\Phi_{0,1}(\varphi_{[2]})h_{(3)}\otimes\mathsf{D}_{0,n}\bigl[S(h_{(1)})\Phi^{+}(\varphi_{[1]})h_{(4)}\bigr]\mathsf{D}_{0,n}\bigl(S(h_{(5)})\bigr)\Phi_{0,n}(w)\mathsf{D}_{0,n}(h_{(6)})

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle S(h_{(2)})\Phi_{0,1}(\varphi_{[2]})h_{(3)}\otimes\mathsf{D}_{0,n}\bigl(S(h_{(1)})\bigr)\mathsf{D}_{0,n}\bigl(\Phi^{+}(\varphi_{[1]})\bigr)\Phi_{0,n}(w)\mathsf{D}_{0,n}(h_{(4)})

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathsf{D}_{0,n+1}\bigl(S(h_{(1)})\bigr)\bigl[\Phi_{0,1}(\varphi_{[2]})\otimes\mathsf{D}_{0,n}\bigl(\Phi^{+}(\varphi_{[1]})\bigr)\Phi_{0,n}(w)\bigr]\mathsf{D}_{0,n+1}(h_{(2)})

which by (
53
) is the desired result. The fourth equality is by induction hypothesis and the last equality uses that
\displaystyle S
is an antimorphism of coalgebras.

The proof for
\displaystyle\Phi_{g,n}
is by induction on
\displaystyle g
and uses exactly the same computation.

2. The hard part is “algebra morphism”, which we do first. As a technical preliminary note that for all
\displaystyle\psi\in H^{\circ}
and
\displaystyle v\in\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
we have

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathcal{R}\bigl(\psi_{(1)}\otimes v_{[1]}\bigr)\mathrm{coad}^{r}\bigl(S^{-1}\bigl(\Phi^{+}(\psi_{(2)})\bigr)\bigr)(v_{[2]})=\mathcal{R}\bigl(\psi_{(1)}\otimes v_{[1]}\bigr)\bigl\langle v_{[2][1]},S^{-1}\bigl(\Phi^{+}(\psi_{(2)})\bigr)\bigr\rangle v_{[2][2]}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathcal{R}\bigl(\psi_{(1)}\otimes v_{[1](1)}\bigr)\bigl\langle S^{-1}(v_{[1](2)}),\Phi^{+}(\psi_{(2)})\bigr\rangle v_{[2]}=\mathcal{R}\bigl(\psi_{(1)}\otimes v_{[1](1)}\bigr)\mathcal{R}\bigl(\psi_{(2)}\otimes S^{-1}(v_{[1](2)})\bigr)v_{[2]}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\varepsilon(\psi)\varepsilon\bigl(v_{[1]}\bigr)v_{[2]}=\varepsilon(\psi)v

by (
8
) and usual properties of a co-R-matrix. The proof of the claim is again by induction on
\displaystyle n
and
\displaystyle g
. Assume for instance that
\displaystyle\Phi_{g,n}
is a morphism of algebras (the case
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,n}
is similar). Let
\displaystyle x\otimes v,y\otimes w\in\mathcal{L}_{g+1,n}(H)=\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
; it follows from (
27
) that their product is

(x\otimes v)(y\otimes w)=\mathcal{R}\bigl(y_{[1]}\otimes v_{[1]}\bigr)\,xy_{[2]}\otimes v_{[2]}w.

Hence

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Phi_{g+1,n}\bigl((x\otimes v)(y\otimes w)\bigr)=\mathcal{R}\bigl(y_{[1]}\otimes v_{[1]}\bigr)\,\Phi_{1,0}\bigl(x_{[2]}y_{[2][2]}\bigr)\otimes\mathsf{D}_{g,n}\bigl(\Phi^{+}(x_{[1]}y_{[2][1]})\bigr)\Phi_{g,n}\bigl(v_{[2]}w\bigr)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathcal{R}\bigl(y_{[1](1)}\otimes v_{[1]}\bigr)\,\Phi_{1,0}\bigl(x_{[2]}\bigr)\Phi_{1,0}\bigl(y_{[2]}\bigr)\otimes\mathsf{D}_{g,n}\bigl(\Phi^{+}(x_{[1]})\bigr)\mathsf{D}_{g,n}\bigl(\Phi^{+}(y_{[1](2)})\bigr)\Phi_{g,n}\bigl(v_{[2]}\bigr)\Phi_{g,n}(w)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathcal{R}\bigl(y_{[1](1)}\otimes v_{[1]}\bigr)\,\Phi_{1,0}\bigl(x_{[2]}\bigr)\Phi_{1,0}\bigl(y_{[2]}\bigr)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\otimes\mathsf{D}_{g,n}\bigl(\Phi^{+}(x_{[1]})\bigr)\Phi_{g,n}\bigl[\mathrm{coad}^{r}\bigl(S^{-1}\bigl(\Phi^{+}(y_{[1](2)})\bigr)\bigr)(v_{[2]})\bigr]\mathsf{D}_{g,n}\bigl(\Phi^{+}(y_{[1](3)})\bigr)\Phi_{g,n}(w)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}\bigl(x_{[2]}\bigr)\Phi_{1,0}\bigl(y_{[2]}\bigr)\otimes\mathsf{D}_{g,n}\bigl(\Phi^{+}(x_{[1]})\bigr)\Phi_{g,n}\bigl(v_{[2]}\bigr)\mathsf{D}_{g,n}\bigl(\Phi^{+}(y_{[1]})\bigr)\Phi_{g,n}(w)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Phi_{g+1,n}(x\otimes v)\Phi_{g+1,n}(y\otimes w)

where the first equality is by definition of
\displaystyle\Phi_{g+1,n}
, the second uses the coaction property and the fact that all maps involved are morphism of algebras, the third uses item 1 of the present Proposition and the fact that
\displaystyle\Phi^{+}
is an anti-morphism of coalgebras, the fourth uses the technical preliminary and the last is by definition of
\displaystyle\Phi_{g+1,n}
.

To prove
\displaystyle H
-linearity, we first show it when
\displaystyle(g,n)=(1,0)
. For all
\displaystyle X\otimes b=X\,\widetilde{b}\in\mathcal{HH}(H^{\circ})=\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})\otimes H
and
\displaystyle h\in H
we have

(57)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathsf{D}_{1,0}\bigl(S(h_{(1)})\bigr)\,X\,\widetilde{b}\,\mathsf{D}_{1,0}(h_{(2)})=\mathsf{D}_{1,0}\bigl(S(h_{(1)})\bigr)X\mathsf{D}_{1,0}(h_{(2)})\mathsf{D}_{1,0}\bigl(S(h_{(3)})\bigr)\widetilde{b}\mathsf{D}_{1,0}(h_{(4)})\\
&\overset{\eqref{QMMHeisDansPreuve}}{=}\bigl(X\cdot h_{(1)}\bigr)\,\mathsf{D}_{1,0}\bigl(S(h_{(3)})\bigr)\widetilde{b}\,\mathsf{D}_{1,0}(h_{(4)})\overset{\eqref{dressingMap}}{=}\bigl(X\cdot h_{(1)}\bigr)\,\widetilde{S(h_{(3)})}S(h_{(2)})\widetilde{b}\,\widetilde{h_{(4)}}h_{(5)}\\
&\overset{\eqref{2sidedproductHeisenberg}}{=}\bigl(X\cdot h_{(1)}\bigr)\,S(h_{(2)})h_{(5)}\widetilde{S(h_{(3)})bh_{(4)}}\overset{\eqref{modAlgStructTwoSidedHeis}}{=}\bigl(X\,\widetilde{b}\,\bigr)\cdot h.\end{split}

Since
\displaystyle\mathsf{D}_{g,n}(h)=\mathsf{D}_{1,0}(h_{(1)})\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathsf{D}_{1,0}(h_{(g)})\otimes h_{(g+1)}\otimes\ldots\otimes h_{(g+n)}
by very definition in (
52
), we conclude that
\displaystyle x\cdot h=\mathsf{D}_{g,n}\bigl(S(h_{(1)})\bigr)x\mathsf{D}_{g,n}(h_{(2)})
for all
\displaystyle x\in\mathcal{HH}(H^{\circ})^{\otimes g}\otimes H^{\otimes n}
, so that item 1 in the present Proposition actually means
\displaystyle H
-linearity.
∎

The computation (
57
) shows that
\displaystyle\mathsf{D}_{1,0}
is a quantum moment map for
\displaystyle\mathcal{HH}(H^{\circ})
. Actually the action of
\displaystyle H
in (
51
) was defined precisely in order to have this property.

We record that the question of injectivity of
\displaystyle\Phi_{g,n}
reduces to the base cases:

Lemma 3.11
.

If
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}
and
\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}
are injective then
\displaystyle\Phi_{g,n}
is injective for all
\displaystyle g,n
.

Proof.

Recall that a tensor product over
\displaystyle\Bbbk
of injective
\displaystyle\Bbbk
-linear maps is again injective (
\displaystyle\Bbbk
is a field). We first do the case
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,n}
by induction on
\displaystyle n
. Let

T_{0,n}:H^{\prime}\otimes H^{\otimes(n-1)}\to H^{\prime}\otimes H^{\otimes(n-1)},\quad\Phi_{0,1}(\alpha)\otimes w\mapsto\Phi_{0,1}(\alpha_{[2]})\otimes\mathsf{D}_{0,n-1}\bigl(\Phi^{+}(\alpha_{[1]})\bigr)w

It is a linear isomorphism, with
\displaystyle T_{0,n}^{-1}\bigl(\Phi_{0,1}(\alpha)\otimes w\bigr)=\Phi_{0,1}(\alpha_{[2]})\otimes\mathsf{D}_{0,n-1}\bigl(\Phi^{+}\bigl(S^{-1}(\alpha_{[1]})\bigr)\bigr)w
, which is seen by the coaction property
\displaystyle\alpha_{[1]}\otimes\alpha_{[2][1]}\otimes\alpha_{[2][2]}=\alpha_{[1](1)}\otimes\alpha_{[1](2)}\otimes\alpha_{[2]}
. Now by very definition we have
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,n}=T_{0,n}\circ(\Phi_{0,1}\otimes\Phi_{0,n-1})
and induction hypothesis applies. The general case
\displaystyle\Phi_{g,n}
is done similarly by induction on
\displaystyle g
with
\displaystyle n
fixed. Write
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{\prime}=\mathrm{im}(\Phi_{1,0})
, and use the isomorphism

\displaystyle\displaystyle T_{g,n}:\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\otimes\bigl(\mathcal{HH}(H^{\circ})^{\otimes(g-1)}\otimes H^{\otimes n}\bigr)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\to\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\otimes\bigl(\mathcal{HH}(H^{\circ})^{\otimes(g-1)}\otimes H^{\otimes n}\bigr)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}(x)\otimes w

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mapsto\Phi_{1,0}(x_{[2]})\otimes\mathsf{D}_{g-1,n}\bigl(\Phi^{+}(x_{[1]})\bigr)w

whose inverse is
\displaystyle T_{g,n}^{-1}\bigl(\Phi_{1,0}(x)\otimes w\bigr)=\Phi_{1,0}(x_{[2]})\otimes\mathsf{D}_{g,n-1}\bigl(\Phi^{+}\bigl(S^{-1}(x_{[1]})\bigr)\bigr)w
. It satisfies
\displaystyle\Phi_{g,n}=T_{g,n}\circ\bigl(\Phi_{1,0}\otimes\Phi_{g-1,n}\bigr)
, whence the result.
∎

Remark 3.12
.

When
\displaystyle g>0
it is not true that
\displaystyle\mu_{g,n}\circ\Phi_{g,n}=\mathsf{D}_{g,n}
. Said differently, the bottom triangle in the following diagram

\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\Phi_{0,1}}
\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\Phi_{g,n}}
\displaystyle\textstyle{H^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\mathsf{D}_{g,n}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\mu_{g,n}}
\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathcal{HH}(H^{\circ})^{\otimes g}\otimes H^{\otimes n}}

does not commute when
\displaystyle g>0
(a proof that it commutes for
\displaystyle g=0
is given in
[
11
, Prop. 6.18]
). For instance in the case
\displaystyle g=1
we have
\displaystyle\mu_{1,0}\circ\Phi_{1,0}=\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{1,0}\neq\mathsf{D}_{1,0}
by Prop.
3.9
. The failure of the diagram comes from the use of elements
\displaystyle\widetilde{h}
instead of elements
\displaystyle\widehat{h}
to define
\displaystyle\mathsf{D}_{g,n}
and hence
\displaystyle\Phi_{g,n}
. In general there is no solution to this problem:
\displaystyle\widehat{h}
might not exist for all
\displaystyle h\in H
and thus to define
\displaystyle\mathsf{D}_{g,n}
we are forced to formally introduce elements
\displaystyle\widetilde{h}
for all
\displaystyle h\in H
with the same commutation relations as
\displaystyle\widehat{h^{\prime}}
, leading to the extension
\displaystyle\mathcal{HH}(H^{\circ})
. In the case where
\displaystyle H
is finite-dimensional it is easy to see that
\displaystyle\widehat{h}
exist for all
\displaystyle h\in H
so we can define a “hat version”
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{g,n}
, yielding an alternative Alekseev morphism
\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi}_{g,n}:\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)\to\mathcal{H}(H^{*})^{\otimes g}\otimes H^{\otimes n}
such that the diagram commutes (
[
14
, §5.3]
, where
\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi}_{g,n}
was denoted
\displaystyle\Phi_{g,n}^{\rm fin}
). In the case where
\displaystyle H=U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
we know precisely how
\displaystyle H^{\prime}
compares to
\displaystyle H
and then we can use a minimal extension
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{H}}
instead of
\displaystyle\mathcal{HH}(H^{\circ})
, yielding again a commutative diagram; see §
5.2
.

The faithful representation
\displaystyle\rho:\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})\to\mathrm{End}_{\Bbbk}(H^{\circ})
from (
42
) can be extended to a morphism
\displaystyle\widetilde{\rho}:\mathcal{HH}(H^{\circ})\to\mathrm{End}_{\Bbbk}(H^{\circ})
defined by

(58)

\widetilde{\rho}\bigl(\varphi a\widetilde{b}\bigr)(\psi)=\varphi\star\bigl(a\rhd\psi\lhd S^{-1}(b)\bigr)\qquad(\forall\,\psi\in H^{\circ}).

Unlike
\displaystyle\rho
, the morphism
\displaystyle\widetilde{\rho}
is not injective: by Lemma
3.8
we have
\displaystyle\widetilde{\rho}(\widehat{h^{\prime}})=\widetilde{\rho}(\widetilde{h^{\prime}})
for all
\displaystyle h^{\prime}\in H^{\prime}
but
\displaystyle\widehat{h^{\prime}}\neq\widetilde{h^{\prime}}
since
\displaystyle\widetilde{h^{\prime}}\not\in\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})
. Consider however the following algebra morphism

(59)

F_{g,n}:\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt\Phi_{g,n}\hskip 8.19447pt}\mathcal{HH}(H^{\circ})^{\otimes g}\otimes H^{\otimes n}\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt\widetilde{\rho}^{\otimes g}\,\otimes\,\mathrm{id}_{H}^{\otimes n}\hskip 8.19447pt}\mathrm{End}_{\Bbbk}(H^{\circ})^{\otimes g}\otimes H^{\otimes n}.

One can show by induction as in Lemma
3.11
that if
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}
and
\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}
are injective then
\displaystyle F_{g,n}
is injective as well. Indeed, let

\begin{array}[]{c}\widetilde{T}_{g,n}:\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\otimes\bigl(\mathrm{End}_{\Bbbk}(H^{\circ})^{\otimes(g-1)}\otimes H^{\otimes n}\bigr)\to\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\otimes\bigl(\mathrm{End}_{\Bbbk}(H^{\circ})^{\otimes(g-1)}\otimes H^{\otimes n}\bigr)\\[3.00003pt]
\Phi_{1,0}(x)\otimes w\mapsto\Phi_{1,0}(x_{[2]})\otimes\bigl((\widetilde{\rho}^{\otimes(g-1)}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{H}^{\otimes n})\circ\mathsf{D}_{g-1,n}\bigr)\bigl(\Phi^{+}(x_{[1]})\bigr)w\end{array}

with
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{\prime}=\mathrm{im}(\Phi_{1,0})
. It is a linear isomorphism (for the inverse, replace
\displaystyle x_{[1]}
by
\displaystyle S^{-1}(x_{[1]})
). Then
\displaystyle F_{g,n}=\bigl(\rho\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathrm{End}_{\Bbbk}(H^{\circ})^{\otimes(g-1)}\otimes H^{\otimes n}}\bigr)\circ\widetilde{T}_{g,n}\circ\bigl(\Phi_{1,0}\otimes F_{g-1,n}\bigr)
. Since
\displaystyle\rho
is injective it follows that
\displaystyle F_{g,n}
is also injective.

Assume that the Hopf algebra
\displaystyle H
is finite-dimensional; then, having the morphisms
\displaystyle\Phi^{\pm}
is equivalent to having an R-matrix
\displaystyle R\in H^{\otimes 2}
. The quasitriangular Hopf algebra
\displaystyle(H,R)
is called
factorizable
if the map
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}:H^{*}\to H
,
\displaystyle\varphi\mapsto(\varphi\otimes\mathrm{id}_{H})(RR^{\prime})
is a bijection.

Proposition 3.13
.

If
\displaystyle H
is finite-dimensional and factorizable then
\displaystyle F_{g,n}
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

These assumptions imply that
\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}
is an isomorphism
[
37
, Th. 4.8]
. In particular
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}
and
\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}
are injective which as noted above implies that
\displaystyle F_{g,n}
is injective. By equality of the dimensions of source and target, it is an isomorphism.
∎

Les us go back to the general situation of a Hopf algebra
\displaystyle H
satisfying the assumptions 1,2 and 3.

The existence of the morphism
\displaystyle F_{g,n}
implies that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
has a representation on the space
\displaystyle(H^{\circ})^{\otimes g}\otimes V_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes V_{n}
for any
\displaystyle H
-modules
\displaystyle V_{1},\ldots,V_{n}
, which is an important point in the theory of graph algebras.

To conclude, we note as a side remark that the diagram in Remark
3.12
becomes commutative through the representation
\displaystyle\widetilde{\rho}
:

Proposition 3.14
.

For all
\displaystyle g,n
, we have a commutative diagram

\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\Phi_{0,1}}
\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\Phi_{g,n}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{F_{g,n}}
\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathcal{HH}(H^{\circ})^{\otimes g}\otimes H^{\otimes n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\rho}^{\otimes g}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{H}^{\otimes n}}
\displaystyle\textstyle{H^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\mathsf{D}_{g,n}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\mu_{g,n}}
\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathcal{HH}(H^{\circ})^{\otimes g}\otimes H^{\otimes n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\rho}^{\otimes g}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{H}^{\otimes n}}
\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathrm{End}_{\Bbbk}(H^{\circ})^{\otimes g}\otimes H^{\otimes n}}

Equivalently, by definition of
\displaystyle\widetilde{\rho}
,
\displaystyle F_{g,n}
and
\displaystyle\mathsf{D}_{g,n}
we can write this property as

(60)

(F_{g,n}\circ\mu_{g,n})(h^{\prime})=\textstyle\sum_{(h^{\prime})}{\rm act}(h^{\prime}_{(1)})\otimes\ldots\otimes{\rm act}(h^{\prime}_{(g)})\otimes h^{\prime}_{(g+1)}\otimes\ldots h^{\prime}_{(g+n)}

for all
\displaystyle h^{\prime}\in H^{\prime}
, where
\displaystyle\mathrm{act}:H\to\mathrm{End}_{\Bbbk}(H^{\circ})
is defined by
\displaystyle\textstyle\mathrm{act}(h)(\varphi)=\sum_{(h)}h_{(2)}\rhd\varphi\lhd S^{-1}(h_{(1)})
.

Proof.

For the case
\displaystyle(g,n)=(1,0)
note that
\displaystyle\rho\circ\mathsf{D}_{1,0}=\rho\circ\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{1,0}
by (
42
) and Lemma
3.8
, so that Prop.
3.9
gives the result in this case. For the general case the proof is completely similar to the one of Prop.
5.12
below, where the reader will find all the details. For the induction step, the only changes are typographical:
\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi}_{g,n}
must be replaced by
\displaystyle F_{g,n}
and
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{g,n}
must be replaced by
\displaystyle\mathrm{act}_{g,n}:=\bigl(\widetilde{\rho}^{\otimes g}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{H}^{\otimes n}\bigr)\circ\mathsf{D}_{g,n}
, which is given by the right-hand side of (
60
).
∎

The following consequence is motivated by Lemma
3.7
regarding injectivity of
\displaystyle\mu_{g,n}
:

Corollary 3.15
.

Assume that
\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}
is injective. Then
\displaystyle\mu_{1,0}
is injective if and only if the linear map
\displaystyle H^{\prime}\to\mathrm{End}_{\Bbbk}(H^{\circ})
,
\displaystyle h\mapsto\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h)
is injective (with the right action
\displaystyle\mathrm{coad}^{r}
from (
21
)).

Proof.

Indeed
\displaystyle F_{1,0}\circ\mu_{1,0}=\mathrm{act}
by Prop.
3.14
, and
\displaystyle F_{1,0}
is injective because so is
\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}
. Consider the linear automorphism
\displaystyle T
of
\displaystyle H^{\circ}
given by
\displaystyle\varphi\mapsto\textstyle\sum_{(\varphi)}\mathcal{R}\bigl(S(\varphi_{(3)})\otimes\varphi_{(1)}\bigr)\varphi_{(2)}
. Then it is not hard to check that
\displaystyle\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h)=T^{-1}\circ\mathrm{act}\bigl(S(h)\bigr)\circ T
for all
\displaystyle h
, whence the result.
∎

4.
Preliminaries on quantum groups

Here we set conventions and collect facts on quantum groups which will eventually allow us to define various instances of graph algebras associated to quantum groups: generic parameter, integral form, specialization at roots of unity and finite-dimensional quotient of these. References for the results discussed in this section are
[
26
,
20
,
73
]
.

4.1.
The quantum enveloping algebra
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}(\mathfrak{g})

Recall from §
2
our notations regarding Lie algebras. Fix a Cartan decomposition of
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}
and a
\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}
-lattice
\displaystyle\Lambda
such that
\displaystyle Q\subset\Lambda\subset P
. We denote by
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}:=U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}(\mathfrak{g})
the
\displaystyle{\mathbb{C}}(q)
-algebra generated by elements
\displaystyle E_{i},F_{i},K_{\lambda}
, (
\displaystyle\lambda\in\Lambda
,
\displaystyle i=1,\ldots,m
) with relations:

\displaystyle\displaystyle K_{0}=1_{U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}},\quad K_{\lambda}K_{\lambda^{\prime}}=K_{\lambda+\lambda^{\prime}},\quad K_{\lambda}E_{i}=q^{(\lambda,\alpha_{i})}E_{i}K_{\lambda},\quad K_{\lambda}F_{i}=q^{-(\lambda,\alpha_{i})}F_{i}K_{\lambda},

\displaystyle\displaystyle E_{i}F_{j}-F_{j}E_{i}=\delta_{i,j}\frac{K_{\alpha_{i}}-K_{\alpha_{i}}^{-1}}{q_{i}-q_{i}^{-1}},

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{r=0}^{1-a_{ij}}(-1)^{r}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}1-a_{ij}\\
r\end{array}\right]_{q_{i}}E_{i}^{1-a_{ij}-r}E_{j}E_{i}^{r}=0\quad{\rm if}\ i\neq j,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{r=0}^{1-a_{ij}}(-1)^{r}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}1-a_{ij}\\
r\end{array}\right]_{q_{i}}F_{i}^{1-a_{ij}-r}F_{j}F_{i}^{r}=0\quad{\rm if}\ i\neq j,\quad\text{where }\left[\begin{array}[]{c}s\\
t\end{array}\right]_{q}=\frac{[s]_{q}!}{[t]_{q}!\,[s-t]_{q}!}.

Usual notations are
\displaystyle K_{i}:=K_{\alpha_{i}}
,
\displaystyle L_{i}:=K_{\varpi_{i}}
and
\displaystyle q_{i}=q^{d_{i}}
(see §
2
). Note that if
\displaystyle\textstyle\lambda=\sum_{i=1}^{m}n_{i}\varpi_{i}\in P
then
\displaystyle\textstyle K_{\lambda}=\prod_{i=1}^{m}L_{i}^{n_{i}}
and
\displaystyle\textstyle K_{i}=\prod_{j=1}^{m}L_{j}^{a_{ji}}
. Also
\displaystyle L_{i}E_{j}=q_{i}^{\delta_{i,j}}E_{j}L_{i}
and
\displaystyle L_{i}F_{j}=q_{i}^{-\delta_{i,j}}F_{j}L_{i}
.

For
\displaystyle\Lambda=P
this is the so-called
simply-connected
quantum group
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
, generated by
\displaystyle E_{i},F_{i},L_{i}
. For
\displaystyle\Lambda=Q
this is the so-called
adjoint
quantum group
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
, generated by
\displaystyle E_{i},F_{i},K_{i}
. We will mostly use these two versions.

\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}
is a Hopf algebra whose coproduct
\displaystyle\Delta
, antipode
\displaystyle S
, and counit
\displaystyle\varepsilon
are given by

\begin{array}[]{c}\Delta(K_{\lambda})=K_{\lambda}\otimes K_{\lambda}\ ,\ \Delta(E_{i})=E_{i}\otimes K_{i}+1\otimes E_{i}\ ,\ \Delta(F_{i})=F_{i}\otimes 1+K_{i}^{-1}\otimes F_{i}\\
S(E_{i})=-E_{i}K_{i}^{-1}\ ,\ S(F_{i})=-K_{i}F_{i}\ ,\ S(K_{\lambda})=K_{-\lambda}=K_{\lambda}^{-1}\\
\varepsilon(E_{i})=\varepsilon(F_{i})=0,\ \varepsilon(K_{\lambda})=1.\end{array}

Note that any
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}
is a Hopf subalgebra of
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
.
The element

\textstyle\ell:=K_{2\rho}=\prod_{j=1}^{m}L_{j}^{2}\in U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}(\mathfrak{g})

is pivotal for the Hopf algebra
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}
, i.e., it is grouplike and satisfies
\displaystyle S^{2}(x)=\ell x\ell^{-1}
for all
\displaystyle x\in U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}
.

The
Cartan subalgebra
of
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}
, denoted by
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}(\mathfrak{h})
, is the (commutative) subalgebra generated by the elements
\displaystyle K_{\lambda}
for all
\displaystyle\lambda\in\Lambda
. We denote by
\displaystyle U_{q}(\mathfrak{n}_{-})
(resp.
\displaystyle U_{q}(\mathfrak{n}_{+})
) the subalgebras of
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}
generated by the elements
\displaystyle F_{i}
(resp.
\displaystyle E_{i}
). Then the
Borel subalgebras
are
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{\pm}):=U_{q}(\mathfrak{n}_{\pm})U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}(\mathfrak{h})
.

We fix a reduced expression
\displaystyle s_{i_{1}}\ldots s_{i_{N}}
of the longest element
\displaystyle w_{0}
of the Weyl group of
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}
. It induces a total ordering of the positive roots,

(69)

\beta_{1}=\alpha_{i_{1}},\beta_{2}=s_{i_{1}}(\alpha_{i_{2}}),\ldots,\beta_{N}=s_{i_{1}}\ldots s_{i_{N-1}}(\alpha_{i_{N}}).

The braid group
\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{g})
acts on
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}
by means of Lusztig’s algebra automorphisms
\displaystyle T_{i}
, associated to the simple roots
\displaystyle\alpha_{i}
. The root vectors of
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}
with respect to the above ordering are defined by

(70)

E_{\beta_{k}}=T_{i_{1}}\ldots T_{i_{k-1}}(E_{i_{k}})\ ,\ F_{\beta_{k}}=T_{i_{1}}\ldots T_{i_{k-1}}(F_{i_{k}}).

The monomials in the elements
\displaystyle E_{\beta}
and
\displaystyle F_{\beta}
(
\displaystyle\beta\in\phi^{+}
) form bases of
\displaystyle U_{q}(\mathfrak{n}_{+})
and
\displaystyle U_{q}(\mathfrak{n}_{-})
, respectively. The elements
\displaystyle K_{\lambda}
,
\displaystyle\lambda\in\Lambda
, form a basis of
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}(\mathfrak{h})
, and a PBW basis of
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}(\mathfrak{g})
is deduced from the polarisation
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}(\mathfrak{g})=U_{q}(\mathfrak{n}_{-})U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}(\mathfrak{h})U_{q}(\mathfrak{n}_{+})
.

The center
\displaystyle{\mathcal{Z}}(U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})
of
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
is isomorphic to the subalgebra of elements in
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}({\mathfrak{h}})
invariant under (a
\displaystyle(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{m}
extension of) the Weyl group; this is the quantum analog of the Harish-Chandra isomorphism.

Let
\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}}
be the category of finite dimensional
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
-modules
of type
\displaystyle 1
, i.e. where the elements
\displaystyle K_{i}
act semisimply with eigenvalues in
\displaystyle q_{i}^{\mathbb{Z}}
. The category
\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}}
is monoidal and rigid (i.e. closed under finite direct sums, tensor products and duals), and it is semisimple. The simple objects up to isomorphism are the highest weight modules
\displaystyle V_{\mu}
of weights
\displaystyle\mu\in P_{+}
.

Occasionally we will use the simple finite dimensional
\displaystyle U(\mathfrak{g})
-modules; they are parametrized by weights
\displaystyle\mu\in P_{+}
and we denote them by
\displaystyle L(\mu).

Recall that
\displaystyle q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
is a formal variable such that
\displaystyle q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}^{D}=q
. Extending scalars to
\displaystyle{\mathbb{C}}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})
, any module
\displaystyle V\in\mathcal{C}
can be regarded as an
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
-module by stipulating that

(71)

\forall\,\mu\in P,\quad K_{\mu}\cdot v_{\lambda}:=q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}^{D(\mu,\lambda)}v_{\lambda}

for every weight vector
\displaystyle v_{\lambda}\in V
of weight
\displaystyle\lambda\in P
(recall that for all
\displaystyle\lambda,\mu\in P
,
\displaystyle\textstyle(\mu,\lambda)\in\frac{1}{D}\mathbb{Z}
). A
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})
-vector space is a type 1
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
-module if
\displaystyle L_{i}
acts semisimply with eigenvalues in
\displaystyle q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}^{\mathbb{Z}}
for all
\displaystyle i
. Formula (
71
) then establishes an equivalence of categories between
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
-modules of type 1 and
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
-modules of type 1 (with scalars extended to
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})
).

The category
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}
is a braided category by means of Drinfeld’s universal
\displaystyle R
-matrix. Namely, the braiding is the natural isomorphism
\displaystyle(\sigma_{V,W}\circ R_{V,W})_{V,W\in Ob(\mathcal{C})}
, where
\displaystyle\sigma_{V,W}\colon v\otimes w\mapsto w\otimes v
, and
\displaystyle R_{V,W}\colon V\otimes W\rightarrow V\otimes W
,
\displaystyle v\otimes w\mapsto R(v\otimes w)
, is defined by
\displaystyle R=\Theta\hat{R}
with

(72)

\Theta(v\otimes w)=q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}^{D(\mu,\nu)}v\otimes w

on weight vectors
\displaystyle v
,
\displaystyle w
of weights
\displaystyle\mu
,
\displaystyle\nu
respectively
6
6

6

The braiding thus requires an extension of scalars of
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}
from
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q)
to
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})
.
and

(73)

\hat{R}=\hat{R}_{\beta_{N}}\ldots\hat{R}_{\beta_{2}}\hat{R}_{\beta_{1}},\quad\text{with }\>\hat{R}_{\beta}=\sum_{s=0}^{\infty}q_{\beta}^{s(s-1)/2}\frac{(q_{\beta}-q_{\beta}^{-1})^{s}}{[s]_{q_{\beta}}!}(E_{\beta})^{s}\otimes(F_{\beta})^{s}

(the order of factors in the product
\displaystyle\hat{R}
is extremely important) for all positive root
\displaystyle\beta\in\phi^{+}
, where
\displaystyle q_{\beta}:=q^{(\beta,\beta)/2}
. Since the elements
\displaystyle E_{\beta}
,
\displaystyle F_{\beta}
act nilpotently on the objects of
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}
, the operator
\displaystyle\hat{R}_{V,W}\in\mathrm{End}_{\mathbb{C}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})}(V\otimes W)
is well-defined for all
\displaystyle V,W\in\mathrm{Ob}(\mathcal{C})
, and thus
\displaystyle R_{V,W}
is well-defined as well. Actually
\displaystyle R
can be regarded as an element in a Hopf algebra which is categorical completion of
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
, as defined e.g. in
[
11
, §2 and §3.2]
; such a completion is an instance of multiplier Hopf algebra in the sense of
[
72
]
and
[
73
]
.

We remark that if we set

E_{\beta}^{(s)}:=\frac{E_{\beta}^{s}}{[s]_{q_{\beta}}!},\quad F_{\beta}^{(s)}:=\frac{F_{\beta}^{s}}{[s]_{q_{\beta}}!},\quad\underline{E}_{\beta}:=(q_{\beta}-q_{\beta}^{-1})E_{\beta},\quad\underline{F}_{\beta}:=(q_{\beta}-q_{\beta}^{-1})F_{\beta}

for every
\displaystyle\beta\in\phi^{+}
and
\displaystyle s\in\mathbb{N}
, then we can rewrite

(74)

\hat{R}_{\beta}=\sum_{s=0}^{\infty}q_{\beta}^{s(s-1)/2}(\underline{E}_{\beta})^{s}\otimes F_{\beta}^{(s)}=\sum_{s=0}^{\infty}q_{\beta}^{s(s-1)/2}E_{\beta}^{(s)}\otimes(\underline{F}_{\beta})^{s}

which is are power series with coefficients in
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}[q^{\pm 1}]
; this will be used in the discussion of integral forms in §
4.4
.

4.2.
The quantum function algebra
\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{q}(G)

The algebra
\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{q}:={\mathcal{O}}_{q}(G)
is the subspace of
\displaystyle(U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}})^{\circ}
spanned by the matrix coefficients of the objects in the subcategory
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}
of type 1 modules; this is a Hopf algebra (Rmk.
3.1
). It is also a
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
-bimodule by means of the left and right coregular actions
\displaystyle\rhd
and
\displaystyle\lhd
. We have the Peter–Weyl decomposition as a bimodule,

(75)

{\mathcal{O}}_{q}=\bigoplus_{\mu\in P_{+}}C(\mu)\quad{\rm where}\;\;C(\mu):=V_{\mu}^{*}\otimes V_{\mu},

where the left action of
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
on
\displaystyle C(\mu)
is on the tensorand
\displaystyle V_{\mu}
, and the right action is its transpose on
\displaystyle V_{\mu}^{*}
. Once a basis of the modules
\displaystyle V_{\mu}
has been fixed for every
\displaystyle\mu\in P_{+}
, the set of matrix coefficients in these bases is a
\displaystyle{\mathbb{C}}(q)
-basis of
\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{q}
. Extending scalars to
\displaystyle{\mathbb{C}}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})
and using (
71
), the
\displaystyle C(\mu)
’s become
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
-bimodules.

The duality (evaluation) bilinear form
\displaystyle\langle\text{-},\text{-}\rangle^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}:{\mathcal{O}}_{q}\times U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}\to{\mathbb{C}}(q)
is a Hopf pairing, and it is non degenerate.
It is non degenerate on the left by the definition of
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}
as a restricted dual, and it is non degenerate on the right because the modules in
\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}}
separate the points of
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
(see, e.g.,
[
73
, Th. 3.46]
). Extending scalars to
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})
and thanks to (
71
), we obtain a non-degenerate pairing

\langle\text{-},\text{-}\rangle^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}:{\mathcal{O}}_{q}\times U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}\to{\mathbb{C}}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}).

The pairing
\displaystyle\langle\text{-},\text{-}\rangle^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
is a quantum analog of the classical non-degenerate Hopf pairing
\displaystyle\langle\text{-},\text{-}\rangle:{\mathcal{O}}(G)\times U(\mathfrak{g})\to{\mathbb{C}}
, defined for
any finite dimensional
Lie algebra (not necessarily semisimple), which is obviously non degenerate on the left, and is non degenerate on the right because of the Harish-Chandra theorem
[
33
, Th. 2.5.7]
.

We put
\displaystyle R^{+}:=R
,
\displaystyle R^{-}:=(R^{fl})^{-1}
, where
\displaystyle R^{fl}
is
\displaystyle R
with tensorands permuted. Although the
\displaystyle R
-matrix does not exist in
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}\otimes U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
, the following maps are well-defined

(76)

\begin{array}[]{rcll}\Phi^{\pm}:&\mathcal{O}_{q}&\longrightarrow&(U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{\rm cop}\\
&\alpha&\longmapsto&(\alpha\otimes\mathrm{id})(R^{\pm}).\end{array}

Indeed let
\displaystyle V
be a finite-dimensional
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
-module with basis of weight vectors
\displaystyle\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{s}\}
and dual basis
\displaystyle\{v^{1},\ldots,v^{s}\}
. Then
\displaystyle\bigl(v^{i}(?\cdot v_{j})\otimes\mathrm{id}\bigr)(\Theta)=\delta_{i,j}K_{\mu_{j}}
where
\displaystyle\mu_{j}
is the weight of
\displaystyle v_{j}
while
\displaystyle\bigl(v^{i}(?\cdot v_{j})\otimes\mathrm{id}\bigr)(\hat{R})
is an element in
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
by nilpotency of the action of the elements
\displaystyle E_{\beta_{r}}
on
\displaystyle V
. Hence
\displaystyle\bigl(v^{i}(?\cdot v_{j})\otimes\mathrm{id}\bigr)(R):=\textstyle\sum_{k}\bigl(v^{i}(?\cdot v_{k})\otimes\mathrm{id}\bigr)(\Theta)\bigl(v^{k}(?\cdot v_{j})\otimes\mathrm{id}\bigr)(\hat{R})
gives an element in
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
. The same argument applies for
\displaystyle R^{-}
.

The maps
\displaystyle\Phi^{\pm}
are morphisms of Hopf algebras which satisfy the assumptions from §
3.1
; we stress that they take values in the simply-connected quantum group
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
. Actually
\displaystyle\Phi^{\pm}
takes values in the subalgebra
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{\mp})^{\rm cop}
.

Denote by
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}(B_{\pm})
the image of
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}
by the Hopf epimorphism dual to the inclusion
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{\pm})\hookrightarrow U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}(\mathfrak{g})
. The maps
\displaystyle\Phi^{\pm}
factor through these projections and yield isomorphisms of Hopf algebras
\displaystyle\Phi^{\pm}:\mathcal{O}_{q}(B_{\pm})\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{\mp})^{\mathrm{cop}}
.

A convenient way of manipulating the maps
\displaystyle\Phi^{\pm}
without using
\displaystyle R
is through pairings, as follows. Consider the pairing
\displaystyle\rho:U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{-})^{\rm cop}\times U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{+})\to\mathbb{C}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})
defined on generators by

(77)

\rho(K_{\lambda},K_{\mu})=q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}^{D(\lambda,\mu)},\quad\rho(K_{\lambda},E_{i})=\rho(F_{i},K_{\lambda})=0,\quad\rho(F_{i},E_{j})=\frac{\delta_{i,j}}{q_{i}-q_{i}^{-1}}

for every
\displaystyle 1\leq i,j\leq m
,
\displaystyle\lambda,\mu\in P
and extended by the Hopf pairing property. Note that the map
\displaystyle(x_{-},x_{+})\mapsto\rho\bigl(x_{-},S^{-1}(x_{+})\bigr)
is the convolution inverse of
\displaystyle\rho
and it holds
\displaystyle\rho\circ(S\otimes S)=\rho
. Also consider the so-called
Drinfeld pairing

(78)

\tau:U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{+})^{\rm cop}\times U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{-})\to\mathbb{C}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}),\quad\tau(x_{+},x_{-})=\rho\bigl(S(x_{-}),x_{+}\bigr).

The pairings
\displaystyle\rho
and
\displaystyle\tau
are non degenerate by the classical arguments (see e.g.
[
73
, Th. 3.92]
). We record the following formula for
\displaystyle\rho
on PBW monomials:

(79)

\rho\left(h_{-}F_{\beta_{N}}^{s_{N}}\ldots F_{\beta_{1}}^{s_{1}},\,h_{+}E_{\beta_{N}}^{t_{N}}\ldots E_{\beta_{1}}^{t_{1}}\right)=\rho(h_{-},h_{+})\prod_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{s_{i},t_{i}}\,q_{\beta_{i}}^{-s_{i}(s_{i}-1)/2}\,\frac{[s_{i}]_{q_{\beta_{i}}}!}{(q_{\beta_{i}}-q_{\beta_{i}}^{-1})^{s_{i}}}

for all
\displaystyle h_{-},h_{+}\in U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}(\mathfrak{h})
; this formula is given e.g. in
[
31
, Th. 2.4]
(and modified here to match with our conventions). The following lemma uncovers the relation between
\displaystyle\rho
,
\displaystyle\tau
and the
\displaystyle R
-matrix:

Lemma 4.1
.

For all
\displaystyle\boldsymbol{s}=(s_{N},\ldots,s_{2},s_{1})\in\mathbb{N}^{N}
let
\displaystyle X_{+}^{\boldsymbol{s}}=E_{\beta_{N}}^{s_{N}}\ldots E_{\beta_{1}}^{s_{1}}
and
\displaystyle X_{-}^{\boldsymbol{s}}=F_{\beta_{N}}^{s_{N}}\ldots F_{\beta_{1}}^{s_{1}}
.

1. We have
\displaystyle\widehat{R}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{s}\in\mathbb{N}^{N}}\frac{X_{+}^{\boldsymbol{s}}\otimes X_{-}^{\boldsymbol{s}}}{\rho(X_{-}^{\boldsymbol{s}},X_{+}^{\boldsymbol{s}})}
, where
\displaystyle\widehat{R}
is defined in (
73
).

2. For all
\displaystyle\alpha\in\mathcal{O}_{q}
and
\displaystyle x_{\pm}\in U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{\pm})
it holds

(80)

\rho\bigl(\Phi^{+}(\alpha),x_{+}\bigr)=\langle\alpha,x_{+}\rangle^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}\quad\text{and}\quad\tau\bigl(\Phi^{-}(\alpha),x_{-}\bigr)=\langle\alpha,x_{-}\rangle^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}.

When the second variable of
\displaystyle\rho
and
\displaystyle\tau
is restricted to
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{\mp})
then these pairings take values in
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q)
and the evaluation pairing
\displaystyle\langle\text{-},\text{-}\rangle^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
can be replaced by
\displaystyle\langle\text{-},\text{-}\rangle^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
in formula (
80
).

Proof.

1. Note first by comparison of (
73
) and (
79
) that for all positive root
\displaystyle\beta\in\phi^{+}
we have
\displaystyle\hat{R}_{\beta}=\textstyle\sum_{s\in\mathbb{N}}\rho(F_{\beta}^{s},E_{\beta}^{s})^{-1}(E_{\beta})^{s}\otimes(F_{\beta})^{s}
. Also by (
79
) we see that
\displaystyle\textstyle\rho(X_{-}^{\boldsymbol{s}},X_{+}^{\boldsymbol{t}})=\prod_{i=1}^{N}\rho(F_{\beta_{i}}^{s_{i}},E_{\beta_{i}}^{t_{i}})
. Hence by definition of
\displaystyle\hat{R}
one gets

\hat{R}=\hat{R}_{\beta_{N}}\ldots\hat{R}_{\beta_{1}}=\sum_{s_{N},\ldots,s_{1}\in\mathbb{N}}\prod_{i=N}^{1}\frac{(E_{\beta_{i}})^{s_{i}}\otimes(F_{\beta_{i}})^{s_{i}}}{\rho(F_{\beta_{i}}^{s_{i}},E_{\beta_{i}}^{s_{i}})}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{s}\in\mathbb{N}^{N}}\frac{X_{+}^{\boldsymbol{s}}\otimes X_{-}^{\boldsymbol{s}}}{\rho(X_{-}^{\boldsymbol{s}},X_{+}^{\boldsymbol{s}})}.

2. Recall that
\displaystyle R=\Theta\hat{R}
. For each finite-dimensional
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}(\mathfrak{g})
-module, denote by
\displaystyle\boldsymbol{1}_{\lambda}
the projection on the subspace of vectors having weight
\displaystyle\lambda\in P
. With this notation, the action of
\displaystyle K_{\mu}
on a module can be written as the formal sum
\displaystyle\textstyle\sum_{\lambda\in P}q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}^{D(\lambda,\mu)}\boldsymbol{1}_{\lambda}
while the element
\displaystyle\Theta
defined in (
72
) is
\displaystyle\textstyle\sum_{\lambda,\mu\in P}q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}^{D(\lambda,\mu)}\boldsymbol{1}_{\lambda}\otimes\boldsymbol{1}_{\mu}
. If
\displaystyle v_{\nu}
is a vector with weight
\displaystyle\nu
we thus have
\displaystyle\textstyle\sum_{\lambda,\mu\in P}q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}^{D(\lambda,\mu)}\boldsymbol{1}_{\lambda}v_{\nu}\otimes\boldsymbol{1}_{\mu}=v_{\nu}\otimes\sum_{\mu\in P}q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}^{D(\nu,\mu)}\boldsymbol{1}_{\mu}=v_{\nu}\otimes K_{\nu}
. It follows that
\displaystyle\textstyle\sum_{\lambda,\mu\in P}q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}^{D(\lambda,\mu)}\boldsymbol{1}_{\lambda}v\otimes\boldsymbol{1}_{\mu}=\sum_{\lambda\in P}\boldsymbol{1}_{\lambda}v\otimes K_{\lambda}
for any vector
\displaystyle v
. This implies that for all
\displaystyle c\in\mathcal{O}_{q}
we have
\displaystyle\textstyle(c\otimes\mathrm{id})(\Theta)=\sum_{\lambda\in P}c(\boldsymbol{1}_{\lambda})K_{\lambda}
. Also observe from (
79
) that
\displaystyle\rho(X_{-}^{\boldsymbol{s}},X_{+}^{\boldsymbol{t}})=\delta_{\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{t}}\rho(X_{-}^{\boldsymbol{s}},X_{+}^{\boldsymbol{t}})
. We thus get, for all
\displaystyle\boldsymbol{a}\in\mathbb{N}^{N}
and
\displaystyle\mu\in P
,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\rho\bigl(\Phi^{+}(\alpha),K_{\mu}X_{+}^{\boldsymbol{a}}\bigr)=\rho\bigl((\alpha\otimes\mathrm{id})(\Theta\hat{R}),K_{\mu}X_{+}^{\boldsymbol{a}}\bigr)=\sum_{\boldsymbol{s},\lambda}\alpha(\boldsymbol{1}_{\lambda}X_{+}^{\boldsymbol{s}})\frac{\rho\bigl(K_{\lambda}X_{-}^{\boldsymbol{s}},K_{\mu}X_{+}^{\boldsymbol{a}}\bigr)}{\rho\bigl(X_{-}^{\boldsymbol{s}},X_{+}^{\boldsymbol{s}}\bigr)}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{\boldsymbol{s},\lambda}q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}^{D(\lambda,\mu)}\alpha(\boldsymbol{1}_{\lambda}X_{+}^{\boldsymbol{s}})\frac{\rho\bigl(X_{-}^{\boldsymbol{s}},X_{+}^{\boldsymbol{a}}\bigr)}{\rho\bigl(X_{-}^{\boldsymbol{s}},X_{+}^{\boldsymbol{s}}\bigr)}=\sum_{\lambda}q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}^{D(\lambda,\mu)}\alpha(\boldsymbol{1}_{\lambda}X_{+}^{\boldsymbol{a}})=\alpha(K_{\mu}X_{+}^{\boldsymbol{a}})

where the second equality uses item 1 of the present lemma and the third equality is by (
79
). The computation for
\displaystyle\tau
is similar.
∎

Let
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})=\mathcal{C}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}(q)}\mathbb{C}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})=\mathcal{O}_{q}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}(q)}\mathbb{C}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})
be the extension of scalars. Since the category
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})
is braided by means of the action of the R-matrix,
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})
is naturally co-quasitriangular. The co-R-matrix can be expressed in terms of the morphisms
\displaystyle\Phi^{+}
as

(81)

\forall\,\varphi,\psi\in\mathcal{O}_{q}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}),\quad\mathcal{R}(\varphi\otimes\psi)=\bigl\langle\psi,\Phi^{+}(\varphi)\bigr\rangle^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}

which corresponds to the definition in §
3.1
. By (
80
),
\displaystyle\mathcal{R}
relates to the Drinfeld pairing
\displaystyle\tau
by

\mathcal{R}(\varphi\otimes\psi)=\tau\bigl(\Phi^{-}(\psi),\Phi^{+}(\varphi)\bigr).

In particular,
\displaystyle\mathcal{R}
factors through the projection
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}^{\otimes 2}\to\mathcal{O}_{q}(B_{-})\otimes\mathcal{O}_{q}(B_{+})
.

Moreover the category
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})
is ribbon, with ribbon natural transformation given by the action of
\displaystyle u\ell^{-1}
, where
\displaystyle u=\textstyle\sum_{(R)}S(R_{(2)})R_{(1)}
is the Drinfeld element and
\displaystyle\ell=K_{2\rho}
(although
\displaystyle u\not\in U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
, its action on objects in
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})
is well-defined). This element acts by scalar on any irreducible module; looking at the highest weight vector reveals that this action on
\displaystyle V_{\lambda}
is
\displaystyle q^{-(\lambda,\lambda+2\rho)}\mathrm{id}_{V_{\lambda}}
for all
\displaystyle\lambda\in P_{+}
. As a result the algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})
is coribbon with
\displaystyle\mathsf{v}:\mathcal{O}_{q}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})\to\mathbb{C}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})
given by

(82)

\mathsf{v}\bigl(_{\lambda}\phi^{f}_{x}\bigr)=q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}^{-D(\lambda,\lambda+2\rho)}f(x)

where
{}_{\lambda}\phi^{f}_{x}\in\mathcal{O}_{q}
is the matrix coefficient of
\displaystyle V_{\lambda}
given by
\displaystyle h\mapsto f(h\cdot x)
for
\displaystyle f\in V_{\lambda}^{*}
,
\displaystyle x\in V_{\lambda}
. We have to use
\displaystyle q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
in order to ensure an integer exponent, because
\displaystyle\textstyle(\lambda,\lambda)\in\frac{1}{D}\mathbb{Z}
.

4.3.
Integral forms of
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}(\mathfrak{g})

Recall that
\displaystyle A=\mathbb{C}[q,q^{-1}]
. An integral form of a
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q)
-algebra
\displaystyle R
is an
\displaystyle A
-subalgebra
\displaystyle R_{A}
such that the canonical map
\displaystyle R_{A}\otimes_{A}\mathbb{C}(q)\to R
is an isomorphism. Given a
\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}
-lattice
\displaystyle\Lambda
such that
\displaystyle Q\subset\Lambda\subset P
we shall use two integrals versions of
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}(\mathfrak{g})
, respectively called unrestricted and restricted.

The
unrestricted integral form

\displaystyle U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}
of
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}
is its smallest
\displaystyle A
-subalgebra containing the elements

\underline{E}_{i}:=(q_{i}-q_{i}^{-1})E_{i}\ ,\ \underline{F}_{i}:=(q_{i}-q_{i}^{-1})F_{i}\ ,\ K_{\lambda}\ \ \mathrm{for}\ i=1,\ldots,m,\>\lambda\in\Lambda

and stable under the action of the braid group
\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{g})
by means of Lusztig’s automorphisms
\displaystyle T_{i}
.

We denote by
\displaystyle U_{A}(\mathfrak{n}_{-})
(resp.
\displaystyle U_{A}(\mathfrak{n}_{+})
, resp.
