\displaystyle U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}(\mathfrak{h})
) the smallest
\displaystyle A
-subalgebra of
\displaystyle U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}
containing the elements
\displaystyle\underline{E}_{i}
(resp.
\displaystyle\underline{F}_{i}
, resp.
\displaystyle K_{\lambda}
for
\displaystyle\lambda\in\Lambda
) and stable under the action of
\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{g})
. We put
\displaystyle U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{\pm}):=U_{A}(\mathfrak{n}_{\pm})U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}(\mathfrak{h})
.

Bases of
\displaystyle U_{A}(\mathfrak{n}_{+})
and
\displaystyle U_{A}(\mathfrak{n}_{-})
are respectively formed by the monomials in the elements

(83)

\underline{E}_{\beta}:=(q_{\beta}-q_{\beta}^{-1})E_{\beta}\quad\text{and}\quad\underline{F}_{\beta}:=(q_{\beta}-q_{\beta}^{-1})F_{\beta_{k}}

for
\displaystyle\beta\in\phi_{+}
, where
\displaystyle q_{\beta}=q^{(\beta,\beta)/2}
and
\displaystyle E_{\beta}
,
\displaystyle F_{\beta}
are the root vectors (
70
). Together with the basis
\displaystyle K_{\lambda}
(
\displaystyle\lambda\in\Lambda
) of
\displaystyle U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}(\mathfrak{h})
, this and the polarisation
\displaystyle U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}(\mathfrak{g})=U_{A}(\mathfrak{n}_{-})U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}(\mathfrak{h})U_{A}(\mathfrak{n}_{+})
yield a PBW basis of
\displaystyle U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}(\mathfrak{g})
.

Note that the center
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}})
of
\displaystyle U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}(\mathfrak{g})
satisfies
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}})\otimes_{A}{\mathbb{C}}(q)=\mathcal{Z}(U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}})
.

Remark 4.2
.

Denote by
\displaystyle A^{\prime}
the localisation of
\displaystyle A
with respect to the elements
\displaystyle q_{i}-q_{i}^{-1},i=1,\ldots,m
. Clearly
\displaystyle U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}\otimes_{A}A^{\prime}
is isomorphic to the
\displaystyle A^{\prime}
-subalgebra of
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}
generated by
\displaystyle E_{i},F_{i},K_{\lambda}
(
\displaystyle i=1,\ldots,m
,
\displaystyle\lambda\in\Lambda
), with no condition involving the braid group action.

There is another unrestricted integral form of
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}
, simpler to define
[
29
]
: it is the
\displaystyle A
-subalgebra of
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}
generated by
\displaystyle E_{i},F_{i},K_{\lambda},[K_{i},0]_{q_{i}}
(
\displaystyle i=1,\ldots,m
,
\displaystyle\lambda\in\Lambda
). We will not use it. It is not
\displaystyle A
-isomorphic to
\displaystyle U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}
, but it is over
\displaystyle A^{\prime}
. This last definition of
\displaystyle U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}
is suited to the study of the quotient (commutative) algebra
\displaystyle U_{A}/(q-1)U_{A}
, and its Poisson geometry
[
32
, §12.4, §14, §15]
.

The
restricted integral form

\displaystyle\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}=\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}(\mathfrak{g})
of
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}
(in the version of De Concini–Lyubashenko
[
31
]
) is the
\displaystyle A
-subalgebra of
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}
generated by the elements

E_{i}^{(p)}=\frac{E_{i}^{p}}{[p]_{q_{i}}!},\ F_{i}^{(p)}=\frac{F_{i}^{p}}{[p]_{q_{i}}!}\ ,\ (K_{i},\,t)_{q_{i}}:=\prod_{s=1}^{t}\frac{K_{i}q_{i}^{-s+1}-1}{q_{i}^{s}-1},\quad K_{\lambda}^{\pm 1}

for all
\displaystyle i=1,\ldots,m
,
\displaystyle p,t\geq 0
and
\displaystyle\lambda\in\Lambda
. Denote
\displaystyle\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}({\mathfrak{h}}):=\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}\cap U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}(\mathfrak{h})
and
\displaystyle\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}({\mathfrak{b}}_{\pm}):=\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}\cap U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{\pm})
. In particular
\displaystyle\Gamma_{A}({\mathfrak{n}}_{\pm}):=\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}\cap U_{q}(\mathfrak{n}_{\pm})
. For all
\displaystyle\beta\in\phi^{+}
, introduce the notations

(84)

E_{\beta}^{(p)}=\frac{E_{\beta}^{p}}{[p]_{q_{\beta}}!},\ F_{i}^{(p)}=\frac{F_{\beta}^{p}}{[p]_{q_{\beta}}!}

with as usual
\displaystyle\textstyle[p]_{q_{\beta}}!=\prod_{i=1}^{p}\frac{q_{\beta}^{i}-q_{\beta}^{-i}}{q_{\beta}-q_{\beta}^{-1}}
. An
\displaystyle A
-basis of
\displaystyle\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
is formed by the elements

(85)

Y({\bf r},{\bf t},{\bf s})=E_{\beta_{N}}^{(r_{N})}\ldots E_{\beta_{1}}^{(r_{1})}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{m}K_{i}^{-\sigma(t_{i})}(K_{i};t_{i})_{q_{i}}\right)F_{\beta_{N}}^{(s_{N})}\ldots F_{\beta_{1}}^{(s_{1})}

where
\displaystyle{\bf r}=(r_{1},...,r_{N}),{\bf s}=(s_{1},...,s_{N})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{N}
,
\displaystyle{\bf t}=(t_{1},...,t_{m})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{m}
and
\displaystyle\sigma(t)
is the integer part of
\displaystyle t/2
. Moreover we have an isomorphism of
\displaystyle A
-modules
\displaystyle\Gamma_{A}^{{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}}\cong A[\Lambda/Q]\otimes_{A}\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
, which gives an
\displaystyle A
-basis of
\displaystyle\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}
for all
\displaystyle\Lambda
.

Remark 4.3
.

The restricted integral form often appears in the literature in Lusztig’s version, denoted by
\displaystyle U_{A}^{{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}},{\rm res}}

[
51
,
52
]
. It is the
\displaystyle A
-subalgebra of
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}
generated by the elements
\displaystyle E_{i}^{(p)}
,
\displaystyle F_{i}^{(p)}
and
\displaystyle K_{\lambda}^{\pm 1}
for
\displaystyle i=1,\ldots,m
,
\displaystyle p\geq 0
and
\displaystyle\lambda\in\Lambda
. In particular,
\displaystyle U_{A}^{{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}},{\rm res}}
is the smallest
\displaystyle A
-subalgebra of
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
containing the elements
\displaystyle E_{i}^{(p)}
,
\displaystyle F_{i}^{(p)}
and
\displaystyle K_{i}^{\pm 1}
for all
\displaystyle i
and
\displaystyle p
. Also
\displaystyle U_{A}^{{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}},{\rm res}}
contains
\displaystyle\textstyle[K_{i};t]_{q_{i}}:=\prod_{s=1}^{t}\frac{K_{i}q_{i}^{-s+1}-K_{i}^{-1}q_{i}^{s-1}}{q_{i}^{s}-q_{i}^{-s}}
for all
\displaystyle t\geq 0
but does not contain
\displaystyle(K_{i},t)_{q_{i}}
. There are thus strict inclusions of algebras
\displaystyle U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}\subset U_{A}^{{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}},{\rm res}}\subset\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}
. We note that suitably-defined categories of representations (§
4.4
) of
\displaystyle U_{A}^{{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}},{\rm res}}
and
\displaystyle\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}
are equivalent. We prefer to use
\displaystyle\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}
because it is from it that De Concini–Lyubashenko defined the integral version of the quantum coordinate algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}(G)
and also because it leads to a version of the small quantum group more suitable for us (see Remark
4.6
).

4.4.
Integral form of
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}(G)

We denote by
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{A}
the category of
\displaystyle\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
-modules which are free
\displaystyle A
-modules of finite rank and which have a basis such that for all
\displaystyle i
the elements
\displaystyle K_{i}
and
\displaystyle(K_{i},t)_{q_{i}}
act diagonally with eigenvalues of the form
\displaystyle q_{i}^{m}
and
\displaystyle(q_{i}^{m},t)_{q_{i}}
for some
\displaystyle m\in\mathbb{Z}
, where
\displaystyle\textstyle(x,t)_{q}=\prod_{s=1}^{t}\frac{xq^{-s+1}-1}{q^{s}-1}
for all
\displaystyle t\in\mathbb{N}
and formal variables
\displaystyle x,q
.

The category
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{A}
is rigid, monoidal, but not semisimple. Every type
\displaystyle 1
finite dimensional simple
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
-module
\displaystyle V_{\mu}
,
\displaystyle\mu\in P_{+}
, has a
\displaystyle\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
-invariant full
\displaystyle A
-sublattice, that we denote by
\displaystyle{}_{A}V_{\mu}
(“full” means that
{}_{A}V_{\mu}\otimes_{A}\mathbb{C}(q)=V_{\mu}
). These
\displaystyle\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
-modules form the simple objects of
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{A}
.

If we extend scalars of
\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}}_{A}
to
\displaystyle A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
, the objects of
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{A}
can be regarded as
\displaystyle\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}
-modules by means of (
71
) for any lattice
\displaystyle Q\subset\Lambda\subset P
.

The Hopf algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{A}=\mathcal{O}_{A}(G)

[
53
]
is the
\displaystyle A
-Hopf subalgebra of
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}
defined as the
\displaystyle A
-span of the matrix coefficients
\displaystyle h\mapsto v^{i}(h\cdot v_{j})
, for every module
\displaystyle V
in the category
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{A}
, where
\displaystyle(v_{j})
is an
\displaystyle A
-basis of
\displaystyle V
with dual
\displaystyle A
-basis
\displaystyle(v^{i})
. It is a free
\displaystyle A
-module, and an integral form of
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}

[
53
,
54
]
. So
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{A}
can be regarded as a restricted dual of
\displaystyle\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
with respect to the category
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{A}
.

The duality (evaluation) Hopf pairing

(86)

\langle\text{-},\text{-}\rangle_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}:{\mathcal{O}}_{A}\times\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}\to A

is non-degenerate because it recovers the usual pairing
\displaystyle\langle\text{-},\text{-}\rangle^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}:\mathcal{O}_{q}\otimes U^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}_{q}\to\mathbb{C}(q)
when scalars are extended from
\displaystyle A
to
\displaystyle{\mathbb{C}}(q)
.

Let
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}=\mathcal{O}_{A}\otimes_{A}A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
,
\displaystyle\Gamma_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}=\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}\otimes_{A}A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
be the extension of ground ring to
\displaystyle A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}=\mathbb{C}[q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}^{\pm 1}]
, with
\displaystyle q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}^{D}=q
. Then we see that
\displaystyle\langle\text{-},\text{-}\rangle_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
extends to a non degenerate Hopf pairing

(87)

\langle\text{-},\text{-}\rangle_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}:{\mathcal{O}}_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}\times\Gamma_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}\to A_{{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}.

which is the integral version of the pairing
\displaystyle\langle\text{-},\text{-}\rangle^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
from §
4.2
.

Observe that the expression of the pairing
\displaystyle\rho:U_{q}(\mathfrak{b}_{-})^{\mathrm{cop}}\times U_{q}(\mathfrak{b}_{+})\to\mathbb{C}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})
in (
79
) can be rewritten as

\rho\left(h_{-}\underline{F}_{\beta_{N}}^{s_{N}}\ldots\underline{F}_{\beta_{1}}^{s_{1}},\,h_{+}E_{\beta_{N}}^{(t_{N})}\ldots E_{\beta_{1}}^{(t_{1})}\right)=\rho(h_{-},h_{+})\prod_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{s_{i},t_{i}}\,q_{\beta_{i}}^{-s_{i}(s_{i}-1)/2}

with notations from (
83
) and (
84
). Clearly
\displaystyle\rho(h_{-},h_{+})\in A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
if
\displaystyle h_{-}\in U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}(\mathfrak{h})
and
\displaystyle h_{+}\in\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}(\mathfrak{h})
. It follows that the pairing
\displaystyle\rho
, and hence
\displaystyle\tau
as well, take values in
\displaystyle A
if we restrict them as follows:

(88)

\rho_{A}\colon U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{-})^{\rm cop}\times\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{+})\to A,\qquad\tau_{A}\colon U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{+})^{\rm cop}\times\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{-})\to A.

These integral pairings are of course still non-degenerate, as restrictions of the non-degenerate pairings
\displaystyle\rho
and
\displaystyle\tau
. This shows that the restricted integral version
\displaystyle\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
and the unrestricted integral version
\displaystyle U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
have triangular factors in duality.

Let
\displaystyle\Phi_{A}^{\pm}
be defined by restriction to
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{A}\subset\mathcal{O}_{q}
of
\displaystyle\Phi^{\pm}:\mathcal{O}_{q}\to(U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{\mathrm{cop}}
from (
76
). By (
73
) and (
74
) it defines a map
\displaystyle\Phi_{A}^{\pm}:\mathcal{O}_{A}\to(U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{\mathrm{cop}}
. By restriction to integral forms, the equalities in (
80
) become

(89)

\rho_{A}\bigl(\Phi_{A}^{+}(\alpha),x_{+}\bigr)=\langle\alpha,x_{+}\rangle_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}},\qquad\tau_{A}\bigl(\Phi_{A}^{-}(\alpha),x_{-}\bigr)=\langle\alpha,x_{-}\rangle_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}

for every
\displaystyle\alpha\in\mathcal{O}_{A}
,
\displaystyle x_{\pm}\in\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{\pm})
. As a result
\displaystyle\Phi_{A}^{\pm}
factors through the projection
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{A}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{A}(B_{\pm})
and yields an isomorphism of Hopf algebras

(90)

\Phi_{A}^{\pm}\colon\mathcal{O}_{A}(B_{\pm})\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{\mp})^{\rm cop}.

Recall the co-R-matrices
\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{(\pm)}
from (
14
). They and the coribbon element on
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}
described in §
4.2
admit integral versions:

Lemma 4.4
.

1. The restriction of the co-R-matrix
\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{(\pm)}:\mathcal{O}_{q}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})^{\otimes 2}\to\mathbb{C}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})
to
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}^{\otimes 2}
takes values in
\displaystyle A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
. Hence we have an integral version
\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}^{(\pm)}:\mathcal{O}_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}\to A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
. It satisfies

(91)

\forall\,\varphi,\psi\in\mathcal{O}_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}},\quad\mathcal{R}_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}^{(\pm)}(\varphi\otimes\psi)=\bigl\langle\psi,\widetilde{i}\bigl(\Phi^{\pm}_{A}(\varphi)\bigr)\bigr\rangle_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}

where
\displaystyle\widetilde{i}:U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}\hookrightarrow\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
is the natural embedding
\displaystyle\underline{E}_{i}\mapsto(q_{i}-q_{i}^{-1})E_{i}^{(1)}
,
\displaystyle\underline{F}_{i}\mapsto(q_{i}-q_{i}^{-1})F_{i}^{(1)}
,
\displaystyle K_{\lambda}\mapsto K_{\lambda}
.

2. The restriction of the coribbon element
\displaystyle\mathsf{v}:\mathcal{O}_{q}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})\to\mathbb{C}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})
to
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}
takes values in
\displaystyle A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
. Hence we have an integral version
\displaystyle\mathsf{v}_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}:\mathcal{O}_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}\to A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
.

Proof.

For all
\displaystyle\lambda\in P_{+}
, there is a canonical
\displaystyle A
-basis
\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}
of
{}_{A}V_{\lambda}
due to Kashiwara–Lusztig, see e.g.
[
26
, Chap. 14]
.

1. It is known that the matrix entries in the basis
\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mu}
of the action of
\displaystyle R^{\pm 1}
on
\displaystyle{}_{A}V_{\lambda}\otimes{}_{A}V_{\mu}
belong to
\displaystyle q^{\pm(\lambda,\mu)}A\subset A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}

[
54
, Th. 32.1.5]
. Moreover
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{A}
is generated as an
\displaystyle A
-algebra by the collection of matrix coefficients of
{}_{A}V_{\lambda}
in the basis
\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}
for all
\displaystyle\lambda\in P_{+}
. It follows that
\displaystyle\mathcal{R}
takes values in
\displaystyle A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
on such matrix coefficients and the co-R-matrix axioms (
10
)–(
11
) together with the fact that
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{A}
is a Hopf
\displaystyle A
-algebra imply that
\displaystyle\mathcal{R}
takes values in
\displaystyle A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
for all elements of
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{A}^{\otimes 2}
. Formula (
91
) is simply obtained by restriction of formula (
81
) to integral versions.

2. By formula (
82
) for
\displaystyle\mathsf{v}:\mathcal{O}_{q}\to\mathbb{C}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})
we see that
\displaystyle\mathsf{v}
takes values in
\displaystyle A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
when applied to matrix coefficients in
\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}
. One then conclude as in the previous item thanks to the axiom (
32
) of
\displaystyle\mathsf{v}
.
∎

4.5.
Specialization of
\displaystyle U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})

Recall the notations
\displaystyle A=\mathbb{C}[q^{\pm 1}]
and
\displaystyle A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}=\mathbb{C}[q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}^{\pm 1}]
with
\displaystyle q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}^{D}=q
, where
\displaystyle D
is the smallest integer such that
\displaystyle DP\subset Q
, see §
2.1
. General facts on specialization at
\displaystyle q=z
were remembered in §
2.3
. Here we will work with specializations at a root of unity. Recall from the introduction that we use the following assumptions:

Assumption:
From now on,
\displaystyle l\geq 3
is an integer such that

(92)

\mathrm{gcd}(l,2)=1,\qquad\mathrm{gcd}(l,D)=1,\quad\text{and also }\mathrm{gcd}(l,3)=1\text{ if }\mathfrak{g}\text{ is of type }G_{2}.

We denote by
\displaystyle\epsilon\in\mathbb{C}
a primitive root of unity or order
\displaystyle l
. Let
\displaystyle\overline{D}
be the inverse of
\displaystyle D
modulo
\displaystyle l
, meaning that
\displaystyle D\overline{D}\equiv 1\pmod{l}
; it exists because
\displaystyle l
and
\displaystyle D
are coprime. We define

(93)

\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}=\epsilon^{\overline{D}}\qquad\text{so that }\>\>\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}^{D}=\epsilon\>\>\text{ and }\>\>\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}^{l}=1.

The specialization of an object defined over
\displaystyle A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}=\mathbb{C}[q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}^{\pm 1}]
is always done with this
\displaystyle\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
; since
\displaystyle\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
depends on
\displaystyle\epsilon
, the specialization of
\displaystyle A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
-modules and of
\displaystyle A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
-(bi)linear maps is denoted with a subscript
\displaystyle\epsilon
(instead of
\displaystyle\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
).

Let
\displaystyle U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}
and
\displaystyle\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}
be respectively the unrestricted and restricted specializations, of the integral forms defined in §
4.3
. By Lemma
2.1
, the PBW basis of
\displaystyle U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}
yields a basis of
\displaystyle U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}
, and the basis (
85
) yields a basis of
\displaystyle\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
, with elements we shall denote by
\displaystyle Y({\bf p},{\bf t},{\bf n})_{\epsilon}
. When
\displaystyle q=1
there is an isomorphism
\displaystyle\Gamma_{1}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}\cong U(\mathfrak{g})

[
31
, §6]
.

In
\displaystyle\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
we have the relations

(94)

\forall\beta\in\phi^{+},\>\>E_{\beta}^{l}=F_{\beta}^{l}=0\quad\text{and}\quad K_{i}^{l}=1\>\>\text{for}\;i=1,\ldots,m.

The first two are obvious, and the last one comes by clearing denominators in the definition of the elements
\displaystyle(K_{i};t)_{q_{i}}
, and then evaluating at
\displaystyle q_{i}=\epsilon_{i}
. Therefore the specialization
\displaystyle i_{\epsilon}:U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}\rightarrow\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
of the inclusion map
\displaystyle i:U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}\hookrightarrow\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
is not injective. This degeneracy (i.e. an injection acquiring a big kernel at
\displaystyle\epsilon
) will be of fundamental importance in the sequel.

Definition 4.5
.

The image of
\displaystyle i_{\epsilon}\colon U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}\rightarrow\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
is a finite-dimensional Hopf algebra called small quantum group and denoted
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}(\mathfrak{g})
or more shortly
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
.

This algebra is considered in
[
56
, App]
. By definition,
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
is the Hopf subalgebra of
\displaystyle\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
generated by the elements
\displaystyle E_{i}
,
\displaystyle F_{i}
and
\displaystyle K_{i}
for
\displaystyle 1\leq i\leq m
.

Remark 4.6
.

In
\displaystyle U_{\epsilon}^{{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}},{\rm res}}
the elements
\displaystyle K_{i}^{l}
are central (but not equal to
\displaystyle 1
) and it holds
\displaystyle K_{i}^{2l}=1
. The image of
\displaystyle U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}\rightarrow U_{\epsilon}^{{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}},{\rm res}}
is thus another finite-dimensional Hopf algebra, denoted by
\displaystyle U_{\epsilon}^{\rm fin}
and introduced by Lusztig (
[
52
,
53
]
,
[
54
, Chap. 36]
; see also
[
26
, §9.3.B]
). We will not use it.

A PBW basis of
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
is formed by the elements

(95)

E_{\beta_{N}}^{s_{N}}\cdots E_{\beta_{1}}^{s_{1}}K_{1}^{r_{1}}\cdots K_{m}^{r_{m}}F_{\beta_{N}}^{t_{N}}\cdots F_{\beta_{1}}^{t_{1}}

where
\displaystyle 0\leq t_{j},s_{j},r_{i}<l
for all
\displaystyle i,j
; this fact is an adaptation of
[
53
, Th. 8.3]
to
\displaystyle\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
. Therefore

(96)

\dim(u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}})=l^{\dim{\mathfrak{g}}}.

Comparing the PBW basis of
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}
and
\displaystyle U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
(see §
4.3
) we see that
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}\cong U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}\big/[{\rm relations}\ \eqref{toto}]
. Following
[
29
,
32
]
, for every sublattice
\displaystyle Q\subset\Lambda\subset P
introduce the central subalgebra

(97)

\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}):=\mathbb{C}\bigl[E_{\beta}^{l},\>F_{\beta}^{l},\>K_{\lambda}^{l}\,\big|\,\beta\in\phi_{+},\>\lambda\in\Lambda\bigr]\subset\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}).

Put
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{\pm}))=\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}})\cap U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{\pm})
,
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{+}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{\pm}))=\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{\pm}))\cap{\rm Ker}(\varepsilon)
. Then the relations (
94
) are rewritten as
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}\cong U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}/\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{+}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}})U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
where
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{+}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}):=\ker(\varepsilon)\cap\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}})
. Since
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{+}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}})U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
is a Hopf ideal, the surjection

(98)

p\colon U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}\twoheadrightarrow U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}/\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{+}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}})U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}

is a morphism of Hopf algebras. By definition we have

(99)

\forall\,h\in U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}},\quad i(h)_{|\epsilon}=i_{\epsilon}(h_{|\epsilon})=p(h_{|\epsilon}).

4.6.
The quantum Frobenius morphism

The morphism
\displaystyle\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}
defined below is the version due to De Concini-Lyubashenko of Lusztig’s Frobenius morphism
\displaystyle U_{\epsilon}^{{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}},\mathrm{res}}\to U(\mathfrak{g})
, introduced in
[
52
]
; see Remarks
4.3
and
4.6
for a comparison between
\displaystyle\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
and
\displaystyle U_{\epsilon}^{{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}},\mathrm{res}}
.

The quantum Frobenius morphism is the epimorphism of Hopf algebras

\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}:\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}\to U(\mathfrak{g})

given by
[
31
, Th. 6.3]

(100)

\begin{array}[]{l}\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}(E_{i}^{(p)})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\frac{e_{i}^{p/l}}{(p/l)!}&{\rm if}\ l\ {\rm divides}\ p\\
0&{\rm otherwise}\end{array}\right.\ ,\ \mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}(F_{i}^{(p)})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\frac{f_{i}^{p/l}}{(p/l)!}&{\rm if}\ l\ {\rm divides}\ p\\
0&{\rm otherwise}\end{array}\right.\\
\\
\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}\bigl((K_{i};p)_{\epsilon_{i}}\bigr)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\frac{h_{i}(h_{i}-1)\ldots(h_{i}-(p/l)+1)}{(p/l)!}&{\rm if}\ l\ {\rm divides}\ p\\
0&{\rm otherwise}\end{array}\right.\end{array}

where
\displaystyle p\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\displaystyle e_{i}
,
\displaystyle f_{i}
,
\displaystyle h_{i}
are the standard Serre generators of
\displaystyle U(\mathfrak{g})
. We have
\displaystyle\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}(K_{i}^{\pm 1})=1
.
\displaystyle\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}
commutes with the braid group action, so the formulas in the first line of (
100
) still hold true by replacing
\displaystyle E_{i}^{(p)}
,
\displaystyle F_{i}^{(p)}
(resp.
\displaystyle e_{i},f_{i}
) with the root vectors
\displaystyle E_{\beta}^{(p)}
,
\displaystyle F_{\beta}^{(p)}
(resp.
\displaystyle e_{\beta},f_{\beta}
) for all
\displaystyle\beta\in\phi^{+}
.

We are going to recall a number of properties of
\displaystyle\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}
that will be crucial in the sequel. First note that in
\displaystyle\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
we have

(101)

\begin{array}[]{c}(K_{j};t+l)_{\epsilon_{j}}=(K_{j};t)_{\epsilon_{j}}(K_{j};l)_{\epsilon_{j}}\text{for all }t\in\mathbb{N},\\[3.00003pt]
\text{and }(K_{j};t)_{\epsilon_{j}}\text{ is a polynomial of degree }t\text{ in }K_{j}\text{ if }0\leq t<l.\end{array}

Also, for all
\displaystyle 0\leq r_{0}<l
,
\displaystyle r_{1}\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\displaystyle\beta\in\phi^{+}
we have

(102)

E_{\beta}^{(r_{0}+lr_{1})}=E_{\beta}^{(r_{0})}\frac{(E_{\beta}^{(l)})^{r_{1}}}{r_{1}!}\ ,\ E_{\beta}^{(r_{0})}\in\mathbb{C}E_{\beta}^{r_{0}},

and similarly by replacing
\displaystyle E_{\beta}
with
\displaystyle F_{\beta}
(see
[
51
]
and, e.g.,
[
26
, Rk. 1 on page 300, and top of page 302]
).

Now recall the basis elements of
\displaystyle\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
in (
85
). Consider the free
\displaystyle A
-submodules
\displaystyle J_{l},\Gamma_{l}\subset\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
defined by

(103)

\displaystyle\displaystyle J_{l}:=

\displaystyle\displaystyle A\left\{\prod_{i=N}^{1}E_{\beta_{i}}^{(p_{i})}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{m}K_{j}^{-\sigma(t_{j})}(K_{j};t_{j})_{q_{j}}\right)\prod_{k=N}^{1}F_{\beta_{k}}^{(n_{k})},\ \exists p_{i},t_{j},n_{k}\not\equiv 0\!\!\pmod{l}\right\}

(104)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Gamma_{l}:=

\displaystyle\displaystyle A\left\{\prod_{i=N}^{1}E_{\beta_{i}}^{(p_{i})}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{m}K_{j}^{-\sigma(t_{j})}(K_{j};t_{j})_{q_{j}}\right)\prod_{k=N}^{1}F_{\beta_{k}}^{(n_{k})},\ \mathrm{all}\ p_{i},t_{j},n_{k}\equiv 0\!\!\pmod{l}\right\}.

We have
\displaystyle\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}=J_{l}\oplus\Gamma_{l}
as a free
\displaystyle A
-module.

Specializing to
\displaystyle q=\epsilon
, the observations (
101
)-(
102
) imply that the vector space
\displaystyle(\Gamma_{l})_{\epsilon}
has a basis given by the elements
\displaystyle\textstyle(\prod_{i=N}^{1}(E_{\beta_{i}}^{(l)})^{p_{i}^{\prime}})M(\prod_{k=N}^{1}(F_{\beta_{k}}^{(l)})^{n_{k}^{\prime}})
, where
\displaystyle p_{i}^{\prime},n_{k}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
and
\displaystyle M
is a polynomial in the elements
\displaystyle(K_{j};l)_{\epsilon_{j}}
,
\displaystyle j\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
. (Beware that
\displaystyle(\Gamma_{l})_{\epsilon}
is not a subalgebra of
\displaystyle\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
- this is immediate from the commutation relations between elements
\displaystyle E_{\beta}^{(l)}
and
\displaystyle F_{\beta}^{(l)}
,
\displaystyle\beta\in\phi^{+}
(see, e.g.,
[
73
, Prop. 3.23]
)).

The observations (
101
)-(
102
) imply also that
\displaystyle\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
is generated as an algebra by the elements
\displaystyle E_{i},F_{i},E_{i}^{(l)},F_{i}^{(l)},K_{i}
and
\displaystyle(K_{i};l)_{\epsilon_{i}}
,
\displaystyle i\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
. Therefore

(105)

\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}\mathrm{\ is\ generated\ by\ }u_{\epsilon}\mathrm{\ and\ }(\Gamma_{l})_{\epsilon}.

Moreover we have
[
31
, Th. 6.3 and Prop 6.5 (i)]
:

(i)

the map
\displaystyle\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}\colon(\Gamma_{l})_{\epsilon}\to U(\mathfrak{g})
is bijective (and hence it is a linear isomorphism).

(ii)

the Hopf ideal
\displaystyle\ker(\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon})
is the space
\displaystyle(J_{l})_{\epsilon}
, it is generated as an ideal by the elements
\displaystyle E_{i}
,
\displaystyle F_{i}
and
\displaystyle K_{i}-1
(
\displaystyle i\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
), and it has a
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}
-basis formed by the monomials

(106)

\left(\prod_{i=N}^{1}E_{\beta_{i}}^{(p_{i})}\right)M\left(\prod_{k=N}^{1}F_{\beta_{k}}^{(n_{k})}\right)

such that there are divided powers
\displaystyle p_{i},n_{k}
which are not divisible by
\displaystyle l
, or
\displaystyle M
is in the ideal of
\displaystyle\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}(\mathfrak{h})
generated by the elements
\displaystyle K_{i}-1
,
\displaystyle i\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
.

Remark 4.7
.

Because
\displaystyle S
is an isomorphism of
\displaystyle\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{+})
,
\displaystyle\ker(\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon})
has also a
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}
-basis formed by the monomials
\displaystyle\textstyle S\!\left(\prod_{k=N}^{1}E_{\beta_{k}}^{(n_{k})}\right)M\left(\prod_{i=N}^{1}F_{\beta_{i}}^{(p_{i})}\right)
, with the same assumptions on the integers
\displaystyle p_{i}
,
\displaystyle n_{k}
and on
\displaystyle M
as in (ii) above.

It follows from (
95
) and (
106
) that

(107)

\ker(\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon})=\text{two-sided ideal in }\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}\text{ generated by }\ker(\varepsilon)\cap u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}.

From this, the small quantum group
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
is often called the
Frobenius-Lusztig kernel
. In particular the composition of maps

(108)

\displaystyle\textstyle{u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{{\rm incl.\ }}
\displaystyle\textstyle{\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}\ }
\displaystyle\textstyle{U(\mathfrak{g})}

sends
\displaystyle h\in u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
to
\displaystyle\varepsilon(h)1_{U(\mathfrak{g})}
.

4.7.
Specialisation of
\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{q}(G)

By specializing
\displaystyle q
to
\displaystyle\epsilon
, define
\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}={\mathcal{O}}_{A}\otimes_{A}{\mathbb{C}}_{\epsilon}
. Since
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{A}
is generated as an algebra by matrix coefficients of the modules
\displaystyle{}_{A}V_{\varpi_{i}}
(
\displaystyle i=1,\ldots,m
)
[
53
]
,
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}
is generated as an algebra by matrix coefficients of the modules
\displaystyle V_{\varpi_{i},\epsilon}:={{}_{A}V_{\varpi_{i}}}\otimes_{A}\mathbb{C}_{\epsilon}
(
\displaystyle i=1,\ldots,m
).

The Hopf pairing (
86
) specializes to the Hopf pairing

(109)

\langle\text{-},\text{-}\rangle_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}:{\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}\times\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}\to{\mathbb{C}}\quad\text{such that }\forall\,\varphi\in\mathcal{O}_{A},\>\>\forall\,x\in\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}},\>\>\langle\varphi_{|\epsilon},x_{|\epsilon}\rangle_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}=\bigl(\langle\varphi,x\rangle^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}_{A}\bigr)_{|\epsilon}.

By specializing to
\displaystyle q=\epsilon
the pairings (
88
) one obtains non degenerate pairings (see
[
31
, §6]
, and
[
12
, Th. 2.20 and 2.29]
in the present notations)

(110)

\tau_{\epsilon}\colon U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{+})^{\rm cop}\times\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{-})\to\mathbb{C},\qquad\rho_{\epsilon}\colon U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{-})^{\rm cop}\times\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{+})\to\mathbb{C}.

By the observation (
3
), the isomorphisms (
90
) specialize to isomorphisms

(111)

\Phi_{\epsilon}^{\pm}\colon\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}(B_{\pm})\to U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{\mp})^{\rm cop}

satisfying, for every
\displaystyle\alpha\in\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}
and
\displaystyle x_{\pm}\in\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{\pm})
,

(112)

\rho_{\epsilon}\bigl(\Phi_{\epsilon}^{+}(\alpha),x_{+}\bigr)=\langle\alpha,x_{+}\rangle_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}},\qquad\tau_{\epsilon}\bigl(\Phi_{\epsilon}^{-}(\alpha),x_{-}\bigr)=\langle\alpha,x_{-}\rangle_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}.

Recall from (
93
) the
\displaystyle l
-root of unity
\displaystyle\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
such that
\displaystyle\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}^{D}=\epsilon
, and to which
\displaystyle q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
is specialized.

Lemma 4.8
.

The pairing
\displaystyle\langle\text{-},\text{-}\rangle_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}:{\mathcal{O}}_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}\times\Gamma_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}\to A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
from (
87
) specializes at
\displaystyle q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}=\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
to a non-degenerate Hopf pairing
\displaystyle\langle\text{-},\text{-}\rangle_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}:{\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}\times\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
.

Proof.

It is non degenerate on the left because if
\displaystyle\varphi\in{\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}
vanishes on
\displaystyle\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
, then it is the zero linear form (by definition of
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}
). Our proof that it is non degenerate on the right uses Lusztig’s modified quantum group and is thus postponed to Appendix
B
.
∎

Consider the dual map
\displaystyle\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}^{*}:U(\mathfrak{g})^{*}\to(\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}})^{*}
of the quantum Frobenius morphism
\displaystyle\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}
. By restriction to
\displaystyle U(\mathfrak{g})^{\circ}
it becomes a monomorphism of Hopf algebras
\displaystyle U(\mathfrak{g})^{\circ}=\mathcal{O}(G)\to(\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}})^{\circ}
. Actually it holds
\displaystyle\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}^{*}(U(\mathfrak{g})^{\circ})\subset\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}=((\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}})^{\circ})_{\epsilon}\subset(\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}})^{\circ}
, because for every
\displaystyle\lambda\in P^{+}
the
\displaystyle\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
-module
\displaystyle V_{l\lambda,\epsilon}:={}_{A}V_{l\lambda}\otimes_{A}\mathbb{C}_{\epsilon}
is isomorphic to the pull-back by
\displaystyle\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}
of the irreducible
\displaystyle U(\mathfrak{g})
-module
\displaystyle L(\lambda)
with highest weight
\displaystyle\lambda
(see
[
31
, Prop. 6.4]
,
[
26
, Prop. 11.2.11]
or
[
20
, §III.7.2]
). This leads to the following definition:

Definition 4.9
.

We denote

(113)

\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}):=\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}^{*}\bigl({\mathcal{O}}(G)\bigr),

which is a Hopf subalgebra of
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}(G)
.

Here is another characterization of this subspace:

Lemma 4.10
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon})\subset\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}
is the orthogonal of
\displaystyle\ker(\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon})\subset\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
with respect to the non-degenerate pairing
\displaystyle\langle\text{-},\text{-}\rangle^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}_{\epsilon}
. Said differently, for all
\displaystyle\varphi\in\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}
we have

\varphi\in\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon})\>\iff\>\bigl\langle\varphi,\ker(\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon})\bigr\rangle^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}_{\epsilon}=0.

Proof.

If
\displaystyle\varphi\in\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon})
then by definition in (
113
) there exists
\displaystyle\psi\in\mathcal{O}(G)
such that
\displaystyle\varphi=\psi\circ\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}
and hence
\displaystyle\varphi
vanishes on
\displaystyle\ker(\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon})
. Conversely, assume that
\displaystyle\varphi\in\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}
vanishes on
\displaystyle\ker(\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon})
. Since
\displaystyle\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}
is surjective we can define a linear form
\displaystyle\psi\in U(\mathfrak{g})^{*}
by
\displaystyle\psi\bigl(\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}(h)\bigr)=\varphi(h)
for all
\displaystyle h\in\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
. We have to show that such a
\displaystyle\psi
belongs to
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(G)
. Denote by
\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}}_{u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}}
and
\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}}_{\epsilon}
the categories of finite dimensional
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
-modules and
\displaystyle\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
-modules (they are weight modules in the sense of
[
26
, §11.2]
, and modules of type
\displaystyle 1
since
\displaystyle K_{i}^{l}=1
in
\displaystyle\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
). Denote also by
\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}}_{G}
the category of finite dimensional
\displaystyle G
-modules (or equivalently
\displaystyle U(\mathfrak{g})
-modules). The sequence (
108
) yields a sequence of functors

(114)

\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathcal{C}_{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{({\mathbb{F}}r_{\epsilon})^{\sharp}}
\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{{\rm res}^{\sharp}\ }
\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathcal{C}_{u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}}}

where
\displaystyle{\rm{\rm res}}^{\sharp}
is the restriction of modules along the inclusion
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}\subset\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
, and
\displaystyle({\mathbb{F}}r_{\epsilon})^{\sharp}
pulls-back modules along
\displaystyle{\mathbb{F}}r_{\epsilon}
(so, given
\displaystyle M
in
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{G}
the action of
\displaystyle\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
on
\displaystyle{\mathbb{F}}r_{\epsilon}^{\sharp}(M)
is given by the formulas (
100
)). As observed in
[
5
, §3.11]
, the sequence (
114
) is exact in the middle. That is, for any module
\displaystyle V
in
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{G}
the
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
-module
\displaystyle({\rm{\rm res}}^{\sharp}\circ{\mathbb{F}}r_{\epsilon}^{\sharp})(V)
is trivial (i.e., with action given by the counit), and for any
\displaystyle M
in
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}
such that
\displaystyle{\rm res}^{\sharp}(M)
is a trivial
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
-module, there is a module
\displaystyle V
in
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{G}
such that
\displaystyle M={\mathbb{F}}r_{\epsilon}^{\sharp}(V)
. The two claims follow from the linear isomorphism
\displaystyle\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}\colon(\Gamma_{l})_{\epsilon}\to U(\mathfrak{g})
(see (i) above (
106
)), together with (
107
) and (
108
) (see
[
9
, Prop. 1.5 (2)]
for some details). To prove the lemma, take in particular the modules in
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}
induced by specialization from modules in
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{A}
. Consider the elements of
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}
, i.e. the span of matrix coefficients of such modules, vanishing on
\displaystyle\ker(\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon})
. They define through
\displaystyle{\rm res}^{\sharp}
linear combinations of matrix coefficients of trivial
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
-modules, so they belong to
\displaystyle\mathrm{im}(\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}^{*})\cong\mathcal{O}(G)
by exactness of (
114
). It follows that
\displaystyle\psi\in\mathcal{O}(G)
.
∎

Remark 4.11
.

By the isomorphism
\displaystyle V_{l\lambda,\epsilon}\cong({\mathbb{F}}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon})^{\sharp}(L(\lambda))
discussed above (
113
), the functor
\displaystyle({\mathbb{F}}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon})^{\sharp}
is fully faithful.

The following important theorem collects results from
[
31
,
34
,
23
]
(for the correspondence with the present notations, see
[
12
, Th. 2.29]
).

Theorem 4.12
.

(1)
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon})
is a central Hopf subalgebra of
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}
, generated by certain matrix coefficients of simple
\displaystyle\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
-modules of highest weights
\displaystyle l\lambda
,
\displaystyle\lambda\in P_{+}
, and
\displaystyle Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon})\bigr)
is an extension of
\displaystyle Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon})\bigr)
of degree
\displaystyle l^{m}
.

(2)
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}
is a domain, and it is a free
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon})
-module of rank
\displaystyle l^{dim\mathfrak{g}}
. Moreover the central localization
\displaystyle Q(\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}):=Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon})\bigr)\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon})}\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}
is a central simple algebra of PI degree
\displaystyle l^{N}
, where
\displaystyle N
is the number of positive roots of
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}
.

Remark 4.13
.

The sequence of inclusions
\displaystyle{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon})\subset{\mathcal{Z}}({\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon})\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}
is a PI Hopf triple in the sense of
[
19
,
20
]
. This notion is a unifying concept which encompasses properties of
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}
described in Theorem
4.12
.

We saw in (
99
) that the injection
\displaystyle i:U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}\to\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
specializes to a surjection
\displaystyle p:U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}\to u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
. By very definition of
\displaystyle\langle\text{-},\text{-}\rangle_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
, this may be written as:

(115)

\forall\,\varphi\in\mathcal{O}_{A},\>\>\forall\,h\in U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}\subset\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}},\quad\bigl(\langle\varphi,h\rangle_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}\bigr)_{|\epsilon}=\bigl\langle\varphi_{|\epsilon},p(h_{|\epsilon})\bigr\rangle_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}.

Denote by
\displaystyle(u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}})^{*}
the Hopf algebra dual to
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
and define a map
\displaystyle\pi:{\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}\rightarrow(u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}})^{*}
by restriction,
i.e.

(116)

\forall\,\varphi\in{\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon},\>\>\forall\,x\in u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}\subset\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}},\quad\pi(\varphi)(x):=\langle\varphi,x\rangle_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}

We have the sequence of maps

(117)

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-3.01385pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 27.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-3.01385pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathcal{O}(G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 56.41809pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@hook{1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 64.27611pt\raise 6.54709pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.79709pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\ (\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon})^{*}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 110.41809pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 80.41809pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-3.01385pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 110.41809pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-3.01385pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 140.5317pt\raise 4.50694pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.50694pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\pi}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 152.02887pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 152.02887pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-3.01385pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{(u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}})^{*}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

The following result is proved in e.g.
[
20
, Th. III.7.10]
. A delicate point of the proof is the surjectivity of
\displaystyle\pi
, obtained by means of representation theory. We give a different proof, based on the canonical monomorphism
\displaystyle\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}\hookrightarrow\hat{{\mathbf{U}}}_{\epsilon}
from Lemma
B.1
.

Proposition 4.14
.

(1) The map
\displaystyle\pi
is a surjective morphism of Hopf algebras.

(2) We have
\displaystyle\ker(\pi)={\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{+}(\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon})
, where
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{+}(\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}):=\ker\bigl(\varepsilon|_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon})}\bigr)=\ker(\varepsilon_{\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}})\cap{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon})

Proof.

(1) Showing that
\displaystyle\pi
is Hopf algebra morphism is trivial, since
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
is a sub-Hopf algebra of
\displaystyle\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
and
\displaystyle\langle\text{-},\text{-}\rangle_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
is a Hopf pairing. For surjectivity, let
\displaystyle\{x_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
be a basis of
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
. By the inclusions
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}\subset\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}\subset\hat{{\mathbf{U}}}_{\epsilon}
in Lemma
B.1
, we can write

\displaystyle\textstyle x_{i}=\sum_{b\in\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{B}}}}A_{ib}b_{\epsilon}
with
\displaystyle A_{ib}\in{\mathbb{C}}.
The family
\displaystyle(x_{i})
being free, if
\displaystyle(\lambda_{i})\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
satisfies
\displaystyle\textstyle\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}A_{ib}=0
for all
\displaystyle b\in\>\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{B}}}
, then
\displaystyle\lambda_{1}=\ldots=\lambda_{n}=0
. Therefore
\displaystyle\textstyle\bigcap_{b\in\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{B}}}}H_{b}=\{0\}
, where
\displaystyle H_{b}\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
is the hyperplane with equation
\displaystyle\textstyle\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}A_{ib}=0
. We can extract exactly
\displaystyle n
hyperplanes such that
\displaystyle\textstyle\bigcap_{j=1}^{n}H_{b_{j}}=\{0\},
which amounts to say that the matrix
\displaystyle(A_{ib_{j}})
is invertible.
Let
\displaystyle\overline{A}
be the inverse of this matrix, and recall the canonical basis
\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{B}}}{\!\!{}^{*}}=\{\varphi_{b}\}_{b\in\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{B}}}}
of
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{A}
, which is dual to
\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{B}}}
. Then the elements
\displaystyle\psi_{j}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}
defined by
\displaystyle\textstyle\psi_{j}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\overline{A}_{b_{k},j}\varphi_{b_{k}}^{\epsilon}\in\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}
satisfy
\displaystyle\langle\psi_{j},x_{i}\rangle_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}=\delta_{ij}
. Hence
\displaystyle\bigl\{\pi(\psi_{j})\bigr\}_{1\leq j\leq n}
is a basis of
\displaystyle(u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}})^{*}
and
\displaystyle\pi
is surjective.

(2) We first show that
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{+}(\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon})\subset\ker(\pi)
. Because
\displaystyle\pi
is an algebra morphism it is sufficient to show that
\displaystyle\pi\bigl({\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{+}(\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon})\bigr)=\{0\}
. Let
\displaystyle\varphi\in{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{+}(\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon})
. Because
\displaystyle{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon})
is a sub-Hopf algebra of
\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}
and
\displaystyle\langle\text{-},\text{-}\rangle_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
is a Hopf pairing, it is sufficient to show that
\displaystyle\langle\varphi,\text{-}\rangle_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
vanishes on the generators
\displaystyle E_{i},F_{i},K_{i}
of
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
. We have
\displaystyle\varphi=\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}^{*}(\psi)
where
\displaystyle\psi\in{\mathcal{O}}(G)
satisfies
\displaystyle\varepsilon(\psi)=\psi(1)=0
(because
\displaystyle\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}^{*}
is an injective morphism of Hopf algebras). By definitions
\displaystyle\langle\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}^{*}(\psi),E_{i}\rangle_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}=\langle\psi,\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}(E_{i})\rangle=0
, similarly
\displaystyle\langle\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}^{*}(\psi),F_{i}\rangle_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}=0
, and
\displaystyle\langle\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}^{*}(\psi),K_{i}\rangle_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}=\langle\psi,\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}(K_{i})\rangle=\psi(1)=0
as desired.

Hence we can define the quotient morphism
\displaystyle\bar{\pi}:{\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}/{\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{+}(\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon})\rightarrow(u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}})^{*}
, which is still surjective. From
[
20
, §III.7.7]
, the algebra
\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}/{\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{+}(\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon})
has dimension
\displaystyle l^{\dim(\mathfrak{g})}
, which is exactly
\displaystyle\dim\bigl((u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}})^{*}\bigr)
. By equality of dimensions, we deduce that
\displaystyle\bar{\pi}
is an isomorphism, proving the claim.
∎

4.8.
Quasitriangular structures at roots of unity

Our goal here is to relate the specialization at
\displaystyle\epsilon
of the co-R-matrix on
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{A}
(§
4.4
) with the usual R-matrix on the small quantum
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
, which by finite-dimensionality is equivalent to a co-R-matrix on
\displaystyle(u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}})^{*}
. We start by recalling this standard quasitriangular structure of
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
.

4.8.1.
R-matrix for the small quantum group

We explain the R-matrix of
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
by means of the Drinfeld double construction. Recall that
\displaystyle\tau
denotes the Drinfeld pairing (
78
) and
\displaystyle\tau_{\epsilon}:U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{+})^{\rm cop}\times\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{-})\to\mathbb{C}
is its specialized version (
110
). Recall also the notation (
97
).

Lemma 4.15
.

For all
\displaystyle x\in\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{+}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{+}))
and
\displaystyle h\in u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{-})\subset\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{-})
we have
\displaystyle\tau_{\epsilon}(x,h)=0
. Therefore we have a well-defined pairing

\bar{\tau}_{\epsilon}:u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{+})^{\rm cop}\times u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{-})\to\mathbb{C}\quad\text{given by }\>\>\bar{\tau}_{\epsilon}(p(x),h)=\tau_{\epsilon}(x,h).

Moreover
\displaystyle\bar{\tau}_{\epsilon}
is a non degenerate Hopf pairing.

Proof.

The first claim is immediate on generators, and the fact that
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{+}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{+}))U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{+})
is a Hopf ideal. It implies that
\displaystyle\bar{\tau}_{\epsilon}
is well-defined; it is a Hopf pairing since
\displaystyle\tau_{\epsilon}
is a Hopf pairing and
\displaystyle p:U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}\to u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
is a Hopf morphism. Because
\displaystyle\tau_{\epsilon}
is non degenerate and
\displaystyle p
is surjective,
\displaystyle\bar{\tau}_{\epsilon}
is non degenerate on the right, and therefore yields an injective map
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{-})\rightarrow u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{+})^{*}
. By equality of dimensions of the source and target spaces this map is an isomorphism. Therefore
\displaystyle\bar{\tau}_{\epsilon}
is a non degenerate Hopf pairing.
∎

We also let
\displaystyle\bar{\rho}_{\epsilon}:u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{-})^{\rm cop}\times u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{+})\to\mathbb{C}
be the pairing defined by

\bar{\rho}_{\epsilon}(x,y):=\bar{\tau}_{\epsilon}(y,S^{-1}(x))=\bar{\tau}_{\epsilon}(S(y),x).

Since
\displaystyle S
is an isomorphism, this pairing is non degenerate. Note that we also have, for every
\displaystyle x\in U_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{b}_{-})
,
\displaystyle h\in u_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{b}_{+})
:

(118)

\bar{\rho}_{\epsilon}(p(x),h)=\rho_{\epsilon}(x,h)

with
\displaystyle\rho_{\epsilon}:U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{-})^{\rm cop}\times\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{+})
the specialization of
\displaystyle\rho
in (
77
).

The Drinfeld double
\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(\bar{\tau}_{\epsilon})
is the coalgebra
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{-})\otimes u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{+})
endowed with the product

(119)

\quad(x^{-}\otimes x^{+})(y^{-}\otimes y^{+})=\sum_{(x^{+}),(y^{-})}\bar{\tau}_{\epsilon}\bigl(S(x^{+}_{(1)}),y^{-}_{(1)}\bigr)\bar{\tau}_{\epsilon}\bigl(x^{+}_{(3)},y^{-}_{(3)}\bigr)x^{-}y^{-}_{(2)}\otimes x^{+}_{(2)}y^{+}.

Then
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
is isomorphic to the quotient of
\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(\bar{\tau}_{\epsilon})
by the relations
\displaystyle 1\otimes K_{i}-K_{i}\otimes 1
for all
\displaystyle i
. As for any Drinfeld double, the R-matrix of
\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(\bar{\tau}_{\epsilon})^{\otimes 2}
is
\displaystyle\widetilde{R}=\textstyle\sum_{i}(1\otimes h^{+}_{i})\otimes(h_{i}^{-}\otimes 1)
, where
\displaystyle(h_{i}^{\pm})
are a pair of dual bases for
\displaystyle\bar{\tau}_{\epsilon}
. The image of
\displaystyle\widetilde{R}
through the quotient morphism
\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(\bar{\tau}_{\epsilon})\twoheadrightarrow u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
is denoted
\displaystyle\overline{R}\in(u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}})^{\otimes 2}
and is the R-matrix that we fix for
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
; for an explicit formula see
[
56
, App. A]
. We have morphisms of Hopf algebras
\displaystyle\bar{\Phi}^{\pm}_{\epsilon}:(u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}})^{*}\to(u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}})^{\rm cop}
given by

(120)

\bar{\Phi}^{+}_{\epsilon}(\varphi)=(\varphi\otimes\mathrm{id})(\overline{R}),\qquad\bar{\Phi}_{\epsilon}^{-}(\varphi)=(\mathrm{id}\otimes\varphi)(\overline{R}^{-1}).

By the very definition of
\displaystyle\overline{R}
, they factorize through
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{\pm})^{*}
and take values in
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{b}_{\mp})
. Moreover they are easily seen to satisfy

(121)

\bar{\rho}_{\epsilon}\bigl(\bar{\Phi}_{\epsilon}^{+}(\alpha),h_{+}\bigr)=\alpha_{+}(h_{+}),\quad\bar{\tau}_{\epsilon}\bigl(\bar{\Phi}_{\epsilon}^{-}(\alpha),h_{-}\bigr)=\alpha_{-}(h_{-})

for every
\displaystyle\alpha\in(u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}})^{*}
and
\displaystyle h_{\pm}\in u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{\pm})
. It follows that they descend to isomorphisms of Hopf algebras
\displaystyle\bar{\Phi}^{\pm}_{\epsilon}:u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{\pm})^{*}\overset{\sim}{\to}u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{\mp})^{\rm cop}
.

4.8.2.
Relating (co)quasitriangular structures

In this section we prove results that will be used in §
5.4
.

Recall the duality pairing
\displaystyle\langle\text{-},\text{-}\rangle_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}:\mathcal{O}_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}\times\Gamma_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}\to A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
from (
87
) which extends
\displaystyle\langle\text{-},\text{-}\rangle_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}:\mathcal{O}_{A}\times\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}\to A
. The
\displaystyle A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
subscripts indicate extension of ground ring to
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}[q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}^{\pm 1}]
; recall that we specialize
\displaystyle q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
to the
\displaystyle l
-th root of unity
\displaystyle\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
fixed in (
93
).

Lemma 4.16
.

1. The surjection
\displaystyle p:U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}\to u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
in (
98
) can be extended to a morphism of Hopf algebras
\displaystyle\widetilde{p}:U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}\to u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
(note that the target is unchanged).

2. Let
\displaystyle\widetilde{i}:U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}\hookrightarrow\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
be the natural inclusion. For every
\displaystyle\alpha\in\mathcal{O}_{A}
and
\displaystyle h\in U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
we have

\bigl(\bigl\langle\alpha,\widetilde{i}(h)\bigr\rangle_{\!A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}\bigr)_{|\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}=\bigl\langle\alpha_{|\epsilon},\widetilde{p}(h_{|\epsilon})\bigr\rangle_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}=\pi(\alpha_{|\epsilon})\bigl(\widetilde{p}(h_{|\epsilon})\bigr).

3. The pairings
\displaystyle\bar{\tau}_{\epsilon}:u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{+})^{\rm cop}\times u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{-})\to\mathbb{C}
from Lem.
4.15
and
\displaystyle\tau_{\epsilon}:U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{+})^{\rm cop}\times\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{-})\to\mathbb{C}
from (
112
) satisfy

\forall\,x\in U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{+}),\quad\forall\,h\in u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{-})\subset\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{-}),\quad\bar{\tau}_{\epsilon}\bigl(\widetilde{p}(x),h\bigr)=\tau_{\epsilon}(x,h).

The same property holds with the pairings
\displaystyle\rho_{\epsilon}
and
\displaystyle\bar{\rho}_{\epsilon}
.

Proof.

Recall from (
92
) that
\displaystyle D
and
\displaystyle l
are assumed to be coprime, and we denote by
\displaystyle\overline{D}
the multiplicative inverse of
\displaystyle D
modulo
\displaystyle l
, so that
\displaystyle(\epsilon^{\overline{D}})^{D}=\epsilon
.

1. The version
\displaystyle U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
is obtained from
\displaystyle U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
by adding the generators
\displaystyle L_{i}=K_{\varpi_{i}}
for all
\displaystyle 1\leq i\leq m
. Remember that the Cartan matrix
\displaystyle(a_{ij})
has an inverse with coefficients in
\displaystyle\textstyle\frac{1}{D}\mathbb{N}
, which we denote by
\displaystyle(\overline{a}_{ij})
. The relations
\displaystyle\textstyle\alpha_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}a_{ji}\varpi_{j}
for
\displaystyle 1\leq i\leq m
are equivalent to
\displaystyle\textstyle\varpi_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\overline{a}_{ji}\alpha_{j}
. From the relation
\displaystyle K_{i}^{l}=1
in
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
, we see that
\displaystyle K_{i}^{\overline{D}}
can be used to play the role of
\displaystyle K_{i}^{1/D}
. We thus let
\displaystyle\textstyle\widetilde{p}(L_{i})=\prod_{s=1}^{m}(K_{s}^{\overline{D}})^{D\overline{a}_{si}}
. The only non-trivial thing to check is compatibility with the relations
\displaystyle L_{i}E_{j}=\epsilon_{i}^{\delta_{i,j}}E_{j}L_{i}
(and similarly with
\displaystyle F_{j}
):

\widetilde{p}(L_{i})E_{j}=\prod_{s=1}^{m}(\epsilon^{\overline{D}})^{Dd_{j}a_{js}\overline{a}_{si}}E_{j}(K_{s}^{\overline{D}})^{D\overline{a}_{si}}=\epsilon^{d_{j}\overline{D}D\delta_{i,j}}E_{j}\widetilde{p}(L_{i})=\epsilon_{i}^{\delta_{i,j}}E_{j}\widetilde{p}(L_{i})

where we used that
\displaystyle K_{s}E_{j}=K_{\alpha_{s}}E_{\alpha_{j}}=q^{(\alpha_{s},\alpha_{j})}E_{j}K_{s}=q^{d_{j}a_{js}}E_{j}K_{s}
and that
\displaystyle\epsilon^{\overline{D}D}=\epsilon
.

2. It suffices to check this when
\displaystyle h
is a generator of
\displaystyle U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
, since
\displaystyle\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
is a Hopf pairing. We use again that
\displaystyle U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
is an extension of
\displaystyle U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
by the elements
\displaystyle L_{i}
. If
\displaystyle h\in U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
we have
\displaystyle\widetilde{i}(h)=i(h)
with
\displaystyle i:U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}\hookrightarrow\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
and thus

\bigl(\langle\alpha,\widetilde{i}(h)\rangle_{\!A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}\bigr)_{|\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}=\bigl(\langle\alpha,i(h)\rangle_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}\bigr)_{|\epsilon}=\langle\alpha_{|\epsilon},i(h)_{|\epsilon}\rangle_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}\overset{\eqref{injectionBecomesProjection}}{=}\langle\alpha_{|\epsilon},p(h_{|\epsilon})\rangle_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}\overset{\eqref{piPdef}}{=}\pi(\alpha_{|\epsilon})\bigl(p(h_{|\epsilon})\bigr).

Now consider the case
\displaystyle h=L_{i}
. By definition of
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{A}
(§
4.4
), we can assume that
\displaystyle\alpha
is a matrix coefficient
\displaystyle f(?\cdot v)
of some
{}_{A}V\in\mathcal{C}_{A}
, with
\displaystyle f\in\mathrm{Hom}_{A}(_{A}V,A)
and
\displaystyle v\in{{}_{A}V}
a vector of weight
\displaystyle\mu\in P
. Then on one hand
\displaystyle\bigl(\langle\alpha,\tilde{i}(L_{j})\rangle_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}\bigr)_{|\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}=f(L_{j}\cdot v)_{|\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}=\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}^{D(\varpi_{j},\mu)}f(v)_{|\epsilon}
by (
71
). On the other hand, in the specialization
{}_{\epsilon}V
, which is a
\displaystyle\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
-module, we have

\textstyle\bigl\langle f(?\cdot v)_{|\epsilon},\widetilde{p}(L_{i})\bigr\rangle_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}=f_{|\epsilon}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{m}(K_{j}^{\overline{D}})^{D\overline{a}_{ji}}\cdot v_{|\epsilon}\right)=\prod_{j=1}^{m}\epsilon^{\overline{D}D\overline{a}_{ji}(\alpha_{j},\mu)}f(v)_{|\epsilon}=\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}^{D(\varpi_{i},\mu)}f(v)_{|\epsilon}

where we used the relation between fundamental weights
\displaystyle(\varpi_{i})
and simple roots
\displaystyle(\alpha_{j})
.

3. By definition of
\displaystyle\bar{\tau}_{\epsilon}
, this is true for the restriction
\displaystyle p
of
\displaystyle\widetilde{p}
to
\displaystyle U^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{b}_{+})
. By the Hopf pairing property, it remains only to check this when
\displaystyle x=L_{i}
and
\displaystyle h
is a generator of
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
. For
\displaystyle h=E_{j}
and
\displaystyle h=F_{j}
this is obvious from the definition of
\displaystyle\tau
in (
78
). For
\displaystyle h=K_{i}
this follows by an argument which is completely similar to the ones in previous items.
∎

The “quasitriangular datum” (in the sense of §
3.1
)
\displaystyle\Phi_{\epsilon}^{\pm}
from (
111
) and
\displaystyle\bar{\Phi}^{\pm}_{\epsilon}
from (
120
) can now be related by means of the surjections
\displaystyle\pi
from (
116
) and
\displaystyle\widetilde{p}
from Lemma
4.16
:

Lemma 4.17
.

We have a commutative diagram of Hopf algebra morphisms

\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\Phi_{\epsilon}^{\pm}\hskip 8.19447pt}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\pi}
\displaystyle\textstyle{U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{\mp})^{\rm cop}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\widetilde{p}}
\displaystyle\textstyle{(u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}})^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\ \bar{\Phi}_{\epsilon}^{\pm}\hskip 8.19447pt}
\displaystyle\textstyle{u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{\mp})^{\rm cop}}

Proof.

For every
\displaystyle h\in u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{-})
and
\displaystyle\alpha\in\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}
we have

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bar{\tau}_{\epsilon}\bigl(\widetilde{p}\circ\Phi_{\epsilon}^{-}(\alpha),h\bigr)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\tau_{\epsilon}(\Phi_{\epsilon}^{-}(\alpha),h)=\langle\alpha_{,}h\rangle_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}=\pi(\alpha)(h)=\bar{\tau}_{\epsilon}\bigl(\bar{\Phi}_{\epsilon}^{-}\circ\pi(\alpha),h\bigr).

The first equality follow from Lemma
4.16
(3), the second from (
112
), the third from (
116
), and the fourth from (
121
). Since
\displaystyle\bar{\tau}_{\epsilon}
is non degenerate, the desired equality follows. The proof for
\displaystyle\Phi^{+}_{\epsilon}
is similar, but now using the pairing
\displaystyle\bar{\rho}_{\epsilon}
.
∎

Let
\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\epsilon}:\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}\to\mathbb{C}
be the specialization to
\displaystyle q=\epsilon
(with
\displaystyle q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}=\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}=\epsilon^{\overline{{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}}
, see (
93
)) of the co-R-matrix
\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}:\mathcal{O}_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}\to A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
. Also, the R-matrix
\displaystyle\overline{R}\in(u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}})^{\otimes 2}
yields the co-R-matrix
\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{R}}:(u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}})^{*}\otimes(u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}})^{*}\to\mathbb{C}
given by
\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{R}}(\alpha\otimes\beta)=(\alpha\otimes\beta)(\overline{R})=\beta\bigl(\bar{\Phi}^{+}_{\epsilon}(\alpha)\bigr)
. The next corollary will have important consequences in §
5.4
.

Corollary 4.18
.

1. The projection
\displaystyle\pi:\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}\to(u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}})^{*}
defined in (
116
) is a morphism of co-quasitriangular Hopf algebras, i.e.

\forall\,\varphi,\psi\in\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon},\quad\mathcal{R}_{\epsilon}(\varphi\otimes\psi)=\overline{\mathcal{R}}\bigl(\pi(\varphi)\otimes\pi(\psi)\bigr).

2. For any
\displaystyle\varphi\in\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}
and
\displaystyle\psi\in\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon})
, it holds

\mathcal{R}_{\epsilon}(\varphi\otimes\psi)=\mathcal{R}_{\epsilon}(\psi\otimes\varphi)=\varepsilon_{\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}}(\varphi)\,\varepsilon_{\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}}(\psi)

where
\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}}
is the counit of
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}
, given by
\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}}(\varphi)=\langle\varphi,1\rangle_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
.

Proof.

1. Let
\displaystyle\varphi^{\prime},\psi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{O}_{A}
be such that
\displaystyle\varphi^{\prime}_{|\epsilon}=\varphi
and
\displaystyle\psi^{\prime}_{|\epsilon}=\psi
. Then

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\epsilon}(\varphi\otimes\psi)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}(\varphi^{\prime}\otimes\psi^{\prime})_{|\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}=\bigl\langle\psi^{\prime},\widetilde{i}\bigl(\Phi^{+}_{A}(\varphi^{\prime})\bigr)\bigr\rangle_{\!A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}|\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}=\pi(\psi)\bigl[\,\widetilde{p}\bigl(\Phi^{+}_{A}(\varphi^{\prime})_{|\epsilon}\bigr)\bigr]

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\pi(\psi)\bigl[\,\widetilde{p}\bigl(\Phi^{+}_{\epsilon}(\varphi)\bigr)\bigr]=\pi(\psi)\bigl[\bar{\Phi}^{+}_{\epsilon}\bigl(\pi(\varphi)\bigr)\bigr]=\overline{\mathcal{R}}\bigl(\pi(\varphi)\otimes\pi(\psi)\bigr)

where the first equality is by definition of the specialization of a bilinear map (see §
2.3
), the second is by definition of
\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}
in (
91
), the third is by Lemma
4.16
(2), the fourth is by definition of
\displaystyle\Phi^{+}_{\epsilon}
, the fifth is by Lemma
4.17
and the last is by definition of
\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{R}}
.

2. By Prop.
4.14
(2) we have
\displaystyle\pi(\psi)=\varepsilon_{\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}}(\psi)\varepsilon
for
\displaystyle\psi\in\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon})
, where
\displaystyle\varepsilon
is the counit of
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
. Any co-R-matrix
\displaystyle\mathcal{R}:F\otimes F\to\Bbbk
on a Hopf algebra
\displaystyle F
satisfies
\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(1_{F}\otimes\text{-})=\mathcal{R}(\text{-}\otimes 1_{F})=\varepsilon_{F}
, and the previous item gives the result with
\displaystyle F=\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}
.
∎

5.
Quantum graph algebras at roots of unity

Recall from §
3.2
the definition of the graph algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
associated to a Hopf algebra endowed with morphisms
\displaystyle\Phi^{\pm}:H^{\circ}\to H^{\rm cop}
which are substitute for quasitriangularity. By a
quantum graph algebra
we mean a graph algebra obtained from a quantum group
\displaystyle U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
. Here we introduce the specialization at a root of unity of such an algebra and one of our main results is that, in some sense, it can be decomposed in two parts: the finite-dimensional part
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}})
and the “classical” part
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}\bigl(U(\mathfrak{g})\bigr)
.

5.1.
Quantum graph algebras and their specialization

Recall that
\displaystyle q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
is a formal variable such that
\displaystyle q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}^{D}=q
, where
\displaystyle D
is the smallest integer such that
\displaystyle DP\subset Q
, see §
2.1
.

For the sake of precision, we introduce notations which indicate extension of scalars from
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q)
to
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})
:

\mathcal{O}_{q}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}):=\mathcal{O}_{q}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}(q)}\mathbb{C}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}),\quad U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}):=U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}(q)}\mathbb{C}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})

where as usual
\displaystyle\Lambda
is a lattice such that
\displaystyle Q\subset\Lambda\subset P
.

By definition
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}
is a restricted dual for
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
based on type 1
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
-modules (§
4.2
). However, we noticed in (
71
) that
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})
naturally acts on
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
-modules provided scalars are extended to
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})
, and this yields a non-degenerate pairing
\displaystyle\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}:\mathcal{O}_{q}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})\times U^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}_{q}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})\to\mathbb{C}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})
. As a result we can make the following definition:

Definition 5.1
.

We let
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(\mathfrak{g})
be the
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})
-algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
for
\displaystyle H=U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})
as in §
3.2
, except that we use
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})
instead of
\displaystyle H^{\circ}
. Very often we will write
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
instead of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(\mathfrak{g})
.

Explicitly:

•

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
is
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})^{\otimes(2g+n)}
as a
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})
-vector space.

•

The algebra structure on
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
from §
3.2
is here governed by the co-R-matrix
\displaystyle\mathcal{R}:\mathcal{O}_{q}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})\otimes\mathcal{O}_{q}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})\to\mathbb{C}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})
defined by
\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(\varphi\otimes\psi)=\langle\psi,\Phi^{+}(\varphi)\rangle^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
with
\displaystyle\Phi^{+}
from (
76
). We have to make the definition of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
with
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
because
\displaystyle\Phi^{\pm}
takes values in it.

The algebras
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
are finitely generated and were shown to be Noetherian domains in
[
14
]
. When
\displaystyle g=0
it is possible to work over
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q)
because the appearances of the variable
\displaystyle q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
compensate each other
[
11
, Prop. 6.2]
.

The right coadjoint action of
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})
on
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})
is defined by (
21
); so we have

(122)

\textstyle\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h)(\varphi)=\sum_{(h),(\varphi)}\bigl\langle\varphi_{(1)},h_{(1)}\bigr\rangle^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}\,\bigl\langle\varphi_{(3)},S(h_{(2)})\bigr\rangle^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}\,\varphi_{(2)}.

for all
\displaystyle h\in U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})
and
\displaystyle\varphi\in\mathcal{O}_{q}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})
. Using the coproduct of
\displaystyle U_{q}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})
we have the diagonal action
\displaystyle\mathrm{coad}^{r}
on
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
, which makes it a
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})
-module-algebra (see (
22
)). Again due to (
71
), this action can be extended to
\displaystyle U^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}_{q}
. However the subalgebras of invariant elements (
23
) are the same under
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
and
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
. To avoid overweighted notations, we thus denote by
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{U_{q}}
this subalgebra of invariant elements.

Let
