\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}=\mathcal{O}_{A}\otimes_{A}A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
, which is the integral form of
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})
. We saw in §
4.4
that the co-R-matrix restricts to an
\displaystyle A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
-bilinear form
\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}:\mathcal{O}_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}\otimes_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}\mathcal{O}_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}\to A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
. As a result we can make the following definition:

Definition 5.2
.

We let
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}=\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}(\mathfrak{g})
be the
\displaystyle A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
-subalgebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}^{\otimes_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}(2g+n)}
in
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}
is a free
\displaystyle A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
-module because
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{A}
is a free
\displaystyle A
-module (§
4.4
) and extension of scalars preserves freeness as it commutes with direct sums. As already recalled just above, when
\displaystyle g=0
we can work over
\displaystyle A
instead of
\displaystyle A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
. We thus allow ourselves to use the notation
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,n}^{A}
, which matches with
[
11
,
12
]
. Of course,
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,n}^{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}=\mathcal{L}_{0,n}^{A}\otimes_{A}A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
.

The right coadjoint action
\displaystyle\mathrm{coad}^{r}:\mathcal{O}_{q}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})\otimes U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})\to\mathcal{O}_{q}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})
from (
122
) restricts to a right action
\displaystyle\mathrm{coad}^{r}:\mathcal{O}_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}\otimes_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}\Gamma_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}\to\mathcal{O}_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}
. Note that
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}
is stable under this action because it is a Hopf
\displaystyle A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
-algebra. We denote by
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\Gamma_{A}}
the
\displaystyle A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
-subalgebra of invariant elements under this action. It is an integral form of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{U_{q}}
, meaning that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\Gamma_{A}}\otimes_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}\mathbb{C}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})=\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{U_{q}}
. We note that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{U_{A}}=\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\Gamma_{A}}
, where
\displaystyle U_{A}\subset\Gamma_{A}
is the unrestricted integral form (§
4.3
, note that the choice of the lattice
\displaystyle\Lambda
has no effect here).

Recall that
\displaystyle\epsilon
is a root of unity of order
\displaystyle l
, subject to the assumptions (
92
), and that
\displaystyle\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
is chosen in (
93
) to be
\displaystyle\epsilon^{\overline{D}}
where
\displaystyle\overline{D}
is the inverse of
\displaystyle D
modulo
\displaystyle l
.

Definition 5.3
.

We let
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}=\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{g})
be the
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}
-algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}\otimes_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}\mathbb{C}_{\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}
, that is the specialization of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}
at
\displaystyle q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}=\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
.

Note that we write
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
instead of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}
because
\displaystyle\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
is defined in terms of
\displaystyle\epsilon
in (
93
), contrarily to the variable
\displaystyle q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
which realizes a genuine extension of
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q)
. As a
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}
-vector space
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{g})
is
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}(G)^{\otimes(2g+n)}
and the algebra structure is given by the formulas of §
3.2
, but using the specialized co-R-matrix
\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\epsilon}:\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}\to\mathbb{C}
(specialized in the sense of §
2.3
).

The right action
\displaystyle\mathrm{coad}^{r}:\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}\otimes_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}\Gamma_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}\to\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}
gives by specialization a right action of
\displaystyle\Gamma^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}_{\epsilon}
on
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
and we denote by
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\Gamma_{\epsilon}}
the subalgebra of elements which are invariant under it. Said differently, it is the specialization at
\displaystyle q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}=\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
of the integral form
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\Gamma_{A}}
. There is however another subalgebra of invariant elements which deserves interest:

(123)

\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}}=\bigl\{x\in\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}\,\big|\,\forall\,h\in u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}\subset\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}},\>\>\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h)(x)=\varepsilon(h)x\bigr\}.

Here again there is no effect if one replaces
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
with
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}
for any lattice
\displaystyle Q\subset\Lambda\subset P
. Of course,
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\Gamma_{\epsilon}}\subset\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}}
but the converse is not true. Another notation for
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}}
is
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{U_{\epsilon}}
because the inclusion
\displaystyle U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}\subset\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}
factorizes after specialization as
\displaystyle U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}\twoheadrightarrow u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}\subset\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}
, see (
99
) and (
115
). But beware that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{U_{\epsilon}}
is
not
the specialization of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{U_{A}}=\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\Gamma_{A}}
, so we prefer to use
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}}
to prevent confusion.

Finally, note that (
105
), and the isomorphism (i) below it, imply

(124)

\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\Gamma_{\epsilon}}=(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}})^{U(\mathfrak{g})}.

5.2.
Modified Alekseev morphism
\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi}_{g,n}

For notational convenience and readability, extension of scalars from
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q)
to
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})
is kept implicit in this subsection. For instance we write
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}
(resp.
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{A}
) instead of
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})
(resp.
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}
).

Let
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{q}=\mathcal{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
be the Heisenberg double of
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}(G)
; it is the
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})
-vector space
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}\otimes U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
with product such that
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}\otimes 1_{U_{q}}\cong\mathcal{O}_{q}
,
\displaystyle 1_{\mathcal{O}_{q}}\otimes U^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}_{q}\cong U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
are subalgebras and

(125)

\textstyle\forall\,a\in U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}},\>\>\forall\,\varphi\in\mathcal{O}_{q},\quad\varphi a=\varphi\otimes a,\quad a\varphi=\sum_{(a),(\varphi)}\langle\varphi_{(2)},a_{(1)}\rangle^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}\,\varphi_{(1)}a_{(2)}

with the pairing
\displaystyle\langle\text{-},\text{-}\rangle^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}:\mathcal{O}_{q}\times U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}\to\mathbb{C}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})
from §
4.2
. Thus an element in
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{q}
is a sum of elements
\displaystyle\varphi a
with
\displaystyle\varphi\in\mathcal{O}_{q}
and
\displaystyle a\in U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
.

Recall from (
29
) that the two maps
\displaystyle\Phi^{\pm}:\mathcal{O}_{q}\to U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
in (
76
) combine into a map
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}
. It can be written as follows (although
\displaystyle RR^{fl}\not\in U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}\otimes U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
, this formula makes sense by the same argument as for (
76
))

(126)

\begin{array}[]{rcll}\Phi_{0,1}:&\mathcal{O}_{q}&\longrightarrow&U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}\\
&\alpha&\longmapsto&(\alpha\otimes\mathrm{id})(RR^{fl})\end{array}

where
\displaystyle R^{fl}
means that tensorands are permuted. We denote by
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\prime}
the image of the map
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}
. From (
45
) we have elements
\displaystyle\widehat{h^{\prime}}
which exist for all
\displaystyle h^{\prime}\in U^{\prime}_{q}
. Our goal is to construct an extension
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
of
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
which for all
\displaystyle h\in U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
contains an element
\displaystyle\widehat{h}
satisfying suitable commutation relations. This will allow us to define a modified (compared to §
3.5
) Alekseev morphism
\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi}_{g,n}:\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(\mathfrak{g})\to\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})^{\otimes g}\otimes U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}(\mathfrak{g})^{\otimes n}
.

It is known that
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}
is an embedding of
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
-modules, where
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}
is endowed with the right coadjoint action
\displaystyle{\rm coad}^{r}
from (
21
) and
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
with the right adjoint action
\displaystyle{\rm ad}^{r}
from (
30
). Moreover
\displaystyle U^{\prime}_{q}:=\Phi_{0,1}(\mathcal{O}_{q})
equals
\displaystyle(U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{\rm lf}
, namely the (coideal) subalgebra of locally finite elements of
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
for the action
\displaystyle{\rm ad}^{r}
(
i.e.
, the elements of
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
whose orbit under the right adjoint action (
30
) of
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
is a finite-dimensional subspace). A proof of these properties can be found in
[
15
]
and
[
73
, Th. 3.113 and Prop. 3.116]
up to slightly different conventions. The relation between
\displaystyle(U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{\mathrm{lf}}
and
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
has been elucidated by Joseph–Letzter
[
47
]
. Let us introduce a few notations to explain it. Consider the multiplicative subset
\displaystyle\mathbb{T}_{2-}=\bigl\{K_{-2\lambda}\,\big|\,\lambda\in P_{+}\bigr\}
. It holds
\displaystyle\mathbb{T}_{2-}\subset(U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{\mathrm{lf}}
and
\displaystyle\mathbb{T}_{2-}
satisfies the Ore condition (obvious), so we have the localization
\displaystyle(U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{\mathrm{lf}}\mathbb{T}_{2-}^{-1}
. Consider moreover
\displaystyle\mathbb{T}=\bigl\{K_{\lambda}\,\big|\,\lambda\in P\bigr\}
and
\displaystyle\mathbb{T}_{2}=\bigl\{K_{2\lambda}\,\big|\,\lambda\in P\bigr\}
. Note that
\displaystyle\mathbb{T}/\mathbb{T}_{2}
is an abelian group of cardinal
\displaystyle 2^{m}
,
\displaystyle m=\mathrm{rank}(\mathfrak{g})
, and choose representatives
\displaystyle s_{1},\ldots,s_{2^{m}}
. Then
[
47
, §6.4]
asserts that
\displaystyle\textstyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{2^{m}}\bigl((U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{\mathrm{lf}}\mathbb{T}_{2-}^{-1}\bigr)s_{j}
. It is convenient to restate this as an isomorphism of algebras

(127)

U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}\cong(U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{\mathrm{lf}}\underset{\mathbb{C}(q)[\mathbb{T}_{2-}]}{\otimes}\mathbb{C}(q)[\mathbb{T}]

with product
\displaystyle(h^{\prime}\otimes K_{\lambda})(h^{\prime\prime}\otimes K_{\eta})=h^{\prime}\bigl(K_{\lambda}h^{\prime\prime}K_{\lambda}^{-1}\bigr)\otimes K_{\lambda+\eta}
on the right-hand side; note that
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q)[\mathbb{T}]
is just
\displaystyle U_{q}(\mathfrak{h})
. Of course all this remains true after extension of scalars to
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})
.

Since
\displaystyle K_{-2\lambda}\in U_{q}^{\prime}
, we have for all
\displaystyle\lambda\in P_{+}
an element
\displaystyle\widehat{K_{-2\lambda}}\in\mathcal{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
defined in (
45
). There is thus a natural right action of
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})[\mathbb{T}_{2-}]
on
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{q}
given by
\displaystyle x\cdot K_{2\lambda}=x\,\widehat{K_{2\lambda}}
. As a result we may consider the following algebra.

Definition 5.4
.

We let
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{q}(\mathfrak{g}):=\mathcal{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})\underset{\mathbb{C}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})[\mathbb{T}_{2-}]}{\otimes}\mathbb{C}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})[\mathbb{T}]
be the algebra with the associative product defined by the following rules (where
\displaystyle\otimes=\otimes_{\mathbb{C}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})[\mathbb{T}_{2}]}
):

•
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})\otimes K_{\boldsymbol{0}}
and
\displaystyle 1_{\mathcal{H}_{q}}\otimes\mathbb{C}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})[\mathbb{T}]
are subalgebras isomorphic to
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
and
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})[\mathbb{T}]
.

•
\displaystyle\bigl(\varphi a\otimes K_{\boldsymbol{0}}\bigr)\bigl(1_{\mathcal{H}_{q}}\otimes K_{\lambda}\bigr)=\varphi a\otimes K_{\lambda}
and
\displaystyle\bigl(1_{\mathcal{H}_{q}}\otimes K_{\lambda}\bigr)\bigl(\varphi a\otimes K_{\boldsymbol{0}}\bigr)=(\varphi\lhd K_{-\lambda})a\otimes K_{\lambda}
.

We often write
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{q}
instead of
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
. Given
\displaystyle h\in U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
, use (
127
) to decompose it as
\displaystyle h=\textstyle\sum_{j}h_{j}\otimes K_{\lambda_{j}}
with
\displaystyle h_{j}\in(U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{\mathrm{lf}}
and introduce the notation

(128)

\widehat{h}=\textstyle\sum_{j}\widehat{h_{j}}\otimes K_{\lambda_{j}}\in\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{q}

with
\displaystyle\widehat{h_{j}}
defined in (
45
); note in particular that
\displaystyle\varphi a\otimes_{\mathbb{C}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})[\mathbb{T}_{2}]}K_{\lambda}=\varphi a\,\widehat{K_{\lambda}}
. With these definitions it holds in
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
:

(129)

\forall\,h,g\in U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}},\>\>\forall\,\varphi x\in\mathcal{H}_{q},\quad\widehat{hg}=\widehat{h}\,\widehat{g},\quad\widehat{h}\,\varphi a=\sum_{(h)}\bigl(\varphi\lhd S^{-1}(h_{(2)})\bigr)a\,\widehat{h_{(1)}}.

The relations (
129
) are formally the same as (
50
), replacing
\displaystyle\widetilde{h}
by
\displaystyle\widehat{h}
. The modified Alekseev morphism is obtained by using elements
\displaystyle\widehat{h}
instead of
\displaystyle\widetilde{h}
. Namely, let
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{g,n}:U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}\to\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{q}^{\otimes g}\otimes(U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{\otimes n}
be the following morphism of
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})
-algebras

(130)

\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{g,n}(h)=\sum_{(h)}\widehat{h_{(1)}}\,h_{(2)}\otimes\ldots\otimes\widehat{h_{(2g-1)}}\,h_{(2g)}\otimes h_{(2g+1)}\otimes\ldots\otimes h_{(2g+n)}.

Define
\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi}_{g,n}:\mathcal{L}_{g,n}\to\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{q}^{\otimes g}\otimes(U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{\otimes n}
inductively by (compare with (
53
)–(
54
)):

(131)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\begin{split}\forall\,\varphi\in\mathcal{L}_{0,1},\>\>\forall\,w\in\mathcal{L}_{0,n},\quad\widehat{\Phi}_{0,n+1}(\varphi\otimes w)&=\sum_{[\varphi]}\Phi_{0,1}(\varphi_{[2]})\otimes\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{0,n}\bigl(\Phi^{+}(\varphi_{[1]})\bigr)\widehat{\Phi}_{0,n}(w),\\
\forall\,x\in\mathcal{L}_{1,0},\>\>\forall\,w\in\mathcal{L}_{g,n},\quad\widehat{\Phi}_{g+1,n}(x\otimes w)&=\sum_{[x]}\Phi_{1,0}(x_{[2]})\otimes\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{g,n}\bigl(\Phi^{+}(x_{[1]})\bigr)\widehat{\Phi}_{g,n}(w)\end{split}

using the left coaction which is dual to
\displaystyle\mathrm{coad}^{r}
, see (
24
). The base cases are
\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi}_{0,1}=\Phi_{0,1}:\mathcal{L}_{0,1}\to U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
defined in (
29
) and
\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi}_{1,0}=\Phi_{1,0}:\mathcal{L}_{1,0}\to\mathcal{H}_{q}\subset\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{q}
defined in (
43
).

The algebra morphism
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{g,n}
yields a right action of
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
on
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{q}
:

\displaystyle\displaystyle(\varphi a\otimes_{\mathbb{C}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})[\mathbb{T}_{2}]}K_{\lambda})\cdot h

\displaystyle\displaystyle:=\sum_{(h)}\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{1,0}\bigl(S(h_{(1)})\bigr)\,(\varphi a\otimes_{\mathbb{C}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})[\mathbb{T}_{2}]}K_{\lambda})\,\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{1,0}(h_{(2)})

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\sum_{(h)}\bigl(S(h_{(2)})\rhd\varphi\lhd h_{(3)}\bigr)S(h_{(1)})ah_{(6)}\,\widehat{S(h_{(4)})K_{\lambda}h_{(5)}}

where the second equality is obtained from (
129
) by a computation completely analogous to (
57
). It readily endows
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{q}
with the structure of a right
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
-module-algebra. By iterated coproduct
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}\to(U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{\otimes(g+n)}
we get a right
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
-module-algebra structure on
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{q}^{\otimes g}\otimes(U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{\otimes n}
, using the right adjoint action (
30
) on the
\displaystyle(U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{\otimes n}
part.

Proposition 5.5
.

In the algebra
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{q}^{\otimes g}\otimes(U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{\otimes n}
endowed with the usual tensor-wise product it holds

\forall\,h\in U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}},\>\>\forall\,x\in\mathcal{L}_{g,n},\quad\widehat{\Phi}_{g,n}\bigl(\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h)(x)\bigr)=\sum_{(h)}\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{g,n}\bigl(S(h_{(1)})\bigr)\,\widehat{\Phi}_{g,n}(x)\,\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{g,n}(h_{(2)}).

so that
\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi}_{g,n}
is
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
-linear. Moreover
\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi}_{g,n}
is an algebra morphism which is injective.

Proof.

The formula and the algebra morphism claim are established by
exactly
the same computations than the ones used to prove Prop.
3.10
. The only changes are typographical:
\displaystyle\Phi_{g,n}
(resp.
\displaystyle\mathsf{D}_{g,n}
) must be replaced by
\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi}_{g,n}
(resp.
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{g,n}
) and the “tilda elements”
\displaystyle\widetilde{x}
must be replaced by the “hat elements”
\displaystyle\widehat{x}
. For the
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
-linearity claim, it suffices to note that the action of
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
on
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{q}^{\otimes g}\otimes(U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{\otimes n}
can be written as
\displaystyle w\cdot h=\textstyle\sum_{(h)}\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{g,n}\bigl(S(h_{(1)})\bigr)\,w\,\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{g,n}(h_{(2)})
. For the injectivity, we know that
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}
and
\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}
are injective
[
14
, Th. 3.13]
. The same arguments as in Lemma
3.11
(with only typographical changes) allow us to conclude that
\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi}_{g,n}
is injective.
∎

Remark 5.6
.

By (
128
) and Lemma
3.8
the representation
\displaystyle\rho:\mathcal{H}_{q}\to\mathrm{End}_{\mathbb{C}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})}(\mathcal{O}_{q})
from (
42
) satisfies
\displaystyle\widehat{\rho}(h^{\prime})(\psi)=\psi\lhd S^{-1}(h^{\prime})
for all
\displaystyle h^{\prime}\in(U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{\rm lf}
. Then
\displaystyle\rho
can be extended to
\displaystyle\widehat{\rho}:\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{q}\to\mathrm{End}_{\mathbb{C}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})}(\mathcal{O}_{q})
simply by
\displaystyle\widehat{\rho}\bigl(\widehat{K_{\lambda}}\bigr)(\psi)=\psi\lhd K_{-\lambda}
for all
\displaystyle\lambda\in P
. Another extension is
\displaystyle\widetilde{\rho}:\mathcal{HH}_{q}\to\mathrm{End}_{\mathbb{C}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})}(\mathcal{O}_{q})
defined in general in (
58
), where
\displaystyle\mathcal{HH}_{q}=\mathcal{HH}(\mathcal{O}_{q})=\mathcal{H}_{q}\otimes U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
is the two-sided Heisenberg double (§
3.5
). We have a commutative diagram

\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathcal{L}_{g,n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\widehat{\Phi}_{g,n}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\Phi_{g,n}}
\displaystyle\textstyle{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{q}^{\otimes g}\otimes(U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{\otimes n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\widehat{\rho}^{\otimes g}\otimes\mathrm{id}^{\otimes n}}
\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathcal{HH}_{q}^{\otimes g}\otimes(U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{\otimes n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\rho}^{\otimes g}\otimes\mathrm{id}^{\otimes n}}
\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathrm{End}_{\mathbb{C}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})}(\mathcal{O}_{q})^{\otimes g}\otimes(U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{\otimes n}}

meaning that the usual Alekseev morphism
\displaystyle\Phi_{g,n}
from §
3.5
and its modified version
\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi}_{g,n}
yield the same families of representations.

The constructions above admit integral versions,
i.e.
over
\displaystyle A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}=\mathbb{C}[q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}^{\pm 1}]
. Indeed, using the fact that
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{A}
is a Hopf
\displaystyle A
-algebra and the integral versions
\displaystyle\Phi^{\pm}_{A}
of
\displaystyle\Phi^{\pm}
(§
4.4
), we get an integral version
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}^{A}:\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{A}\to U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
of
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}
. The following result is the analog over
\displaystyle A
of the equality (
127
) from
[
47
, §6.4]
.

Lemma 5.7
.

Denote by
\displaystyle U^{\prime}_{A}
the image of
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}^{A}
and
\displaystyle U_{A}^{\mathrm{lf}}:=U_{q}^{\mathrm{lf}}\cap U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
. Given a family
\displaystyle(s_{j})
of representatives for
\displaystyle\mathbb{T}/\mathbb{T}_{2}
, we have

\textstyle U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{2^{m}}\bigl(U^{\prime}_{A}\mathbb{T}_{2-}^{-1}\bigr)s_{j}=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{2^{m}}\bigl(U_{A}^{\rm lf}\mathbb{T}_{2-}^{-1}\bigr)s_{j}.

This can also be written as
\displaystyle U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}=U^{\prime}_{A}\otimes_{A[\mathbb{T}_{2-}]}A[\mathbb{T}]
.

Proof.

Recall the pivotal element
\displaystyle\ell=K_{2\rho}
. We have
\displaystyle l^{-1}\in U^{\prime}_{A}
but
\displaystyle l\not\in U^{\prime}_{A}
. Consider the subalgebra
\displaystyle U_{A}^{\prime}[\ell]\subset U_{A}
generated by
\displaystyle U_{A}^{\prime}
and
\displaystyle\ell
, which can also be seen as the localization of
\displaystyle U_{A}^{\prime}
at
\displaystyle\ell^{-1}
. We claim that
\displaystyle U^{\prime}_{A}[\ell]=\mathbb{T}_{2-}^{-1}U_{A}^{\prime}\mathbb{T}_{2-}^{-1}
. Indeed, we have
\displaystyle K_{-2\lambda}\in U_{A}^{\prime}
for all
\displaystyle\lambda\in P_{+}

[
12
, Prop. 2.24]
so in particular
\displaystyle L_{i}^{-2}\in U_{A}^{\prime}
for all
\displaystyle 1\leq i\leq m
. Since
\displaystyle\ell=\textstyle\prod_{i=1}^{m}L_{i}^{2}
we deduce that
\displaystyle L_{i}^{2}\in U_{A}^{\prime}[\ell]
for all
\displaystyle i
. Therefore
\displaystyle\mathbb{T}_{2-}^{-1}\subset U_{A}^{\prime}[\ell]
, whence the claim.
Now we use that there is an element
\displaystyle d\in\mathcal{L}^{A}_{0,1}
such that
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}(d)=\ell^{-1}
. The multiplicative subset

\displaystyle\bigl\{d^{n}\,\big|\,n\in\mathbb{N}\bigr\}\subset\mathcal{L}^{A}_{0,1}
satisfies the Ore condition
[
12
, Cor. 2.23]
, so we denote by
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{A}[d^{-1}]
the resulting localization. It is known from
[
12
, Prop. 2.24]
that
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}\bigl(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{A}[d^{-1}]\bigr)=U_{A}^{\mathrm{lf}}\mathbb{T}_{2-}^{-1}
. As a result

U_{A}^{\mathrm{lf}}\mathbb{T}_{2-}^{-1}=\Phi_{0,1}\bigl(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{A}[d^{-1}]\bigr)=\Phi_{0,1}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{A})\bigl[\Phi_{0,1}(d^{-1})\bigr]=U^{\prime}_{A}[\ell]=U^{\prime}_{A}\mathbb{T}_{2-}^{-1}.

Finally by
[
12
, Prop. 2.24]
we have
\displaystyle\textstyle U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{2^{m}}\bigl(U_{A}^{\mathrm{lf}}\,\mathbb{T}_{2-}^{-1}\bigr)s_{j}
, thus proving the lemma.
∎

Remark 5.8
.

1. It follows from Cor.
A.5
that
\displaystyle U_{A}^{\prime}=U_{A}^{\rm lf}
. This fact gives another proof of Lemma
5.7
(but less elementary as it relies on the quantum Killing form).

2. Note that, because
\displaystyle L_{i}^{-2}\in U_{A}^{\prime}
for all
\displaystyle 1\leq i\leq m
, Lemma
5.7
implies that the centralizer of
\displaystyle U_{A}^{\prime}
in
\displaystyle U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
is equal to
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})
. In particular,
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(U_{A}^{\prime})\subset\mathcal{Z}(U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})
.

Now consider the
\displaystyle A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
-module
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{A}=\mathcal{O}_{A}\otimes_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}\subset\mathcal{H}_{q}
, which is an integral form of
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{q}
. By the existence of
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}^{A}
, for all
\displaystyle h^{\prime}\in U^{\prime}_{A}\subset U^{\prime}_{q}
, the element
\displaystyle\widehat{h^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{H}_{q}
defined in (
45
) actually lies in
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{A}
. Since
\displaystyle K_{-2\lambda}\in U^{\prime}_{A}
for all
\displaystyle\lambda\in P_{+}

[
12
, Prop. 2.24]
we have
\displaystyle\widehat{K_{-2\lambda}}\in\mathcal{H}_{A}
and thus it makes sense to define

\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{A}:=\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}[\mathbb{T}_{2-}]}A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}[\mathbb{T}]

which is an integral version of
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{q}
in (
5.4
). Then by Lemma
5.7
for all
\displaystyle h\in U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
there is an element
\displaystyle\widehat{h}\in\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{A}
defined as in (
128
). It follows that restriction of (
130
) to
\displaystyle U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
gives an integral version
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{g,n}^{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}:U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}\to\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{A}^{\otimes g}\otimes(U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{\otimes n}
.

When restricted to
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}\subset\mathcal{L}_{0,1}
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}^{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}\subset\mathcal{L}_{1,0}
, the morphisms
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}
and
\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}
take values in
\displaystyle U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{A}
. We deduce by induction from the definition of
\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi}_{g,n}
that it has an integral version
\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi}_{g,n}^{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}:\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}\to\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{A}^{\otimes g}\otimes(U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{\otimes n}
; it is obviously injective as restriction of
\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi}_{g,n}
(Prop.
5.5
).

Let
\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}:\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}\to\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{\epsilon}^{\otimes g}\otimes(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{\otimes n}
be the specialization of
\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi}_{g,n}^{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}
at the root of unity
\displaystyle\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
(again recall from (
93
) that
\displaystyle\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
depends on
\displaystyle\epsilon
, whence the notation).

Proposition 5.9
.

The morphism of algebras
\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
is injective.

Proof.

By the same argument as in Lemma
3.11
, it suffices to check the cases
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}^{\epsilon}
and
\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}^{\epsilon}
. Injectivity of
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}^{\epsilon}
is proved in
[
12
, Cor. 2.25]
(eventually the dependence of
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}^{\epsilon}
on
\displaystyle\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
reduces to
\displaystyle\epsilon
). For
\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}^{\epsilon}
, the proof of
[
14
, Th. 3.13]
for
\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}
can be adapted verbatim, by using properly defined weight spaces
{}_{\lambda}(\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon})_{\mu}
of
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}
. The details are given in App.
C
.
∎

Remark 5.10
.

The faithful representation
\displaystyle\rho:\mathcal{H}_{q}\to\mathrm{End}_{\mathbb{C}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})}(\mathcal{O}_{q})
specializes to a representation
\displaystyle\rho_{\epsilon}:\mathcal{H}_{\epsilon}\to\mathrm{End}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon})
which is no longer faithful: if
\displaystyle h\in\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}})
then
\displaystyle\rho_{\epsilon}(h)(\varphi)=\textstyle\sum_{(h)}\langle\varphi_{(1)},h\rangle_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}\,\varphi_{(2)}=\varepsilon(h)\varphi
by (
115
). This fact implies that
\displaystyle\rho_{\epsilon}
factorizes through
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}\,\otimes\,u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
, where
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
is the small quantum group defined in §
4.5
. Also the morphism
\displaystyle F_{g,n}
defined in (
59
) is no longer injective when
\displaystyle q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
is specialized to
\displaystyle\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
.

5.3.
Quantum moment map and invariant elements

Recall that
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}:\mathcal{L}_{0,1}\to U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
is injective and we denote its image by
\displaystyle U^{\prime}_{q}
. Then as in (
37
) we have the quantum moment map

\mu_{g,n}:U_{q}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt\Phi_{0,1}^{-1}\hskip 8.19447pt}\mathcal{L}_{0,1}\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}\hskip 8.19447pt}\mathcal{L}_{g,n}

which is a morphism of
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})
-algebras.

Proposition 5.11
.

\displaystyle\mu_{g,n}
is injective.

Proof.

The morphism
\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}
is injective
[
14
, Th. 3.13]
, so by Cor.
3.15
it is enough to show that
\displaystyle\mathrm{coad}^{r}:U_{q}\to{\rm End}_{\mathbb{C}(q)}(\mathcal{O}_{q})
is injective. Given an
\displaystyle U_{q}
-module
\displaystyle V
, denote by
\displaystyle\mathrm{Ann}(V)
the set of elements
\displaystyle h\in U_{q}
such that
\displaystyle h.V=\{0\}
. Since
\displaystyle\bigl(\mathcal{O}_{q},\mathrm{coad}^{r}\bigr)=\textstyle\bigoplus_{\lambda\in P_{+}}V_{\lambda}\otimes V_{\lambda}^{*}
as
\displaystyle U_{q}
-modules, we have to prove that
\displaystyle\textstyle\bigcap_{\lambda\in P_{+}}\mathrm{Ann}(V_{\lambda}\otimes V_{\lambda}^{*})=\{0\}
. Given
\displaystyle\mu\in P_{+}
, the vector space
\displaystyle{\rm Hom}_{U_{q}}(V_{\lambda}\otimes V_{\lambda}^{*},V_{\mu})\cong{\rm Hom}_{U_{q}}(V_{\lambda},V_{\mu}\otimes V_{\lambda})
is non-zero only if
\displaystyle\mu\in Q_{+}
. It is shown in
[
67
, Prop. 17 and Def. 5]
that for generic
\displaystyle\lambda
relative to
\displaystyle\mu
, this space is isomorphic to the
\displaystyle 0
(
\displaystyle=\lambda-\lambda
) weight subspace
\displaystyle V_{\mu}^{0}
of
\displaystyle V_{\mu}
. Because
\displaystyle\mu\in Q_{+}
, we have
\displaystyle V_{\mu}^{0}\neq\{0\}
. Therefore, for every
\displaystyle\mu\in Q_{+}
there exists
\displaystyle\lambda\in P_{+}
such that
\displaystyle{\rm Hom}_{U_{q}}(V_{\lambda}\otimes V_{\lambda}^{*},V_{\mu})\neq\{0\}
. It follows that, if
\displaystyle\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h)=0
then
\displaystyle h\in\textstyle\bigcap_{\mu\in Q_{+}}\mathrm{Ann}(V_{\mu})
. By
[
46
, Lem. 7.1.9]
, if we prove that
\displaystyle Q_{+}
is Zariski dense in
\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}^{*}
then
\displaystyle\textstyle\bigcap_{\mu\in Q_{+}}\mathrm{Ann}(V_{\mu})=\{0\}
. Now, given
\displaystyle x\in U_{q}(\mathfrak{h})
, identified to
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q)[\mathfrak{h}^{*}]
in the usual way, if
\displaystyle x(\alpha)=0
for every
\displaystyle\alpha\in Q_{+}
, then
\displaystyle x=0
, which implies that
\displaystyle Q_{+}
is Zariski dense in
\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}^{*}
. This follows from the following basic fact: if
\displaystyle K
is a field and
\displaystyle P\in K[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
vanishes on a set
\displaystyle A_{1}\times\ldots\times A_{n}\subset K^{n}
with all
\displaystyle A_{i}
infinite, then
\displaystyle P=0
. Applying this to
\displaystyle K=\mathbb{C}(q)
and
\displaystyle A_{i}=\mathbb{N}\alpha_{i}
, this concludes the proof.
∎

The following result is the reason why we have introduced the modified Alekseev morphism
\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi}_{g,n}
in §
5.2
; compare with Remark
3.12
. Recall that
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{g,n}
is defined in (
130
).

Proposition 5.12
.

It holds
\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi}_{g,n}\circ\mu_{g,n}(h^{\prime})=\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{g,n}(h^{\prime})
for all
\displaystyle h^{\prime}\in U_{q}^{\prime}
. In other words we have a commutative diagram

\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathcal{L}_{0,1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\Phi_{0,1}}
\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathcal{L}_{g,n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\widehat{\Phi}_{g,n}}
\displaystyle\textstyle{U^{\prime}_{q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\mu_{g,n}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{g,n}}
\displaystyle\textstyle{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{q}^{\otimes g}\otimes(U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{\otimes n}}

Proof.

For
\displaystyle g=0
this is proven in
[
11
, Prop. 6.18]
; note that in this case
\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi}_{0,n}=\Phi_{0,n}
and
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{0,n}
is the iterated coproduct
\displaystyle\Delta^{(n)}
. For
\displaystyle(g,n)=(1,0)
we have
\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi}_{1,0}(\varphi)=\Phi_{1,0}(\varphi)
for all
\displaystyle\varphi\in\mathcal{L}_{0,1}
, and thus the claim is proved by Prop.
3.9
. It remains to do induction on
\displaystyle g
. For all
\displaystyle\varphi\in\mathcal{L}_{0,1}
and with implicit summation for the coproduct we have

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bigl(\widehat{\Phi}_{g+1,n}\circ\mathfrak{d}_{g+1,n})(\varphi)=\widehat{\Phi}_{g+1,n}\bigl(\mathfrak{d}_{1,0}(\varphi_{(1)})\otimes\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}(\varphi_{(2)})\bigr)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi}_{1,0}\bigl(\mathfrak{d}_{1,0}(\varphi_{(1)})_{[2]}\bigr)\otimes\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{g,n}\bigl(\Phi^{+}\bigl(\mathfrak{d}_{1,0}(\varphi_{(1)})_{[1]}\bigr)\bigr)\widehat{\Phi}_{g,n}\bigl(\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}(\varphi_{(2)})\bigr)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi}_{1,0}\bigl(\mathfrak{d}_{1,0}(\varphi_{(2)})\bigr)\otimes\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{g,n}\bigl(\Phi^{+}\bigl(S(\varphi_{(1)})\star\varphi_{(3)}\bigr)\bigr)\widehat{\Phi}_{g,n}\bigl(\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}(\varphi_{(4)})\bigr)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{1,0}\bigl(\Phi_{0,1}(\varphi_{(2)})\bigr)\otimes\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{g,n}\bigl[\Phi^{+}\bigl(\varphi_{(1)}\star S(\varphi_{(3)})\bigr)\Phi_{0,1}(\varphi_{(4)})\bigr]

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{1,0}\bigl(\Phi_{0,1}(\varphi_{(2)})\bigr)\otimes\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{g,n}\bigl[\Phi^{+}(\varphi_{(1)})\Phi^{-}(S(\varphi_{(3)}))\bigr]

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{1,0}\bigl(\Phi_{0,1}(\varphi)_{(1)}\bigr)\otimes\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{g,n}\bigl(\Phi_{0,1}(\varphi)_{(2)})\bigr)=\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{g+1,n}\bigl(\Phi_{0,1}(\varphi)\bigr)

where the first equality is by definition of
\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{g+1,n}
, the second is by (
131
), the third uses that
\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{1,0}
is a morphism of
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
-modules
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}\to\mathcal{L}_{1,0}
(see (
36
)) hence it is a morphism of left
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}
-comodules meaning that
\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{1,0}(\alpha)_{[1]}\otimes\mathfrak{d}_{1,0}(\alpha)_{[2]}=\bigl(\alpha_{(1)}\star S(\alpha_{(3)})\bigr)\otimes\mathfrak{d}_{1,0}(\alpha_{(2)})
by (
24
), the fourth uses the induction hypothesis, the fifth uses the definition of
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}
in (
29
) and the fact that
\displaystyle\Phi^{+}
is an algebra morphism to apply the antipode axiom, the sixth is by (
38
) and the last is by definition of
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{g+1,n}
.
∎

The restriction of
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}
to the
\displaystyle A
-subalgebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{A}
gives the integral version
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}^{A}:\mathcal{O}_{A}\hookrightarrow U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
. Similarly,
\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}
defined in §
3.3
has an integral version
\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}^{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}:\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}\to\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}
because
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{A}
is a Hopf
\displaystyle A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
-algebra and
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}^{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}
is an
\displaystyle A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
-algebra (this last argument is for the “
\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{1,0}
part” of the definition). Hence we have the integral version of the quantum moment map

\mu_{g,n}^{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}:U_{A}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt(\Phi_{0,1}^{A})^{-1}\hskip 8.19447pt}\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}^{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}\hskip 8.19447pt}\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}

where
\displaystyle U^{\prime}_{A}
is the image of
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}^{A}
. We denote its specialization by
\displaystyle U_{\epsilon}^{\prime}
, and let
\displaystyle\mu_{g,n}^{\epsilon}:U^{\prime}_{\epsilon}\to\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
be the specialization of
\displaystyle\mu_{g,n}^{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}
.

Theorem 5.13
.

1. The subalgebra of
\displaystyle\mathrm{coad}^{r}
-invariant elements
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{U_{q}}
is the centralizer of
\displaystyle\mu_{g,n}(U_{q}^{\prime})
in
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
.

2. The subalgebra of
\displaystyle\mathrm{coad}^{r}
-invariant elements
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{U_{A}}
is the centralizer of
\displaystyle\mu_{g,n}^{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}(U_{A}^{\prime})
in
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}
.

3. The subalgebra of
\displaystyle\mathrm{coad}^{r}
-invariant elements
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}}
under the action of
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}\subset\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
is the centralizer of
\displaystyle\mu_{g,n}^{\epsilon}(U_{\epsilon}^{\prime})
in
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
.

In particular note that item 3 implies:

Corollary 5.14
.

We have
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\subset\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}})
.

Proof.

The proofs of the three items are completely similar. We note first thanks to the QMM property (
39
) that there is an obvious inclusion

\bigl\{\text{invariant elements for }\mathrm{coad}^{r}\bigr\}\subset\bigl\{\text{elements commuting with }\mu_{g,n}(h^{\prime})\text{ for all }h^{\prime}\in H^{\prime}\bigr\}

where
\displaystyle H
means either
\displaystyle U_{q}
,
\displaystyle U_{A}
or
\displaystyle U_{\epsilon}
. In the root of unity case, the appearance of
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}
instead of
\displaystyle U_{\epsilon}
is due to the degeneracy explained after (
123
).

The converse inclusion, much harder, relies on the same arguments in all three cases; we discuss it in the case of item 3. Let
\displaystyle x\in\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
such that
\displaystyle x\mu_{g,n}^{\epsilon}(h^{\prime})=\mu_{g,n}^{\epsilon}(h^{\prime})x
for all
\displaystyle h^{\prime}\in U^{\prime}_{\epsilon}
. We consider
\displaystyle y=\widehat{\Phi}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}(x)\in\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{\epsilon}^{\otimes g}\otimes(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{\otimes n}
, which by Prop.
5.12
satisfies
\displaystyle y\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}(h^{\prime})=\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}(h^{\prime})y
for all
\displaystyle h^{\prime}\in U^{\prime}_{\epsilon}
, where
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
is the specialization of the integral version
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathsf{D}}^{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}_{g,n}
obtained after Lemma
5.7
. The essential difference between
\displaystyle\mu_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
and
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
is that
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
is defined on the whole
\displaystyle U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
and not just on
\displaystyle U^{\prime}_{\epsilon}
. It is known
[
12
, Proof of Prop. 2.24]
that

(132)

K_{-2\lambda}\in U^{\prime}_{\epsilon},\quad L_{i}^{-2}E_{i}\in U^{\prime}_{\epsilon},\quad F_{i}K_{i}L_{i}^{-2}\in U^{\prime}_{\epsilon}\qquad(\forall\,\lambda\in P_{+},\>\>\forall\,1\leq i\leq m).

By assumption
\displaystyle y
commutes with the images of these elements through
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
. Let us prove that
\displaystyle y
actually commutes with
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}(K_{\lambda})=\bigl(\widehat{K_{\lambda}}\,K_{\lambda}\bigr)^{\otimes g}\otimes K_{\lambda}^{\otimes n}
for all
\displaystyle\lambda\in P
. As an
\displaystyle A
-module,
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{A}=\mathcal{O}_{A}\otimes U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
. Since
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{A}
has an
\displaystyle A
-basis
\displaystyle(\varphi_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
of weight vectors for the coadjoint action
[
12
, Th. 2.8]
and
\displaystyle U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
has an
\displaystyle A
-basis
\displaystyle(x_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{J}}
of weight vectors for the adjoint action, we have the
\displaystyle A
-basis
\displaystyle(\varphi_{i}x_{j})_{i,j}
which satisfies

\forall\,\lambda\in P,\quad\widehat{K_{\lambda}}K_{\lambda}\varphi_{i}x_{j}=\bigl(K_{\lambda}\rhd\varphi_{i}\lhd K_{-\lambda}\bigr)K_{\lambda}x_{j}K_{\lambda}^{-1}\,\widehat{K_{\lambda}}K_{\lambda}=q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}^{D(\lambda,w_{i}+w_{j})}\varphi_{i}x_{j}\widehat{K_{\lambda}}K_{\lambda}

where
\displaystyle w_{i},w_{j}\in P
are the weights of
\displaystyle\varphi_{i}
,
\displaystyle x_{j}
. It follows that
\displaystyle\mathcal{F}=\bigl(\varphi_{i}x_{j}\otimes_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}[\mathbb{T}_{2-}]}K_{2\mu}s_{k}\bigr)_{\mu\in P_{+},i,j,k}
is an
\displaystyle A
-basis of
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{A}
whose elements
\displaystyle q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
-commute with
\displaystyle\widehat{K_{\lambda}}K_{\lambda}
for all
\displaystyle\lambda
, where we recall that
\displaystyle(s_{k})_{k}
is a family of representatives for
\displaystyle\mathbb{T}/\mathbb{T}_{2}
. Then
\displaystyle\mathcal{B}:=\mathcal{F}^{\otimes g}\otimes\bigl[(x_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\bigr]^{\otimes n}
is a basis of
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{A}^{\otimes g}\otimes(U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{\otimes n}
whose elements
\displaystyle q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
-commute with
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathsf{D}}^{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}_{g,n}(K_{\lambda})
for all
\displaystyle\lambda
. The same fact remains true after specialization at
\displaystyle\epsilon
, with basis denoted by
\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon}
. Assume that
\displaystyle b\in\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon}
satisfies
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathsf{D}}^{\epsilon}_{g,n}(K_{2\lambda})\,b\,\widehat{\mathsf{D}}^{\epsilon}_{g,n}(K_{-2\lambda})=b
for all
\displaystyle\lambda\in P_{+}
. By definition of
\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon}
, there exists
\displaystyle\mu\in P
such that
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathsf{D}}^{\epsilon}_{g,n}(K_{\lambda})\,b\,\widehat{\mathsf{D}}^{\epsilon}_{g,n}(K_{-\lambda})=\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}^{D(\lambda,\mu)}b
for all
\displaystyle\lambda
. It thus satisfies
\displaystyle 2D(\lambda,\mu)\equiv 0\pmod{l}
. Since
\displaystyle\mathrm{gcd}(D,l)=\mathrm{gcd}(2,l)=1
by assumption on
\displaystyle\epsilon
, it holds
\displaystyle(\lambda,\mu)\equiv 0\pmod{l}
and thus
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathsf{D}}(K_{\lambda})
commutes with
\displaystyle b
for all
\displaystyle\lambda
. The conclusion is that if an element in
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{\epsilon}^{\otimes g}\otimes(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{\otimes n}
commutes with
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathsf{D}}^{\epsilon}_{g,n}(K_{-2\lambda})
for all
\displaystyle\lambda\in P_{+}
then it commutes with
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathsf{D}}^{\epsilon}_{g,n}(K_{\lambda})
for all
\displaystyle\lambda\in P
. We deduce from (
132
) that
\displaystyle y=\widehat{\Phi}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}(x)
commutes with
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}(K_{\lambda})
,
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathsf{D}}^{\epsilon}_{g,n}(E_{i})
and
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathsf{D}}^{\epsilon}_{g,n}(F_{i})
for all
\displaystyle\lambda
and all
\displaystyle i
. Hence
\displaystyle y
commutes pointwise with the whole
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathsf{D}}^{\epsilon}_{g,n}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})
. For all
\displaystyle h\in U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
, Proposition
5.5
thus gives

\widehat{\Phi}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}\bigl(\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h)(x)\bigr)=\sum_{(h)}\widehat{\mathsf{D}}^{\epsilon}_{g,n}\bigl(S(h_{(1)})\bigr)\,y\,\widehat{\mathsf{D}}^{\epsilon}_{g,n}(h_{(2)})=\varepsilon(h)y=\widehat{\Phi}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}\bigl(\varepsilon(h)x\bigr).

Since
\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
is injective (Prop.
5.9
) we conclude that
\displaystyle x
is invariant under
\displaystyle\mathrm{coad}^{r}
.
∎

Remark 5.15
.

1. The centralizer of
\displaystyle\mu_{g,n}(U_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}^{\prime})
is the same thing as the centralizer of
\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}^{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}})
, with
\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}^{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}
defined before Theorem
5.13
. In particular, for
\displaystyle g=0
,
\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{0,n}:\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}\to\mathcal{L}_{0,n}^{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}
is the iterated coproduct used in
[
11
, §6.5]
and Th.
5.13
recovers
[
11
, Prop. 6.19]
.

2. Note that the difficulty in proving Th.
5.13
is due to the fact that
\displaystyle\mu_{g,n}
is not defined on the whole
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
but only on
\displaystyle U^{\prime}_{q}
. Otherwise it would suffice to rewrite (
39
) as
\displaystyle\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h)(x)=\textstyle\sum_{(h)}\mu_{g,n}(S(h_{(1)}))x\mu_{g,n}(h_{(2)})
to conclude that elements in the centralizer of
\displaystyle\mu_{g,n}
are invariant. But this equality does not make sense because
\displaystyle\Delta(U_{q}^{\prime})\subset U_{q}^{\prime}\otimes U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
and
\displaystyle S(U^{\prime}_{q})\not\subset U^{\prime}_{q}
. Fortunately this equality makes sense through
\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi}_{g,n}
with
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{g,n}
instead of
\displaystyle\mu_{g,n}
, which is the main idea of our proof (and the reason why we had to introduce
\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi}_{g,n}
instead of using
\displaystyle\Phi_{g,n}
).

5.4.
Structure results for
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}

We continue to make the assumptions (
92
) and (
93
) for the root of unity
\displaystyle\epsilon
. Recall that
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon})\subset\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}
is the image of the morphism of Hopf algebras
\displaystyle\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}^{*}_{\epsilon}:\mathcal{O}(G)\to\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}(G)
which is dual to the Frobenius morphism
\displaystyle\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}:\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}(\mathfrak{g})\to U(\mathfrak{g})
; see §
4.6
and §
4.7
. Since
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
is
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}^{\otimes(2g+n)}
as a
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}
-vector space, the following definition makes sense.

Definition 5.16
.

We let
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})=\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon})^{\otimes(2g+n)}\subset\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
.

The vector space
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}
is a
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}
-algebra, as specialization of the
\displaystyle A
-algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{A}
. Hence
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}^{\otimes(2g+n)}
can be endowed with the usual structure on a tensor product of algebras; we denote this product by
\displaystyle x\star y
for all
\displaystyle x,y\in\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}^{\otimes(2g+n)}
. But
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}^{\otimes(2g+n)}
has also the product of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
, which we denote by
\displaystyle xy
.

Proposition 5.17
.

For all
\displaystyle x\in\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})
and
\displaystyle y\in\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
we have
\displaystyle xy=yx=x\star y
.

It follows that:

(1)
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})
is a central subalgebra of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
, isomorphic to
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(G)^{\otimes(2g+n)}
through
\displaystyle(\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}^{*})^{\otimes(2g+n)}
. In particular
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})
is a Noetherian ring.

(2) As
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})
-modules,
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}^{\otimes(2g+n)}
coincide.

(3)
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
is a free
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})
-module of rank
\displaystyle l^{(2g+n)\dim(\mathfrak{g})}
and is a Noetherian ring, where
\displaystyle l
is the order of the root of unity
\displaystyle\epsilon
. In particular
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})
is a Noetherian ring.

Remark 5.18
.

It follows from item 3 that any subalgebra of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
containing
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})
is a finitely generated
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})
-submodule, and hence a Noetherian ring (since
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})
is a Noetherian ring). We will apply this to
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}}
and its center
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}})
in the proof of Th.
6.4
.

Proof.

First we have
\displaystyle x\star y=y\star x
because
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon})\subset\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon})
. Let us show that
\displaystyle xy=x\star y
. Consider the embeddings
\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{b_{i}},\mathfrak{i}_{a_{i}},\mathfrak{i}_{m_{g+j}}:\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}\to\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}^{\otimes(2g+n)}
as in (
16
), and from which the product of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
is defined by the rules in §
3.2
. Note that the product of
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}^{\otimes(2g+n)}
is characterized by the rules:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\varphi_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\varphi_{2g+n}=\mathfrak{i}_{b_{1}}(\varphi_{1})\star\mathfrak{i}_{a_{1}}(\varphi_{2})\star\ldots\star\mathfrak{i}_{b_{g}}(\varphi_{2g-1})\star\mathfrak{i}_{a_{g}}(\varphi_{2g})

\displaystyle\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\>\>\star\mathfrak{i}_{m_{g+1}}(\varphi_{2g+1})\star\ldots\star\mathfrak{i}_{m_{g+n}}(\varphi_{2g+n}),

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{s}(\varphi)\star\mathfrak{i}_{s}(\psi)=\mathfrak{i}_{s}(\varphi\star\psi),\qquad\mathfrak{i}_{s}(\varphi)\star\mathfrak{i}_{t}(\psi)=\mathfrak{i}_{t}(\psi)\star\mathfrak{i}_{s}(\varphi)

for all
\displaystyle s,t\in\bigl\{b_{1},a_{1},\ldots,b_{g},a_{g},m_{g+1},\ldots,m_{g+n}\bigr\}
with
\displaystyle s\neq t
. So it suffices to prove the result for every couple
\displaystyle(s,t)
, taking
\displaystyle x=\mathfrak{i}_{s}(\varphi)
with
\displaystyle\varphi\in\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon})
and
\displaystyle y=\mathfrak{i}_{t}(\psi)
with
\displaystyle\psi\in\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}
. For instance, in the case
\displaystyle s=t
we apply (
18
) and get:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{s}(\varphi)\mathfrak{i}_{s}(\psi)=\sum_{(\varphi),(\psi)}\mathcal{R}_{\epsilon}\bigl(\psi_{(1)}\otimes S(\varphi_{(3)})\bigr)\mathcal{R}_{\epsilon}\bigl(\psi_{(3)}\otimes\varphi_{(2)}\bigr)\,\mathfrak{i}_{s}\bigl(\varphi_{(1)}\star\psi_{(2)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{(\varphi),(\psi)}\varepsilon_{\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}}(\psi_{(1)})\varepsilon_{\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}}\bigl(S(\varphi_{(3)})\bigr)\varepsilon_{\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}}(\psi_{(3)})\varepsilon_{\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}}(\varphi_{(2)})\mathfrak{i}_{s}\bigl(\varphi_{(1)}\star\psi_{(2)}\bigr)=\mathfrak{i}_{s}(\varphi\star\psi)=\mathfrak{i}_{s}(\varphi)\star\mathfrak{i}_{s}(\psi)

In the the second equality we used the fact that
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon})
is a Hopf subalgebra (and hence
\displaystyle S(\varphi_{(3)}),\varphi_{(2)}\in\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon})
) and Cor.
4.18
(2). Similarly, it is readily seen that the two exchange relations (
19
)-(
20
) in
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
reduce to the desired commutativity relation between tensorands in
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}^{\otimes(2g+n)}
.

(1) The first claim is due to the preliminary property and the fact that
\displaystyle\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}^{*}
is injective. For the second claim, note that
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(G)
is finitely generated as an algebra (by matrix coefficients of irreducible modules with highest weight
\displaystyle\varpi_{i}
,
\displaystyle i=1,\ldots,m
). Hence
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(G)^{\otimes(2g+n)}
is also a finitely generated commutative algebra, thus it is Noetherian by Hilbert’s basis theorem.

(2) is obvious from the preliminary property.

(3) It is known that
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}
is a free
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon})
-module of rank
\displaystyle l^{\dim(\mathfrak{g})}
(Th.
4.12
(2)). Hence, freeness and rank of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
over
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})
are deduced from item 2. Noetherianity is due to this classical fact: if a ring
\displaystyle R
is finitely generated as a module over a subring
\displaystyle S
and if
\displaystyle S
is a Noetherian ring, then
\displaystyle R
is a Noetherian ring
[
10
, Prop. 7.2]
; apply with
\displaystyle R=\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
and
\displaystyle S=\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})
. The same argument applies to
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})
.
∎

Now recall that
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
denotes the
small quantum group
(Def.
4.5
) and that the inclusion
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}\subset\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
gives by duality a surjection
\displaystyle\pi:\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}\to(u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}})^{*}
defined in (
116
). Since
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
is a finite-dimensional quasitriangular Hopf algebra, we have the algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}})
obtained by taking
\displaystyle H=u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
in §
3.2
. We have
\displaystyle H^{\circ}=(u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}})^{*}
by finite-dimensionality and the co-R-matrix is
\displaystyle\varphi\otimes\psi\mapsto(\varphi\otimes\psi)(\overline{R})
, where
\displaystyle\overline{R}
is the
\displaystyle R
-matrix of
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
(§
4.8.1
). As a
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}
-vector space
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}})=[(u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}})^{*}]^{\otimes(2g+n)}
, so the following result makes sense:

Proposition 5.19
.

\displaystyle\pi^{\otimes(2g+n)}:\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}\to\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}})
is a surjective morphism of algebras.

Proof.

Immediate from Lemma
3.4
and Corollary
4.18
(1).
∎

Let
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}^{+}(G)=\ker(\varepsilon)\cap\mathcal{O}(G)
be the augmentation ideal of
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(G)
, which consists of the functions
\displaystyle G\to\mathbb{C}
vanishing at the unit element of
\displaystyle G
.

Theorem 5.20
.

We have an exact sequence of algebras
7
7

7

The first term in this sequence is a non-unital algebra; exactness at the middle term means that
\displaystyle{\rm Ker}(\pi^{\otimes(2g+n)})
is the ideal
\displaystyle(\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}^{*}(\mathcal{O}^{+}(G))^{\otimes(2g+n)}\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
. This follows in part the notion of exact sequence of Hopf algebras introduced, e.g., in
[
6
]
.

(133)

0\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}^{+}(G)^{\otimes(2g+n)}\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt(\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}^{*})^{\otimes(2g+n)}\hskip 8.19447pt}\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt\pi^{\otimes(2g+n)}\hskip 8.19447pt}\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}})\longrightarrow 0.

Said differently,
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
is a central extension of the
\displaystyle l^{(2g+n)\dim(\mathfrak{g})}
-dimensional algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}})
by
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}^{+}(G)^{\otimes(2g+n)}
, where
\displaystyle l
is the order of the root of unity
\displaystyle\epsilon
.

Proof.

This is an immediate consequence of Prop.
4.14
, Prop.
5.17
(1) and Prop.
5.19
.
∎

Proposition 5.21
.

The algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
is a domain.

Proof.

The proof is contained in App.
D
, as it consists in an easy adaptation of arguments from
[
14
]
. ∎

Since
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
is a domain, we can consider the central localization (see §
2.4
)

(134)

Q(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}):=Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\bigr)\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})}\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}.

Note that since
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
is finitely generated and free as
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})
-module (Prop.
5.17
), Lemma
2.2
(2) allows us to rewrite

Q(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\cong Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\bigr)\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})}\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}.

Moreover, by Lemma
2.3
,
\displaystyle Q(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})
is a division algebra; it is thus in particular a central simple algebra and we wish to compute its PI-degree. Some technical preliminaries are in order.

Proposition 5.22
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
has an irreducible representation of dimension
\displaystyle l^{g\,\mathrm{dim}(\mathfrak{g})+nN}
, where
\displaystyle l
is the order of
\displaystyle\epsilon
and
\displaystyle N
is the number of positive roots of
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}
.

Proof.

Lyubashenko proved that the small quantum group
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
is factorizable when the order of
\displaystyle\epsilon
is odd
[
56
, Cor. A.3.3]
. Hence by Prop.
3.13
we have an isomorphism of algebras

(135)

\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}})\cong\mathrm{End}_{\mathbb{C}}\bigl((u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}})^{*}\bigr)^{\otimes g}\otimes(u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}})^{\otimes n}.

Moreover, it is known that
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
has an irreducible representation
\displaystyle V
of dimension
\displaystyle l^{N}

[
20
, §III.6.6]
. Of course, there is the natural irreducible representation of
\displaystyle\mathrm{End}_{\mathbb{C}}\bigl((u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}})^{*}\bigr)
on
\displaystyle(u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}})^{*}
which has dimensional
\displaystyle l^{\dim(\mathfrak{g})}
. Over an algebraically closed field, the external tensor product of irreducible representations is irreducible
[
35
, §3.10]
. Hence (
135
) gives an irreducible representation of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}})
on
\displaystyle\bigl((u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}})^{*}\bigr)^{\otimes g}\otimes V^{\otimes n}
, with desired dimension. The surjection
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}})
from Th.
5.20
transports it to an irreducible representation of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
.
∎

Denote by
\displaystyle[R:F]\in\mathbb{N}^{*}\cup\{\infty\}
the dimension of a ring
\displaystyle R
over a central subfield
\displaystyle F
.

Lemma 5.23
.

Let
\displaystyle\Bbbk
be a field, and
\displaystyle R^{\prime},R^{\prime\prime}
be commutative
\displaystyle\Bbbk
-algebras which are domains, and are such that the
\displaystyle\Bbbk
-algebra
\displaystyle R^{\prime}\otimes_{\Bbbk}R^{\prime\prime}
is a domain.

1. There is a natural inclusion
\displaystyle e:Q(R^{\prime})\otimes_{\Bbbk}Q(R^{\prime\prime})\hookrightarrow Q(R^{\prime}\otimes_{\Bbbk}R^{\prime\prime})
.

2. For any subalgebras
\displaystyle S^{\prime}\subset R^{\prime}
and
\displaystyle S^{\prime\prime}\subset R^{\prime\prime}
, if
\displaystyle\bigl(r^{\prime}_{i}\bigr)_{i}\subset Q(R^{\prime})
is free over
\displaystyle Q(S^{\prime})
and
\displaystyle\bigl(r^{\prime\prime}_{j}\bigr)_{j}\subset Q(R^{\prime\prime})
is free over
\displaystyle Q(S^{\prime\prime})
then
\displaystyle\bigl(e(r^{\prime}_{i}\otimes_{\Bbbk}r^{\prime\prime}_{j})\bigr)_{i,j}\subset Q(R^{\prime}\otimes_{\Bbbk}R^{\prime\prime})
is free over
\displaystyle Q(S^{\prime}\otimes_{\Bbbk}S^{\prime\prime})
. In particular

\bigl[Q(R^{\prime}\otimes_{\Bbbk}R^{\prime\prime}):Q(S^{\prime}\otimes_{\Bbbk}S^{\prime\prime})\bigr]\geq\bigl[Q(R^{\prime}):Q(S^{\prime})\bigr]\,\bigl[Q(R^{\prime\prime}):Q(S^{\prime\prime})\bigr].

Proof.

1. It is straightforward to check that the map
\displaystyle Q(R^{\prime})\times Q(R^{\prime\prime})\to Q(R^{\prime}\otimes_{\Bbbk}R^{\prime\prime})
given by
\displaystyle\Bigl(\frac{p^{\prime}}{q^{\prime}},\frac{p^{\prime\prime}}{q^{\prime\prime}}\Bigr)\mapsto\frac{p^{\prime}\otimes p^{\prime\prime}}{q^{\prime}\otimes q^{\prime\prime}}
is well-defined and
\displaystyle\Bbbk
-bilinear. It thus yields the
\displaystyle\Bbbk
-linear map
\displaystyle e
which satisfies
\displaystyle e\Bigl(\frac{p^{\prime}}{q^{\prime}}\otimes\frac{p^{\prime\prime}}{q^{\prime\prime}}\Bigr)=\frac{p^{\prime}\otimes p^{\prime\prime}}{q^{\prime}\otimes q^{\prime\prime}}
and is readily seen to be a morphism of
\displaystyle\Bbbk
-algebras. Let us prove that
\displaystyle e
is injective. Take an element
\displaystyle x=\sum_{c}\frac{p^{\prime}_{c}}{q^{\prime}_{c}}\otimes\frac{p^{\prime\prime}_{c}}{q^{\prime\prime}_{c}}\in Q(R^{\prime})\otimes_{\Bbbk}Q(R^{\prime\prime})
and assume
\displaystyle e(x)=0
. Set
\displaystyle d=\textstyle\prod_{c}q^{\prime}_{c}\otimes q^{\prime\prime}_{c}
so that
\displaystyle dx\in R^{\prime}\otimes_{\Bbbk}R^{\prime\prime}\subset Q(R^{\prime})\otimes_{\Bbbk}Q(R^{\prime\prime})
. Then
\displaystyle 0=e(d)e(x)=e(dx)=dx
. But since
\displaystyle d
is invertible in
\displaystyle Q(R^{\prime})\otimes_{\Bbbk}Q(R^{\prime\prime})
, we have
\displaystyle d^{-1}dx=x=0
.

2. Assume that a finite sum vanishes:
\displaystyle\textstyle\sum_{i,j}f_{i,j}e(r^{\prime}_{i}\otimes r^{\prime\prime}_{j})=0
, with
\displaystyle f_{i,j}\in Q(S^{\prime}\otimes_{\Bbbk}S^{\prime\prime})
. Collecting all denominators in the
\displaystyle f_{i,j}
’s, we get a non-zero element
\displaystyle P\in S^{\prime}\otimes_{\Bbbk}S^{\prime\prime}
such that
\displaystyle Pf_{i,j}\in S^{\prime}\otimes_{\Bbbk}S^{\prime\prime}
for all
\displaystyle i,j
. Let
\displaystyle(s^{\prime}_{k})
be a basis of
\displaystyle S^{\prime}
over
\displaystyle\Bbbk
; then
\displaystyle(s^{\prime}_{k})
is a free family over
\displaystyle\Bbbk
in
\displaystyle Q(S^{\prime})
and it follows that
\displaystyle\bigl(s^{\prime}_{k}r^{\prime}_{i}\,\bigr)_{i,k}
is a free family over
\displaystyle\Bbbk
in
\displaystyle Q(R^{\prime})
. Writing
\displaystyle Pf_{i,j}=\textstyle\sum_{k}s^{\prime}_{k}\otimes x^{i,j}_{k}\in S^{\prime}\otimes_{\Bbbk}S^{\prime\prime}
and using that
\displaystyle e(Pf_{i,j})
is
\displaystyle Pf_{i,j}
viewed in
\displaystyle Q(R^{\prime}\otimes_{\Bbbk}R^{\prime\prime})
, we find

\textstyle 0=\sum_{i,j}Pf_{i,j}e(r^{\prime}_{i}\otimes r^{\prime\prime}_{j})=e\left(\sum_{i,j,k}s^{\prime}_{k}r^{\prime}_{i}\otimes x^{i,j}_{k}r^{\prime\prime}_{j}\right)=e\left(\sum_{i,k}s^{\prime}_{k}r^{\prime}_{i}\otimes\Bigl(\sum_{j}x^{i,j}_{k}r^{\prime\prime}_{j}\Bigr)\right).

Since
\displaystyle e
is injective, the freeness of
\displaystyle\bigl(s^{\prime}_{k}r^{\prime}_{i}\bigr)_{i,k}
over
\displaystyle\Bbbk
implies
\displaystyle\textstyle\sum_{j}x^{i,j}_{k}r^{\prime\prime}_{j}=0
for all
\displaystyle i,k
. But since
\displaystyle(r^{\prime\prime}_{j})
is free over
\displaystyle Q(S^{\prime\prime})
, it follows in turn that
\displaystyle x^{i,j}_{k}=0
for all
\displaystyle i,j,k
. Hence
\displaystyle Pf_{i,j}=0
for all
\displaystyle i,j
and since
\displaystyle P\neq 0
we conclude that
\displaystyle f_{i,j}=0
for all
\displaystyle i,j
.
∎

Theorem 5.24
.

The algebra
\displaystyle Q(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})
is a division algebra, and a central simple algebra of PI-degree
\displaystyle l^{g\,\mathrm{dim}(\mathfrak{g})+nN}
.

Proof.

We already noted that it is a division algebra and a central simple algebra. Denote by
\displaystyle\mathfrak{r}\in\mathbb{N}
its PI-degree; so
\displaystyle Q(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})
has dimension
\displaystyle\mathfrak{r}^{2}
over
\displaystyle Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\bigr)
, and any irreducible representation of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
has dimension
\displaystyle\leq\mathfrak{r}
by Th.
2.5
(2). Hence Prop.
5.22
gives
\displaystyle\mathfrak{r}\geq l^{g\,\mathrm{dim}(\mathfrak{g})+nN}
.

To establish the other inequality, we make use of a central subalgebra
\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{Z}_{0}}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})
larger than
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})
. In order to define it, recall the
\displaystyle\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
-module
{}_{A}V_{\lambda}
which is a free
\displaystyle A
-lattice in the highest weight
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
-module
\displaystyle V_{\lambda}
for all
\displaystyle\lambda\in P_{+}
. Take an
\displaystyle A
-basis
\displaystyle(x_{i})
of weight vectors, with dual basis
\displaystyle(x^{i})
, and let

(136)

\textstyle\mathrm{qTr}_{\lambda}:\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}\to A,\quad h\mapsto\sum_{i}x^{i}\bigl(\ell h\cdot x_{i}\bigr)=\mathrm{Tr}\bigl(\mathrm{rep}_{\lambda}(\ell\,h)\bigr)

where
\displaystyle\ell\in U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}\subset\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
is the pivotal element and
\displaystyle\mathrm{rep}_{\lambda}:\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}\to\mathrm{End}_{A}(_{A}V_{\lambda})
is the representation morphism. Then
\displaystyle\mathrm{qTr}_{\lambda}\in\mathcal{O}_{A}
, as an
\displaystyle A
-linear combination of matrix coefficients of
{}_{A}V_{\lambda}
. Moreover,
\displaystyle\mathrm{qTr}_{\lambda}
is invariant under the coadjoint action (
122
) of
\displaystyle\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
by cyclicity of the trace and the pivotal property; said differently
\displaystyle\mathrm{qTr}_{\lambda}\in\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\Gamma_{A}}
. As usual denote by
\displaystyle(\mathrm{qTr}_{\lambda})_{|\epsilon}
the specialization of
\displaystyle\mathrm{qTr}_{\lambda}
to
\displaystyle q=\epsilon
. Now in that specialization let

\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}):=\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})\bigl[(\mathrm{qTr}_{\lambda})_{|\epsilon}\,\big|\,\lambda\in P_{+}\bigr]

be the subalgebra obtained by extending
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})
with the specializations of the elements
\displaystyle\mathrm{qTr}_{\lambda}
. Then, taking advantage of the vector space decomposition
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}=\mathcal{L}_{g,0}^{\epsilon}\otimes(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})^{\otimes n}
we consider the subspace

(137)

\hat{\mathcal{Z}_{0}}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}):=\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,0}^{\epsilon})\otimes\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})^{\otimes n}=\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\bigl[\mathfrak{i}_{g+j}(\mathrm{qTr}_{\lambda})_{|\epsilon}\,\big|\,\lambda\in P_{+},\>1\leq j\leq n\bigr].

By Prop.
5.17
(1) and Lemma
3.3
(ii) it is a central subalgebra of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
.
Of course, the inclusions
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\subset\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\subset\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})
still hold true at the level of fraction fields. Moreover, it is known that
\displaystyle\bigl[Q\bigl(\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})\bigr):Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})\bigr)\bigr]=l^{m}
by
[
12
, Rmk. 5.3]
8
8

8

The proof of
[
12
, Th. 5.2]
, which is used in
[
12
, Rmk. 5.3]
, implicitly uses Cor.
A.3
of the present paper.
. Hence

(138)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\begin{split}&\bigl[Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\bigr):Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\bigr)\bigr]\\
=\>&\bigl[Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\bigr):Q\bigl(\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\bigr)\bigr]\bigl[Q\bigl(\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\bigr):Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\bigr)\bigr]\\
\geq\>&\bigl[Q\bigl(\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\bigr):Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\bigr)\bigr]\\
=\>&\bigl[Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,0}^{\epsilon})\otimes\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})^{\otimes n}\bigr):Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,0}^{\epsilon})\otimes\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})^{\otimes n})\bigr)\bigr]\\
\geq\>&\bigl[Q\bigl(\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})^{\otimes n}\bigr):Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})^{\otimes n})\bigr)\bigr]\geq\bigl[Q\bigl(\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})\bigr):Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}))\bigr)\bigr]^{n}=l^{mn}\end{split}

where the two last inequalities are by Lemma
5.23
. Combining this with Prop.
5.17
(3) we conclude that

(139)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathfrak{r}^{2}=\bigl[Q(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}):Q(\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}))\bigr]&=\frac{\bigl[Q(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}):Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\bigr)\bigr]}{\bigl[Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\bigr):Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\bigr)\bigr]}\\
&\leq\bigl[Q(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}):Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\bigr)\bigr]l^{-mn}\\
&=l^{(2g+n)\mathrm{dim}(\mathfrak{g})}l^{-mn}=l^{2(g\,\mathrm{dim}(\mathfrak{g})+nN)}\end{split}

because
\displaystyle\dim(\mathfrak{g})=2N+m
. Therefore
\displaystyle\mathfrak{r}=l^{g\,\mathrm{dim}(\mathfrak{g})+nN}
.
∎

From the previous proof we can isolate facts which are interesting on their own:

Corollary 5.25
.

1.
\displaystyle\bigl[Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\bigr):Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\bigr)\bigr]=l^{mn}
, where
\displaystyle m=\mathrm{rank}(\mathfrak{g})
.

2.
\displaystyle Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\bigr)=Q\bigl(\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\bigr)
, where the central subalgebra
\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{Z}_{0}}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})
was defined in (
137
).

Proof.

1. The conclusion of the proof of Theorem
5.24
shows that the inequality in (
139
) is actually an equality, giving the claim.

2. By the previous item all inequalities in (
138
) are actually equalities. It follows that
\displaystyle\bigl[Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\bigr):Q\bigl(\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\bigr)\bigr]=1
.
∎

Remark 5.26
.

We will see in
\displaystyle\lx@sectionsign
6
that
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})=\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})
. This answers
[
12
, Rk . 5.3]
.

5.5.
PI degree of
\displaystyle Q(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}})

Recall from (
123
) that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}}
is the subalgebra of invariant elements under the action
\displaystyle\mathrm{coad}^{r}
of the small quantum group
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}\subset\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
. By Proposition
5.21
it is a domain. Therefore we can consider its central localization (see §
2.4
)

Q(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}})=Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}})\bigr)\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}})}\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}}.

By Cor.
5.14
we can write

\bigl[Q(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}}):Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}})\bigr)\bigr]\leq\bigl[Q(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}}):Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}^{\epsilon}_{g,n})\bigr)\bigr]\leq\bigl[Q(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}):Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\bigr)\bigr]<\infty

since the last term was computed in Theorem
5.24
. Lemma
2.3
then indicates that
\displaystyle Q(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}})
is a division algebra and a central simple algebra. Our goal is to compute its PI-degree. Note that by Lemma
2.2
(2) we also have

Q(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}})\cong Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\bigr)\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})}\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}}\cong Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\bigr)\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})}\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}}.

Recall the quantum moment map (QMM)
\displaystyle\mu_{g,n}:H^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\Phi_{0,1}^{-1}}\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)\xrightarrow{\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}}\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
defined for a general ribbon Hopf algebra
\displaystyle H
in §
3.3
and further studied for
\displaystyle H=U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}(\mathfrak{g})
in §
5.3
; we saw in particular that it has a specialized version
\displaystyle\mu_{g,n}^{\epsilon}:U_{\epsilon}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{(\Phi_{0,1}^{\epsilon})^{-1}}\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\xrightarrow{\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}}\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
. We now show that
\displaystyle\mu_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
is injective by a “reduction to the classical case” based on the two first items in the following proposition:

Proposition 5.27
.

1. We have a commutative diagram

\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathcal{O}(G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}^{\mathrm{cl}}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\sim}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}^{*}_{\epsilon}}
\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\mathrm{(subalg.)}}
\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\pi}
\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}^{(u_{\epsilon})}}
\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathcal{O}(G)^{\otimes(2g+n)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{(\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}^{*}_{\epsilon})^{\otimes(2g+n)}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\sim}
\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\mathrm{(subalg.)}}
\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\pi^{\otimes(2g+n)}}
\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}})}

where
\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}^{(u_{\epsilon})}
is defined by taking
\displaystyle H=u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}(\mathfrak{g})
in (
36
) while
\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}^{\mathrm{cl}}
is defined by

\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}^{\mathrm{cl}}(f):\bigl(B_{1},A_{1},\ldots,B_{g},A_{g},M_{1},\ldots,M_{n}\bigr)\mapsto f\Bigl[\Bigl({\textstyle\prod_{i=1}^{g}}B_{i}A_{i}^{-1}B_{i}^{-1}A_{i}\Bigr)\Bigl({\textstyle\prod_{j=1}^{n}}M_{j}\Bigr)\Bigr]

for all
\displaystyle f\in\mathcal{O}(G)
, using the identification
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(G)^{\otimes(2g+n)}=\mathcal{O}\bigl(G^{\times(2g+n)}\bigr)
.

2. The morphism
\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}^{\mathrm{cl}}:\mathcal{O}(G)\to\mathcal{O}(G)^{\otimes(2g+n)}
is injective.

3. The morphism
\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
and the specialized QMM
\displaystyle\mu^{\epsilon}_{g,n}:U_{\epsilon}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
are injective.

Therefore the three leftmost vertical maps in the diagram are embeddings, and
