\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})\subset\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})
.

Proof.

1. We prove commutation of the left rectangle. Because of formula (
36
) defining
\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}
it suffices to do the case
\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{1,0}
. We need three preliminary facts:

(i) Recall from (
35
) the antipode
\displaystyle S_{\mathcal{L}}
of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}
. Thanks to (
48
) and Corollary
4.18
(2) we see that
\displaystyle S_{\mathcal{L}}\circ\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}^{*}=\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}^{*}\circ S_{\mathcal{O}}
with
\displaystyle S_{\mathcal{O}}:\mathcal{O}(G)\to\mathcal{O}(G)
given by
\displaystyle S_{\mathcal{O}}(f)(g)=f(g^{-1})
.

(ii) Let
\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}^{\mathrm{cl}}_{b},\mathfrak{i}^{\mathrm{cl}}_{a}:\mathcal{O}(G)\to\mathcal{O}(G)^{\otimes 2}
given by
\displaystyle f\mapsto f\otimes\varepsilon_{\mathcal{O}}
and
\displaystyle f\mapsto\varepsilon_{\mathcal{O}}\otimes f
respectively, with
\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mathcal{O}}:f\mapsto f(e_{G})
where
\displaystyle e_{G}
is the unit element of
\displaystyle G
. Let
\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}^{\epsilon}_{b},\mathfrak{i}^{\epsilon}_{a}:\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\to\mathcal{L}_{1,0}^{\epsilon}
be defined similarly. By definition we have
\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}^{\epsilon}_{s}\circ\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}^{*}=(\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}^{*})^{\otimes 2}\circ\mathfrak{i}_{s}^{\mathrm{cl}}
for
\displaystyle s\in\{a,b\}
.

(iii) Let
\displaystyle\mathsf{v}_{\epsilon}:\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}\to\mathbb{C}
be the specialization of the coribbon element of
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})
(see (
82
) and Lemma
4.4
). We claim that
\displaystyle\mathsf{v}_{\epsilon}\circ\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}^{*}=\varepsilon_{\mathcal{O}}
; said differently
\displaystyle\mathsf{v}_{\epsilon}
is
\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}}
on
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon})
. To see this note first that
\displaystyle\mathsf{v}_{\epsilon}
is multiplicative on the sub-Hopf algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon})
, thanks to the axiom (
32
) and Corollary
4.18
(2). Hence it suffices to check the claim on a subset of algebra generators. It is known that matrix coefficients of
\displaystyle\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
-modules of the form
{}_{A}V_{l\lambda}\otimes_{A}\mathbb{C}_{\epsilon}
generate
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon})
(Th.
4.12
). For such a matrix coefficient
\displaystyle c
, we have by (
82
):

\mathsf{v}_{\epsilon}(c)=\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}^{-D(l\lambda,l\lambda+2\rho)}c(1)=(\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}^{l})^{-D(\lambda,l\lambda+2\rho)}c(1)=c(1)=\varepsilon_{\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}}(c)

thanks to the choice of
\displaystyle\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
as an
\displaystyle l
-th root of unity in (
93
).

As a result, for all
\displaystyle f\in\mathcal{O}(G)
we find

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{1,0}^{\epsilon}\circ\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}^{*}(f)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{(\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}^{*}(f))}\mathsf{v}_{\epsilon}^{2}\bigl(\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}^{*}(f)_{(1)}\bigr)\mathfrak{i}^{\epsilon}_{b}\bigl(\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}^{*}(f)_{(2)}\bigr)\mathfrak{i}^{\epsilon}_{a}\bigl(S_{\mathcal{L}}\bigl(\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}^{*}(f)_{(3)}\bigr)\bigr)\mathfrak{i}^{\epsilon}_{b}\bigl(S_{\mathcal{L}}\bigl(\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}^{*}(f)_{(4)}\bigr)\bigr)\mathfrak{i}^{\epsilon}_{a}\bigl(\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}^{*}(f)_{(5)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{(f)}\mathsf{v}_{\epsilon}^{2}\bigl(\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}^{*}(f_{(1)})\bigr)\mathfrak{i}^{\epsilon}_{b}\bigl(\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}^{*}(f_{(2)})\bigr)\mathfrak{i}^{\epsilon}_{a}\bigl(S_{\mathcal{L}}\bigl(\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}^{*}(f_{(3)})\bigr)\bigr)\mathfrak{i}^{\epsilon}_{b}\bigl(S_{\mathcal{L}}\bigl(\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}^{*}(f_{(4)})\bigr)\bigr)\mathfrak{i}^{\epsilon}_{a}\bigl(\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}^{*}(f_{(5)})\bigr)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{(f)}(\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}^{*})^{\otimes 2}\Bigl(\mathfrak{i}^{\mathrm{cl}}_{b}(f_{(1)})\mathfrak{i}^{\mathrm{cl}}_{a}\bigl(S_{\mathcal{O}}(f_{(2)})\bigr)\mathfrak{i}^{\mathrm{cl}}_{b}\bigl(S_{\mathcal{O}}(f_{(3)})\bigr)\mathfrak{i}^{\mathrm{cl}}_{a}(f_{(4)})\Bigr)=(\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}^{*})^{\otimes 2}\circ\mathfrak{d}_{1,0}^{\mathrm{cl}}(f)

where the first equality is by definition (
34
) of
\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{1,0}
, the second uses that
\displaystyle\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}^{*}
is a Hopf algebra morphism, the third uses the previous remarks and the fact that
\displaystyle(\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}^{*})^{\otimes 2}:\mathcal{O}(G)\to\mathcal{L}_{1,0}^{\epsilon}
is an algebra morphism (Prop.
5.17
), and the last is by definition.

The commutativity of the right square is established by similar arguments, now using Corollary
4.18
(1) and Proposition
5.19
. One has to check the compatibility of the ribbon structures,
i.e.

\displaystyle\pi(\varphi)(v)=\mathsf{v}_{\epsilon}(\varphi)
for all
\displaystyle\varphi\in\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}
, where
\displaystyle v\in u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
is the ribbon element
[
56
, eq. (A.3.1)]
). This is a direct computation, using the expression of the
\displaystyle R
-matrix of
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
given by Lyubashenko
[
56
, App.]
.

2. By the argument in the proof of Lemma
3.7
it suffices to show that
\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{1,0}^{\mathrm{cl}}
is injective. But this follows from the fact, proved in
[
64
]
, that in a connected semi-simple algebraic group defined over an algebraically closed field, every element is a commutator.

3. Since
\displaystyle\mu_{g,n}^{\epsilon}=\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}\circ(\Phi_{0,1}^{\epsilon})^{-1}
, it is enough to show that
\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
is injective. By item 1 we know that the restriction
\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}|_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}^{\epsilon}_{0,1})}
takes values in
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})
, and this restriction is injective by item 2. As a result
\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})\setminus\{0\}\bigr)\subset\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\setminus\{0\}\subset\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\setminus\{0\}
. Injectivity of
\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
thus follows by taking
\displaystyle Z_{0}=\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})
in Lemma
2.4
.
∎

Let us recall the statement of Theorem
1.4
, and give its proof.

Theorem 5.28
.

If
\displaystyle(g,n)\neq(0,1)
, the algebra
\displaystyle Q(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}})
is a division algebra, and a central simple algebra of PI-degree
\displaystyle l^{g.\mathrm{dim}(\mathfrak{g})+N(n-1)-m}
, where
\displaystyle N
is the number of positive roots of
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}
and
\displaystyle m
is the rank of
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}
.

The end of this subsection is dedicated to the proof of this theorem. To keep clear the line of reasoning we postpone the proof of some arguments to §
6
.

We will use the following shorter notations:

R:=\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon},\quad S:=\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})\cong\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon},\quad\mathcal{Z}_{0}(S)=\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})\bigr),\quad\mathcal{Z}_{1}(S)=\mathfrak{d}^{\epsilon}_{g,n}\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})\bigr)

and
\displaystyle(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1})(S)=\mathcal{Z}_{0}(S)\cap\mathcal{Z}_{0}(S)
, where
\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}:\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\to\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
from §
5.3
was proven injective in Prop.
5.27
, and
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})
is defined in (
144
) below. Recall from Prop.
5.27
that
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(S)\subset\mathcal{Z}_{0}(R)\subset\mathcal{Z}(R)
; thus
\displaystyle Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(R)\bigr)
is a
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(S)
-module and the following lemma makes sense:

Lemma 5.29
.

\displaystyle Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(R)\bigr)\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}(S)}S
is a simple algebra whose center is
\displaystyle Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(R)\bigr)\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}(S)}\mathcal{Z}(S)
, and this center can also be written as
\displaystyle Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(R)\bigr)\otimes_{Q(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(S))}Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(S)\bigr)
.

Proof.

Observe that

\displaystyle\displaystyle Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(R)\bigr)\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}(S)}S

\displaystyle\displaystyle\cong Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(R)\bigr)\otimes_{Q(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(S))}Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(S)\bigr)\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}(S)}S\quad\text{\footnotesize(trick)}

(140)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\cong Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(R)\bigr)\otimes_{Q(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(S))}Q(S)\quad\text{\footnotesize by Lemma \ref{lemTrucsGenerauxQR}(3)}

\displaystyle\displaystyle\cong\Bigl(Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(R)\bigr)\otimes_{Q(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(S))}Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(S)\bigr)\Bigr)\otimes_{Q(\mathcal{Z}(S))}Q(S)\quad\text{\footnotesize(trick)}

\displaystyle\displaystyle\cong\Bigl(Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(R)\bigr)\otimes_{Q(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(S))}Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(S)\bigr)\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}(S)}\mathcal{Z}(S)\Bigr)\otimes_{Q(\mathcal{Z}(S))}Q(S)\quad\text{\footnotesize by Lemma \ref{lemTrucsGenerauxQR}(3)}

\displaystyle\displaystyle\cong\Bigl(Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(R)\bigr)\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}(S)}\mathcal{Z}(S)\Bigr)\otimes_{Q(\mathcal{Z}(S))}Q(S)

We claim that
\displaystyle Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(R)\bigr)\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}(S)}\mathcal{Z}(S)
is a field (and in particular is a simple algebra), which by
[
68
, Th. 1.7.27]
9
9

9

This theorem asserts: If
\displaystyle C
is a field,
\displaystyle R
is a central simple
\displaystyle C
-algebra and
\displaystyle R^{\prime}
is a simple
\displaystyle C
-algebra, then
\displaystyle R^{\prime}\otimes_{C}R
is a simple
\displaystyle C
-algebra.
implies that the last algebra in the above chain of isomorphisms is a simple algebra, as desired. To prove this claim we use the fact that the multiplication map
\displaystyle j:\mathcal{Z}_{0}(S)\otimes_{(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1})(S)}\mathcal{Z}_{1}(S)\to\mathcal{Z}(S)
is an isomorphism of algebras, which follows from Th.
6.1
(1) applied to
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\cong S
, and the fact that the multiplication map
\displaystyle j_{2}:\mathcal{Z}(R)\otimes_{(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1})(S)}\mathcal{Z}_{1}(S)\to R
is injective (actually taking values in
\displaystyle R^{u_{\epsilon}}
) which is proven in Th.
6.1
(2).
10
10

10

Th.
6.1
is proved below in §
6
. We stress that surjectivity of
\displaystyle j
uses Cor.
5.25
and its injectivity uses arguments which are independent of Th.
5.28
; the surjectivity of
\displaystyle j_{2}
will use Th.
5.28
but its injectivity is independent of it (hence there is no loop in the arguments). We found it clearer to put all the properties of
\displaystyle j
and
\displaystyle j_{2}
in §
6
below.
Thanks to these facts we find

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(R)\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}(S)}\mathcal{Z}(S)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{Z}(R)\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}(S)}\mathcal{Z}_{0}(S)\otimes_{(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1})(S)}\mathcal{Z}_{1}(S)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{Z}(R)\otimes_{(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1})(S)}\mathcal{Z}_{1}(S)\xrightarrow{\>\text{injection}\>}R.

Hence
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(R)\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}(S)}\mathcal{Z}(S)
is a domain because so is
\displaystyle R
(Prop.
5.21
). Therefore
\displaystyle Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(R)\bigr)\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}(S)}\mathcal{Z}(S)
is a domain as well. Since
\displaystyle\bigl[Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(S)\bigr):Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(S)\bigr)\bigr]=l^{m}
by Cor.
5.25
(1), we get moreover that
\displaystyle Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(R)\bigr)\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}(S)}\mathcal{Z}(S)\cong Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(R)\bigr)\otimes_{Q(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(S))}Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(S)\bigr)
has dimension
\displaystyle l^{m}
over
\displaystyle Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(R)\bigr)
. But any finite-dimensional commutative domain is a field. In this way we have proved the first part of the lemma.

The center is now computed as follows:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}\Bigl(Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(R)\bigr)\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}(S)}S\Bigr)\cong\mathcal{Z}\Bigl(Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(R)\bigr)\otimes_{Q(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(S))}Q(S)\Bigr)\cong Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(R)\bigr)\otimes_{Q(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(S))}Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(S)\bigr)\bigr)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\cong\>\,

\displaystyle\displaystyle Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(R)\bigr)\otimes_{Q(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(S))}Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(S)\bigr)\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}(S)}\mathcal{Z}(S)\cong Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(R)\bigr)\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}(S)}\mathcal{Z}(S)

where the first isomorphism uses (
140
), the second uses the fact that the center of a tensor product of algebras over a field is the tensor product of their centers
[
68
, Cor. 1.7.24]
together with Lemma
2.2
(2) and the third is by Lemma
2.2
(3).
∎

Proof of Theorem
5.28
.

We already noted at the beginning of the present subsection that
\displaystyle Q(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}})
is a division algebra. Its PI-degree is the square root of
\displaystyle\bigl[Q(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}}):Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}})\bigr)\bigr]
. The key-point for the computation of this dimension is to use that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}}=\mathbf{C}_{R}(S)
, namely the centralizer of
\displaystyle S:=\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})
in
\displaystyle R:=\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
(Th.
5.13
). In particular
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(R)\subset\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}}
. First, it is important to realize that the central localization
\displaystyle Q(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}})
as defined in §
2.4
can be seen as a subalgebra of
\displaystyle Q(R)
. To see this, note by Prop.
5.17
(3) that
\displaystyle R
is Noetherian as a
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(R)
-module. It follows that the
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(R)
-submodule
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}})
is finitely generated, whence
\displaystyle\bigl[Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}})\bigr):Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(R)\bigr)\bigr]\leq\bigl[Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}})\bigr):Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(R)\bigr)\bigr]<\infty
. Using Lemma
2.2
(3), we thus obtain

Q\bigl(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}}\bigr)\cong Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(R)\bigr)\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}(R)}\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}}\xrightarrow{\>\text{inclusion}\>}Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(R)\bigr)\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}(R)}R\overset{\text{def}}{=}Q(R)

where the inclusion
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}}\subset R
survives because
\displaystyle Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(R)\bigr)
is a flat
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(R)
-module as any localization. Under this inclusion we have

(141)

Q(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}})=Q\bigl(\mathbf{C}_{R}(S)\bigr)=\mathbf{C}_{Q(R)}\bigl(Q_{R}(S)\bigr),

with the subalgebra

(142)

Q_{R}(S):=\mathrm{span}_{Q(\mathcal{Z}(R))}\bigl\{1\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}(R)}s\,|\,s\in S\bigr\}\subset Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(R)\bigr)\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}(R)}R\overset{\text{def}}{=}Q(R)

whose definition makes sense because
\displaystyle Q(R)
is naturally a
\displaystyle Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(R)\bigr)
-vector space.
11
11

11

This can also be written as
\displaystyle Q_{R}(S)=Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(R)\bigr)\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}(R)}\mathcal{Z}(R)S
, where
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(R)S
is the
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(R)
-submodule generated by
\displaystyle S
in
\displaystyle R
. Note that
\displaystyle Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(R)\bigr)\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}(R)}S
does not make sense because
\displaystyle S
is not a
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(R)
-module.
Since
\displaystyle Q_{R}(S)
is of finite-dimension over
\displaystyle Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(R)\bigr)
(because so is
\displaystyle Q(R)
) it is a division algebra, and in particular a simple subalgebra of the central simple algebra
\displaystyle Q(R)
(Th.
5.24
). As a result, the double centralizer theorem (see e.g.
[
68
, Th. 7.1.9]
or
[
70
, Th. 7.1]
) implies that
\displaystyle\mathbf{C}_{Q(R)}\bigl(Q_{R}(S)\bigr)
is a central simple algebra and

\bigl[Q(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}}):Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\bigr)\bigr]\overset{\eqref{InvAsCentralizer}}{=}\bigl[\mathbf{C}_{Q(R)}\bigl(Q_{R}(S)\bigr):Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(R)\bigr)\bigr]=\frac{\bigl[Q(R):Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(R)\bigr)\bigr]}{\bigl[Q_{R}(S):Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(R)\bigr)\bigr]}.

The numerator equals
\displaystyle l^{2(g\,\mathrm{dim}(\mathfrak{g})+nN)}
by Th.
5.24
. To compute the denominator note that, since
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(S)\subset\mathcal{Z}_{0}(R)\subset\mathcal{Z}(R)
by Prop.
5.27
, there is a well-defined map

(143)

Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(R)\bigr)\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}(S)}S\to Q_{R}(S),\quad q\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}(S)}s\mapsto q\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}(R)}s.

It is an isomorphism: surjectivity is clear while injectivity is due to the fact that the source is a simple algebra (Lemma
5.29
), thus forcing the kernel to be
\displaystyle 0
. Since
\displaystyle S
is a free
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(S)
-module of rank
\displaystyle l^{\dim(\mathfrak{g})}
by Prop.
5.17
(3) applied to
\displaystyle S\cong\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}
, we deduce that
\displaystyle\bigl[Q_{R}(S):Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(R)\bigr)\bigr]=l^{\dim(\mathfrak{g})}=l^{2N+n}
.
As a result

\bigl[Q(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}}):Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\bigr)\bigr]=l^{2(g\,\mathrm{dim}(\mathfrak{g})+nN)-(2N+m)}=l^{2(g\,\mathrm{dim}(\mathfrak{g})+(n-1)N)-m}.

Another dimension computation is required in order to conclude the proof. Observe that

\displaystyle\displaystyle Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}})\bigr)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\mathcal{Z}\bigl(Q(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}})\bigr)=\mathcal{Z}\bigl[\mathbf{C}_{Q(R)}\bigl(Q_{R}(S)\bigr)\bigr]=\mathbf{C}_{Q(R)}\bigl[\mathbf{C}_{Q(R)}\bigl(Q_{R}(S)\bigr)\bigr]\cap\mathbf{C}_{Q(R)}\bigl(Q_{R}(S)\bigr)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=Q_{R}(S)\cap\mathbf{C}_{Q(R)}\bigl(Q_{R}(S)\bigr)=\mathcal{Z}\bigl(Q_{R}(S)\bigr)\cong Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(R)\bigr)\otimes_{Q(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(S))}Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(S)\bigr)

where the first equality is by Lemma
2.2
(2), the second is by (
141
), the third is trivial, the fourth is by the double centralizer theorem, the fifth is trivial and the sixth is by the isomorphism (
143
) and Lemma
5.29
. As a result

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bigl[Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}})\bigr):Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\bigr)\bigr]

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\bigl[Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(R)\bigr)\otimes_{Q(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(S))}Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(S)\bigr):Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(R)\bigr)\bigr]

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\bigl[Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(S)\bigr):Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(S)\bigr)\bigr]=l^{m}

where the last equality is by Cor.
5.25
applied to
\displaystyle S\cong\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}
. We are now in position to conclude the proof:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bigl[Q(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}}):Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}})\bigr)\bigr]

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\frac{\bigl[Q(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}}):Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\bigr)\bigr]}{\bigl[Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}})\bigr):Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\bigr)\bigr]}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=l^{2(g\,\mathrm{dim}(\mathfrak{g})+(n-1)N)-m}\,l^{-m}=l^{2(g\,\mathrm{dim}(\mathfrak{g})+(n-1)N-m)}.

This shows
\displaystyle Q(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}})
has PI degree
\displaystyle l^{g\,\mathrm{dim}(\mathfrak{g})+(n-1)N-m}
, as claimed.
∎

Analogously to Corollary
5.25
, the rank computation in the above proof implies:

Corollary 5.30
.

We have
\displaystyle\bigl[Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}})\bigr):Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\bigr)\bigr]=l^{m(n+1)}
, where
\displaystyle m=\mathrm{rank}(\mathfrak{g})
.

6.
Centers of graph algebras at roots of unity

Recall the “quantum trace” elements
\displaystyle\mathrm{qTr}_{\lambda}\in\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\Gamma_{A}}
defined in (
136
) for all
\displaystyle\lambda\in P_{+}
and let
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})
be the subalgebra of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}
generated by their specializations
\displaystyle(\mathrm{qTr}_{\lambda})_{|\epsilon}
. Thanks to the vector space decomposition
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}=(\mathcal{L}_{1,0}^{\epsilon})^{\otimes g}\otimes(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})^{\otimes n}
, define the subalgebra

(144)

\mathcal{Z}_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}):=(1_{\mathcal{L}_{1,0}^{\epsilon}})^{\otimes g}\otimes\mathcal{Z}_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})^{\otimes n}\subset\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})

where the inclusion is due to Lemma
3.3
(2). Also recall the morphism
\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}:\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\to\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
which enters in the definition of the quantum moment map (§
5.3
).

This section is mainly devoted to the proof of the following result:

Theorem 6.1
.

Let us use the notation
\displaystyle(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1})(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})=\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})
.

(1) The multiplication map
\displaystyle j\colon\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\otimes_{(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1})(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})}\mathcal{Z}_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\to\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})
is an isomorphism of algebras, and
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})
is a Noetherian and integrally closed ring.

(2) The multiplication map
\displaystyle j_{2}\colon\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\otimes_{\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}\left((\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1})(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})\right)}\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})\bigr)\to\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}})
is an isomorphism of algebras, and
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}})
is a Noetherian and integrally closed ring.

The first claim in the theorem means in particular that
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})
is the subalgebra generated by
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})
(it was denoted by
\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})
in the proof of Th.
5.24
). The second claim implies that
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}})
is a finite extension of
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})
by
\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})\bigr)
; we will see below that it has degree
\displaystyle l^{m}
.

The proof of Th.
6.1
consists in reducing the statement to problems already solved by De Concini, Kac and Procesi in their proof that the multiplication map

(145)

\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})\otimes_{(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1})(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})}\mathcal{Z}_{1}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})

is an isomorphism of algebras (see
[
30
, §6.4]
,
[
32
, §21]
), where we denote
\displaystyle(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1})(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})=\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})
. We recall from (
97
) that
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})
is the subalgebra of
\displaystyle U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
generated by
\displaystyle E_{\beta}^{l}
,
\displaystyle F_{\beta}^{l}
,
\displaystyle K_{\lambda}^{l}
for
\displaystyle\beta\in\phi^{+}
and
\displaystyle\lambda\in P
, while
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{1}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})
is the specialization at
\displaystyle q=\epsilon
of the center
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})
of the simply-connected unrestricted integral form
\displaystyle U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
defined in §
4.3
.

This reduction starts with the following observation. From the very definition of
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})
and the decomposition
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})=\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,0}^{\epsilon})\otimes\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})^{\otimes n}
we have

(146)

\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\otimes_{(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1})(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})}\mathcal{Z}_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})=\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,0}^{\epsilon})\otimes\left(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})\otimes_{(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1})(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})}\mathcal{Z}_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})\right)^{\otimes n}.

The rightmost terms in (
146
) are formally close to the factorization of
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})
in (
145
). Indeed, recall that
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}^{A}\colon\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{A}\to U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
is an embedding of
\displaystyle\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
-module algebras (§
5.2
). We have:

Lemma 6.2
.

The embedding
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\colon\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\to U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
restricts to isomorphisms
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})\to\mathcal{Z}_{1}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})\to\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}).

Proof.

The first claim is just obtained by specialization of Corollary
A.3
in App.
A
below, as we noted in (
3
) that specialization is a functor and thus preserves isomorphisms.

Consider the second claim. The inclusion
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})\bigr)\subset\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})
follows from results of
[
31
]
(see the proof of
[
12
, Prop. 4.2]
). By the first claim, it is thus enough to show that
\displaystyle(\Phi_{0,1}^{\epsilon})^{-1}\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})\bigr)\subset\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})
. Take
\displaystyle\alpha\in(\Phi_{0,1}^{\epsilon})^{-1}\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})\bigr)
. According to Lemma
4.10
, we must prove that
\displaystyle\alpha
vanishes on
\displaystyle\ker(\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon})\subset\Gamma^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}_{\epsilon}
. But by specialization of formula (
160
) in App.
A
below it holds
\displaystyle\langle\alpha,y\rangle^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}_{\epsilon}=\bigl(\Phi_{0,1}^{\epsilon}(\alpha)\,\big|\,y\bigr)_{\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}
for all
\displaystyle y\in\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
, where
\displaystyle(\>\,|\,\>)_{\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}:U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}\times\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}\to\mathbb{C}
is the specialization of the integral quantum Killing form
\displaystyle(\>\,|\,\>)_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}
defined in (
159
) of the same appendix. The desired vanishing then immediately follows from Lemma
A.7
, which asserts that the subspaces
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})
and
\displaystyle\ker(\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon})
are orthogonal for
\displaystyle(\>\,|\,\>)_{\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}
.
∎

The proof of Theorem
6.1
will also use the next two results. The following Lemma is classical:

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
\displaystyle A
,
\displaystyle B
and
\displaystyle C
be commutative rings.

1. Assume that
\displaystyle A\subset B
, that
\displaystyle B
is a domain and a Noetherian
\displaystyle A
-module, that
\displaystyle A
is integrally closed, and that
\displaystyle Q(A)=Q(B)
. Then
\displaystyle A=B
.

2. Assume that
\displaystyle B
and
\displaystyle C
are domains, and that we have ring morphisms
\displaystyle f\colon A\to B
and
\displaystyle g\colon B\to C
such that
\displaystyle f
and
\displaystyle g\circ f
are injective, and
\displaystyle B
is integral over
\displaystyle A
via
\displaystyle f
. Then
\displaystyle g
is injective.

Proof.

1. Let
\displaystyle b\in B
. Since
\displaystyle B
is Noetherian over
\displaystyle A
, the
\displaystyle A
-submodule
\displaystyle A[b]
is finitely generated, and therefore
\displaystyle b
is integral over
\displaystyle A

[
10
, Prop. 5.1]
. Since
\displaystyle A
is integrally closed and
\displaystyle b\in Q(B)=Q(A)
, it follows
\displaystyle b\in A
.

2. Since
\displaystyle C
is a domain,
\displaystyle{\rm Ker}(g)
is a prime ideal of
\displaystyle B
, and since
\displaystyle g\circ f
is injective,
\displaystyle{\rm Ker}(g)\cap f(A)=\{0\}
,
i.e.
the null ideal
\displaystyle 0f(A)
of
\displaystyle f(A)
. Moreover,
\displaystyle 0f(A)
and
\displaystyle 0B
are prime ideals of
\displaystyle f(A)
and
\displaystyle B
, respectively, since these are domains (for
\displaystyle f(A)
, because
\displaystyle f
and
\displaystyle g\circ f
are injective). Now, as
\displaystyle B
is integral over
\displaystyle f(A)
there are no inclusions between prime ideals of
\displaystyle B
lying over a given prime ideal of
\displaystyle f(A)

[
60
, Th. 9.3 (ii)]
. Therefore the inclusion
\displaystyle 0B\subset{\rm Ker}(g)
is an equality, whence
\displaystyle g
is injective.
∎

The following Proposition combines (
146
) and Lemma
6.2
with results contained in
[
30
, §6]
,
[
32
, §21]
. For completeness we will recall briefly some of their arguments.

Proposition 6.4
.

The ring
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\otimes_{(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1})(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})}\mathcal{Z}_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})
is an integrally closed domain.

Proof.

Let
\displaystyle V:=\mathrm{MaxSpec}\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\otimes_{(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1})(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})}\mathcal{Z}_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\bigr)
. We will show below that
\displaystyle V
is a complete intersection affine variety which is smooth outside a subvariety
\displaystyle Z
of codimension
\displaystyle\geq 2
. This will conclude the proof as follows. Since
\displaystyle V
is an irreducible algebraic set,
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(V)
is a domain, and
\displaystyle V
being a complete intersection,
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(V)
is a complete intersection ring and hence a Cohen-Macaulay ring. This and
\displaystyle{\rm codim}(Z,V)\geq 2
imply that
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(V)
satisfies both conditions of Serre’s normality criterium (see
[
60
, Th. 23.8]
and the comments above it), and thus it is a normal ring in the sense of
[
60
, Rk. on p. 64]
. Being also a domain (hence with the
\displaystyle 0
ideal its unique minimal prime), it follows that
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(V)
is integrally closed.

We now show that
\displaystyle V
is a complete intersection variety which is smooth outside of a subvariety
\displaystyle Z
of codimension
\displaystyle\geq 2
.

We need to recall some results from
[
32
,
\displaystyle\lx@sectionsign
21.2-21.3]
,
[
30
,
\displaystyle\lx@sectionsign
6]
. In these papers, De Concini–Kac–Procesi showed that
\displaystyle\mathrm{MaxSpec}\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})\otimes_{(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1})(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})}\mathcal{Z}_{1}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})\bigr)
is an open subset in an unramified covering of the fiber product (where we used the notations of §
2.1
for
\displaystyle T
,
\displaystyle W
and
\displaystyle G
)

(147)

X_{G}=G\times_{T/W}T/W,

defined by the quotient map
\displaystyle G\to G/\!/G\cong T/W
and the map
\displaystyle T/W\to T/W
induced by the
\displaystyle l
-th power map
\displaystyle t\mapsto t^{l}
. More precisely, they proved that
\displaystyle X_{G}
is a variety, smooth away from a subvariety of codimension
\displaystyle\geq 2
. Moreover
\displaystyle\mathrm{MaxSpec}\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})\bigr)\cong G^{*}
, the dual Poisson-Lie group of
\displaystyle G
, which has an unramified covering map
\displaystyle\sigma\colon G^{*}\to G^{0}
of degree
\displaystyle 2^{m}
, where
\displaystyle G^{0}
is the big cell
\displaystyle G^{0}\subset G
(see
[
12
, Th. 2.27]
in the present notations). Then

(148)

\mathrm{MaxSpec}\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})\otimes_{(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1})(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})}\mathcal{Z}_{1}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})\bigr)\cong G^{*}\times_{T/W}T/W,

where the left map in this fiber product is the composition
\displaystyle G^{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sigma}}{{\rightarrow}}G^{0}\hookrightarrow G\to G/\!/G\cong T/W
, and the map
\displaystyle T/W\to T/W
is as above.

Also, De Concini–Kac–Procesi showed that: (a)
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{1}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})
is a complete intersection over its subring
\displaystyle(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1})(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})
, and (b)
\displaystyle(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1})(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})\cong\mathcal{O}(G)^{G}
as a subalgebra of
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})\cong\mathcal{O}(G^{*})
via the quantum Harish-Chandra isomorphism at
\displaystyle q=\epsilon
(see Rk.
A.4
). Now
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(G^{*})
is a complete intersection ring (because
\displaystyle G^{*}
is a smooth algebraic subgroup of
\displaystyle G\times G
), and
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(G^{*})
is a free (hence flat)
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(G)^{G}
-module (since
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(G)
is so by Richardson’s theorem
[
66
]
,
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(G^{0})
is a localization of
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(G)
and hence a free
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(G)^{G}
-module as well, and
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(G^{*})
is a free
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(G^{0})
-module (of rank
\displaystyle 2^{m}
)). These two properties together with (a) and (b) imply that
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})\otimes_{(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1})(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})}\mathcal{Z}_{1}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})
is a complete intersection ring (by, e.g., the arguments of
[
69
, Lem. 10.135.1]
, taking
\displaystyle S=\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})
and replacing the field
\displaystyle k
by
\displaystyle(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1})(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})
and
\displaystyle K
by
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})
).

We are now ready to conclude the proof. Consider the embedding
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(G)\to\mathcal{O}(G^{*})
dual to the dominant map
\displaystyle G^{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sigma}}{{\rightarrow}}G^{0}\hookrightarrow G
. We know that
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(G)
is isomorphic to
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})
, and
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}^{\epsilon}
embeds
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})
into
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})\cong\mathcal{O}(G^{*})
; in fact the inclusion
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}^{\epsilon}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}))\subset\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})
is naturally identified with the embedding
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(G)\to\mathcal{O}(G^{*})
(see
[
12
, Lem. 4.2 and Rk. 4.3]
). By using Lemma
6.2
to lift via
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}^{\epsilon}
the inclusion map
\displaystyle(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1})(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})\hookrightarrow\Phi_{0,1}^{\epsilon}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}))\subset\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})
, similarly as (b) above we find that
\displaystyle(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1})(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})\cong\mathcal{O}(G)^{G}
as a subalgebra of
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})\cong\mathcal{O}(G)
. It is a complete intersection ring, so as in the previous discussion, using (a) one obtains that the ring
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})\otimes_{(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1})(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})}\mathcal{Z}_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})
is a complete intersection. Then (
146
) implies immediately that
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\otimes_{(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1})(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})}\mathcal{Z}_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})
is a complete intersection ring as well.

Moreover, using the embedding
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(G)\to\mathcal{O}(G^{*})
the same arguments as (
148
) imply that we have an isomorphism

(149)

\mathrm{MaxSpec}\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})\otimes_{(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1})(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})}\mathcal{Z}_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})\bigr)\cong X_{G}.

By (
146
) it follows that

V=\mathrm{MaxSpec}\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\otimes_{(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1})(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})}\mathcal{Z}_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\bigr)\cong G^{\times 2g}\times X_{G}^{\times n}.

By the properties of
\displaystyle X_{G}
this variety is smooth outside of a subvariety of codimension
\displaystyle\geq 2
. As explained at the beginning of the proof, by Serre’s normality criterium we can conclude that
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\otimes_{(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1})(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})}\mathcal{Z}_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})
is an integrally closed domain.∎

Proof of Theorem
6.1
.

In this proof we write
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}:=\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})
,
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}:=\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})
, and
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{1}:=\mathcal{Z}_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})
. Also we write
\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}=j(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1}}\mathcal{Z}_{1})
, where
\displaystyle j\colon\mathcal{Z}_{0}\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1}}\mathcal{Z}_{1}\to\mathcal{Z}
is the multiplication map.

(1) That the multiplication map
\displaystyle j\colon\mathcal{Z}_{0}\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1}}\mathcal{Z}_{1}\to\mathcal{Z}
is injective follows from Lemma
6.3
(2), taking
\displaystyle A:=\mathcal{Z}_{0}
,
\displaystyle B:=\mathcal{Z}_{0}\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1}}\mathcal{Z}_{1}
,
\displaystyle C:=\mathcal{Z}
,
\displaystyle f
equal to the identification map of
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}
with
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1}}1\subset\mathcal{Z}_{0}\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1}}\mathcal{Z}_{1}
, and
\displaystyle g:=j
. Let us show that all hypothesis of Lemma
6.3
(2) are satisfied. First
\displaystyle j_{|A}
is obviously injective, and
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1}}\mathcal{Z}_{1}
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}
are domains (Prop.
6.4
and
5.21
). Moreover
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{1}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})
is a free module of rank
\displaystyle l^{m}
over its subring
\displaystyle(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1})(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})
(
[
32
,
\displaystyle\lx@sectionsign
21.2-21.3]
,
[
30
,
\displaystyle\lx@sectionsign
6]
). By Lemma
6.2
the
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})
-module
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})\otimes_{(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1})(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})}\mathcal{Z}_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})
is therefore free of rank
\displaystyle l^{m}
, and from (
146
) we deduce that
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1}}\mathcal{Z}_{1}
is a free
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}
-module of finite rank (=
\displaystyle l^{mn}
). Since
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cong\mathcal{O}(G^{2g+n})
is Noetherian (by Prop.
5.17
(1) and because
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(G^{\times(2g+n)})
is finitely generated), this last fact implies that
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1}}\mathcal{Z}_{1}
is integral over
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}
by the argument in Lemma
6.3
(1). This concludes the proof of injectivity.

The surjectivity of
\displaystyle j
follows from Lemma
6.3
(1), taking
\displaystyle A:=\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}
and
\displaystyle B:=\mathcal{Z}
. Indeed, we already know that
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}
is a domain, and by injectivity of
\displaystyle j
(proved above) and Proposition
6.4
the ring
\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}
is integrally closed. Since
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}
is a Noetherian ring and
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1}}\mathcal{Z}_{1}
is a finite
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}
-module (see above),
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1}}\mathcal{Z}_{1}
is a Noetherian ring, and therefore
\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}
is a Noetherian ring as well. But
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}
is a finite
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}
-module (Prop.
5.17
(3)), and
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}=j(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\otimes 1)\subset\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}
, so
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}
is also a finite
\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}
-module, and therefore a Noetherian
\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}
-module. We noted in Corollary
5.25
that
\displaystyle Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}\bigr)=Q\bigl(\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}\bigr)
. Therefore Lemma
6.3
(1) applies, and proves that
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}=\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}
.

(2) Since
\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}:\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\to\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
is injective (Prop.
5.27
), the conclusion of item (1) above and a similar reasoning imply that
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}\otimes_{\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}\left((\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1})(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})\right)}\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})\bigr)
is integrally closed, and that
\displaystyle j_{2}
is injective. To prove surjectivity, we use again Lemma
6.3
(1), taking
\displaystyle A=\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}}):=\mathrm{Im}(j_{2})
and
\displaystyle B=\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}})
. It remains to show that
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}})
is a Noetherian
\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}})
-module and that
\displaystyle Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}})\bigr)=Q\bigl(\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}})\bigr)
.

For the first claim we know that
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}})
is a finite Noetherian
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}
-module and a Noetherian ring (Rk.
5.18
), and
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}=j_{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\otimes 1)\subset\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}})\subset\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}})
. Therefore
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}})
is a finitely generated
\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}})
-module. Also
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}\otimes_{\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}\left((\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1})(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})\right)}\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})\bigr)
is finitely generated over
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}\otimes 1
, so it is a Noetherian ring. Therefore its image by
\displaystyle j_{2}
,
\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}})
, is also a Noetherian ring. As we saw
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}})
is finite over
\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}})
, it is thus a Noetherian
\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}})
-module, as expected.

Finally let us check that
\displaystyle Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}})\bigr)=Q\bigl(\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}})\bigr)
. We have
\displaystyle Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}})\bigr))=\mathcal{Z}\bigl(Q(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}})\bigr)
by Lemma
2.2
(2). Let us write

S:=\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}),\quad\mathcal{Z}_{1}(S):=\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})\bigr),\quad(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1})(S):=\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})\bigr).

We have

Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}\otimes_{(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1})(S)}\mathcal{Z}_{1}(S)\bigr)=Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}\bigr)\otimes_{(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1})(S)}\mathcal{Z}_{1}(S)

since the latter is a field (it embeds by
\displaystyle j_{2}
in the domain
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}}
). Then

Q\bigl(\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}})\bigr)=j_{2}\bigl(Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}\otimes_{(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1})(S)}\mathcal{Z}_{1}(S)\bigr)\bigr)=j_{2}\bigl(Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}\bigr)\otimes_{(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1})(S)}\mathcal{Z}_{1}(S)\bigr).

We saw at the end of the proof of Theorem
5.28
that this is
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(Q(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}}))
, so
\displaystyle Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}})\bigr)=Q\bigl(\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}})\bigr)
and the proof of surjectivity of
\displaystyle j_{2}
is completed.
∎

Let
\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}
be either
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
or
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}}
. The following result is of the same flavour as
[
32
, Prop. 20.2(ii)]
.

Corollary 6.5
.

The ring
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(\mathfrak{A})
coincides with the trace ring of
\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}
, and it is a free
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})
-module of rank
\displaystyle l^{mn}
(for
\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}=\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
) or
\displaystyle l^{m(n+1)}
(for
\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}=\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}}
).

Proof.

The first claim follows from Th.
6.1
(
i.e.
, that
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(\mathfrak{A})
is integrally closed and Noetherian) and Th.
5.24
. Indeed, if a domain
\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}
has Noetherian and integrally closed center
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}
and is such that
\displaystyle Q(\mathfrak{A})
has finite dimension over
\displaystyle Q(\mathcal{Z})
, then
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}
coincides with the
trace ring
of
\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}
(see
[
65
, Th. 10.1]
). Denote by
\displaystyle t_{\rm red}\colon Q(\mathfrak{A})\rightarrow Q(\mathcal{Z}\bigl(\mathfrak{A})\bigr)
the reduced trace map of the central simple algebra
\displaystyle Q(\mathfrak{A})
(see eg.
[
65
, §9]
). Because
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(\mathfrak{A})
is the trace ring of
\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}
, we have
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(\mathfrak{A})=t_{\rm red}(\mathfrak{A})
. Trivially the inclusion map
\displaystyle i\colon\mathcal{Z}(\mathfrak{A})\rightarrow Q(\mathfrak{A})
satisfies
\displaystyle t_{\rm red}\circ i=\mathrm{id}
, so
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(\mathfrak{A})
is a direct summand of
\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}
as a
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})
-module. But
\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}
is free over
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathfrak{A})
, so
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(\mathfrak{A})
is a projective
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})
-module. Since
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\cong\mathcal{O}\bigl(G^{\times(2g+n)}\bigr)
and
\displaystyle G^{\times(2g+n)}
is simply-connected and semisimple, as at the end of the proof of
[
12
, Th. 4.9]
we can use Bass’ cancellation theorem and Marlin’s computation of
\displaystyle K_{0}(G^{\times(2g+n)})
in
[
59
]
to deduce that the module is free. The rank computation follows from
\displaystyle[Q(\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})):Q(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}))]=l^{mn}
(Cor.
5.25
) and
\displaystyle\bigl[Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}})\bigr):Q\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\bigr)\bigr]=l^{m(n+1)}
(Cor.
5.30
).
∎

In the rest of this section we describe the
\displaystyle\Gamma_{\epsilon}
-invariant elements of the centers in Th.
6.1
. Recall that if
\displaystyle A
is a module-algebra over a Hopf algebra (or a group)
\displaystyle H
, then we denote by
\displaystyle A^{H}
the subalgebra of
\displaystyle H
-invariant elements in
\displaystyle A
. More generally, if
\displaystyle B\subset A
is any subalgebra then the subspace
\displaystyle B^{H}
is actually a subalgebra (of
\displaystyle B
).

Recall that
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(G)^{G}
is the polynomial algebra on the
\displaystyle m
generators
\displaystyle Q_{j}
defined by

(150)

Q_{j}(a):=\mathrm{Tr}\bigl(\rho_{\varpi_{j}}(a)\bigr),

where
\displaystyle\rho_{\varpi_{j}}\colon G\to\mathrm{End}(V_{\varpi_{j}})
is the
\displaystyle j
-th fundamental representation of
\displaystyle G
(see
[
71
, Th. 6.1]
). Also, since
\displaystyle\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}
is a morphism of Hopf algebras with values in
\displaystyle U(\mathfrak{g})
, we have

(151)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\begin{split}&\bigl\langle\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h)\big(\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}^{*}(\varphi)\bigr),x\bigr\rangle^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}_{\epsilon}\overset{\eqref{defCoad}}{=}\bigl\langle\varphi,\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}(h)_{(1)}\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}(x)S\bigl(\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}(h)_{(2)}\bigr)\bigr\rangle_{\mathrm{cl}}\\
=\>\,&\bigl\langle\varphi,\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}(x)S\bigl(\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}(h)_{(2)}\bigr)\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}(h)_{(1)}\bigr\rangle_{\mathrm{cl}}=\varepsilon(h)\bigl\langle\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}^{*}(\varphi),x\bigr\rangle^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}_{\epsilon}\end{split}

for every
\displaystyle\varphi\in\mathcal{O}(G)^{G}
and
\displaystyle h,x\in\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
, where
\displaystyle\langle\text{-},\text{-}\rangle_{\mathrm{cl}}:\mathcal{O}(G)\times U(\mathfrak{g})\to\mathbb{C}
is the usual duality pairing; the second equality is by the trace-like property of
\displaystyle\varphi
while the last equality uses that
\displaystyle U(\mathfrak{g})
is co-commutative.

The equality (
151
) shows that
\displaystyle\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}^{*}
embeds
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(G)^{G}
in
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}^{\Gamma_{\epsilon}}=\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\Gamma_{\epsilon}}
.

Denote

\mathcal{O}\bigl[\mathcal{X}_{G}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ})\bigr]:=\bigl(\mathcal{O}(G)^{\otimes(2g+n)}\bigr)^{G}

where the invariant elements are defined with respect to the diagonal coadjoint action of
\displaystyle G
. The notation
\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{G}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ})
stands for the character variety
\displaystyle\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Grp}}\bigl(\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ}),G)\bigr)/\!/G
of the oriented surface of genus
\displaystyle g
with
\displaystyle n+1
boundary components, and the above equality is the definition of its coordinate ring. We moreover define

\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{\rm inv}:=(\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}^{*})^{(2g+n)}\bigl(\mathcal{O}\bigl[\mathcal{X}_{G}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ})\bigr]\bigr)\subset\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}).

Also, let us write
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}:=\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})
,
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}:=\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})
,
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{1}:=\mathcal{Z}_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})
and
\displaystyle(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1})(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})=\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})
.

Corollary 6.6
.

(i) The following multiplication maps are isomorphisms of algebras

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{\mathrm{inv}}\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{\mathrm{inv}}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1}}\mathcal{Z}_{1}

\displaystyle\displaystyle\to\mathcal{Z}^{\Gamma_{\epsilon}}

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\Gamma_{\epsilon}}\otimes_{\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}((\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1})(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}))}\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}(\mathcal{Z}_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}))

\displaystyle\displaystyle\to\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{u_{\epsilon}})^{\Gamma_{\epsilon}}.

(ii) We have

(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1})(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})=\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}^{*}\bigl(\mathcal{O}(G)^{G}\bigr)=\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}^{*}\bigl(\mathbb{C}[Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{m}]\bigr)

and the equalities
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{u_{\epsilon}}=\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\Gamma_{\epsilon}}=\mathcal{Z}_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})
.

(iii) The elements
\displaystyle(\mathrm{qTr}_{l\lambda})_{|\epsilon}
,
\displaystyle\lambda\in P_{+}
, defined after (
136
) form a basis of
\displaystyle(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1})(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})
.

Proof.

(i) As
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}=(\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}^{*})^{(2g+n)}\bigl(\mathcal{O}(G)^{\otimes(2g+n)}\bigr)
, the definition of
\displaystyle\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}
and the equivariance (
151
) imply

\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{\Gamma_{\epsilon}}=(\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}^{*})^{(2g+n)}\left(\bigl(\mathcal{O}(G)^{\otimes(2g+n)}\bigr)^{U(\mathfrak{g})}\right)=\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{\rm inv}.

Also
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{1}
is
\displaystyle(\Gamma_{l})_{\epsilon}
-invariant (since it is the specialization of the
\displaystyle U_{q}
-invariant algebra
\displaystyle 1^{\otimes 2g}\otimes\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{0,n})
), and using the isomorphism
\displaystyle j
of Th.
6.1
it is immediate that
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}
is a
\displaystyle\Gamma_{\epsilon}
-module algebra. These facts imply that the first multiplication map is an isomorphism. Since
\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
is
\displaystyle\Gamma_{\epsilon}
-equivariant, one obtains from Theorem
6.1
(2) that the second multiplication map is an isomorphism.

(ii) The second equality follows from (
150
). Let us identify the maximal torus
\displaystyle T\subset G
with
\displaystyle{\rm Hom}_{\rm Grp}(2lP,\mathbb{C}^{\times})
, and thus
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}[2lP]
with the coordinate ring
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(T)
. The Chevalley isomorphism,
i.e.
the restriction map
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(G)^{G}\to\mathcal{O}(T)^{W}
, is an isomorphism. Let us identify
\displaystyle U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}(\mathfrak{h})
with the group algebra
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}[P]
via the map
\displaystyle K_{\mu}\mapsto\mu
for all
\displaystyle\mu\in P
. Then
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}(\mathfrak{h})\bigr):=U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}(\mathfrak{h})\cap\mathcal{Z}_{0}\bigl(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}\bigr)=\mathbb{C}[K_{l\mu},\mu\in P]
is identified with
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}[lP]
, and it is proved in
[
30
, Prop. 6.3]
(or
[
32
, §21.2]
) that
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})
is identified with
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}[2lP]^{W}=\mathcal{O}(T)^{W}=\mathcal{O}(G)^{G}
as a subalgebra of
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})
. Since
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\colon\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})\to\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})
is
\displaystyle\Gamma_{\epsilon}
-equivariant and an isomorphism (Lemma
6.2
), the equality
\displaystyle(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1})(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})=\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}^{*}\bigl(\mathcal{O}(G)^{G}\bigr)
follows.

One obtains the equality
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{u_{\epsilon}}=\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})
from the equivariant embedding
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}\colon\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\to U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
(as usual the source and target are given the actions
\displaystyle{\rm coad}^{r}
(
21
) and
\displaystyle{\rm ad}^{r}
(
30
)) and the fact that
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})=(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}}
. Since
\displaystyle\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}^{*}\bigl(\mathcal{O}(G)^{G}\bigr)=(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1})(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})\subset\mathcal{Z}_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})
, the equality
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{u_{\epsilon}}=\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})
and the fact that the multiplication map
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{\rm inv}\otimes_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{\rm inv}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1}}\mathcal{Z}_{1}\to\mathcal{Z}^{\Gamma_{\epsilon}}
is an isomorphism eventually give

\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\Gamma_{\epsilon}}=\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})^{\Gamma_{\epsilon}}=\mathcal{Z}_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}).

(iii) From App.
A
we have
\displaystyle(\mathcal{P}_{\rho}\circ\Phi_{0,1})(\mathrm{qTr}_{l\lambda})=\mathrm{ch}^{(2)}_{l\lambda}
. The elements
\displaystyle\mathrm{ch}^{(2)}_{l\lambda}
,
\displaystyle\lambda\in P_{+}
, form a basis of the ring
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}[K_{2l\mu},\mu\in P]^{W}
, and
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})=\mathbb{C}[K_{2l\mu},\mu\in P]^{W}
(see (ii) above). The conclusion follows from Lemma
6.2
.

This concludes the proof. ∎

Remark 6.7
.

Recall that
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon})
is the specialization to
\displaystyle q=\epsilon
of the central algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{A})=(\Phi_{0,1}^{A})^{-1}\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})\bigr)
(see Lemma
6.2
). Since
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{A}=\mathcal{O}_{A}
as
\displaystyle\Gamma_{A}
-modules and
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}^{A}
is an algebra embedding, the inclusion
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}^{A}\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{A})\bigr)\subset\mathcal{Z}(U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})
from Remark
5.8
(2) together with Cor.
A.3
below imply that
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{A})=\mathcal{Z}_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{A})=\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\Gamma_{A}}
. Thus we can reformulate the last claim of Cor.
6.6
as the fact that

\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\Gamma_{\epsilon}}=(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\Gamma_{A}})_{|q=\epsilon}.

More generally, for any
\displaystyle g
,
\displaystyle n
one can prove that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\Gamma_{\epsilon}}=(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\Gamma_{A}})_{|q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}=\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}
. This will be developed in
[
13
]
.

Appendix A
The quantum Killing form and
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}

This appendix justifies some properties of
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}
used in the proof of Lemma
6.2
. They rely on the quantum Killing form.

Recall the pairing
\displaystyle\rho:U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{-})^{\rm cop}\times U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}(\mathfrak{b}_{+})\to\mathbb{C}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})
from (
77
). Observe that any element of
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
can be written as a linear combination of elements of the form
\displaystyle S(X_{-})K_{\lambda}X_{+}
with
\displaystyle X_{\pm}\in U_{q}(\mathfrak{n}_{\pm})
and
\displaystyle\lambda\in P
; the same remark applies with elements of the form
\displaystyle S(X_{+})K_{\lambda}X_{-}
.
12
12

12

Note that this is different from the triangular decompositions
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}=U_{q}(\mathfrak{n}_{-})U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}(\mathfrak{h})U_{q}(\mathfrak{n}_{+})
and
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}=U_{q}(\mathfrak{n}_{+})U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}(\mathfrak{h})U_{q}(\mathfrak{n}_{-})
because the subalgebras
\displaystyle U_{q}(\mathfrak{n}_{\pm})
are not stable by the antipode
\displaystyle S
.
Extend the ground field
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})
by an element
\displaystyle q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}^{1/2}
such that
\displaystyle(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}^{1/2})^{2}=q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
. We define a bilinear map
\displaystyle(\>\,|\,\>):U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}\times U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}\to\mathbb{C}\bigl(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}^{1/2}\bigr)
by

(152)

\bigl(S(X_{-})K_{\lambda}X_{+}\,\big|\,S(Y_{+})K_{\mu}Y_{-}\bigr)=q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}^{D(\lambda,\mu)/2}\,\rho\bigl(X_{-},Y_{+}\bigr)\,\rho\bigl(Y_{-},X_{+}\bigr)

for all
\displaystyle X_{\pm},Y_{\pm}\in U_{q}(\mathfrak{n}_{\pm})
, and
\displaystyle\lambda,\mu\in P
. Recall that
\displaystyle q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
is such that
\displaystyle(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})^{D}=q
and we have to use this extension of
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q)
because
\displaystyle\textstyle(\lambda,\mu)\in\frac{1}{D}\mathbb{Z}
in general.

The bilinear map
\displaystyle(\>\,|\,\>)
is a variant of the
quantum Killing form

\displaystyle\kappa
defined in
[
73
, Def. 3.78]
. We have to introduce a different version of this bilinear form because here we use the map
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}:\mathcal{O}_{q}\to U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
,
\displaystyle\alpha\mapsto(\alpha\otimes\mathrm{id})(RR^{fl})
while
[
73
]
uses the map
\displaystyle I:\mathcal{O}_{q}\to U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
,
\displaystyle\alpha\mapsto(\alpha\otimes\mathrm{id})(R^{-1}(R^{fl})^{-1})
. The defining formula (
152
) is indeed designed so that the following theorem holds:

Theorem A.1
.

(i) For all
\displaystyle\alpha\in\mathcal{O}_{q}
and
\displaystyle x\in U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
we have

(153)

\bigl(\Phi_{0,1}(\alpha)\,\big|\,x\bigr)=\langle\alpha,x\rangle^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}

where we recall that
\displaystyle\langle\alpha,-\rangle^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
denotes evaluation of
\displaystyle\alpha
extended from
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
to
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
, see §
4.2
.

(ii) The form
\displaystyle(\>\,|\,\>)
is non degenerate and is invariant under the adjoint action, in the following sense:

\forall\,h,x,y\in U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}},\quad\bigl(\mathrm{ad}^{r}(h)(x)\,|\,y\bigr)=\bigl(x\,\big|\,\mathrm{ad}^{l}(h)(y)\bigr)

where
\displaystyle\mathrm{ad}^{r}(h)(x)=\textstyle\sum_{(h)}S(h_{(1)})xh_{(2)}
and
\displaystyle\mathrm{ad}^{l}(h)(x)=\textstyle\sum_{(h)}h_{(1)}xS(h_{(2)})
.

(iii) Recall that
\displaystyle(U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{\mathrm{lf}}
denotes the subspace of elements in
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
which have finite-dimensional orbit under
\displaystyle\mathrm{ad}^{r}
. There is a linear map
\displaystyle J_{q}\colon(U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{\rm lf}\to\mathcal{O}_{q}
uniquely characterized by

\forall\,x\in(U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{\mathrm{lf}},\>\>\forall\,y\in U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}},\quad\langle J_{q}(x),y\rangle^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}=(x\,|\,y).

The map
\displaystyle J_{q}
is the inverse of
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}\colon\mathcal{O}_{q}\to(U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{\rm lf}
, and hence an isomorphism of
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
-modules
\displaystyle\bigl((U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{\mathrm{lf}},\mathrm{ad}^{r}\bigr)\overset{\sim}{\to}\bigl(\mathcal{O}_{q},\mathrm{coad}^{r}\bigr)
, with
\displaystyle\mathrm{coad}^{r}
from (
21
)

Proof.

(i) We use the notation
\displaystyle X_{\pm}^{\boldsymbol{s}}
for PBW monomials of
\displaystyle U_{q}(\mathfrak{n}_{\pm})
introduced in Lemma
4.1
, and the notation
\displaystyle\boldsymbol{1}_{\lambda}
for projection on weight spaces introduced in the proof of item 2 of that lemma. In the second equality below we take advantage of the fact that
\displaystyle R=S^{\otimes 2}(R)=S^{\otimes 2}(\Theta\hat{R})=S^{\otimes 2}(\hat{R})\Theta
. Let
\displaystyle\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathbb{N}^{N}
and recall from (
79
) the orthogonality of PBW monomials with respect to
\displaystyle\rho
,
i.e.

\displaystyle\rho(X_{-}^{\boldsymbol{s}},X_{+}^{\boldsymbol{a}})=\delta_{\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a}}\rho(X_{-}^{\boldsymbol{a}},X_{+}^{\boldsymbol{a}})
. We thus have

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bigl(\Phi_{0,1}(\alpha)\,\big|\,S(X_{+}^{\boldsymbol{a}})K_{\mu}X_{-}^{\boldsymbol{b}}\bigr)=\bigl((\alpha\otimes\mathrm{id})(RR^{fl})\,\big|\,S(X_{+}^{\boldsymbol{a}})K_{\mu}X_{-}^{\boldsymbol{b}}\bigr)\quad\text{\footnotesize by def of $\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}$}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bigl((\alpha\otimes\mathrm{id})\bigl(S^{\otimes 2}(R)R^{fl}\bigr)\,\big|\,S(X_{+}^{\boldsymbol{a}})K_{\mu}X_{-}^{\boldsymbol{b}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bigl((\alpha\otimes\mathrm{id})\bigl(S^{\otimes 2}(\hat{R})\Theta^{2}\hat{R}^{fl}\bigr)\,\big|\,S(X_{+}^{\boldsymbol{a}})K_{\mu}X_{-}^{\boldsymbol{b}}\bigr)\quad\text{\footnotesize by remark above}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{t},\lambda}\alpha\bigl(S(X_{+}^{\boldsymbol{s}})\boldsymbol{1}_{\lambda}X_{-}^{\boldsymbol{t}}\bigr)\frac{\bigl(S(X_{-}^{\boldsymbol{s}})K_{2\lambda}X_{+}^{\boldsymbol{t}}\,\big|\,S(X_{+}^{\boldsymbol{a}})K_{\mu}X_{-}^{\boldsymbol{b}}\bigr)}{\rho(X_{-}^{\boldsymbol{s}},X_{+}^{\boldsymbol{s}})\,\rho(X_{-}^{\boldsymbol{t}},X_{+}^{\boldsymbol{t}})}\quad\text{\footnotesize by Lemma \ref{lemRhoVsRMat}(1)}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{t},\lambda}\alpha\bigl(S(X_{+}^{\boldsymbol{s}})\boldsymbol{1}_{\lambda}X_{-}^{\boldsymbol{t}}\bigr)\,q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}^{D(\lambda,\mu)}\frac{\rho(X_{-}^{\boldsymbol{s}},X_{+}^{\boldsymbol{a}})\rho(X_{-}^{\boldsymbol{b}},X_{+}^{\boldsymbol{t}})}{\rho(X_{-}^{\boldsymbol{s}},X_{+}^{\boldsymbol{s}})\rho(X_{-}^{\boldsymbol{t}},X_{+}^{\boldsymbol{t}})}\quad\text{\footnotesize by \eqref{qKillingnous}}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda}\alpha\bigl(S(X_{+}^{\boldsymbol{a}})\boldsymbol{1}_{\lambda}X_{-}^{\boldsymbol{b}}\bigr)\,q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}^{D(\lambda,\mu)}\frac{\rho(X_{-}^{\boldsymbol{a}},X_{+}^{\boldsymbol{a}})\rho(X_{-}^{\boldsymbol{b}},X_{+}^{\boldsymbol{b}})}{\rho(X_{-}^{\boldsymbol{a}},X_{+}^{\boldsymbol{a}})\rho(X_{-}^{\boldsymbol{b}},X_{+}^{\boldsymbol{b}})}\quad\text{\footnotesize by orthogonality}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\alpha\!\left[S(X_{+}^{\boldsymbol{a}})\left({\textstyle\sum_{\lambda}}q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}^{D(\lambda,\mu)}\boldsymbol{1}_{\lambda}\right)X_{-}^{\boldsymbol{b}}\right]=\alpha\bigl(S(X_{+}^{\boldsymbol{a}})K_{\mu}X_{-}^{\boldsymbol{b}}\bigr)\quad\text{\footnotesize by def of $\displaystyle\boldsymbol{1}_{\lambda}$.}

(ii) The non-degeneracy of
\displaystyle(\>\,|\,\>)
can be deduced from the non-degeneracy of
\displaystyle\rho

[
73
, Th. 3.92]
. The ad-invariance is obtained by direct computations similar to the ones in
[
73
, Prop. 3.79]
.

(iii) The formula
\displaystyle\langle J_{q}(x),y\rangle^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}:=(x\,|\,y)
for all
\displaystyle x,y\in U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
defines a linear map
\displaystyle J_{q}:U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}\to(U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{*}
. It is injective, since
\displaystyle(\>\,|\,\>)
is non degenerate. Moreover, due to the ad-invariance property of
\displaystyle(\>\,|\,\>)
, the map
\displaystyle J_{q}
intertwines the
\displaystyle H
-actions
\displaystyle\mathrm{ad}^{r}
and
\displaystyle\mathrm{coad}^{r}
:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bigl\langle\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h)\bigl(J_{q}(x)\bigr),y\bigr\rangle^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\bigl\langle J_{q}(x),\mathrm{ad}^{l}(h)(y)\bigr\rangle^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\bigl(x\,\big|\,\mathrm{ad}^{l}(h)(y)\bigr)=\bigl(\mathrm{ad}^{r}(h)(x)\,\big|\,y\bigr)=\bigl\langle J_{q}\bigl(\mathrm{ad}^{r}(h)(x)\bigr),y\bigr\rangle^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}.

This fact allows one to deduce that
\displaystyle J_{q}
actually takes values in
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}\subset(U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{*}
, as done in
[
73
, Prop. 3.116]
. Let us give some details within our conventions for completeness. The formula (
152
) shows that for all
\displaystyle\lambda\in P_{+}
the linear form
\displaystyle J_{q}(K_{-2\lambda})
vanishes on elements
\displaystyle S(Y_{+})K_{\mu}Y_{-}
such that
\displaystyle Y_{+}
or
\displaystyle Y_{-}
belongs to
\displaystyle\ker(\varepsilon_{U_{q}})
. Recall that for
\displaystyle\lambda\in P_{+}
,
\displaystyle V_{-w_{0}(\lambda)}
is the simple
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
-module with highest weight
\displaystyle-w_{0}(\lambda)
, hence with lowest weight
\displaystyle-\lambda\in-P_{+}
. Let
\displaystyle v
be a (unique up to scalar) lowest weight vector. Define
\displaystyle\psi_{-\lambda}\in\mathcal{O}_{q}
by
\displaystyle\psi_{-\lambda}(x)=v^{*}(x\cdot v)
for every
\displaystyle x\in U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
, where
\displaystyle v^{*}\in V_{-w_{0}(\lambda)}^{*}
is such that
\displaystyle v^{*}(v)=1
and
\displaystyle v^{*}
vanishes on all other weight spaces in
\displaystyle V_{-w_{0}(\lambda)}
. Then we have

\forall\,\mu\in P,\quad\bigl\langle J_{q}(K_{-2\lambda}),K_{\mu}\bigr\rangle^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}=(K_{-2\lambda}\,|\,K_{\mu})=q^{-(\lambda,\mu)}=\langle\psi_{-\lambda},K_{\mu}\rangle^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}.

Also, it is readily seen by very definition of
\displaystyle\psi_{-\lambda}
that

\displaystyle\displaystyle\begin{split}\bigl\langle\psi_{-\lambda},S(Y_{+})K_{\mu}Y_{-}\bigr\rangle^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}&=\varepsilon(Y_{+})\psi_{-\lambda}(K_{\mu})\varepsilon(Y_{-})\\
&=\bigl(K_{-2\lambda}\,\big|\,S(Y_{+})K_{\mu}Y_{-}\bigr)=J_{q}(K_{-2\lambda})\bigl(S(Y_{+})K_{\mu}Y_{-}\bigr)\end{split}

for all
\displaystyle Y_{\pm}\in U_{q}(\mathfrak{n}_{\pm})
and
\displaystyle\mu\in P
. Therefore
\displaystyle J_{q}(K_{-2\lambda})=\psi_{-\lambda}\in\mathcal{O}_{q}
. Now recall that we have
\displaystyle(U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{\rm lf}=\textstyle\bigoplus_{\lambda\in P_{+}}\mathrm{ad}^{r}(U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})(K_{-2\lambda})
(see
[
73
, Th. 3.113]
, where the different adjoint action implies that
\displaystyle K_{-2\lambda}
is replaced with
\displaystyle K_{2\lambda}
in their formula). Hence, by equivariance of
\displaystyle J_{q}
,

J_{q}\bigl((U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{\rm lf}\bigr)=\textstyle\bigoplus_{\lambda\in P_{+}}\mathrm{coad}^{r}(U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})\bigl(J_{q}(K_{-2\lambda})\bigr)=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in P_{+}}\mathrm{coad}^{r}(U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})\bigl(\psi_{-\lambda}\bigr)\subset\mathcal{O}_{q}.

It now makes sense to check that
\displaystyle J_{q}
and
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}
are inverse each other, using non-degeneracy of the evaluation pairing and of the quantum Killing form,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bigl\langle J_{q}\bigl(\Phi_{0,1}(\alpha)\bigr),y\bigr\rangle^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}=\bigl(\Phi_{0,1}(\alpha)\,\big|\,y\bigr)=\langle\alpha,y\rangle^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}},

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bigl(\Phi_{0,1}\bigl(J_{q}(x)\bigr)\,\big|\,y\bigr)=\bigl\langle J_{q}(x),y\bigr\rangle^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}=(x\,|\,y)

for all
\displaystyle x,y\in U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
and
\displaystyle\alpha\in\mathcal{O}_{q}
.
∎

For the next result, let
\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\rho}:U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}\to U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}(\mathfrak{h})
be the projection given by

\forall\,X_{\pm}\in U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}(\mathfrak{n}_{\pm}),\>\>\forall\,\mu\in P,\quad\mathcal{P}_{\rho}(X_{+}K_{\mu}X_{-})=\varepsilon(X_{+})\varepsilon(X_{-})q^{-(\rho,\mu)}K_{\mu}

where
\displaystyle\rho
here denotes the half-sum of positive roots. Also let
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}^{U_{q}}
be the subspace of invariant elements under the right action
\displaystyle\mathrm{coad}^{r}
defined in (
21
).

Proposition A.2
.

(i)
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}\colon\mathcal{O}_{q}^{U_{q}}\to\mathcal{Z}(U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})
is an algebra isomorphism, where
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}^{U_{q}}\subset\mathcal{O}_{q}
is the subspace of invariant elements under the coadjoint action
\displaystyle{\rm coad}^{r}
from (
21
).

(ii) The quantum trace elements
\displaystyle\mathrm{qTr}_{\lambda}
(
\displaystyle\lambda\in P_{+}
) defined in (
136
) form a basis of
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}^{U_{q}}
.

(iii) The map
\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\rho}
restricts to an algebra embedding
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})\hookrightarrow U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}(\mathfrak{h})
and we have

(\mathcal{P}_{\rho}\circ\Phi_{0,1})(\mathrm{qTr}_{\lambda})=\sum_{\mu\in P(V_{\lambda})}K_{2\mu}.

for all
\displaystyle\lambda\in P_{+}
, where the sum runs over the set of weights
\displaystyle P(V_{\lambda})
of
\displaystyle V_{\lambda}
counted with multiplicity.

Proof.

For item (i) recall that
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})=(U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{U_{q}}=((U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{\rm lf})^{U_{q}}
, which is easy to see. By Th.
A.1
(iii),
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}
is an equivariant isomorphism
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}\overset{\sim}{\to}(U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{\rm lf}
. It thus establishes a vector space isomorphism between
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}^{U_{q}}
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})
. This is moreover an algebra morphism by formula (
28
) and the fact that
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}
is an algebra morphism
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}\to U_{q}
.

Item
\displaystyle(ii)
is proved as
[
73
, Lem. 3.119]
, and item
\displaystyle(iii)
is part of
[
73
, Th. 3.122]
where
\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\rho}
is denoted by
\displaystyle\gamma^{-1}\circ\mathcal{P}
(in
[
73
]
the last formula uses
\displaystyle K_{-2\mu}
because of their different conventions on
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}
).
∎

For all
\displaystyle\lambda\in P_{+}
let

\textstyle\mathrm{ch}^{(2)}_{\lambda}:=\sum_{\mu\in P(V_{\lambda})}K_{2\mu}\in\mathbb{Z}[K_{2\varpi_{1}}^{\pm 1},\ldots,K_{2\varpi_{m}}^{\pm 1}]\subset U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.6}{2P}}(\mathfrak{h}).

Note that the ring
\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}[K_{2\varpi_{1}}^{\pm 1},\ldots,K_{2\varpi_{m}}^{\pm 1}]
is isomorphic to
\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}[2P]
, and hence has a natural action of the Weyl group
\displaystyle W
. The elements
\displaystyle\mathrm{ch}^{(2)}_{\lambda}
are clearly
\displaystyle W
-invariant. Therefore Proposition
A.2
is entirely summed-up by the following commutative diagram

\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathbb{C}(q)[P_{+}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\mathrm{ch}^{(2)}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\mathrm{qTr}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\cong\hskip 16.38895pt\hskip 16.38895pt\hskip 16.38895pt\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\circlearrowleft$}}
\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathbb{C}(q)[K_{2\varpi_{1}}^{\pm 1},\ldots,K_{2\varpi_{m}}^{\pm 1}]^{W}=U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.6}{2P}}(\mathfrak{h})^{W}}
\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{q}^{U_{q}}=\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\Phi_{0,1}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\cong}
\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathcal{Z}(U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\mathcal{P}_{\rho}}

where
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q)[P_{+}]
is the algebra of the abelian monoid
\displaystyle P_{+}
with coefficients in
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q)
. The vertical arrow on the right is called
quantum Harish-Chandra map
. By a classical argument
[
44
, §22.5]
we have:

(154)

\text{the family }\bigl\{\mathrm{ch}^{(2)}_{\lambda}\,\big|\,\lambda\in P_{+}\bigr\}\text{ is a }\mathbb{Z}\text{-basis of the ring }\mathbb{Z}[K_{2\varpi_{1}}^{\pm 1},\ldots,K_{2\varpi_{m}}^{\pm 1}]^{W}.

It follows that the map
\displaystyle\mathrm{ch}^{(2)}
in the above diagram is an algebra isomorphism. We conclude that

\mathcal{P}_{\rho}:\mathcal{Z}(U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}(q)[K_{2\varpi_{1}}^{\pm 1},\ldots,K_{2\varpi_{m}}^{\pm 1}]^{W}=U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.6}{2P}}(\mathfrak{h})^{W}

is an algebra isomorphism, which is the quantum version of Harish-Chandra’s theorem.
13
13

13

Note that this proof, originally due to Baumann
[
16
]
, is very different from the one given, e.g., in
[
32
, §18]
, which reconstructs an element
\displaystyle z\in\mathcal{Z}(U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})
from
\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\rho}(z)
by induction, using a complicated algorithm.

To be on the safe side, we record that the commutation of the above diagram means that

(155)
