\textstyle\mathcal{P}_{\rho}^{-1}\left(\sum_{\lambda\in P_{+}}c_{\lambda}\,\mathrm{ch}^{(2)}_{\lambda}\right)=\Phi_{0,1}\left(\sum_{\lambda\in P_{+}}c_{\lambda}\,\mathrm{qTr}_{\lambda}\right)

where the coefficients
\displaystyle c_{\lambda}\in\mathbb{C}(q)
are equal to
\displaystyle 0
except for a finite number of them.

The properties of
\displaystyle\Phi_{A}^{\pm}
discussed in §
4.4
readily imply that the restriction of
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}
to
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{A}
takes values in
\displaystyle U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
. Hence we have an integral version

\Phi_{0,1}^{A}:\mathcal{O}_{A}\to(U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{\mathrm{lf}}:=U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}\cap(U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{\rm lf},\quad\alpha\mapsto(\alpha\otimes\mathrm{id})(RR^{fl}).

As a restriction of
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}
, it is an embedding of
\displaystyle\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
-modules. This allows for an integral version of Proposition
A.2
(i):

Corollary A.3
.

Denote by
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{A})
the
\displaystyle A
-subalgebra of
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{A}^{\Gamma_{A}}
generated by the quantum trace elements
\displaystyle\mathrm{qTr}_{\lambda}
,
\displaystyle\lambda\in P_{+}
. The restriction of
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}^{A}
to
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{A})
yields an algebra isomorphism
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{A})\overset{\sim}{\to}\mathcal{Z}(U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})
, and therefore
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{A})=\mathcal{O}_{A}^{\Gamma_{A}}
.

Proof.

This restriction is injective because so is
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}^{A}
. For surjectivity, let
\displaystyle z\in\mathcal{Z}(U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})
. By extension of scalars from
\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}
to
\displaystyle A
in (
154
), the elements
\displaystyle\textstyle\mathrm{ch}^{(2)}_{\lambda}
form an
\displaystyle A
-basis of the
\displaystyle A
-algebra
\displaystyle A[K_{2\varpi_{1}}^{\pm 1},\ldots,K_{2\varpi_{m}}^{\pm 1}]^{W}
, so we can write
\displaystyle\textstyle\mathcal{P}_{\rho}(z)=\sum_{\lambda}c_{\lambda}\,\mathrm{ch}^{(2)}_{\lambda}
with a finite set of coefficients
\displaystyle c_{\lambda}\in A
for
\displaystyle\lambda\in P_{+}
. From (
155
) it follows that
\displaystyle\textstyle\Phi_{0,1}^{A}\bigl(\sum_{\lambda}c_{\lambda}\,\mathrm{qTr}_{\lambda}\bigr)=z
.

For the second claim, recall that
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}^{A}
is an
\displaystyle U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
-equivariant injection
\displaystyle(\mathcal{O}_{A},\mathrm{coad}^{r})\to(U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}},\mathrm{ad}^{r})
. Since
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})=(U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{U_{A}}
(which is true for any Hopf algebra), we deduce from the first claim that
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{A})=\mathcal{O}_{A}^{U_{A}}=\mathcal{O}_{A}^{\Gamma_{A}}
.
∎

Remark A.4
.

The corollary implies that the quantum Harish-Chandra map
\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\rho}
restricts to an isomorphism
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})\to A[K_{2\varpi_{1}}^{\pm 1},\ldots,K_{2\varpi_{m}}^{\pm 1}]^{W}\subset U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}(\mathfrak{h})
. This was proved somewhat implicitly in
[
32
, Th. 18.3 and §21]
, by showing that their algorithm for the computation of
\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\rho}^{-1}
does not introduce denominators.

To conclude we discuss an integral form
\displaystyle(\>\,|\,\>)_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}
of the pairing (
152
) and its specialization at
\displaystyle\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
. First, at the level of Cartan parts, let us define
\displaystyle(\>\,|\,\>)_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}:U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}(\mathfrak{h})\times\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}(\mathfrak{h})\to\mathbb{C}\bigl[q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}^{\pm 1/2}\bigr]
by

(156)

\biggl(K_{\mu}\,\bigg|\,K_{s}\>{\textstyle\prod}_{i=1}^{m}\,K_{i}^{-\sigma(t_{i})}(K_{i};t_{i})_{q_{i}}\biggr)_{\!\!\!A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}=q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}^{D(\mu,s)/2}\prod_{i=1}^{m}q^{-(\mu,\alpha_{i})\sigma(t_{i})/2}\begin{pmatrix}(\mu,\alpha_{i})/2\\
t_{i}\end{pmatrix}_{\!\!q_{i}}

where
\displaystyle s
belongs to a chosen set
\displaystyle S\subset P
of representatives for the cosets in
\displaystyle P/Q
(we use the basis of
\displaystyle\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}(\mathfrak{h})
explained in §
4.3
). Now, using the notations introduced in (
83
) and (
84
) for the generators of
\displaystyle U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
and
\displaystyle\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
, observe from the expression of the pairing
\displaystyle\rho
in (
79
) that

(157)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\rho\!\left(\underline{F}_{\beta_{N}}^{s_{N}}\ldots\underline{F}_{\beta_{1}}^{s_{1}},E_{\beta_{N}}^{(r_{N})}\ldots E_{\beta_{1}}^{(r_{1})}\right)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\textstyle\prod_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{s_{i},t_{i}}q_{\beta_{i}}^{-s_{i}(s_{i}-1)/2},

(158)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\rho\!\left(F_{\beta_{N}}^{(s_{N})}\ldots F_{\beta_{1}}^{(s_{1})},\underline{E}_{\beta_{N}}^{t_{N}}\ldots\underline{E}_{\beta_{1}}^{t_{1}}\right)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\textstyle\prod_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{s_{i},t_{i}}q_{\beta_{i}}^{-s_{i}(s_{i}-1)/2}.

By definition of the integral forms
\displaystyle U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
and
\displaystyle\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
and their basis in §
4.3
, we get two pairings

\displaystyle\displaystyle\rho^{\prime}_{A}:U_{A}(\mathfrak{n}_{-})\times\Gamma_{A}(\mathfrak{n}_{+})\to A\quad\text{given by \eqref{intDrinfeldOnNilpotent1}}

\displaystyle\displaystyle\rho^{\prime\prime}_{A}:\Gamma_{A}(\mathfrak{n}_{-})\times U_{A}(\mathfrak{n}_{+})\to A\quad\text{given by \eqref{intDrinfeldOnNilpotent2}}

We can thus define
\displaystyle(\>\,|\,\>)_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}:U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}\times\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}\to\mathbb{C}\bigl[q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}^{\pm 1/2}\bigr]
by

(159)

\bigl(S(X_{-})K_{\nu}X_{+}\,|\,S(Y_{+})MY_{-}\bigr)_{\!A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}=(K_{\nu}\,|\,M)_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}\,\rho^{\prime}_{A}(X_{-},Y_{+})\,\rho^{\prime\prime}_{A}(Y_{-},X_{+})

for all
\displaystyle X_{\pm}\in U_{A}(\mathfrak{n}_{\pm})
,
\displaystyle Y_{\pm}\in\Gamma_{A}(\mathfrak{n}_{\pm})
, and
\displaystyle M\in\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}(\mathfrak{h})
. Note that
\displaystyle(\>\,|\,\>)_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}
is non-degenerate as it recovers
\displaystyle(\>\,|\,\>)
from (
152
) after extending scalars to
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}^{1/2})
. It is moreover ad-invariant in the sense of Theorem
A.1
, as a restriction of
\displaystyle(\>\,|\,\>)
.

Corollary A.5
.

\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}^{A}:\mathcal{O}_{A}\to(U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{\rm lf}
is an isomorphism of
\displaystyle\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
-modules, whose inverse is the restriction
\displaystyle J_{A}
of
\displaystyle J_{q}
to
\displaystyle(U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{\rm lf}
.

Proof.

By restriction to integral forms in Theorem
A.1
, we have

(160)

\forall\,\alpha\in\mathcal{O}_{A},\>\>\forall\,y\in\Gamma_{A}^{{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}},\quad\langle\alpha,y\rangle_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}=\bigl(\Phi_{0,1}^{A}(\alpha))\,\big|\,y\bigr)_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}\in A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}

and
\displaystyle J_{q}
restricts to
\displaystyle J_{A}:(U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{\mathrm{lf}}\to\mathcal{O}_{A}
characterized by
\displaystyle\bigl\langle J_{A}(x),y\bigr\rangle_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}=(x\,|\,y)_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}
for all
\displaystyle x\in(U_{A}^{{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}})^{\mathrm{lf}}
. Since
\displaystyle(\>\,|\,\>)_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}
is non degenerate, we have the result.
∎

Remark A.6
.

This corollary implies in particular that
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}^{A}(\mathcal{O}_{A})=(U_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{\rm lf}
, which answers positively a question asked before Prop. 2.24 in
[
12
]
.

Choose an arbitrary square root
\displaystyle\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}^{1/2}
of the
\displaystyle l
-th root of unity
\displaystyle\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
defined in (
93
) and denote by
\displaystyle(\>\,|\,\>)_{\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}:U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}\times\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}\to\mathbb{C}
the specialization of the pairing
\displaystyle(\>\,|\,\>)_{A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}
at
\displaystyle q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}^{1/2}\mapsto\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}^{1/2}
. We record the following property, which is a key-point in the proof of Lemma
6.2
:

Lemma A.7
.

The subspaces
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}_{\epsilon})\subset U^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}_{\epsilon}
and
\displaystyle\mathrm{ker}(\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon})\subset\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
are orthogonal with respect to the pairing
\displaystyle(\>\,|\,\>)_{\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}
, i.e.

\forall\,z_{0}\in\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}_{\epsilon}),\>\>\forall\,b\in\mathrm{ker}(\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}),\quad(z_{0}\,|\,b)_{\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}=0

Proof.

Using the formulas in (
157
) and (
158
), let us compute the pairing
\displaystyle(\>\,|\,\>)_{\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}
when the first argument belongs to
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(U^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}_{\epsilon})
, and the second argument is one of the basis elements of
\displaystyle\mathbb{F}\mathrm{r}_{\epsilon}
in Remark
4.7
:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\textstyle\biggl(S\!\left(\prod_{k=N}^{1}F_{\beta_{k}}^{lp_{k}}\right)K_{\mu}^{l}\left(\prod_{t=N}^{1}E_{\beta_{t}}^{ls_{t}}\right)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\,\bigg|\,S\!\textstyle\left(\prod_{i=N}^{1}E_{\beta_{i}}^{(n_{i})}\right)M\left(\prod_{j=N}^{1}F_{\beta_{j}}^{(r_{j})}\right)\biggr)_{\!\!\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>\,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\textstyle(K_{l\mu}\,|\,M)_{\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}\,\prod_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{n_{i},lp_{i}}\prod_{j=1}^{N}\delta_{r_{j},ls_{j}}.

This is
\displaystyle 0
when some
\displaystyle n_{i}
or
\displaystyle r_{j}
are not divisible by
\displaystyle l
, or
\displaystyle M
is in the ideal of
\displaystyle\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}(\mathfrak{h})
generated by the elements
\displaystyle K_{i}-1=K_{i}-K_{0}
(since
\displaystyle(K_{l\mu}\,|\,K_{i}-1)_{\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}=\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}^{D(\alpha_{i},l\mu)}-1=\epsilon^{l(\alpha_{i},\mu)}-1=0
).
∎

Appendix B
Lusztig’s modified quantum group and canonical basis

The aim of this appendix is to provide the details of the arguments in Lemma
4.8
, using Lusztig’s modified quantum group; the key-point is Lemma
B.1
.

B.1.
Definitions and relationships with
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{A}
and
\displaystyle\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}

The Lusztig
modified quantum group

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{U}}}
associated to the simply-connected root datum
[
54
, Chaps. 2, 3 and 23]
can be defined as follows (actually
\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{U}}}
here is obtained by replacing
\displaystyle Y=\check{Q}
with
\displaystyle Q
in Lusztig’s
\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{U}}}
). The elements of
\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{U}}}
are finite
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q)
-linear combinations of elements
\displaystyle x1_{\lambda}
with
\displaystyle x\in U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
and
\displaystyle\lambda\in P
; of course we have
\displaystyle(x_{1}+cx_{2})1_{\lambda}=(x_{1}1_{\lambda})+c(x_{2}1_{\lambda})
for all
\displaystyle c\in\mathbb{C}(q)
. The product in
\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{U}}}
is entirely determined by the product in
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
together with the following relations

1_{\lambda}1_{\mu}=\delta_{\lambda,\mu}1_{\lambda},\qquad K_{\alpha}1_{\lambda}=1_{\lambda}K_{\alpha}=q^{(\alpha,\lambda)}1_{\lambda},\qquad w1_{\lambda}=1_{\lambda+\nu}w

for all
\displaystyle\alpha\in Q
,
\displaystyle x,y\in U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
and
\displaystyle w\in U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
which has weight
\displaystyle\nu
under the adjoint action (
i.e.

\displaystyle K_{\alpha}wK_{\alpha}^{-1}=q^{(\alpha,\nu)}w
).

One can regard
\displaystyle 1_{\lambda}
as a substitute for the projector onto the weight subspaces of weight
\displaystyle\lambda
of the
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
-modules of type
\displaystyle 1
, and
\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{U}}}
as obtained from
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
by replacing
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}(\mathfrak{h})
with
\displaystyle\textstyle\bigoplus_{\lambda\in P}\mathbb{C}(q)1_{\lambda}
.

Note the decompositions
\displaystyle\textstyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{U}}}\,=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in P}U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}1_{\lambda}
and
\displaystyle\textstyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{U}}}\,=\bigoplus_{\lambda^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime\prime}\in P}1_{\lambda^{\prime}}U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}1_{\lambda^{\prime\prime}}
as vector spaces, corresponding to weight spaces for the right and left actions of
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
on
\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{U}}}
. There is also a substitute of the PBW basis: given bases
\displaystyle\{b^{\pm}\}
of
\displaystyle U_{q}(\mathfrak{n}_{\pm})
, the set of elements
\displaystyle b^{+}1_{\lambda}b^{-}
(or
\displaystyle b^{-}1_{\lambda}b^{+}
, or
\displaystyle b^{+}b^{-}1_{\lambda}
), where
\displaystyle\lambda\in P
, is a basis of
\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{U}}}
.

The integral form
\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{U}}}_{A}
is the
\displaystyle A
-algebra generated by the elements
\displaystyle E_{i}^{(p)}1_{\lambda}
and
\displaystyle F_{i}^{(p)}1_{\lambda}
, for all
\displaystyle i\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
,
\displaystyle p\in\mathbb{N}
,
\displaystyle\lambda\in P
. Given bases
\displaystyle\{b_{A}^{\pm}\}
of
\displaystyle\Gamma_{A}(\mathfrak{n}_{\pm})
, the set of elements
\displaystyle b_{A}^{+}1_{\lambda}b_{A}^{-}
(or
\displaystyle b_{A}^{-}1_{\lambda}b_{A}^{+}
, or
\displaystyle b_{A}^{+}b_{A}^{-}1_{\lambda}
), where
\displaystyle\lambda\in P
, is a basis of
\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{U}}}_{A}
. It inherits from
\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{U}}}
a structure of
\displaystyle\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
-bimodule; by extending the ground ring to
\displaystyle A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
and using (
71
), it becomes a
\displaystyle\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
-bimodule. By construction
\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{U}}}_{A}\!\!\!{}\textstyle\,=\bigoplus_{\lambda^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime\prime}\in P}1_{\lambda^{\prime}}\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}1_{\lambda^{\prime\prime}}=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in P}\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}1_{\lambda}
.

The algebra
\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{U}}}_{A}
has a canonical
\displaystyle A
-basis
\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{B}}}
having many remarkable properties
[
54
, Chap. 25 and 29]
. In particular, its elements are weight vectors under the left and right actions of
\displaystyle\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}(\mathfrak{h})
, and there is a canonical partition into finite sets

\textstyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{B}}}=\coprod_{\lambda\in P_{+}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{B}}}\!\![\lambda].

The algebra
\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{U}}}_{A}
has no unit, and it is not a Hopf algebra in a strict sense but is an instance of multiplier Hopf algebra in the sense of
[
72
]
. The canonical basis provides a very natural completion
\displaystyle\hat{{\mathbf{U}}}_{A}
, introduced by Lusztig: it is the set of (possibly infinite) sums of the form
\displaystyle\textstyle\sum_{b\in\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{B}}}}n_{b}b
, with
\displaystyle n_{b}\in A
. It is an algebra
[
55
]
, with unit
\displaystyle\textstyle\sum_{\lambda\in P}1_{\lambda}
. If we moreover denote by
\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{U}}}_{A}\!\!{}^{\hat{\otimes}2}
the set of (possibly infinite) sums of the form
\displaystyle\textstyle\sum_{b,b^{\prime}\in\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{B}}}}n_{b,b^{\prime}}b\otimes b^{\prime}
with
\displaystyle n_{b,b^{\prime}}\in A
, then we can define
\displaystyle\Delta:\>\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{U}}}_{A}\,\to\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{U}}}_{A}\!\!{}^{\hat{\otimes}2}
,
\displaystyle S:\>\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{U}}}_{A}\,\to\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{U}}}_{A}
and
\displaystyle\varepsilon:\>\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{U}}}_{A}\,\to A
by

\Delta(x1_{\lambda})=\sum_{(x),\>\mu\in P}x_{(1)}1_{\mu}\otimes x_{(2)}1_{\lambda-\mu},\quad S(x1_{\lambda})=1_{-\lambda}S(x),\quad\varepsilon(x1_{\lambda})=\varepsilon(x)\delta_{\lambda,0}

for all
\displaystyle x\in\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
and
\displaystyle\lambda\in P
, using the Hopf structure of
\displaystyle\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
on
\displaystyle x\in\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
.

For every lattice
\displaystyle Q\subset\Lambda\subset P
there is a natural algebra morphism
\displaystyle\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}\to\hat{{\mathbf{U}}}_{A}\otimes_{A}A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
given by

(161)

x^{+}yx^{-}\mapsto\sum_{\mu\in P}x^{+}y_{\mu}x^{-},\qquad\text{where }x^{\pm}\in\Gamma_{A}(\mathfrak{n}^{\pm}),\>\>y\in\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}(\mathfrak{h}),\>\>y_{\mu}=y1_{\mu}\in A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}1_{\mu}.

Indeed, by a reasoning on weights it can be shown that the sum is indeed of the form
\displaystyle\textstyle\sum_{b\in\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{B}}}}n_{b}b
, with
\displaystyle n_{b}\in A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
.

The morphism (
161
) is injective, and the proof of this fact is completely similar to the one of Lemma
B.1
below, which treats the specialized version.

The category
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{A}
(§
4.4
) is ribbon equivalent to the category of finite rank
unital

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{U}}}_{A}
-modules
\displaystyle M

[
54
, §23.1.4, §31.1.5-9, Th. 32.1.5]
. Here, unital means that each vector of
\displaystyle M
is annihilated by all but a finite number of the idempotents
\displaystyle 1_{\lambda}\in\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{U}}}_{A}
, and
\displaystyle\textstyle\sum_{\lambda\in P}1_{\lambda}
acts as the identity. The weight space decomposition
\displaystyle M=\textstyle\oplus_{\lambda\in P}M^{\lambda}
is given by
\displaystyle M^{\lambda}=1_{\lambda}M
; the action of
\displaystyle x\in\Gamma_{A}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
is given by
\displaystyle xm=(x1_{\lambda})m
for any
\displaystyle\lambda\in P
,
\displaystyle m\in M^{\lambda}
.

This permits us to consider
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{A}
as the dual space of
\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{U}}}_{A}
. More precisely, in
[
54
, §29.5]
Lusztig showed that
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{A}
is a free
\displaystyle A
-module, with a basis
\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{B}}}{\!\!{}^{*}}:=\{\varphi_{b}\}_{b\in\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{B}}}}
in duality with
\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{B}}}
. Therefore the bilinear form defined by

(162)

\langle\text{-},\text{-}\rangle_{A}^{\vbox{\hbox{\,\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}\,}}}:{\mathcal{O}}_{A}\times\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{U}}}_{A}\to A\ ,\ \langle\varphi_{b},b^{\prime}\rangle_{A}^{\vbox{\hbox{\,\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}\,}}}:=\delta_{b,b^{\prime}}

is a perfect pairing, that is: the maps
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{A}\to{\rm Hom}_{A}(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{U}}}_{A},A)
,
\displaystyle\varphi\mapsto\langle\varphi,\text{-}\rangle_{A}^{\vbox{\hbox{\,\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}\,}}}
, and
\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{U}}}_{A}\to{\rm Hom}_{A}(\mathcal{O}_{A},A)
,
\displaystyle x\mapsto\langle\text{-},x\rangle_{A}^{\vbox{\hbox{\,\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}\,}}}
, are bijective. Moreover
\displaystyle\langle\text{-},\text{-}\rangle_{A}^{\vbox{\hbox{\,\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}\,}}}
is a (multiplier) Hopf pairing. Note that, by very definition, the pairing
\displaystyle\langle\text{-},\text{-}\rangle_{A}^{\vbox{\hbox{\,\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}\,}}}
can be extended to
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{A}\times\hat{\mathbf{U}}_{A}\to A
. Using the embedding (
161
) for
\displaystyle\Lambda=Q
, the bilinear forms
\displaystyle\langle\text{-},\text{-}\rangle_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
and
\displaystyle\langle\text{-},\text{-}\rangle_{A}^{\vbox{\hbox{\,\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}\,}}}
are related as follows: given
\displaystyle x^{\pm}\in{\Gamma_{A}}(\mathfrak{n}^{\pm})
,
\displaystyle y\in\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}(\mathfrak{h})
we have (the sum has a finite number of non zero terms):

(163)

\langle\varphi,x^{+}yx^{-}\rangle_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}=\sum_{\mu\in P}\langle\varphi,x^{+}y_{\mu}x^{-}\rangle_{A}^{\vbox{\hbox{\,\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}\,}}},\qquad\text{with }y_{\mu}=y1_{\mu}\in A1_{\mu}.

By duality the elements of
\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{B}}}{\!\!{}^{*}}
are weight vectors for the left and right coregular actions of
\displaystyle\Gamma_{A}(\mathfrak{h})
. There is a decomposition into weight subspaces

(164)

\mathcal{O}_{A}=\bigoplus_{\mu,\nu\in P}{}_{\mu}\!\left(\mathcal{O}_{A}\right)_{\nu},

where

(165)

{}_{\mu}\!\left(\mathcal{O}_{A}\right)_{\nu}:=\left\{f\in\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}\mid K_{i}\rhd f=q_{i}^{(\check{\alpha}_{i},\mu)}f,(K_{i};l)_{q_{i}}\rhd f=(q_{i}^{(\check{\alpha}_{i},\mu)};l)_{q_{i}}f,\right.\\
\left.f\lhd K_{i}=q_{i}^{(\check{\alpha}_{i},\nu)}f,f\lhd(K_{i};l)_{q_{i}}=(q_{i}^{(\check{\alpha}_{i},\nu)};l)_{q_{i}}f,i=1,\ldots,m\right\}.

Using
\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{B}}}{\!\!{}^{*}}
Lusztig proved in
[
55
, Prop. 3.3]
that
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{A}
is generated as an algebra by certain matrix coefficients of the modules
\displaystyle{}_{A}V_{\varpi_{i}}
, with highest weights the fundamental weights
\displaystyle\varpi_{i}
,
\displaystyle i=1,\ldots,m
.

Clearly the existence of the dual basis
\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{B}}}{\!\!{}^{*}}
implies the identification
\displaystyle\hat{{\mathbf{U}}}_{A}\cong{\rm Hom}_{A}(\mathcal{O}_{A},A)
. Also,
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{A}
inherits from
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}
a bimodule structure over
\displaystyle\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
(and
\displaystyle\hat{{\mathbf{U}}}_{A}
), with actions
\displaystyle\rhd
,
\displaystyle\lhd
.

The next lemma uses the decomposition
\displaystyle\Gamma^{{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}}_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{g})=\Gamma_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{n}^{+})\Gamma^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{h})\Gamma_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{n}^{-})
, obtained by specializing to
\displaystyle q=\epsilon
the decomposition of
\displaystyle\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}
explained in §
4.3
.

Lemma B.1
.

For every lattice
\displaystyle Q\subset\Lambda\subset P
, the morphism (
161
) specializes to
\displaystyle q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}=\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
into a monomorphism

\iota^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}_{\epsilon}\colon\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}\hookrightarrow\hat{{\mathbf{U}}}_{\epsilon},\quad x^{+}yx^{-}\mapsto\sum_{\mu\in P}x^{+}y_{\mu}x^{-}\in\hat{{\mathbf{U}}}_{\epsilon}

where
\displaystyle x^{\pm}\in\Gamma_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{n}^{\pm})
,
\displaystyle y\in\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}(\mathfrak{h})
and
\displaystyle y_{\mu}:=y1_{\mu}\in\mathbb{C}1_{\mu}\subset\>\hat{\mathbf{U}}_{\epsilon}
.

Proof.

Let
\displaystyle\{b^{\pm}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be the canonical basis of
\displaystyle\Gamma_{A}(\mathfrak{n}_{\pm})
. Then the elements
\displaystyle b^{+}_{i}k_{s}b^{-}_{j}
form a basis of
\displaystyle\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}
, where
\displaystyle\{k_{s}\}_{s}
is a basis of
\displaystyle\Gamma_{A}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}(\mathfrak{h})
. There is a trigonal transformation with coefficients in
\displaystyle A
from the collection of elements
\displaystyle b^{+}_{i}1_{\mu}b^{-}_{j}\in\>\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{U}}}_{A}
to the canonical basis
\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{B}}}
of
\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{U}}}_{A}

[
54
, §25.2]
. It follows that any element in
\displaystyle\hat{\mathbf{U}}_{A}
can be written uniquely as an infinite sum
\displaystyle\textstyle\sum_{i,j\in I,\>\mu\in P}c_{i,j,\mu}b^{+}_{i}1_{\mu}b^{-}_{j}
with
\displaystyle c_{i,j,\mu}\in A
, and the same is true in the specialization to
\displaystyle\epsilon
. With these notations, the value of the morphism
\displaystyle\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}\to\hat{{\mathbf{U}}}_{\epsilon}
is

\displaystyle\textstyle\sum_{i,j,s}x_{i,j,s}b^{+}_{i}k_{s}b^{-}_{j}\mapsto\sum_{i,j,s}\sum_{\lambda\in P}x_{i,j,s}b^{+}_{i}(k_{s}1_{\lambda})b^{-}_{j}
, where the sum on the left is finite. If we assume this image is
\displaystyle 0
, the uniqueness implies that
\displaystyle\textstyle\sum_{s}x_{i,j,s}k_{s}1_{\lambda}=0
for all
\displaystyle i,j,\lambda
. We are thus left to show that the restriction
\displaystyle\textstyle\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}(\mathfrak{h})\to\prod_{\lambda\in P}\mathbb{C}1_{\lambda}
is injective. We first do the case
\displaystyle\Lambda=Q
, which relies on an argument completely similar to
[
31
, Th. 3.1]
. Note that on the basis elements
\displaystyle Y(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{t},\mathbf{0})_{\epsilon}
of
\displaystyle\Gamma_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{h})=\Gamma^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{h})
from (
85
), we have

(166)

\iota_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}\left(Y(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{t},\mathbf{0})_{\epsilon}\right)=\sum_{\lambda\in P}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{m}\epsilon^{-\sigma(t_{i})(\check{\alpha}_{i},\lambda)}_{i}\left(q_{i}^{(\check{\alpha}_{i},\lambda)};t_{i}\right)_{\epsilon_{i}}\right)1_{\lambda}\qquad\forall\,\mathbf{t}=(t_{1},\ldots,t_{m})\in\mathbb{N}^{m}

where the
\displaystyle\epsilon_{i}
subscript denotes specialization.
Assume that an element
\displaystyle\textstyle Y=\sum_{\mathbf{t}\in\mathbf{T}}c_{\mathbf{t}}Y(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{t},\mathbf{0})_{\epsilon}

\displaystyle\neq 0
is sent to
\displaystyle 0
. Here
\displaystyle\varnothing\neq\mathbf{T}\subset\mathbb{N}^{m}
is such that
\displaystyle c_{\mathbf{t}}\neq 0
for all
\displaystyle\mathbf{t}\in\mathbf{T}
. Let
\displaystyle\mathbf{t}^{\prime}=(t^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,t^{\prime}_{m})\in\mathbf{T}
be the minimal element (here minimality refers to the lexicographic order on
\displaystyle\mathbb{N}^{m}
) and consider
\displaystyle\textstyle\lambda=\sum_{i=1}^{m}t^{\prime}_{i}\varpi_{i}\in P
. For all
\displaystyle\mathbf{t}\in\mathbf{T}
, there at least one
\displaystyle 1\leq i\leq m
such that
\displaystyle t^{\prime}_{i}<t_{i}
. Hence
\displaystyle\textstyle\prod_{i=1}^{m}\bigl(q_{i}^{(\check{\alpha}_{i},\lambda)};t_{i}\bigr)_{q_{i}}=\prod_{i=1}^{m}\left(q_{i}^{t^{\prime}_{i}};t_{i}\right)_{q_{i}}=0
, and the same is of course true after specialization. We conclude that
\displaystyle\textstyle\iota_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}(Y)1_{\lambda}=c_{\mathbf{t}^{\prime}}\prod_{i=1}^{m}\epsilon^{-\sigma(t^{\prime}_{i})(\check{\alpha}_{i},\lambda)}_{i}\left(q_{i}^{t^{\prime}_{i}};t^{\prime}_{i}\right)_{\epsilon_{i}}1_{\lambda}=c_{\mathbf{t}^{\prime}}\prod_{i=1}^{m}\epsilon^{-\sigma(t^{\prime}_{i})t^{\prime}_{i}}_{i}1_{\lambda}
which equals
\displaystyle 0
by assumption on
\displaystyle Y
. Hence
\displaystyle c_{\mathbf{t}^{\prime}}=0
, a contradiction. Finally we prove the claim for
\displaystyle\Lambda=P
, since any
\displaystyle\Gamma^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\Lambda$}}_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{h})
is a subalgebra of
\displaystyle\Gamma^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{h})
. Take a system of representatives
\displaystyle\{\mu_{0},\ldots,\mu_{s}\}
of
\displaystyle P/Q
where
\displaystyle\mu_{0}=0
; then the elements
\displaystyle Y(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{t},\mathbf{0})K_{\mu_{i}}
form an
\displaystyle A
-basis of
\displaystyle\Gamma^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{h})
. If
\displaystyle\textstyle Y=\sum_{i,\mathbf{t}}c_{i,\mathbf{t}}Y(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{t},\mathbf{0})K_{\mu_{i}}
is sent to
\displaystyle 0
in
\displaystyle\hat{\mathbf{U}}_{\epsilon}
, the result for
\displaystyle Q
just proven yields
\displaystyle\textstyle\sum_{i=0}^{s}c_{i,\mathbf{t}}\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}^{D(\mu_{i},\lambda)}=0
for all
\displaystyle\mathbf{t}
and
\displaystyle\lambda\in P
. By direct inspection of root systems
[
18
, Chap. 6]
, we see that the elements
\displaystyle\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{s}
can be choosen among the fundamental weights:
\displaystyle\mu_{i}=\varpi_{j_{i}}
for some
\displaystyle j_{i}
. Fix an arbitrary
\displaystyle\mathbf{t}
; for
\displaystyle\lambda=0
we get
\displaystyle\textstyle\sum_{i=0}^{s}c_{i,\mathbf{t}}=0
while for
\displaystyle\lambda=\alpha_{j_{k}}
we get
\displaystyle\textstyle\epsilon^{d_{j_{k}}}c_{k,\mathbf{t}}+\sum_{i\neq k}c_{i,\mathbf{t}}=0
. The unique solution to this system of
\displaystyle s+1
linear equations with
\displaystyle s+1
variables is
\displaystyle c_{i,\mathbf{t}}=0
for all
\displaystyle i
.
∎

B.2.
Proof of Lemma
4.8
.

By specialization of the perfect pairing (
162
), the specialized basis elements
\displaystyle\varphi_{b}^{\epsilon}:=\varphi_{b}\otimes_{A}1_{\epsilon}
of
\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}
and
\displaystyle b_{\epsilon}:=b\otimes_{A}1_{\epsilon}
of
\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{U}}}_{\epsilon}
, where
\displaystyle b\in\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{B}}}
, are in duality. Therefore there is a perfect (multiplier) Hopf pairing
\displaystyle\langle\text{-},\text{-}\rangle_{\epsilon}^{\vbox{\hbox{\,\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}\,}}}:{\mathcal{O}}_{\epsilon}\,\times\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{U}}}_{\epsilon}\>\to{\mathbb{C}}
which can be extended to
\displaystyle\hat{\mathbf{U}}_{\epsilon}
as for (
162
). Using the monomorphism
\displaystyle\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}\hookrightarrow\hat{{\mathbf{U}}}_{\epsilon}
from Lemma
B.1
, it is related to the pairing
\displaystyle\langle.,.\rangle_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}:\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}\times\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}\to\mathbb{C}
as follows:

(167)

\langle\varphi,x^{+}yx^{-}\rangle_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}=\sum_{\mu\in P}\langle\varphi,x^{+}y_{\mu}x^{-}\rangle_{\epsilon}^{\vbox{\hbox{\,\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}\,}}},\quad\text{with }y_{\mu}=y1_{\mu}\in\mathbb{C}1_{\mu}.

Then, given
\displaystyle X\in\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm Q}$}}
in the right kernel of
\displaystyle\langle\text{-},\text{-}\rangle_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
, embed it as an element
\displaystyle\textstyle\hat{X}=\sum_{b\in\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\mathbf{B}}}}X_{b}b_{\epsilon}\in\hat{{\mathbf{U}}}_{\epsilon}
. Note that
\displaystyle\langle\varphi_{b}^{\epsilon},\hat{X}\rangle^{\vbox{\hbox{\,\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}\,}}}_{\epsilon}=X_{b}
. Hence each coefficient
\displaystyle X_{b}
is zero, so is
\displaystyle\hat{X}
, and therefore
\displaystyle X=0
.

Appendix C
Injectivity of
\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}^{\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}}

This appendix proves the last argument of Proposition
5.9
:

Proposition C.1
.

The morphism
\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}^{\epsilon}:\mathcal{L}_{1,0}^{\epsilon}\to\mathcal{H}_{\epsilon}
is an embedding.

Proof.

We proceed similarly as for
[
14
, Th. 3.11]
, which treats the case of generic
\displaystyle q
. The only novel point is to use the specialization of (
164
) and(
165
), to get weight spaces
\displaystyle{}_{\mu}\!\left(\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}\right)_{\nu}
and a decomposition
\displaystyle\textstyle\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}=\bigoplus_{\mu,\nu\in P}{}_{\mu}\!\left(\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}\right)_{\nu}
. It implies decompositions

\mathcal{L}_{1,0}^{\epsilon}=\bigoplus_{\lambda,\sigma\in P}{{}_{\lambda}(\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon})_{\sigma}}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\epsilon}\ ,\ \mathcal{H}_{\epsilon}=\bigoplus_{\lambda,\sigma\in P}{{}_{\lambda}(\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon})_{\sigma}}\otimes U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}.

Let us spell out the main steps to conclude. In the notations of
[
14
, Th. 3.13]
, the map
\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}^{\epsilon}\colon\mathcal{L}_{1,0}^{\epsilon}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{\epsilon}
, which is obtained by restriction of
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}
from
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}})
to
\displaystyle A_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
and then specialization to
\displaystyle q=\epsilon
with
\displaystyle q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}=\epsilon^{\overline{D}}
as in (
93
), satisfies

\Phi_{1,0}^{\epsilon}(\beta\otimes 1)=\epsilon^{(\lambda,\sigma)}\beta\otimes K_{\lambda+\sigma}+\bigoplus_{(\lambda^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})<(\lambda,\sigma)}{{}_{\lambda^{\prime}}(\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon})_{\sigma^{\prime}}}\otimes U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}},\quad\forall\beta\in{{}_{\lambda}(\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon})_{\sigma}},

where
\displaystyle\leq
is the partial order on
\displaystyle P^{2}
defined by
\displaystyle(\lambda^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})\leq(\lambda,\sigma)
if and only if
\displaystyle\lambda-\lambda^{\prime}\in Q_{+}
and
\displaystyle\sigma-\sigma^{\prime}\in Q_{+}
. For any non-zero
\displaystyle x\in\mathcal{L}_{1,0}^{\epsilon}
one can find
\displaystyle(\lambda,\sigma)
maximal for the order
\displaystyle\leq
, and a finite set
\displaystyle I_{\lambda,\sigma}
, such that

\Phi_{1,0}^{\epsilon}(x)\in\epsilon^{(\lambda,\sigma)}\sum_{i\in I_{\lambda,\sigma}}\beta_{\lambda,\sigma,i}\otimes K_{\lambda+\sigma}\Phi_{0,1}^{\epsilon}(\alpha_{\lambda,\sigma,i})+\bigoplus_{(\lambda^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})\neq(\lambda,\sigma)}{{}_{\lambda^{\prime}}(\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon})_{\sigma^{\prime}}}\otimes U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}},

where
\displaystyle\bigl(\beta_{\lambda,\sigma,i}\bigr)_{i\in I_{\lambda,\sigma}}
is a family of linearly independent elements and
\displaystyle\alpha_{\lambda,\sigma,i}\neq 0
for at least one
\displaystyle i\in I_{\lambda,\sigma}
. Since
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}^{\epsilon}
is injective
[
12
, Cor. 2.25]
and
\displaystyle K_{\lambda+\sigma}
is invertible, we conclude that
\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}^{\epsilon}(x)\neq 0
. This proves injectivity of
\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}^{\epsilon}
.
∎

Appendix D
Proof that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
is a domain

This appendix proves Proposition
5.21
. Since the morphism
\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}:\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}\to\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{\epsilon}^{\otimes g}\otimes(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{\otimes n}
is injective (Prop.
5.9
), it is enough to show:

Corollary D.1
.

The algebra
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{\epsilon}^{\otimes g}\otimes(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{\otimes n}
is a domain.

Proof.

It is an adaptation of the proof of
[
14
, Prop. 5.7]
, which treats the case of generic
\displaystyle q
. We construct an algebra filtration
\displaystyle\mathcal{T}
on
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{\epsilon}^{\otimes g}\otimes(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{\otimes n}
such that the associated graded algebra
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{T}}(\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{\epsilon}^{\otimes g}\otimes(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{\otimes n})
is quasi-polynomial over
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}^{\otimes g}\cong\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}(G^{\times g})
. The latter algebra does not have non-trivial zero divisors, by the same arguments as for Theorem
4.12
(2). Then the claim follows from general results (see e.g.
[
61
, §1.2.9]
).

The construction of
\displaystyle\mathcal{T}
is the same as in
[
14
, Prop. 5.7]
, replacing the two-sided Heisenberg doubles
\displaystyle\mathcal{HH}_{q}
with the modified ones
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{q}
from (
5.4
), and taking specialization at
\displaystyle q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}=\epsilon_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}
.

Let us spell out the changes. In
[
14
, Prop. 5.7]
we produced an algebra filtration
\displaystyle\mathcal{G}
of
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{q}
, such that the associated graded algebra
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{G}}(\mathcal{H}_{q})
is the quasi-polynomial ring over
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}
generated over
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}
by
\displaystyle\overline{E_{\beta_{i}}}
,
\displaystyle\overline{F_{\beta_{i}}}
,
\displaystyle\overline{K_{\nu}}
(with
\displaystyle 1\leq i\leq N
,
\displaystyle\nu\in P
) satisfying the relations

(168)

\begin{array}[]{lll}\overline{E_{\beta_{i}}}\,\overline{E_{\beta_{j}}}=q^{(\beta_{i},\beta_{j})}\overline{E_{\beta_{j}}}\,\overline{E_{\beta_{i}}},&\overline{F_{\beta_{i}}}\,\overline{F_{\beta_{j}}}=q^{(\beta_{i},\beta_{j})}\overline{F_{\beta_{j}}}\,\overline{F_{\beta_{i}}}\ \ \mathrm{for}\ i<j,&\overline{E_{\beta_{i}}}\,\overline{F_{\beta_{j}}}=\overline{F_{\beta_{j}}}\,\overline{E_{\beta_{i}}},\\[5.0pt]
\overline{K_{\mu}}\,\overline{E_{\beta_{j}}}=q^{(\mu,\beta_{j})}\overline{E_{\beta_{j}}}\,\overline{K_{\mu}},&\overline{K_{\mu}}\,\overline{F_{\beta_{j}}}=q^{-(\mu,\beta_{j})}\overline{F_{\beta_{j}}}\,\overline{K_{\mu}},&\overline{K_{\mu}}\,\overline{K_{\nu}}=\overline{K_{\mu+\nu}}.\end{array}

and

(169)

\overline{E_{\beta_{k}}}\,{{}_{V}\phi^{i}_{j}}={{}_{V}\phi^{i}_{j}}\,\overline{E_{\beta_{k}}},\quad\overline{F_{\beta_{k}}}\,{{}_{V}\phi^{i}_{j}}=q^{-(\beta_{k},\epsilon_{j})}{{}_{V}\phi^{i}_{j}}\,\overline{F_{\beta_{k}}},\quad\overline{K_{\nu}}\,{{}_{V}\phi^{i}_{j}}=q^{(\nu,\epsilon_{j})}{{}_{V}\phi^{i}_{j}}\,\overline{K_{\nu}}.

By keeping the same notation,
\displaystyle\mathcal{G}
extends trivially to a filtration
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{G}}
on
\displaystyle\textstyle\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{q}
. Indeed, by (
5.4
) it has the additional generators
\displaystyle\widehat{K_{\lambda}}
; we give them degree
\displaystyle 0
in
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{G}}
, and then the third relation in (
129
) implies

(170)

\widehat{K_{\nu}}\,{{}_{V}\phi^{i}_{j}}x=q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}^{-(\nu,\epsilon_{i})}{{}_{V}\phi^{i}_{j}}x\,\widehat{K_{\nu}},

and the analogous relations for the cosets
\displaystyle\overline{\widehat{K_{\nu}}}
of the elements
\displaystyle\widehat{K_{\nu}}
in
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\widehat{\mathcal{G}}}(\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{q_{\scalebox{0.5}{D}}})
. All this is valid also for the specialization
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{\epsilon}
.

As in
[
14
, Proposition 5.7]
one can then combine the filtration
\displaystyle\mathcal{G}
on the copies of
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{\epsilon}
with a filtration
\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{DCK}}
on the copies of
\displaystyle U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}}
to get an algebra filtration
\displaystyle\mathcal{T}
on
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{\epsilon}^{\otimes g}\otimes(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{\otimes n}
such that
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{T}}\bigl(\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{\epsilon}^{\otimes g}\otimes(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{\otimes n}\bigr)=\mathrm{gr}_{\widehat{\mathcal{G}}}(\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{\epsilon})^{\otimes g}\otimes\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{DCK}}}(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{\otimes n}
. By (
168
)-(
169
)-(
170
),
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{T}}\bigl(\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{\epsilon}^{\otimes g}\otimes(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{\otimes n}\bigr)
is quasi-polynomial over
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}^{\otimes 2g}
, as desired.
∎

Remark D.2
.

We used above that the algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}^{\otimes(2g)}\cong\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}(G^{\times(2g)})
does not have non-trivial zero divisors. It it is also a Noetherian ring (since it is a finite
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{O}_{e})
-module and
\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\mathcal{O}_{e})\cong\mathcal{O}(G)
is a Noetherian ring), and it is a maximal order (
[
31
]
, see Theorem
4.12
). Then the fact proved above that
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{T}}\bigl(\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{\epsilon}^{\otimes g}\otimes(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{\otimes n}\bigr)
is quasi-polynomial over
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}^{\otimes(2g+n)}\cong\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}(G^{\times(2g+n)})
and the following result imply that
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{\epsilon}^{\otimes g}\otimes(U_{\epsilon}^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle{\rm P}$}})^{\otimes n}
is a maximal order
[
61
, Th. 5.1.6]
: if
\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}
be a filtered ring whose associated graded ring
\displaystyle{\rm gr}(\mathfrak{A})
is a Noetherian domain which is a maximal order in its quotient ring, then
\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}
has the same properties.
